# The Dead and the Dustbowl: Volume Two
## The Red Earth Requiem
### By Gabriel Simon

---

The Dead and the Dustbowl - Volume Two

By Gabriel Simon



Story One: The Hooverville Sickness

Chapter One: The Breadline

The winter of 1932 did not arrive in Chicago with a gentle dusting of snow; it descended like a heavy iron vault door slamming shut over the city. The wind blowing off Lake Michigan was a vicious, living thing, carrying microscopic shards of ice that scoured the exposed skin of the thousands of men and women who had nowhere left to hide. The Great Depression had hollowed out the industrial heart of the nation, leaving behind a sprawling, rusted carcass of silent factories and shuttered steel mills. 

For the people who had lost everything—their jobs, their homes, their dignity—there was only the sprawling, chaotic expanse of the Hooverville. 

It was a city of the dispossessed, built on the frozen mud of a vacant industrial lot on the South Side. It stretched for miles, a chaotic, improvised labyrinth of shacks constructed from corrugated tin, rotting lumber scavenged from demolished buildings, flattened cardboard boxes, and heavy tar paper. The air in the shantytown was permanently thick with the acrid, choking smoke of hundreds of fires burning in rusted oil drums. The residents burned whatever they could find to stave off the lethal cold: old tires, broken furniture, damp newspaper, and sometimes, the very walls of abandoned shacks. 

Arthur stood in a line that stretched for three frozen city blocks. He was a man whittled down by three years of relentless failure. Once a proud puddler at a major steel mill, his broad shoulders now slumped under the weight of a threadbare, patched wool coat that offered no real protection against the biting wind. His boots, once thick leather work boots, were worn completely through the soles, insulated only by folded layers of the Chicago Tribune he stuffed inside every morning. 

He shuffled forward two inches. The man in front of him, a gaunt, skeletal figure wrapped in a filthy woolen blanket, coughed violently. It was a wet, rattling sound that seemed to tear at the man's lungs. 

"Keep moving," a gruff voice yelled from the front of the line. 

It was the soup kitchen. A local Catholic charity had set up a massive cast-iron cauldron over a roaring wood fire near the edge of the Hooverville, serving a thin, watery broth of potatoes and salted cabbage. It was the only meal most of these men would eat all day. 

Arthur buried his chin deeper into the collar of his coat, trying to conserve whatever meager body heat he had left. The line moved with an agonizing, glacial slowness. The despair in the air was palpable, thicker than the black smoke from the oil drums. These were men who had built skyscrapers, laid miles of railroad track, and poured the foundations of the modern world. Now, they were reduced to begging for a tin cup of hot water. 

At the front of the line, shielded slightly from the wind by a canvas tarp strung between two wooden poles, Pearl worked with a frantic, rhythmic efficiency. 

Pearl was twenty-two, a volunteer from the parish who had defied her wealthy parents to come down to the Hooverville. She wore a heavy wool sweater and a thick apron stained with months of spilled soup and desperate tears. Her hands were raw, red, and cracked from the constant exposure to the freezing air and the hot, soapy water used to wash the tin cups. 

"Next," Pearl said, her voice hoarse from hours of shouting over the wind. 

She took a battered tin cup from a trembling, dirt-caked hand. She dipped the heavy iron ladle into the cauldron, stirring from the bottom to ensure she caught at least a few pieces of boiled potato, and poured the steaming broth into the cup. She handed it back, offering a small, exhausted smile that the man did not return. He simply grabbed the cup and hurried away, shielding the precious heat with his body. 

"Next," Pearl repeated. 

The man who stepped forward was the gaunt, skeletal figure who had been standing in front of Arthur. 

Pearl noticed immediately that something was deeply wrong with him. The cold in the Hooverville was brutal, but the shivering that wracked this man's frame was unnatural. It was a violent, spastic tremor that shook him from his boots to his head. His skin, visible beneath the grime, was a sickly, translucent grey, pulled tight over his cheekbones. His eyes were wide, bloodshot, and completely unfocused. 

He did not hold out a cup. He just stood there, swaying slightly in the freezing wind, staring blankly at the steaming cauldron. 

"Sir?" Pearl asked gently, reaching across the wooden folding table. "Do you have a cup? I can find you a bowl if you've lost yours."

The man opened his mouth. He tried to speak, but the only sound that emerged was a wet, ragged hiss. 

Behind him, Arthur stepped forward, recognizing the severity of the man's condition. "He's been like this for an hour, miss. I think he's got the pneumonia. He's freezing to death on his feet."

"Father Thomas!" Pearl yelled over her shoulder, turning toward the small wooden shack where the priest was organizing the meager supplies. "We need a blanket and a cot! We have a sick man!"

She turned back to the man, reaching out to gently touch his arm. "It's going to be alright, sir. We'll get you inside by the stove."

The moment her fingers brushed the coarse wool of his blanket, the man collapsed. 

He did not fall backward; he pitched forward, crashing heavily into the wooden folding table. The impact knocked the heavy iron cauldron slightly askew, spilling a gallon of scalding potato broth onto the frozen mud. 

The crowd in the line immediately surged backward, shouting in alarm to avoid the boiling liquid. 

"Help him!" Pearl screamed, dropping her ladle and scrambling around the table to reach the fallen man. 

Arthur did not hesitate. Despite his own exhaustion and freezing limbs, the instinct to help a fellow worker overrode everything else. He stepped out of the line, dropping to his knees in the freezing mud beside Pearl. 

The man was convulsing violently now. His back arched off the frozen ground, his heels drumming a rapid, chaotic rhythm against the mud. 

"Hold his head!" Arthur yelled over the rising panic of the crowd. "Don't let him bite his tongue!"

Arthur grabbed the man by the shoulders, trying to pin him to the ground to prevent him from injuring himself. The man possessed a sudden, terrifying strength. His muscles were rigid, locked in an agonizing spasm. 

Pearl knelt by the man's head, reaching out with her raw, cracked hands to cradle his skull. 

As she touched his temples, the man's eyes rolled completely back into his head, exposing the jaundiced whites. His jaw clamped shut with an audible crack of teeth. 

Then, the terrifying symptom appeared. 

It was not the white foam of a seizure. Seeping from the corners of the man's tightly clamped lips was a thick, viscous fluid. It was as black as engine oil, carrying a sickeningly sweet, rotting odor that instantly overpowered the smell of the spilled soup and the woodsmoke. 

"What is that?" Pearl gasped, pulling her hands back in horror. 

Arthur stared at the black fluid, a cold knot of pure dread forming in his stomach. Rumors had been whispering through the hobo camps and the freight trains for months. Stories from the Dustbowl states, from Oklahoma and Kansas. Stories of a sickness that came with the black blizzards, a disease that did not just kill, but changed the dead into something else. The government had dismissed it as mass hysteria brought on by starvation and despair. The newspapers called it "Dust Pneumonia."

But the men who rode the rails knew better. 

"Get back, miss," Arthur said, his voice suddenly sharp and hard. He let go of the man's shoulders and scrambled backward in the mud. "Get away from him right now!"

"We can't just leave him!" Pearl protested, reaching forward again. 

Another volunteer, a young, earnest seminary student named Peter, rushed out of the supply shack holding a thick wool blanket. He dropped to his knees beside the convulsing man, ignoring Arthur's warning. 

"I've got him," Peter said, reaching down to wrap the blanket around the man's thrashing torso. 

The man's convulsions stopped instantly. The sudden stillness was more terrifying than the violent spasms. 

For a fraction of a second, the only sound was the howling wind off Lake Michigan. 

Then, the man's eyes snapped open. They were no longer bloodshot or unfocused. The irises had clouded over entirely, turning a milky, dead white. 

He moved with a sudden, jerky, mechanical speed that defied his emaciated frame. He lunged upward, his hands clamping onto the young seminary student's forearms with an iron grip. 

"Hey!" Peter yelled, struggling to pull back. "Let go!"

The dead man did not speak. He pulled Peter forward with terrifying strength, his jaw unhinging slightly. 

He drove his rotting, black-stained teeth directly into the thick meat of Peter's neck, tearing through the wool collar and severing the carotid artery in a single, brutal bite. 

A fountain of bright red arterial blood sprayed across the frozen mud, splashing across Pearl's apron and Arthur's face. 

Peter screamed, a gurgling, wet sound of absolute agony, thrashing wildly as the dead man clamped down harder, tearing a massive chunk of flesh from his throat. 

The breadline erupted into pure, unadulterated chaos. Hundreds of starving, desperate men screamed in terror, surging backward, trampling each other in a frantic attempt to escape the horrifying violence. The folding table was smashed to splinters. The cast-iron cauldron was overturned completely, sending the rest of the boiling soup hissing into the frozen mud. 

Arthur scrambled to his feet, wiping the hot blood from his face. The rumors were true. The sickness from the plains had not been contained. The government had lied. 

The nightmare had reached the sprawling, densely packed slums of Chicago. And there was nowhere left to run.



Chapter Two: The Stampede

The sheer, concussive force of the panic was more dangerous in the first few seconds than the monster at the center of it. 

When the bright arterial blood sprayed across the frozen mud, the collective mind of the breadline shattered. The men standing closest to the wooden folding table did not try to fight. The unnatural speed of the gaunt man, the milky white of his dead eyes, and the sheer brutality of the bite triggered a primal, absolute terror. 

They surged backward. It was a blind, frantic contraction of human muscle. 

Arthur was caught in the leading edge of the shockwave. He was shoved hard in the chest by a burly dockworker, his boots slipping on the freezing mud and the spilled, scalding potato soup. He went down hard, the wind knocked from his lungs as his shoulder struck the frozen earth. 

All around him, the world dissolved into a cacophony of shrieks and the sound of tearing metal. 

The Hooverville was not designed for mass movement. It was a haphazard labyrinth built on desperation, with narrow, winding alleys barely wide enough for two men to pass shoulder-to-shoulder. When three thousand terrified people tried to move in the same direction simultaneously, the frail architecture of the shantytown simply gave way. 

Walls of corrugated tin buckled and screamed as the mob pressed against them. Roofs made of flattened cardboard boxes and heavy tar paper collapsed, burying the people inside under a deluge of rotting timber and freezing dirt. 

Arthur rolled violently to his left, narrowly avoiding the heavy, hobnailed boot of a fleeing man that would have crushed his skull. He scrambled to his knees, his hands sinking into the freezing, bloody mud. 

"Pearl!" he roared, his voice tearing at his throat. 

He looked back toward the ruins of the charity station. The canvas tarp had been torn down, blanketing the overturned iron cauldron. 

Pearl was still on the ground. She had been knocked backward by the initial surge, her heavy wool sweater soaked in the blood of the young seminary student. She was staring in catatonic horror at the nightmare unfolding inches from her boots. 

The gaunt man—the thing that had been a starving man only moments ago—had not stopped. It had torn a massive chunk of flesh from Peter's throat, exposing the pale white cartilage of the trachea. 

Peter was dying rapidly, his hands clutching uselessly at his ruined neck, his blood painting the snow red. 

But the dead man was not finished. It chewed the raw meat with a frantic, mechanical speed, the black fluid leaking from its jaws mixing with the bright red blood. It swallowed, the thick muscle of its throat convulsing, and immediately lunged again. 

This time, it grabbed the ankle of a heavy-set man who had tripped over the shattered folding table. The dead man bit completely through the heavy leather of the man's boot, severing the Achilles tendon with a sound like a snapping violin string. 

The heavy-set man screamed, a high, piercing wail that cut through the roar of the stampede. 

Arthur did not think. The deep-seated solidarity of the working class, the instinct to protect those who were trying to help, propelled him forward. 

He lunged back toward the chaotic epicenter, dodging the frantic bodies of fleeing men. He reached Pearl, grabbing her roughly by the thick fabric of her sweater. 

"Get up!" he screamed directly into her face. "You have to get up now!"

Pearl blinked, the shock momentarily breaking. She looked at Arthur, her eyes wide with a terror that defied comprehension. She scrambled to her feet, her boots slipping in the mud. 

"Peter," she sobbed, reaching a trembling hand toward the dying student. "We have to take Peter."

"He's gone," Arthur said grimly, grabbing her wrist and pulling her away. "If we stay, we're next."

He dragged her backward, retreating away from the ruined charity station. 

The situation was deteriorating exponentially. The heavy-set man whose ankle had been bitten was thrashing wildly on the ground, his screams drawing the attention of the dead man. But the dead man did not bite him again. It simply stood up, its milky white eyes scanning the chaotic crowd. 

And then, the true horror of the sickness revealed itself. 

Peter, the seminary student, whose throat had been completely torn open, suddenly stopped thrashing. His hands fell away from his neck. The arterial spurting slowed to a sluggish, dark trickle. 

For a terrifying five seconds, he lay perfectly still. 

Then, his eyes snapped open. The warm, brown irises were gone, replaced instantly by the same milky, dead white as the gaunt man. Peter pushed himself off the frozen mud with a jerky, unnatural speed. His head flopped loosely to the side, the severed muscles of his neck unable to support the weight of his skull, but he did not fall. 

He let out a wet, gurgling hiss and launched himself at the back of a fleeing woman. 

"It's spreading," Arthur breathed, the chilling reality of the Dustbowl rumors finally sinking in. The infection wasn't just a sickness; it was a contagion. And it was moving through the densely packed Hooverville faster than a wildfire. 

"We need to get to the city," Pearl yelled, panic finally giving way to a desperate need for survival. "The police line. At the viaduct. They have barricades."

Arthur shook his head, his eyes scanning the chaotic, shifting maze of tin shacks. 

Above the roar of the stampede and the screams of the dying, a new sound cut through the freezing air. It was a sharp, rapid, mechanical stutter. 

*Tat-tat-tat-tat-tat-tat.*

It was the unmistakable sound of a Thompson submachine gun. 

"The cops aren't letting anyone out," Arthur said, his voice hard. "They're firing into the crowd to hold the quarantine line. The city is sealed."

"Then where do we go?" Pearl asked, shivering violently in the freezing wind. 

"The rail yard," Arthur decided, pulling her toward a narrow alley between two massive walls of stacked, rotting lumber. "It's on the eastern edge of the camp. If we can get to the tracks, we might find a freight train heading out. It's the only way out of Chicago."

They plunged into the narrow alley, leaving the massacre at the breadline behind them. But the Hooverville was a labyrinth, and the dead were already multiplying. 



Chapter Three: The Maze of Scrap

The Hooverville was burning. 

It was not a single, centralized fire. In the chaotic, blind panic of the stampede, dozens of the rusted fifty-gallon oil drums used for heating had been violently overturned. The burning coals and flaming trash spilled across the frozen mud, instantly igniting the highly flammable materials that made up the shantytown. The walls of flattened cardboard, the dry, rotting lumber, and the roofs of heavy black tar paper went up like kindling soaked in kerosene. 

The wind whipping off Lake Michigan fanned the flames, turning small localized fires into raging infernos that jumped from shack to shack with terrifying speed. 

Arthur and Pearl navigated the narrow, winding alleyway, moving as fast as the debris-choked path would allow. The air was a toxic soup of thick, black smoke and the metallic tang of blood. Visibility was reduced to a few feet, turning the labyrinth of scrap into a claustrophobic nightmare. 

"Keep your head down," Arthur coughed, his hand gripping Pearl's wrist tightly as they squeezed past the rusting carcass of a stripped Model A Ford. "The smoke will kill us before they do."

Pearl tied the bottom half of her bloodstained apron around her nose and mouth, a meager filter against the choking ash. She was trembling violently, the sheer scale of the horror threatening to shatter her sanity. Everywhere she looked, the shadows in the smoke seemed to move with that jerky, unnatural speed. 

They emerged from the narrow alley into a slightly wider clearing. A group of about a dozen desperate people were frantically trying to reinforce the walls of a large, comparatively sturdy shack built from stolen railroad ties and corrugated iron. 

"Help us push this!" a frantic, middle-aged man yelled, straining his back against a massive, waterlogged wooden crate. He was trying to wedge it against the only door of the shack. 

"You can't stay here," Arthur yelled over the roar of the spreading fires. He stepped toward the man, his eyes wide with urgency. "The walls won't hold. Once a dozen of those things press against the tin, the frame will snap. You have to run for the rail yard."

"We can't run!" a woman inside the shack screamed, clutching two small, terrified children to her chest. "There are too many of them out there! They're eating people! We have to hide!"

"They don't hunt with their eyes," Arthur pleaded, the bitter truth he had heard from the boxcar drifters finally making horrific sense. "They hunt by sound. By smell. They'll smell the blood on the wind. They'll hear you breathing. A wooden box won't stop them; it's just a trap."

The middle-aged man ignored him, his face a mask of blind panic. He finally jammed the heavy crate against the door, effectively sealing himself and his family inside the dark, windowless structure. 

Arthur took a step forward, intending to pull the crate away, to force them to run. 

But he never got the chance. 

The wall of the shack directly adjacent to the clearing did not just collapse; it exploded outward. 

A shower of rusted corrugated tin and splintered wood rained down onto the frozen mud. Stumbling through the wreckage were three figures. 

They were covered head-to-toe in a slurry of wet ash and dark, coagulated blood. One of them was a woman missing her left arm completely below the elbow. The bone protruded sharply from the torn sleeve of her dress, dripping that same terrifying, black fluid. The other two were men, their faces chewed into unrecognizable masks of raw meat. 

The three infected did not pause to orient themselves. The moment they cleared the wreckage, their milky white eyes locked onto the people standing in the clearing. They let out a synchronized, guttural hiss that vibrated deep in Arthur's chest, and lunged. 

"Run!" Arthur roared at the people barricaded inside the shack, knowing it was too late. 

He didn't have a weapon. His worn-out boots and patched coat offered zero protection against teeth that could tear through a leather collar. 

Arthur scrambled backward, his hands frantically searching the debris littering the mud. His fingers closed around a heavy, rusted length of iron rebar, nearly four feet long and thick as a broom handle. It was incredibly heavy, throwing him slightly off balance as he hoisted it with both hands. 

The woman missing her arm was the fastest. She cleared the distance with a terrifying, uneven sprint, her jaw snapping wildly as she reached for Pearl. 

Arthur stepped into her path, bringing the heavy iron rebar around in a brutal, two-handed swing. 

The rusted iron connected solidly with the side of the woman's head. The impact was sickeningly wet, the skull collapsing inward with a dull crunch. The force of the blow threw her completely off her feet, sending her crashing into the mud. She did not get back up. 

But the swing had cost Arthur his balance. The heavy rebar pulled him forward, leaving his left flank completely exposed. 

The second infected man crashed into Arthur's shoulder, tackling the steelworker to the frozen ground. 

Arthur dropped the rebar, bringing both of his thick, calloused hands up to grab the dead man's throat. The creature's jaw snapped furiously, inches from Arthur's face, the foul-smelling black saliva dripping onto his cheek. The physical strength of the corpse was terrifying, a relentless, mechanical pressure that didn't wane or tire. 

Arthur's muscles burned as he strained to hold the snapping teeth away. He could hear the third infected moving toward them, its boots dragging heavily in the mud. He was pinned. He was dead. 

Suddenly, a massive wall of intense heat washed over him. 

Pearl had not frozen in terror. Seeing Arthur go down, she had acted with a desperate, feral instinct. She grabbed the edge of a nearby, roaring oil drum fire with her bare, cracked hands. Ignoring the searing pain as the hot iron blistered her palms, she threw her entire body weight against it. 

The heavy fifty-gallon drum tipped over. 

A cascade of burning, white-hot coals, flaming trash, and boiling grease poured directly over the third infected man and splashed heavily onto the back of the corpse pinning Arthur. 

The dead did not feel the heat, but their bodies were not immune to it. The dry, filthy rags they wore ignited instantly. The flesh itself, desiccated by starvation and the strange alchemy of the sickness, caught fire like dry kindling. 

The corpse on top of Arthur suddenly convulsed, its muscles reacting to the sudden, massive tissue damage. Arthur used the momentary distraction, violently shoving the burning creature off him and scrambling backward in the mud. 

The two infected were entirely engulfed in flames. They did not scream. They did not try to stop, drop, and roll. They simply stood up, their bodies functioning as human torches, and continued to stumble forward, their jaws still snapping blindly. 

"Get up!" Pearl yelled, her voice cracking. Her hands were bright red and blistering. 

Arthur grabbed the heavy iron rebar from the mud and pulled himself to his feet. 

The fire from the overturned drum had already spread to the shack built of railroad ties. The heavy, creosote-soaked wood ignited with a terrifying roar, sending a massive pillar of black smoke into the sky. Inside, the trapped family began to scream. 

Arthur looked at the burning shack, the heavy rebar trembling in his hands. He wanted to help. He wanted to break the crate away and drag them out. 

But a dozen more figures were already emerging from the thick smoke at the end of the alley, drawn by the fire and the screams. 

"We can't save them," Arthur said, his voice breaking with the sheer, unbearable weight of the truth. "We have to move."

He turned his back on the burning shack, his heart turning to stone in his chest, and ran into the blinding smoke, Pearl right beside him.



Chapter Four: The Orphan

The heat of the inferno was a physical weight, pressing against their backs like a massive, burning hand. The roaring of the flames was deafening, a continuous, rushing sound that completely drowned out the shrieks of the dying and the guttural hisses of the infected. 

Arthur and Pearl ran blindly through the choking, oily black smoke. They were navigating purely by instinct, letting the slope of the frozen mud and the changing direction of the bitter wind guide them east, toward the vast, open expanse of the Chicago rail yard. 

Arthur's lungs burned with every breath. The smoke was thick with the toxic fumes of burning tar paper, melting rubber tires, and incinerated trash. His grip on the heavy iron rebar was slick with his own sweat and the coagulated blood of the woman he had crushed. He kept his left hand firmly clamped around Pearl's wrist, terrified that if he let go for even a fraction of a second, the swirling smoke would swallow her completely. 

They burst through a wall of particularly dense smoke and stumbled into a small, relatively clear area. It was a slight depression in the mud, sheltered by the rusted, overturned hull of an old delivery truck. The fire had not yet reached this sector, though the heat radiating from the adjacent alleys was unbearable. 

"Stop," Arthur gasped, dropping to his knees behind the rusted axle of the truck. "We need to catch our breath. The smoke is going to suffocate us."

Pearl collapsed beside him, her chest heaving violently. Her apron was ruined, her face smeared with soot and ash. She rested her blistered, red hands gently on her knees, wincing in pain. 

Arthur peered around the heavy rubber tire of the truck. Through the drifting haze, he could see the towering, rusted silhouettes of massive grain silos and the skeletal steel frames of electrical towers. 

"The yard," Arthur said, his voice raspy and raw. "We made it. We're on the eastern edge. The fence is just beyond those silos."

Before Pearl could answer, a small, muffled sound drifted from beneath the rusted undercarriage of the delivery truck. 

It wasn't a hiss. It wasn't the shuffling drag of a dead foot. It was the sharp, hitched intake of breath that only came from absolute, uncontrolled sobbing. 

Arthur tightened his grip on the rebar and leaned slowly under the rusted chassis. 

Huddled against the frozen mud, curled into a tight, trembling ball beneath the oil pan, was a young boy. He couldn't have been older than seven or eight. He wore a man's suit jacket that was three sizes too large, the sleeves rolled up to his elbows. He was clutching a small, filthy cloth sack to his chest. 

"Hey," Arthur said softly, lowering the iron bar. 

The boy flinched violently, pressing his small back against the rusted metal of the truck. His eyes were wide, white circles in a face completely blackened by soot. 

Pearl leaned under the truck beside Arthur. The maternal instinct, stripped bare by the horrors of the last hour, flared instantly. 

"It's okay, sweetheart," Pearl whispered, her voice surprisingly steady. She ignored the excruciating pain in her blistered hands and reached out, her palm open and empty. "We aren't going to hurt you. What's your name?"

The boy stared at her hand for a long moment, then looked at the blood drying on Arthur's canvas coat. 

"Leo," he whispered, his voice trembling so hard the word barely formed. 

"Where are your folks, Leo?" Arthur asked, scanning the surrounding smoke for any sign of adult companions. 

"Ma," Leo choked out, tears finally spilling over his ash-caked cheeks. "The crowd... the people running. They pushed her down. She didn't get up. Then the sick people came."

Arthur closed his eyes for a fraction of a second. He knew exactly what had happened. In the blind panic of the stampede, the weak were trampled instantly. And the dead did not discriminate; they fell upon the injured just as eagerly as the slow. 

"We have to take him," Pearl said, turning to Arthur. It wasn't a question or a request; it was an absolute statement of fact. 

Arthur looked at the small, terrified boy, and then at the massive, iron rebar in his hand. Survival in this new, nightmare world was going to be a matter of brutal mathematics. Speed and silence were their only advantages. A terrified, exhausted seven-year-old was a massive liability. 

But Arthur was a puddler, a man who had spent his life standing before blast furnaces that could melt a man to the bone in seconds. He knew the value of human life. The apocalypse had stripped away the laws of the nation, but it had not stripped away his soul. To leave the boy under the truck was a death sentence, a cruelty that Arthur simply could not abide. The sickness was stealing their humanity; he refused to surrender it willingly. 

"Come here, son," Arthur said, his voice rough but undeniably kind. He held out his large, calloused hand. 

Leo hesitated, then scrambled out from beneath the rusted chassis, clutching Arthur's thick fingers. 

"We have to move fast," Arthur told the boy, looking him squarely in the eyes. "You stay right between us. You hold onto Pearl's sweater. Do not let go, and do not make a sound. Understand?"

Leo nodded rapidly, grabbing a fistful of Pearl's heavy wool sweater. 

"Let's go," Arthur said, standing up and raising the iron rebar. 

They left the shelter of the overturned truck and moved quickly toward the towering silhouettes of the grain silos. 

The path to the rail yard was not clear. The stampede had pushed a massive wave of debris toward the eastern fence, creating a chaotic bottleneck of flattened shacks, overturned carts, and abandoned belongings. 

Worse, the bottleneck had trapped several of the infected. 

As Arthur, Pearl, and Leo approached the twisted pile of debris, four figures emerged from the smoke. They were trapped against a wall of collapsed timber, their rotting hands clawing mindlessly at the wood, entirely driven by the noise of the distant stampede. 

When the three survivors approached, the scent of fresh sweat and warm blood reached the dead. 

The four infected turned simultaneously, their milky eyes locking onto Arthur. They let out that synchronized, guttural hiss and charged. 

"Stay behind me!" Arthur roared. 

He didn't wait for them to close the distance. He charged forward, using his momentum to swing the heavy iron rebar like a baseball bat. 

He caught the first infected—a man wearing a shredded business suit—squarely in the chest. The impact shattered the man's ribs, but the blow lacked the precision to destroy the brain. The corpse simply stumbled backward, its jaw still snapping. 

The second infected, a large woman in a torn apron, lunged past Arthur, her rotting hands reaching directly for Leo. 

Pearl stepped in front of the boy. She didn't have a weapon, but the ground was littered with the sharp debris of the ruined shantytown. She snatched up a large, jagged piece of corrugated tin roofing. 

As the dead woman lunged, Pearl drove the jagged edge of the tin directly into the creature's face. The sharp metal bit deeply into the rotting flesh of the cheek, slicing through the muscle and wedging itself against the jawbone. 

The corpse thrashed violently, its snapping teeth missing Pearl's neck by inches. 

Arthur recovered his balance, reversing his grip on the rebar. He brought the heavy iron bar down in a brutal, overhead strike. 

The iron crushed the skull of the infected woman, driving her instantly to the frozen mud. 

Arthur didn't pause. He spun around, swinging the rebar in a wide arc that caught the remaining two infected in the knees. The heavy iron shattered their brittle, decaying bones, sending them collapsing into the mud. They continued to thrash and crawl, their jaws snapping wildly, but their mobility was destroyed. 

"Go!" Arthur yelled, pointing toward the towering chain-link fence that separated the Hooverville from the massive switching yard. 

They scrambled over the chaotic pile of debris, their boots slipping on the frozen mud and scattered trash. 

They reached the fence. It was ten feet tall, topped with three strands of rusted barbed wire. Beyond it lay a vast, chaotic expanse of steel rails, towering signal gantries, and dozens of massive freight trains. 

"We have to climb," Arthur said, dropping his heavy iron rebar. He turned to the fence, lacing his fingers through the freezing metal chain-link. 

The fire behind them was roaring louder, the thick black smoke beginning to billow over the top of the fence. And the shrieks of the infected were getting closer.



Chapter Five: Over the Wire

The chain-link fence was a formidable obstacle. It stood ten feet high, the galvanized steel mesh rusted from years of exposure to the bitter Chicago winters. At the very top, three thick strands of jagged barbed wire were strung taut between angled metal brackets, designed explicitly to keep desperate men out of the commercial rail yard. 

Now, it was the only thing keeping them from being burned alive or torn to pieces. 

"Leo, you're first," Arthur ordered, his voice barely audible over the roaring inferno that was rapidly consuming the final row of shacks just thirty yards away. 

Arthur interlaced his thick, calloused fingers, creating a stirrup with his hands. He pressed his back firmly against the vibrating chain-link to brace himself. 

"Step up," he told the boy. 

Leo didn't hesitate. The absolute terror of the situation had stripped away any childhood apprehension. He placed his small, scuffed shoe into Arthur's hands. Arthur hoisted him upward with a grunt of exertion, his worn-out boots finding purchase in the frozen mud. 

Leo grabbed the rusty diamonds of the chain-link mesh and began to scramble upward with the agility of a frightened squirrel. He reached the top bracket quickly. 

"Careful of the wire!" Pearl screamed, looking up. The heat from the approaching fire was already blistering the back of her neck. 

Leo threw his right leg over the top strand of barbed wire. The rusted barbs caught the loose fabric of his oversized suit jacket, tearing a long, jagged rip in the wool, but the thick fabric protected his skin. He swung his other leg over, straddling the top bar for a terrifying second, before dropping into the darkness on the other side. 

He landed heavily in the thick gravel of the rail yard with a sharp crunch, rolling to his feet immediately. 

"I'm over!" Leo yelled back, his voice small and thin. 

"Your turn," Arthur said, turning to Pearl. He held his hands out again. 

Pearl looked at her own hands. The palms were a mass of angry, raised blisters from where she had shoved the burning oil drum. To grab the rusted, freezing metal of the fence would be excruciating. 

"I can't," Pearl sobbed, a wave of despair finally breaking through her adrenaline. "My hands... the metal..."

"You can, and you will," Arthur said, his voice hard, lacking any trace of pity. Pity was a luxury that got people killed. "If you stay on this side of the fence, you die. I am not leaving you here. Step up."

A massive, burning section of a cardboard roof was lifted into the air by the thermal updraft of the fire, spinning wildly before crashing into the mud just ten feet away. A shower of white-hot embers rained down on them. 

Pearl squeezed her eyes shut, fighting back a scream, and placed her boot into Arthur's hands. 

He launched her upward. Pearl grabbed the chain-link. The agony in her blistered palms was immediate and blinding. She gasped, her grip faltering for a terrifying second. 

"Don't let go!" Arthur roared, pushing his hands against the soles of her boots to keep her pinned to the fence. "Climb!"

Pearl forced herself to move, ignoring the tearing of her blisters. She scrambled upward, her boots finding the small gaps in the mesh. She reached the top just as the first wave of the horde emerged from the blinding smoke. 

There were at least a dozen of them. They were not running; they were driven forward by the sheer, unthinking momentum of the panic behind them and the roaring flames. Several of them were actively burning, their clothes engulfed in fire, but they did not stop. Their milky eyes locked onto Arthur, standing exposed at the base of the fence. 

"Arthur!" Pearl screamed from the top of the fence, the barbed wire tearing at her heavy wool sweater. 

"Get over the wire!" Arthur yelled, spinning around to face the oncoming horde. 

He didn't have his iron rebar. He had dropped it to lift Leo. He was completely unarmed. 

The first infected, a man wearing a burning, soot-stained butcher's apron, slammed into the chain-link fence directly beside Arthur. 

The heavy steel mesh bowed outward with a terrifying, metallic groan. 

Then, three more infected hit the fence. Then five more. 

They did not try to climb. They simply pressed their dead weight against the steel, their hands reaching blindly through the diamond-shaped gaps in the mesh, their jaws snapping at the air. 

The fence was old, the support posts rusted at the base. Under the immense, collective weight of a dozen bodies, the entire section of the fence began to lean inward toward the rail yard. 

Pearl was still straddling the top bar, struggling to untangle her sweater from the barbed wire. As the fence began to buckle, she lost her balance, tipping backward toward the Hooverville side—directly over the reaching, snapping jaws of the burning horde. 

"No!" Arthur roared. 

He threw himself against the buckling chain-link. He grabbed the thick steel tension wire running horizontally across the middle of the fence and pulled backward with every ounce of his tremendous strength. His muscles screamed in protest, his boots sliding in the frozen mud. 

He was essentially playing tug-of-war with a dozen corpses, using his own body weight as an anchor to keep the fence upright long enough for Pearl to escape. 

"I'm stuck!" Pearl cried, the barbed wire firmly snagged in the thick wool of her sweater. 

"Tear it!" Arthur screamed, his teeth gritted in absolute agony. The fence was leaning further; he couldn't hold it for much longer. "Leave the sweater! Drop!"

Pearl stopped trying to unhook the fabric. She twisted her body violently to the right, letting the rusted barbs tear the heavy wool completely down the back. The sweater ripped free. 

She tumbled forward, falling the ten feet into the rail yard. She hit the thick, sharp gravel hard, crying out as her shoulder took the brunt of the impact. 

"I'm down!" Pearl yelled. 

Arthur didn't hesitate. He let go of the tension wire. 

The sudden release of his anchoring weight caused the entire section of the fence to buckle inward violently. 

Arthur scrambled up the collapsing mesh, his boots finding purchase in the gaps. It was like trying to climb a falling ladder. He reached the top bracket just as the rusted steel support post completely snapped at the base with a sound like a gunshot. 

The ten-foot fence began to fall forward, crashing down onto the gravel of the rail yard, taking Arthur with it. 

He didn't have time to navigate the barbed wire. He threw himself forward into the empty air, diving over the jagged top of the collapsing structure. 

He hit the thick gravel of the rail yard with a bone-jarring thud, rolling twice to bleed off the momentum. 

A second later, the massive section of the chain-link fence crashed down into the gravel right behind him. 

The dozen infected who had been pressing against it fell forward with the fence, landing heavily in the dirt on the Hooverville side. The heavy steel mesh now lay flat on the ground, a tangled, impassable barrier of rusted wire and screaming, burning bodies. 

Arthur lay on his back in the freezing gravel, staring up at the dark, starless sky. His chest heaved violently. The air here was still thick with smoke from the burning shantytown, but the immediate threat of the teeth had been halted by the collapsed steel. 

"Arthur," Pearl gasped, crawling over the sharp stones toward him. Leo was right behind her, his small face streaked with tears and soot. 

"I'm alive," Arthur grunted, pushing himself up onto his elbows. He looked at his hands. They were scraped raw by the gravel and the chain-link, but he was unbitten. 

He looked past Pearl, into the vast, dark expanse of the Chicago rail yard. 

Hundreds of massive steel boxcars sat on the parallel tracks, silent and imposing in the freezing night. The only light came from the towering, distant floodlights of the switching towers. 

"We made it out of the camp," Arthur said, his voice a raspy whisper. "Now we just need to find a train heading west. Any train."



Chapter Six: The Iron Giants

The air in the rail yard was fundamentally different from the Hooverville. The stench of burning trash, rotting food, and unwashed bodies was replaced by the sharp, industrial odors of heavy diesel grease, hot iron, and creosote-soaked wooden ties. The ground beneath their boots was no longer soft, freezing mud, but deep, uneven layers of sharp, crushed granite ballast. 

It was a world of iron giants. 

Hundreds of massive steel boxcars, flatbeds, and coal hoppers sat idle on dozens of parallel tracks, creating a vast, dark labyrinth that dwarfed the three survivors. The silence here was heavy, punctuated only by the distant, echoing metallic clang of a coupling being joined somewhere miles away in the dark. 

"We can't stay still," Arthur whispered, his teeth beginning to chatter. 

The adrenaline of the escape was fading rapidly, replaced by the brutal reality of the Chicago winter. The temperature was dropping below freezing, and the wind whipping off Lake Michigan funneled down the narrow corridors between the trains like a wind tunnel. 

Pearl was shivering violently. Without her heavy wool sweater, which she had sacrificed to the barbed wire on the fence, she was wearing only a thin, sweat-soaked cotton blouse beneath her ruined, bloodstained apron. Her lips were turning a faint, terrifying shade of blue. 

Arthur did not hesitate. He stripped off his heavy, patched canvas work coat. 

"Put this on," he said, holding the coat out to her. 

"Arthur, no," Pearl protested through chattering teeth, looking at his thin, faded flannel shirt. "You'll freeze to death. You don't have enough meat on your bones to stay warm."

"I spent ten years standing in front of open blast furnaces," Arthur lied, forcing a small, grim smile. "I've got heat stored up in my marrow. Put the coat on, Pearl. We need you alert."

Pearl didn't have the strength to argue further. She slipped her arms into the oversized coat, instantly grateful for the residual body heat trapped within the heavy canvas. Arthur rolled the sleeves up so she could use her hands, wincing as he accidentally brushed against the angry red blisters on her palms. 

Leo was huddled against Pearl's leg, clutching his small cloth sack to his chest. He was shivering, but the oversized suit jacket he wore offered some protection. 

"We need a train heading outbound," Arthur said, wrapping his arms around his own chest and rubbing his forearms rapidly. "West, south, it doesn't matter. Just out of the city."

They began to walk, stepping carefully over the heavy steel rails and the thick wooden ties. 

The darkness between the trains was absolute. The towering floodlights mounted on the massive steel gantries hundreds of feet above cast harsh, pool-like illumination over the main switching tracks, but left the long alleys between the parked freight cars in deep, impenetrable shadow. 

They moved in complete silence, their boots crunching softly on the granite ballast. Arthur led the way, his eyes constantly scanning the dark gaps between the cars, half-expecting to see the milky white eyes of an infected staring back at him. 

But the dead had not yet breached the fence in large numbers. The real danger in the rail yard was not the infected. 

"Wait," Arthur hissed suddenly, throwing his arm out to stop Pearl and Leo. 

He pressed himself flat against the cold, riveted steel side of a massive boxcar. He gestured for Pearl and Leo to do the same. 

A heavy, rhythmic crunch of boots on gravel was approaching from the opposite direction. 

Arthur peered carefully around the corner of the boxcar. 

Walking down the center of the track were two men. They were not wandering aimlessly like the infected. They moved with purpose and brutal authority. They wore heavy wool overcoats and fedoras, their collars turned up against the wind. 

One of them held a massive, heavy-duty flashlight, sweeping the bright beam across the undercarriages of the parked trains. The other man held a Winchester 1897 pump-action shotgun, resting the barrel casually on his shoulder. 

"Pinkertons," Arthur breathed, the word carrying a weight of deep, ingrained dread. 

The Pinkerton National Detective Agency provided ruthless, private security for the major railroad companies. To the desperate men riding the rails during the Depression, the Pinkertons—"bulls," as they were called—were a terror worse than starvation. They were known to beat hoboes half to death, throw them off moving trains, and shoot trespassers without hesitation or consequence. 

"Are they looking for the sick?" Pearl whispered, pressing her back hard against the freezing steel. 

"They don't care if you're sick or healthy," Arthur replied grimly. "They're guarding the rolling stock. If they catch us in the yard, they won't ask questions. They'll shoot."

The sweeping beam of the heavy flashlight cut through the darkness, passing over the tracks just twenty feet ahead of them. 

"Under the car," Arthur ordered, pointing to the narrow gap beneath the boxcar they were hiding behind. "Now. Flat on your stomachs."

Pearl dropped to her knees, wincing as her blistered hands hit the sharp gravel, and slid beneath the massive steel undercarriage of the train. Leo scrambled in right behind her. Arthur followed, pressing his face into the freezing dirt and granite. 

The heavy, rhythmic crunch of the Pinkertons' boots grew louder. 

Arthur held his breath, praying the cold would mask the visible plumes of their exhalations. 

The two bulls stopped directly adjacent to the boxcar. The bright beam of the flashlight swept over the gravel, illuminating the heavy steel wheels and the massive iron axles just inches from Arthur's face. 

"I heard the cops set up a firing line on the viaduct," one of the Pinkertons said, his voice gruff and bored. The unmistakable *snick-clack* of a pump-action shotgun being chambered echoed loudly. "Must be a riot in the Hooverville."

"Let them burn the whole damn slum to the ground," the other man replied, striking a match to light a cigarette. "Less trash for us to clean out of the boxcars tomorrow."

The glowing ember of the cigarette dropped onto the gravel just three feet from Arthur's nose. 

The men stood there for a terrifying, agonizing minute, finishing the cigarette in silence, before finally continuing their patrol down the track. 

Arthur waited until the sound of their boots completely faded before signaling Pearl to move. 

They scrambled out from under the train, covered in grease and freezing dirt. 

"We can't just wander around in the dark," Pearl whispered, her voice shaking with cold and fear. "We'll freeze, or the bulls will find us."

"Listen," Arthur said, holding up a finger. 

Over the whistling of the wind and the distant sirens from the city, a deep, rhythmic sound vibrated through the cold air. It was a heavy, mechanical chuffing, accompanied by the low, mournful wail of a steam whistle. 

"An engine," Arthur said, relief flooding his exhausted face. "A massive steam locomotive, fired up and ready to pull. That's our ticket out."

They turned toward the sound, plunging deeper into the maze of iron giants.



Chapter Seven: The Coal Hopper

The source of the mechanical roar was a Leviathan of black iron and boiling water. 

They crept to the edge of the central switching lane. Sitting on the main outbound track, hissing massive plumes of white steam into the freezing night air, was a massive 2-8-2 Mikado steam locomotive. It was a beast of pure industrial power, its massive driving wheels taller than Arthur, the heavy steel coupling rods slick with dark grease. 

Behind the locomotive and its massive coal tender stretched a seemingly endless line of freight cars, fading into the darkness of the yard. 

"It's heading south," Arthur whispered, his teeth chattering uncontrollably in his thin flannel shirt. He pointed to the massive green signal lamp glowing high on the gantry above the tracks. "The switches are aligned for the outbound mainline."

"Can we get in one of the boxcars?" Pearl asked, her voice trembling from the cold. She pulled Arthur's heavy canvas coat tighter around her shoulders, keeping Leo pressed firmly against her side. 

Arthur scanned the line of cars. The heavy sliding doors of the wooden boxcars were all shut tight, secured with thick metal shipping seals and heavy padlocks. 

"No," Arthur said grimly. "We don't have tools to cut the padlocks, and trying to break the seals with a rock will make too much noise."

He gestured toward the rear of the train. Pacing slowly down the gravel trackbed alongside the freight cars were three more Pinkerton bulls. They were shining their flashlights at the heavy padlocks, ensuring none of the cars had been tampered with before departure. 

"If the bulls catch us trying to break into a sealed car, they won't even yell," Arthur warned. "They'll just open up with those shotguns and let us bleed out in the gravel."

"Then how do we ride?" Pearl asked, despair creeping back into her voice. "We can't walk out of the city. We'll freeze to death before morning."

Arthur's eyes tracked back toward the front of the train, stopping at the cars immediately behind the massive locomotive's tender. 

They were not sealed boxcars. They were open-top coal hoppers, filled to the brim with jagged, dusty lumps of black anthracite. 

"We ride the open cars," Arthur said, pointing to the hoppers. "The bulls don't bother checking the coal cars because no hobo in his right mind would ride in them during winter. It's freezing, you're entirely exposed to the wind, and the coal dust suffocates you."

"But it's out of the city," Pearl realized. 

"It's out of the city," Arthur confirmed. 

Before they could plan their approach, a sound like a physical blow ruptured the silence of the yard. 

The locomotive's massive steam whistle shrieked, a deafening, mournful wail that vibrated in their bones. The heavy brass bell mounted near the smokestack began to clang rapidly. 

The train was leaving. 

"Now!" Arthur roared, abandoning all stealth. 

He broke cover, sprinting across the open gravel toward the idling coal hoppers. Pearl grabbed Leo's hand and ran right behind him, her boots slipping on the sharp ballast. 

With a massive hiss of releasing steam, the locomotive's pistons engaged. The massive driving wheels spun rapidly for a second, screeching loudly against the icy steel rails, showering the gravel with sparks before finally finding purchase. 

The heavy couplings down the line slammed together in a rapid, echoing chain reaction. The train lurched forward, slowly beginning to roll. 

Arthur reached the second coal hopper just as the heavy steel wheels began to turn. 

"Lift him!" Arthur yelled, grabbing the rusted iron ladder welded to the side of the towering black car. 

Pearl grabbed Leo around the waist and hoisted him upward. Arthur reached down with his left arm, his muscles burning in the freezing air, and grabbed the back of the boy's oversized suit jacket. He pulled Leo up with a single, brutal heave, tossing him over the high steel lip of the car onto the pile of black coal inside. 

The train was accelerating, the massive *chuff-chuff-chuff* of the steam exhaust growing more rapid. The steel wheels were grinding loudly against the rails, the speed increasing with every passing second. 

"Give me your hand!" Arthur yelled down to Pearl. 

Pearl reached up, her blistered, agonizingly raw hands stretching toward him. 

The train was moving at a fast jog now. Pearl had to run alongside the massive, rolling steel wall of the hopper, her boots slipping dangerously in the loose gravel near the tracks. If she tripped, the massive iron wheels would crush her instantly. 

Arthur leaned dangerously far over the side of the ladder. He grabbed her right wrist, his calloused fingers locking around her forearm like a vise. 

"Jump!" he roared. 

Pearl pushed off the gravel with all her remaining strength. Arthur pulled upward, using his legs and his back, hauling her dead weight up the side of the moving train. 

She slammed against the freezing, riveted steel of the hopper, gasping in pain as the metal bruised her ribs. She scrambled over the high lip and tumbled down the slope of jagged anthracite, landing in a heap next to Leo. 

Arthur was still clinging to the exterior ladder. The train was picking up speed rapidly, moving faster than a man could run. The freezing wind whipped at his thin flannel shirt, stealing the heat from his body. 

He swung his leg up, aiming for the top rung of the ladder. 

His frozen fingers slipped. 

He fell backward, his grip failing completely. For a terrifying fraction of a second, he was falling toward the blur of grinding steel wheels and sharp granite ballast. 

He didn't hit the ground. 

He had managed to hook the crook of his right elbow over the top rung of the ladder at the absolute last second. The sudden deceleration wrenched his shoulder joint with agonizing force, but it held. 

He dangled against the side of the accelerating train, his legs swinging wildly over the rushing ground. 

He gritted his teeth, letting out a primal, ragged yell, and used his remaining strength to muscle himself upward. He dragged his heavy torso over the top lip of the steel hopper and fell forward, crashing face-first into the pile of jagged coal. 

He lay there in the dark, surrounded by the black dust, his chest heaving violently. 

Above him, the night sky began to blur as the massive steam locomotive picked up speed, thundering out of the rail yard, leaving the burning, infected Hooverville behind in the dark.



Chapter Eight: The Long Night

The escape from the Hooverville was a victory measured only in geography. They had left the immediate slaughter of the city behind, but the environment they had traded it for was actively trying to kill them. 

The open-topped coal hopper offered zero protection from the elements. As the massive steam locomotive accelerated to its cruising speed of fifty miles per hour, the bitter winter wind whipping off the flat Illinois plains became a physical weapon. It howled over the high steel lip of the car, driving a relentless blizzard of sharp, black coal dust into their eyes, noses, and lungs. 

The ride was violently unstable. The heavy steel car bucked and swayed on its suspension, the jagged lumps of anthracite shifting and grinding beneath them with every curve of the track. 

"Dig!" Arthur roared over the deafening clatter of the iron wheels on the rails and the rhythmic chuffing of the steam exhaust. "Dig into the coal! If we stay on top, the wind will freeze the blood in our veins!"

He dropped to his knees, ignoring the sharp edges of the coal tearing at his thin flannel trousers. He began to frantically scoop the heavy, black rocks away with his bare, bleeding hands, creating a shallow trench near the forward wall of the hopper, trying to use the steel bulkhead to block the worst of the headwind. 

Pearl followed his lead, her blistered hands screaming in agony as she dug. She pulled Leo into the shallow depression with her, curling her body around his small frame, using Arthur's heavy canvas coat to form a makeshift tent over the two of them. 

Arthur lay beside them, pressing his back against the freezing steel of the bulkhead. He was shivering so violently that his teeth clicked together with an audible rhythm. Without the heavy coat, the wind was slicing straight through his sweat-soaked flannel shirt. He curled his knees to his chest, tucking his chin down, trying to conserve whatever meager warmth his body still possessed. 

The hours stretched into an agonizing blur of cold, noise, and darkness. 

The train thundered south, passing small, darkened farming towns that offered no signs of life. Occasionally, the harsh beam of the locomotive's headlamp would illuminate an empty crossing or a desolate water tower, before plunging them back into the absolute black of the rural night. 

Inside the trench of coal, Pearl kept her arms wrapped tightly around Leo. 

"Are you okay, sweetheart?" she whispered, her voice raspy from the choking dust. 

Leo didn't answer. He was shivering, a rapid, fine tremor that shook his entire body. 

Pearl shifted slightly, reaching a hand under the heavy canvas coat to check the boy. She touched his cheek. 

She pulled her hand back as if she had been burned. 

The air temperature in the open hopper was easily ten degrees below freezing, but Leo's skin was radiating a terrifying, dry heat. He was burning up with a massive fever. 

"Arthur," Pearl said, her voice rising slightly in panic. She nudged the steelworker's shoulder. "Arthur, wake up."

Arthur grunted, forcing his eyes open. His eyelashes were coated in thick black dust. "What?" he croaked. 

"It's Leo," Pearl said, pushing the coat back slightly. "He's sick. He has a terrible fever. He's sweating through his clothes."

Arthur forced his stiff, freezing muscles to move. He leaned over the trench, pressing his dirty palm against the boy's forehead. 

Pearl was right. The boy's skin was unnaturally hot. Even in the darkness, Arthur could see the sheen of sweat coating Leo's face. 

But it wasn't just sweat. 

Arthur wiped his thumb across the boy's cheek and held it up close to his eyes, squinting in the dim ambient starlight. 

The moisture was thick, viscous, and completely black. 

The cold dread that formed in Arthur's stomach had nothing to do with the winter wind. It was the same dread he had felt standing in the breadline, watching the gaunt man collapse against the soup table. 

"Leo," Arthur said, his voice dropping to a hard, low whisper that cut through the noise of the train. "Leo, look at me."

The boy slowly opened his eyes. They were not milky white yet, but the whites were heavily bloodshot, and the pupils were blown wide, giving him a vacant, glassy stare. 

"Are you hurt, son?" Arthur asked, the urgency bleeding into his tone. "Did you get hurt when the crowd pushed your mother? Did someone... did someone bite you?"

Leo squeezed his eyes shut, a fresh wave of tears mixing with the black sweat on his cheeks. He shook his head weakly, clutching his small cloth sack tighter to his chest. 

"No," Leo whispered. "Nobody bit me."

"Are you sure?" Pearl asked, her own panic rising as she remembered the gaunt man in the line. "Leo, you have to tell us the truth. We can help you, but you have to tell us."

Leo nodded, his chin trembling. "No bites. I promise."

Arthur stared at the boy. The symptoms were undeniable. The massive, sudden fever. The black fluid seeping from the pores. It was the sickness from the plains. 

But if he hadn't been bitten, how had he caught it? 

Arthur thought back to the rumors from the drifters. The sickness wasn't just spread by the teeth of the dead. It came with the dust storms. It was in the air, in the dirt, in the water. That was why they called it Dust Pneumonia before the dead started walking. It was an infection that incubated in the lungs before it killed the brain. 

Leo had been living in the Hooverville for months, breathing the filthy, stagnant air, eating whatever scraps he could find. His small, malnourished body had simply succumbed to the environmental exposure. 

"Arthur," Pearl whispered, reading the grim realization on his face. "What is it?"

"He's got it, Pearl," Arthur said, his voice flat and dead. "The sickness. It's in him."

Pearl stared at the boy in horror, instinctively pulling away slightly before her humanity overrode her fear. She pulled him back against her chest. "But he wasn't bitten. He said he wasn't bitten."

"He didn't have to be," Arthur replied, staring out at the passing darkness. "It's a disease, Pearl. The bite just speeds it up. But if you're weak, if you're starving... it just takes you."

They were trapped. They were riding in an open steel box moving at fifty miles an hour, miles from any town or hospital, with a seven-year-old boy who was slowly, inevitably dying. 

And when he died, the real nightmare would begin.



Chapter Nine: The Pale Dawn

The dawn did not bring warmth. It merely replaced the absolute black of the night with a flat, pale grey light that illuminated the stark, frozen desolation of the southern Illinois farmlands. The fields stretching out on either side of the tracks were blanketed in a thin, unbroken layer of dirty snow, turning the landscape into a vast, lifeless monochrome. 

Inside the coal hopper, the grim reality of their situation was fully visible for the first time. 

Arthur sat against the freezing steel bulkhead, his knees pulled tight to his chest. His face, hands, and the thin flannel of his shirt were completely coated in a thick layer of fine black coal dust, giving him the appearance of a miner who had spent a week underground. His lips were chapped and bleeding, his eyes bloodshot from exhaustion and the relentless, scouring wind. 

But his gaze was fixed entirely on Pearl and the boy. 

Leo's condition had deteriorated rapidly in the hours before sunrise. He was no longer shivering. He lay perfectly still against Pearl's chest, swaddled in the heavy canvas coat. His breathing had become a shallow, wet rattle that sounded like dry leaves scraping across a concrete sidewalk. 

The black fluid that seeped from his pores had dried into a crusty, tar-like substance that stained his face and the collar of the oversized suit jacket. 

Pearl rocked him gently, her blistered hands resting lightly on his back. She was humming a soft, tuneless melody, her eyes staring blankly at the pile of jagged anthracite in the center of the hopper. She knew the boy was dying. She knew what would happen next. But the raw, stubborn core of her humanity simply refused to let her abandon him while his heart still beat. 

Arthur slowly reached into the pile of coal beside him. His fingers closed around a piece of iron he had found during the night. It was a heavy, rusted railroad spike, nearly eight inches long and thick as a man's thumb. It had likely fallen into the hopper from a loading gantry miles away. 

He gripped the heavy, square head of the spike in his right hand. The metal was freezing, biting into his raw palm, but the weight of it was strangely comforting. It was a tool. A solution to a problem that had no other answer. 

"Pearl," Arthur said softly, his voice rasping painfully in his dry throat. 

Pearl stopped humming, but she did not look at him. She just held the boy tighter. 

"Pearl, look at me," Arthur insisted, pushing himself up to his knees in the coal dust. 

She slowly turned her head. Her face was a mask of soot, tear streaks, and profound, crushing despair. 

"His breathing is slowing," Arthur said, holding the heavy iron spike loosely at his side, making sure she saw it. "The rattle is deep in the lungs. It won't be long now. You have to put him down and move to the other side of the car."

"He's just a little boy, Arthur," Pearl whispered, her voice cracking. "He's just a child."

"He was a child," Arthur corrected her gently but firmly. "The sickness doesn't care. When his heart stops, that boy is gone. Whatever wakes up in that body is just the disease wearing his face. You know this. We saw it in the breadline."

"I can't just leave him on the rocks to die," she sobbed, burying her face in the thick canvas of the coat. 

"I'm not asking you to leave him to die," Arthur said, shifting his weight slightly, preparing his muscles. "I'm asking you to step away before he turns. If you are holding him when his eyes open, he will tear your throat out before I can swing this spike. Please, Pearl. Put him down."

Pearl looked down at Leo's soot-stained face. The boy's chest rose in a shallow, stuttering breath. Then, it fell. 

It did not rise again. 

The wet, rattling sound stopped completely. The sudden absence of the noise was deafening, somehow louder than the rhythmic chuffing of the steam engine and the clatter of the iron wheels on the rails. 

"He's gone," Pearl whispered, a fresh wave of tears cutting clean tracks through the coal dust on her cheeks. 

"Move away," Arthur said, his voice dropping to a hard, urgent command. He raised the iron spike, his knuckles turning white as he gripped the heavy metal. 

Pearl hesitated, her fingers lingering on the boy's shoulder. 

Suddenly, the world around them shifted violently. 

A deafening, metallic screech erupted from beneath the coal hopper. The entire train shuddered, a massive jolt traveling down the couplings that threw Arthur completely off balance. The massive steam locomotive at the front of the line had engaged its air brakes. 

Sparks cascaded from the iron wheels as the heavy brake shoes clamped down on the steel rims. The sudden deceleration was brutal, throwing Pearl forward into the pile of coal and sending Arthur crashing heavily against the side of the steel hopper. 

They were approaching a rural water stop or a switching station. The train was stopping. 

Arthur scrambled to recover his balance, shaking the dizziness from his head. He looked toward Pearl, ready to grab her and pull her away. 

But as the train screamed to a halt, throwing the heavy lumps of anthracite around the hopper like dice, Leo's eyes snapped open. 

There was no transition period. No moment of confusion or lingering humanity. The warm, frightened eyes of the orphan boy were gone, replaced instantly by two terrifying orbs of milky, dead white. 

The boy pushed himself off the jagged coal with a sudden, jerky speed that defied the rigor mortis setting into his tiny limbs. His jaw unhinged slightly, exposing teeth stained entirely black. He let out a high, piercing hiss that sounded like escaping steam. 

Pearl was still on her hands and knees, disoriented by the sudden braking of the train. She looked up just as the dead child lunged. 

Leo did not aim for her arms or her torso. Driven by the primal instinct of the sickness, he launched his small body directly at her exposed neck. 

Arthur roared, swinging the heavy railroad spike in a desperate, frantic arc. But the deceleration of the train had thrown him too far away. He was a fraction of a second too late. 

Leo collided with Pearl, his small hands grabbing the collar of the ruined blouse, his jaws snapping wildly as he buried his face into the soft flesh of her throat.



Chapter Ten: The End of the Line

The screech of the massive iron brake shoes against the steel wheels was a deafening, metallic shriek that echoed across the frozen Illinois plains. 

But it was not loud enough to drown out the sound of tearing flesh. 

Leo hit Pearl with the sudden, terrifying momentum of a coiled spring. His small hands, slick with dried black fluid, clamped onto the collar of Arthur's canvas coat that Pearl still wore. His jaw, unhinged and driven entirely by the blind hunger of the sickness, buried itself into the soft juncture where Pearl's neck met her shoulder. 

Pearl screamed, a high, piercing sound of absolute, unadulterated agony that tore through the freezing air. She threw her hands up, her blistered palms pressing against the dead boy's chest, trying desperately to push him away. 

But the physical strength of the infected was unnatural, defying the decay and starvation of the body it inhabited. Leo's jaw locked down, his rotting teeth slicing through the thin cotton of Pearl's blouse and sinking deep into the muscle and vein beneath. 

Arthur scrambled forward in the shifting, jagged coal. The brutal deceleration of the train had thrown him against the steel bulkhead, stunning him for a crucial, fatal second. 

"Get off her!" Arthur roared. 

He didn't hesitate. He didn't see the frightened seven-year-old orphan they had pulled from under the truck. He only saw the monster that had stolen the boy's face. 

Arthur swung the heavy iron railroad spike in a short, brutal, downward arc. 

The square head of the rusted spike connected squarely with the back of Leo's skull. The impact was sickeningly wet, a hollow crunch that snapped the boy's neck instantly and drove his face downward against Pearl's collarbone. 

Leo's small body went completely limp, sliding off Pearl's chest and collapsing into the black coal dust. He did not move again. 

The massive steam train finally shuddered to a complete halt with a heavy, metallic clang that rippled down the mile-long line of freight cars. The hissing of the steam exhaust was the only sound left in the freezing dawn. 

They had stopped at a desolate, towering wooden water tank sitting beside the mainline, miles away from any town or farm. 

Arthur dropped the bloody iron spike and fell to his knees beside Pearl. 

She was slumped against the side of the steel hopper, her hand clamped tightly over the right side of her neck. Bright red arterial blood was pulsing rapidly between her fingers, dripping down her wrist and staining the heavy canvas coat. 

Her face was ashen, the color draining from her cheeks with terrifying speed. She looked up at Arthur, her eyes wide with shock and a profound, crushing sorrow. 

"Arthur," she gasped, her voice barely a whisper. 

Arthur ripped the sleeve of his flannel shirt, intending to press it against the wound to stop the bleeding. "Keep the pressure on it, Pearl. I've got you. The bleeding isn't too deep, we can stop it."

Pearl slowly shook her head. She pulled her bloody hand away from her neck. 

The bite mark was horrific. A massive chunk of flesh had been torn away, exposing the pale white muscle and the rapidly pulsing carotid artery. But the blood loss wasn't the true horror. 

Seeping from the edges of the ragged wound, mixing with the bright red blood, was a thin, dark streak of the viscous black fluid. 

The infection. 

"You can't fix it, Arthur," Pearl said, a strange, terrifying calm settling over her voice. The adrenaline was fading, replaced by the cold, absolute certainty of the rules they had witnessed in the Hooverville. 

There was no cure. There was no immunity. If you were bitten, you turned. 

Arthur stared at the black fluid, his hands trembling violently. The heavy flannel cloth fell from his fingers onto the coal. He was a man who had fought a horde of the undead with a piece of rebar, who had risked his life to save a stranger and a child. But staring at the bite mark on Pearl's neck, he felt utterly, completely broken. 

"No," Arthur whispered, a single tear cutting a path through the soot on his cheek. "We made it out. We beat the fire. We beat the bulls. We made it."

"We did," Pearl said softly, forcing a small, bloodstained smile. "But I can't come with you the rest of the way."

She looked at the heavy iron railroad spike lying in the coal dust near Arthur's knee. 

"You know what happens next, Arthur," she said, her breathing becoming shallow and erratic. "The fever is going to start. Then I'll stop breathing. And then... then I won't be Pearl anymore."

"I can't," Arthur said, his voice breaking. He backed away from the spike, pressing himself against the freezing steel bulkhead. "I won't do it. I won't kill you."

"If you don't, I will kill you when I wake up," Pearl pleaded, her eyes filling with tears. "Please, Arthur. You are a good man. Don't let me become one of those things. Don't let me hurt you."

Arthur buried his face in his large, calloused hands, his shoulders shaking with silent, wracking sobs. The universe had stripped away everything—the laws, the jobs, the safety of the world. And now, it was demanding the last shred of his humanity. 

He couldn't do it. He couldn't lift the iron and crush the skull of the woman who had saved his life in the burning maze. 

Pearl watched him cry. She understood the depth of the burden she was asking him to carry. And she loved him for his inability to bear it. 

The fever was already beginning to burn in her veins. Her vision was blurring at the edges. She knew she had only minutes left before the sickness took control of her mind. 

"It's okay, Arthur," Pearl whispered. 

She used her remaining strength to push herself up onto her feet. The train was stationary. The lip of the steel coal hopper was ten feet above the sharp granite ballast of the trackbed. 

"Pearl, what are you doing?" Arthur asked, looking up, his eyes red and swollen. 

"Taking the choice away from you," she said. 

Before Arthur could stand, before he could reach out and grab her, Pearl threw her weight forward over the high steel lip of the hopper. 

She didn't try to climb down the rusted iron ladder. She simply let herself fall into the freezing morning air. 

Arthur heard the dull, sickening crunch as her body struck the sharp granite rocks below. 

He scrambled up the pile of coal and looked over the edge of the steel car. 

Pearl lay broken on the ballast, her neck twisted at an impossible angle. She was dead. The fall had severed her spinal cord and crushed her skull, destroying the brain instantly. The sickness would have nothing left to reanimate. 

She had saved him. Again. 

Arthur collapsed back into the freezing coal, entirely alone. The massive steam locomotive blew its whistle, the mournful sound echoing across the empty plains, before the train began to slowly roll forward once more, carrying the broken steelworker deeper into the nightmare of the American wasteland.



Story Two: The Getaway

Chapter One: The Boiling Radiator

The flathead V8 engine of the stolen 1932 Ford Coupe was screaming, a high-pitched, metallic whine that vibrated through the floorboards and rattled the fillings in Frank's teeth. The speedometer needle was pinned at sixty-five miles per hour, bouncing violently as the heavy tires slammed into the deep, sun-baked ruts of the Texas dirt road. 

It was August 1934, and the heat was a physical, oppressive force. The air shimmering off the cracked earth of the dead cotton fields looked like liquid glass. Inside the steel cabin of the Ford, with the windows rolled down, it felt like sitting inside a baking oven. 

"She's running too hot, Frank!" Lefty yelled over the roar of the engine and the howling wind. 

Lefty was slumped against the passenger side door, his face the color of spoiled milk. He was twenty-two years old, Frank's younger brother, and currently bleeding heavily onto the upholstery. The bank robbery in the small panhandle town of Muleshoe had been a disaster from the moment they walked through the double doors. They weren't professional criminals; they were desperate sharecroppers who had lost their land to the banks and the drought. They had walked in with unloaded revolvers, hoping the sheer threat would be enough. 

It wasn't. The bank manager had kept a loaded .45 under the counter. 

Lefty had taken a slug through the meaty part of his left thigh as they scrambled back out to the stolen Ford. 

"I can't slow down!" Frank roared back, gripping the wooden steering wheel so hard his knuckles were white. He constantly checked the rearview mirror, terrified of seeing the dust cloud of a Texas Ranger's cruiser rising on the horizon. "We ain't crossed the county line yet!"

"The radiator is going to blow!" Lefty insisted, pointing a trembling, bloodstained finger at the dashboard gauge. The needle on the temperature dial was buried deep in the red zone. "If the block cracks, we're walking!"

Frank ignored him, pressing the accelerator closer to the floor. The landscape whipping past the windows was a desolate blur of dying brown mesquite bushes, rusted barbed wire fences, and absolutely nothing else. The drought had killed this part of Texas years ago, leaving behind a hardpan desert that stretched out to the horizon in every direction. 

"Frank, please," Lefty groaned, his head lolling against the window frame. "I need water. My leg is burning up."

Frank glanced over at his younger brother. The blood had soaked completely through Lefty's canvas trousers and was pooling on the rubber floor mat. The stench of copper and sweat filled the stifling cabin. 

A sharp pang of guilt pierced through Frank's adrenaline. He had promised their mother he would look out for Lefty. He was the one who had convinced the boy that the Muleshoe bank was an easy score, a victimless crime against the institutions that had starved them. 

Now, Lefty was bleeding to death for seventy-two dollars and a handful of silver certificates sitting in a burlap sack on the back seat. 

"Hold on, kid," Frank said, his voice softening slightly. "We'll find a creek or a windmill. Just hold on."

Before Lefty could answer, a loud, concussive *BANG* echoed from beneath the long, louvered hood of the Ford. 

It wasn't a gunshot. It was the sound of pressurized metal giving way. 

Instantly, a massive geyser of scalding, rusty brown water and thick white steam erupted from the front of the car. The boiling fluid blasted across the windshield, blinding Frank completely. 

"Son of a bitch!" Frank yelled, slamming both feet onto the heavy brake pedal. 

The Ford went into a violent, sideways skid. The rear tires locked, tearing through the hard-packed dirt and kicking up a massive cloud of dust. The heavy car spun a full one-hundred-and-eighty degrees, throwing Lefty violently against the dashboard, before finally sliding to a halt in a shallow ditch on the side of the road. 

The sudden silence was absolute, broken only by the violent, wet hissing of the ruptured radiator and the ticking of the cooling metal block. 

Frank sat frozen behind the wheel for a long moment, staring blindly through the steam-covered windshield. The engine was dead. The car was dead. 

"Lefty?" Frank asked, his voice trembling. 

The younger brother groaned, pushing himself up from the floorboards. "I'm okay. But the car ain't."

Frank threw open the driver's side door and stepped out into the brutal Texas heat. The sun beat down on his shoulders like a physical weight. The dust kicked up by their skid was slowly settling back onto the dead earth, coating the black paint of the Ford in a fine, brown powder. 

He walked around to the front of the car. The heavy steel radiator cap had blown completely off, and the twisted metal of the radiator core was exposed, hissing steam into the dry air. The engine block was ruined. 

"We're walking," Frank announced grimly, walking to the passenger side and pulling the door open. 

Lefty looked out at the desolate landscape, his eyes wide with fear. "Walking where, Frank? We're fifty miles from nowhere. I can't walk fifty miles."

"You ain't going to have to," Frank said, trying to project a confidence he absolutely did not feel. He reached into the back seat and grabbed the burlap sack of cash, slinging it over his shoulder.
