He then reached under his seat and pulled out a heavy, blued-steel .38 Special revolver, checking the cylinder before tucking it into the waistband of his trousers. 

He leaned into the car and grabbed Lefty by the arm, hauling the younger man out into the heat. 

Lefty cried out in pain as his boots hit the dirt. He immediately collapsed against the side of the car, entirely unable to put weight on his left leg. The bullet wound was a dark, wet ruin in the center of his thigh. 

Frank draped Lefty's arm over his shoulder, taking the brunt of his brother's weight. 

"Look," Frank said, pointing a calloused finger out across the dying cotton fields. 

About a mile away, shimmering through the heavy, distorting heat waves, was the distinct silhouette of a small farmhouse. Beside it stood a tall, skeletal wooden windmill and a dilapidated barn. 

"There's a well," Frank said, his voice dry. "And maybe a truck. Or at least a horse. We just got to make it to the house."

Lefty squinted against the glaring sun. "It looks abandoned, Frank. Nobody's lived out here since the dust started."

"It don't matter if it's abandoned, as long as the well still draws," Frank replied. He adjusted his grip on Lefty's waist. "Come on. One step at a time."

They began to walk. 

The journey across the dead cotton field was agonizing. The ground was hard and cracked, forcing them to step high over the deep furrows. Every step sent a jolt of pure agony up Lefty's leg. Frank was pouring sweat, his thin cotton shirt clinging to his back like a second skin. 

The silence of the Texas plains was total. There were no birds, no insects, no wind. Just the sound of their own ragged breathing and the crunch of their boots on the dry earth. 

As they drew closer to the farmhouse, the details began to resolve through the heat haze. 

It was a small, two-story structure built of clapboard siding that had been scoured gray by the sand and wind. The porch roof was sagging dangerously, and several of the windows on the second floor were boarded up with heavy wooden planks. 

The wooden windmill stood motionless in the dead air. The barn doors hung open on rusted hinges, revealing a dark, empty interior. 

"It's dead," Lefty gasped, leaning heavily against Frank. "Nobody's home."

"Good," Frank said, wiping the sweat from his eyes. "That means nobody's going to point a shotgun at us when we pump the water."

But as they reached the edge of the dirt yard surrounding the farmhouse, Frank stopped. 

The hairs on the back of his neck prickled. The deep, instinctual warning system that every desperate man possessed suddenly flared to life. 

There was a rusted Ford Model T pickup truck parked beside the house. The tires were flat, and the paint was peeled, but that wasn't what caught Frank's attention. 

Lying in the dirt directly beneath the front bumper of the truck, completely covered in a thick layer of brown dust and buzzing with massive black flies, was the carcass of a large hound dog. 

The dog hadn't died of thirst or starvation. Its side had been torn open, the ribcage exposed and picked clean of meat. 

Frank slowly reached toward his waistband, his fingers closing around the worn wooden grip of the .38 revolver. 

The silence of the farm suddenly felt less like an abandonment, and more like a trap.



Chapter Two: The Cellar Door

Frank thumbed the heavy hammer of the .38 Special back. The loud, metallic double-click echoed loudly in the oppressive silence of the farmyard. 

"What is it?" Lefty asked, his voice tight with pain. He tried to pull his own revolver from his waistband, but his hands were shaking too badly from the blood loss. 

"Just stay quiet," Frank hissed, keeping his eyes locked on the sagging front porch of the farmhouse. 

He tightened his grip around his brother's waist and began the slow, agonizing climb up the three wooden steps to the porch. The floorboards groaned loudly under their combined weight, sounding like a gunshot in the dead air. 

Frank kept the revolver leveled at the front door. The wood was weathered, the white paint peeling away in long strips. The screen door was hanging loosely on one hinge, the wire mesh torn wide open. 

"Hello?" Frank called out, his voice sharp and authoritative, hoping to sound more like a Ranger than a terrified bank robber. "We need water! My brother is hurt!"

The only answer was the faint, rhythmic buzzing of the massive black flies swarming the dead hound in the yard behind them. 

Frank nudged the screen door aside with his shoulder and reached for the brass doorknob of the main door. It turned easily in his hand, unlocked, but the heavy oak door was swollen shut from the oppressive summer humidity. 

He took a step back, still supporting Lefty, and drove the flat of his boot hard against the wood just below the lock. 

The door burst open with a loud crack, hitting the interior wall and bouncing back. 

A wave of stale, suffocating air washed over them. The smell hit Frank like a physical blow—a sickeningly sweet, metallic odor of dried blood, mixed with the sharp stench of voided bowels and rotting meat. 

"Jesus," Lefty gagged, turning his head away and spitting into the dust on the porch. "Smells like a slaughterhouse."

"Breathe through your mouth," Frank ordered, stepping over the threshold into the dark hallway. 

The interior of the farmhouse was a scene of absolute, violent chaos. The fading sunlight creeping through the dirty windows illuminated a living room that had been thoroughly destroyed. The heavy, floral-patterned sofa was overturned. The glass of a china cabinet was shattered, the porcelain plates ground into the faded rug. A heavy oak dining chair was splintered into three pieces, as if it had been swung like a baseball bat. 

And there was blood. 

It wasn't fresh, bright red arterial blood. It was dark, almost black, smeared in long, frantic streaks across the peeling wallpaper and the hardwood floor. It looked as though someone had been dragged, kicking and fighting, from the living room down the narrow hallway toward the back of the house. 

"Set me down, Frank," Lefty groaned, his knees finally buckling under the pain. 

Frank dragged his brother to the overturned sofa, righting it with a heavy grunt. He lowered Lefty onto the dusty cushions. Lefty immediately grabbed his bleeding thigh, his breathing becoming shallow and rapid. 

"You stay right here," Frank said, keeping his voice low. "Keep your gun in your lap. Don't shoot unless you see something move. I'm going to find the kitchen. We need water."

Lefty nodded weakly, pulling his unloaded .32 caliber revolver from his waistband and resting it on his uninjured leg. He didn't bother telling Frank he hadn't had time to reload it during the shootout at the bank. The heavy steel in his hand was just a placebo against the terror. 

Frank stepped away from the sofa, moving slowly down the dark hallway. His boots crunched softly on the shattered glass of framed photographs that had been torn from the walls. 

The smell grew stronger as he moved toward the back of the house. 

He reached the kitchen. The linoleum floor was sticky beneath his boots. The cast-iron hand pump mounted on the sink was completely dry, the handle rusted in the upright position. 

Frank cursed silently. He grabbed the handle and pumped it furiously, the metal squealing in protest, but the well was either dry or the leather gasket had completely rotted away. Nothing came out but a dry rasp of air. 

"Nothing in the tap," Frank muttered to himself, turning away from the sink. 

He opened the heavy wooden door of the pantry. The shelves were mostly bare, stripped of canned goods and flour. The only thing left was a single, dusty jar of pickled beets sitting alone on the top shelf. Frank grabbed it, desperate for the vinegary liquid inside, and shoved it into the pocket of his canvas trousers. 

He stepped back out into the hallway. 

Directly across from the kitchen was another door. Unlike the flimsy interior doors of the bedrooms upstairs, this door was thick, heavy oak, reinforced with two horizontal iron bands. 

It was the door to the root cellar. 

Frank stepped toward it, intending to check the cool darkness below for jars of water or cider. But as his hand reached for the iron ring handle, he stopped cold. 

The door was locked. But not from the inside. 

A heavy, hardened steel padlock was threaded through a thick iron hasp on the *outside* of the door, securing it firmly to the doorframe. 

Frank leaned closer, his eyes narrowing in the dim light. 

The heavy oak wood of the door, right around the seams, was deeply scored and splintered. The scratch marks were frantic, deep gouges that tore through the varnish and into the raw wood beneath. 

Someone had tried to claw their way out of the cellar. Someone with unnatural strength, who didn't care about tearing their fingernails out by the roots. 

The dark smears of dried blood around the edges of the doorframe confirmed the horrific truth. 

The family hadn't abandoned the farmhouse. They had trapped something down there. Something that had likely killed the dog in the yard before being dragged, fighting and biting, into the dark. 

A sudden, sharp chill ran down Frank's spine, entirely cutting through the stifling Texas heat. He slowly backed away from the padlocked door, the heavy .38 revolver trembling slightly in his grip. 

He needed to get Lefty out of here. Now. Even if they had to walk until the sun killed them, it was better than staying in a house built on top of a monster. 

He turned to head back to the living room. 

*Creak.*

The sound was faint, but in the oppressive silence of the farmhouse, it was unmistakable. 

It wasn't coming from the padlocked cellar door. 

It was coming from the ceiling directly above Frank's head. The floorboards of the second story were shifting under a heavy, slow weight. 

Someone was upstairs. 

Frank froze, his eyes darting upward. The sound came again. A slow, dragging shuffle that moved from the space above the kitchen toward the top of the main staircase. 

Frank tightened his grip on the .38. He couldn't leave Lefty in the living room with someone coming down the stairs. 

He abandoned the search for water. He moved silently down the hallway, stepping carefully over the shattered glass, until he reached the base of the wooden staircase. 

He looked up into the darkness of the second-floor landing, thumbing the hammer of the revolver once more, preparing to face whatever the farmhouse had left behind.



Chapter Three: The Upstairs Tenant

The heavy wooden staircase groaned under a slow, uneven weight. 

Frank stood at the bottom of the stairs, the heavy .38 Special gripped tightly in his sweating right hand. The air in the hallway was suffocating, thick with the smell of copper and rot. The sunlight struggling through the dirty front windows barely penetrated the gloom of the second-floor landing. 

He didn't call out again. The scratches on the cellar door and the slaughtered hound in the yard had stripped away any illusions of finding help. 

A figure appeared at the top of the stairs. 

It was a woman, perhaps in her late thirties, wearing a faded floral housedress that was stiff with dried, black blood. Her hair was a tangled, matted bird's nest of brown and gray. But it was the way she moved that sent a cold spike of pure terror straight into Frank's heart. 

She wasn't walking; she was dragging herself. Her right ankle was bent at a grotesque, impossible ninety-degree angle, the bone visibly protruding through the thin cotton of her dress. Yet, she put her full weight onto the shattered limb without a single flinch of pain, lurching forward with a mechanical, relentless momentum. 

Her head lolled to the side, and as she stepped into a shaft of dusty light, Frank saw her eyes. 

They were completely white. The pupils and irises had clouded over into a milky, dead opaqueness. Her jaw hung slack, a thick string of black, viscous saliva dripping onto the collar of her dress. 

She didn't speak. She didn't scream. She just looked down the staircase at Frank, let out a wet, guttural hiss that vibrated in his chest, and threw herself forward. 

She didn't care about the stairs. She tumbled down the first three steps, her body twisting violently, before her hands found the wooden banister. She hauled herself upright, ignoring the sound of her own ribs cracking against the wood, and continued her lurching, rapid descent. 

"Stop right there!" Frank roared, his voice cracking with panic. He raised the revolver, leveling the heavy iron sights at the center of her chest. "I will shoot you, lady! I swear to God!"

The woman didn't hesitate. She covered half the staircase in seconds, her snapping teeth gnashing at the empty air. 

Frank pulled the trigger. 

The roar of the .38 Special in the enclosed hallway was deafening. The muzzle flash briefly illuminated the dark corridor in a stark, strobe-light flash of white. 

The heavy lead slug caught the woman dead center in the sternum. The kinetic force of the bullet threw her backward, slamming her hard against the faded wallpaper. 

Frank exhaled a shaky breath, lowering the gun slightly. 

But the woman didn't fall. 

She stood pinned against the wall for a fraction of a second, a dark, black stain spreading rapidly across the front of her floral dress. Then, she simply pushed herself off the wall and continued down the stairs, her pace entirely unbroken. 

"Jesus Christ," Frank breathed, his eyes widening in absolute horror. He had seen men shot before. A .38 slug to the chest stopped a man cold. It dropped them screaming to the floor. 

It didn't even slow her down. 

She was three steps away. Two. 

Frank didn't think. He reacted with the sheer, desperate instinct of a cornered animal. He raised the revolver, aimed slightly higher, and pulled the heavy trigger twice in rapid succession. 

*BANG! BANG!*

The first shot missed, tearing a massive splinter out of the wooden banister. 

The second shot caught her squarely in the right cheekbone. The heavy bullet shattered her skull, exiting through the back of her head in a spray of black fluid and gray matter. 

The woman collapsed instantly, her body tumbling down the final two steps and landing in a crumpled, motionless heap at Frank's boots. 

Frank stood frozen, his ears ringing violently from the gunshots. The acrid smell of burnt cordite mixed sickeningly with the stench of the rotting blood. His hands were shaking so badly he could barely keep the heavy revolver pointed at the floor. 

"Frank!" Lefty yelled from the living room, his voice pitched high with terror. "Frank, what the hell was that? Who did you shoot?"

Frank couldn't answer. He stared down at the dead woman. Her face was ruined, but the milky white eye that remained intact was staring blankly at the ceiling. 

She hadn't been breathing. Even before he shot her in the head, she hadn't been drawing breath. 

Before Frank could process the impossibility of the corpse at his feet, a new sound erupted in the hallway. 

It didn't come from upstairs. It came from directly behind him. 

*THUD.*

The heavy oak door of the root cellar shuddered violently. 

The three gunshots had broken the silence of the farm. The noise had woken whatever was locked down in the dark. 

*THUD. THUD.*

The impacts were incredibly heavy, shaking the doorframe and rattling the heavy steel padlock against the iron hasp. It sounded like someone was swinging a sledgehammer against the wood from the inside. 

Along with the impacts came a sound that made the blood freeze in Frank's veins. It was a chorus of guttural, wet hissing, accompanied by the frantic, rapid scratching of fingernails against heavy oak. 

It wasn't just one person down there. It sounded like three. Or four. 

"Frank!" Lefty screamed again, struggling to sit up on the overturned sofa. "What's in the cellar?"

"We're leaving," Frank yelled, spinning around and sprinting back into the living room. 

He didn't care about the water anymore. He didn't care about the searing Texas heat. The farmhouse was a tomb. 

He reached the sofa, grabbing Lefty by the collar of his shirt and hauling him roughly to his feet. Lefty cried out in agony as his wounded leg took his weight, but Frank didn't stop. He threw Lefty's arm over his shoulder and began half-carrying, half-dragging his brother toward the front door. 

*CRACK.*

The sound from the hallway was sharp and final. The heavy oak of the cellar door was giving way. 

Frank looked back over his shoulder just as they reached the ruined screen door. 

The iron hasp holding the padlock had been literally torn from the wooden doorframe by brute force. The heavy cellar door swung open, slamming against the hallway wall. 

A wave of absolute, putrid stench rolled out of the dark stairwell. 

And then, they poured out. 

There were four of them. An older man in overalls, two teenage boys, and a young girl no older than ten. They were all covered in the same thick, black fluid. Their eyes were milky white, their jaws unhinged and snapping. 

They didn't pause to orient themselves. The moment they hit the hallway floor, they locked onto the scent of Lefty's fresh blood and the sound of the fleeing men. 

They lunged forward, scrambling over the corpse of the woman at the bottom of the stairs, their boots slipping on the slick, bloody hardwood. 

"Go!" Frank roared, shoving Lefty out the front door and onto the porch. 

He turned back, raising the .38 revolver with a trembling hand, and fired blindly into the hallway. 



Chapter Four: The Windmill

The first shot tore through the shoulder of the older man in overalls, spinning him wildly but failing to drop him. The second shot slammed into the wooden doorframe, splintering the oak. 

Frank didn't wait to see if the third shot connected. He grabbed Lefty's arm, hauling him bodily off the porch and dragging him into the blistering Texas sun. 

"They're coming!" Lefty screamed, hobbling on his good leg, his face pale and slick with terror. 

Frank looked back. The four infected were spilling out onto the porch, their milky eyes locking onto the two brothers. They moved with that terrifying, jerky speed, their bodies ignoring the agonizing heat of the midday sun. 

"The barn!" Frank yelled, pulling Lefty toward the large, dilapidated wooden structure fifty yards away. "We need to get to the barn!"

The sprint across the dirt yard was a nightmare. Lefty was dead weight, his left leg completely useless. Frank's muscles screamed in protest as he practically carried his brother, his boots kicking up clouds of brown dust. 

The infected were gaining ground. The young girl, freed from the confines of the cellar, was surprisingly fast. She scrambled across the dirt on all fours like a rabid animal, her bloodstained dress dragging behind her. 

"Shoot 'em, Frank!" Lefty cried, looking back over his shoulder. 

Frank skidded to a halt, letting Lefty lean heavily against the wooden frame of the towering windmill that stood between the house and the barn. He spun around, raising the .38 Special with both hands to steady his trembling aim. 

He had three rounds left in the cylinder. 

He aimed for the young girl crawling toward them. He squeezed the trigger. 

*BANG.*

The bullet struck the dirt an inch in front of her face, kicking up a spray of dust and gravel. She didn't even flinch. She just kept coming, her jaw unhinged. 

Frank cursed, adjusting his aim. He fired again. 

*BANG.*

The heavy lead slug caught her in the center of her forehead. Her skull snapped back with a wet crunch, and she collapsed into the dust, her small body sliding forward a few feet before coming to a dead stop. 

Frank didn't pause to breathe. He swung the barrel toward the two teenage boys. They were running side-by-side, their arms reaching out blindly. 

He fired his last round. 

*BANG.*

The shot missed entirely, flying wide and striking the rusted fender of the abandoned Ford Model T. 

The hammer clicked on an empty chamber. 

"I'm out!" Frank yelled, dropping the useless revolver into the dust. He grabbed Lefty again. "Come on!"

They reached the massive, open doors of the barn just as the three remaining infected closed the distance to thirty yards. 

The interior of the barn was a cavern of suffocating heat and shadow. Dust motes danced in the shafts of sunlight piercing through the missing roof shingles. The air smelled of dry rot and old manure. 

Frank dragged Lefty inside and slammed the heavy right door shut, dropping the thick iron latch into place. He lunged for the left door. 

But the wood was warped from years of neglect. The hinges screamed in protest as Frank pushed with all his might. The door dragged heavily across the dirt floor, stopping two feet short of closing completely. 

"It's stuck!" Frank roared, pressing his shoulder against the rotting wood, his boots slipping in the dirt. 

"Frank!" Lefty screamed from the center of the barn. 

The older man in overalls hit the gap in the doors before Frank could clear the obstruction. A rotting, bloodstained hand shot through the opening, its broken fingernails clawing wildly at Frank's face. 

Frank threw himself backward, narrowly avoiding the snapping jaws that followed the hand. The infected man began to squeeze his body through the two-foot gap, his overalls snagging and tearing on the splinters of the door. 

"Help me!" Frank yelled to Lefty, frantically scanning the dark interior of the barn for a weapon. 

His eyes landed on a heavy, rusted iron pitchfork leaning against a stall divider. He scrambled across the dirt, grabbing the long wooden handle. 

He spun around just as the older man breached the gap and stumbled into the barn. 

Frank didn't hesitate. He lunged forward, driving the three heavy iron tines of the pitchfork directly into the man's chest. 

The force of the blow lifted the infected man off his feet, pinning him hard against the closed right door of the barn. The iron tines sunk deep into his ribcage, grating against the bone. 

But the man didn't die. He didn't even scream. He simply grabbed the wooden handle of the pitchfork with both hands, his milky eyes locked on Frank, and began to pull himself *forward*, forcing the iron tines deeper into his own chest to reach the man holding the handle. 

"He ain't stopping!" Frank gasped, his arms shaking violently as he struggled to hold the man back. The sheer mechanical strength of the corpse was overpowering him. 

"Frank!" Lefty yelled again. 

Frank glanced toward his brother. Lefty wasn't looking at him. He was pointing his unloaded .32 revolver toward the back of the barn. 

Standing in the shadows near a rusted tractor, perfectly still, were two more figures. 

They weren't wearing farm clothes. They were wearing faded, torn canvas trousers and heavy work boots. They were drifters who had likely sought shelter in the barn weeks ago, only to succumb to the sickness. 

They had heard the shouting. They had heard the struggle at the door. 

As Frank watched, their milky eyes turned toward Lefty, and they began their slow, lurching approach. 

"Lefty, run!" Frank roared, desperately trying to pull the pitchfork free to defend his brother. 

But the older man on the tines suddenly surged forward with terrifying speed. His jaw clamped down hard, missing Frank's wrist by a fraction of an inch, the black fluid spraying across Frank's forearm. 

Frank stumbled backward, losing his grip on the pitchfork entirely. The infected man, the iron handle still protruding from his chest, immediately lunged for him. 

They were trapped. The doors were breached, the barn was occupied, and they were completely out of ammunition. 

The Texas heat had become a slaughterhouse.



Chapter Five: The Steel Island

The stench inside the barn was no longer just dry rot and old manure; it was the sickening, sweet smell of the black fluid that pumped through the veins of the dead. 

Frank's boots slipped backward in the loose dirt. His shoulders screamed in agony as he tried to hold the heavy wooden handle of the pitchfork steady. The infected farmer in the bloodstained overalls didn't care that three iron tines were buried deep in his chest cavity, grinding against his ribs and puncturing his lungs. He felt no pain, no shock, and no exhaustion. His hands, caked in dirt and dried blood, gripped the wooden handle tightly, pulling himself inch by inch up the length of the pitchfork, his snapping jaws drawing closer to Frank's face with every relentless heave. 

"I can't hold him!" Frank roared, the muscles in his arms trembling violently under the strain. 

Across the barn, Lefty was in no position to help. 

The younger brother was frantically dragging himself backward across the dirt floor, his ruined left leg leaving a thick, dark trail of blood. The two infected drifters were closing the distance with terrifying, single-minded focus. They moved with a jerky, uneven gait, their arms outstretched, their milky eyes fixed on Lefty's bleeding thigh. 

Lefty's back slammed hard against the massive, solid rubber rear tire of the rusted Fordson tractor sitting in the center of the barn. He was completely cornered. 

He raised his unloaded .32 revolver, aiming it at the nearest drifter, pulling the trigger over and over again in a blind panic. 

*Click. Click. Click.*

The heavy steel hammer struck empty chambers. The sound was utterly useless against the dead. 

The first drifter, a man wearing a torn, grease-stained undershirt, lunged forward. His hands grabbed Lefty's canvas trousers, his jaw unhinging as he aimed for the fresh meat of the bullet wound. 

"Get off me!" Lefty screamed, using his good right leg to kick the man squarely in the chest. 

The kick pushed the drifter back a few inches, but it didn't break his grip. The corpse immediately surged forward again. 

Lefty's frantic hands scoured the dirt floor around the base of the tractor tire, searching for anything he could use as a weapon. His fingers brushed against something cold, heavy, and metallic. 

It was the heavy iron hand crank used to start the tractor's engine. It was shaped like a massive steel 'L', solid iron and weighing nearly ten pounds. 

Lefty grabbed the crank with both hands. The adrenaline of pure, primal terror flooded his system, temporarily overriding the excruciating pain of his gunshot wound. 

As the drifter lunged again, its snapping teeth inches from Lefty's kneecap, Lefty swung the heavy iron crank in a desperate, horizontal arc. 

The solid iron connected with the side of the drifter's knee joint with a sound like a dry branch snapping. The joint shattered completely. The leg buckled inward at an impossible angle, sending the dead man crashing face-first into the dirt. 

But the Romero-style sickness did not acknowledge the loss of a limb. The drifter didn't scream or clutch its shattered leg. It simply began to crawl forward on its elbows, its jaw still snapping relentlessly. 

Lefty didn't give it a second chance. He rose slightly on his good leg, raising the heavy iron crank high above his head, and brought it down like a hammer directly onto the back of the crawling man's skull. 

The skull caved inward with a wet, hollow crunch. The drifter went completely limp, his face buried in the bloody dirt. 

Before Lefty could recover, the second drifter fell upon him. The man's rotting hands clamped around Lefty's throat, pinning him against the massive rubber tire of the tractor. The grip was like an industrial vice, crushing Lefty's windpipe instantly. 

Back at the barn doors, Frank was losing the battle. The farmer had pulled himself so far up the pitchfork that his snapping, bloody teeth were less than a foot from Frank's hands. Black fluid dripped from the man's chin onto Frank's wrists. 

To make matters worse, the two infected teenage boys from the cellar had finally reached the gap in the doors. They began pushing against the warped wood, their combined weight widening the gap inch by agonizing inch. 

Frank realized he was dead if he held on for another second. 

He didn't try to pull the pitchfork out. He simply let go of the wooden handle entirely and threw himself violently to the right. 

The sudden release of resistance sent the infected farmer pitching forward, the long handle of the pitchfork burying itself into the dirt floor, propping the corpse up at an awkward angle. 

Frank scrambled away, rolling over the dirt. He grabbed a heavy, rusted logging chain that was draped over a wooden sawhorse. It was eight feet of thick, linked steel, heavy enough to tow a truck. 

The farmer, hindered by the long wooden handle sticking out of his chest, struggled to orient himself. 

Frank swung the heavy logging chain in a wide, whistling arc. The heavy steel links struck the side of the farmer's head with devastating kinetic force. The impact shattered the skull and tore the jaw completely off its hinges. The corpse collapsed into a heap of bloody overalls and broken wood. 

"Lefty!" Frank yelled, turning toward the center of the barn. 

He saw his younger brother pinned against the tractor tire, his face turning a terrifying shade of purple as the second drifter crushed his throat. 

Frank didn't have time to swing the chain again. He charged across the barn, treating the heavy steel links like a whip, and wrapped them directly around the drifter's neck. 

He pulled backward with all his tremendous strength, planting his boots in the dirt. 

The heavy steel links bit deeply into the rotting flesh of the drifter's throat, slicing through the muscle and severing the spine with a sickening crunch. The corpse's head flopped backward at a grotesque angle, its grip on Lefty's throat instantly releasing. Frank threw the body aside like a ragdoll. 

Lefty collapsed into the dirt, gasping for air, coughing violently as oxygen rushed back into his crushed windpipe. 

"Get up!" Frank ordered, hauling Lefty up by his shirt collar. 

The sound of splintering wood echoed through the barn. The warped left door finally gave way entirely, the rusted hinges tearing out of the frame. 

The two infected teenage boys stumbled into the barn, their milky eyes locking onto the brothers. And behind them, moving slowly but relentlessly through the blinding glare of the Texas sun, were three more infected—stragglers who had wandered onto the farm from the road. 

"The tractor!" Frank yelled, realizing they couldn't fight five of them in the open dirt. "Get on the tractor!"

He grabbed Lefty around the waist and hoisted him upward. Lefty cried out as he dragged his ruined leg over the massive rubber tire, scrambling onto the heavy cast-iron engine cowling of the Fordson tractor. 

Frank climbed up right behind him, pulling the heavy iron hand crank up with him as the first wave of the infected closed in around the base of their makeshift steel island.



Chapter Six: The Siege

The Fordson tractor was a massive, immovable block of cast iron and steel. Sitting high atop the curved engine cowling, Frank and Lefty were approximately five feet off the dirt floor. 

It was just high enough. 

The five infected surrounded the base of the tractor, pressing their decaying bodies against the massive rubber tires and the rusted iron chassis. They reached upward, their bloodstained fingers stretching toward the brothers, their jaws snapping wildly at the air. But lacking the coordination to climb or the cognitive function to use the surrounding debris as a step, they could only claw uselessly at the steel, their fingernails scraping loudly against the iron. 

"They can't get us," Lefty gasped, lying flat against the hot metal cowling. He was clutching his bleeding thigh, his face a terrifying shade of translucent gray. "We're safe."

"We ain't safe, Lefty," Frank replied, his voice grim. "We're just waiting."

The heat inside the barn was escalating from oppressive to lethal. The afternoon Texas sun beat down mercilessly on the corrugated tin roof above them, turning the enclosed space into a massive, suffocating oven. The air was stagnant, thick with the smell of the infected and the copper tang of fresh blood. 

Frank stripped off his sweat-soaked cotton shirt, ripping it into long strips with his teeth and his remaining strength. 

"Hold still," Frank ordered, leaning over his brother. 

He wrapped the torn cotton tightly around Lefty's upper thigh, creating a crude, brutal tourniquet just above the bullet wound. He pulled the knot so tight that Lefty screamed in agony, the sound echoing loudly in the rafters. 

The scream only agitated the dead below. The two infected teenage boys pushed harder against the side of the tractor, their guttural hisses increasing in volume, their milky eyes fixed blindly on the source of the noise. 

"I need water, Frank," Lefty rasped, his head falling back against the hot steel. "I'm burning up."

Frank looked at the burlap sack of cash sitting beside him on the engine cowling. It contained seventy-two dollars. In 1934, it was a fortune to a starving sharecropper. It was enough to buy a used truck, or pay off a land debt, or buy enough groceries to last a year. 

Right now, sitting in a baking barn surrounded by the walking dead, it was the most useless object on the planet. He couldn't drink it. He couldn't shoot it. He couldn't use it to bandage a wound. 

"There ain't no water, kid," Frank said softly, wiping a stream of stinging sweat from his eyes. 

He looked down at the dead. They didn't sweat. They didn't pant. The blistering heat that was slowly killing Frank and Lefty had absolutely no effect on the corpses. They could stand there, reaching and snapping, for days. Weeks, even. The siege was perfectly asymmetric; the environment was actively aiding the enemy. 

"Frank," Lefty whispered, his voice growing incredibly weak. The blood loss and the heat stroke were combining into a lethal cocktail. "I don't want to die on a tractor."

"You ain't going to die," Frank lied, looking desperately around the sweltering interior of the barn. 

The main doors were blocked by the infected. The walls were solid wood, devoid of low windows. 

Then, Frank looked up. 

Fifteen feet above the dirt floor was the hayloft. The wooden floorboards of the loft extended halfway across the width of the barn. The wooden ladder used to reach it was nailed to the far wall, completely inaccessible. 

However, running directly above the center of the barn, right over the tractor, was a massive, hand-hewn oak support beam. It was thick, sturdy, and covered in decades of dust and spiderwebs. 

"Look up," Frank said, pointing to the beam. 

Lefty cracked his eyes open, squinting through the gloom. "I see it. So what?"

"If I stand on the steering wheel, I can jump and grab that beam," Frank calculated, his mind working frantically. "I can pull myself up into the loft. Once I'm up there, I can find a rope, or a heavy leather strap, and haul you up."

"Frank, you can't jump that high," Lefty said, coughing weakly. "You're exhausted."

"I have to," Frank replied, his jaw setting into a hard line. "If we stay down here, the heat kills us before the sun sets. Up there, we might find a window leading to the roof. We might find a way out that doesn't involve walking through them."

He stood up carefully, his boots finding purchase on the uneven cast iron of the engine block. He gripped the heavy iron steering wheel of the tractor to steady himself. 

Below him, the movement drew the attention of the infected. A man in a torn suit reached up, his decaying fingers brushing the rubber sole of Frank's boot. 

Frank kicked down hard, his heel connecting with the man's face, sending the corpse stumbling backward into the dirt. 

"When I go, they're going to get riled up," Frank warned his brother. "You stay flat against the metal. Don't let your arms or legs hang over the side."

Lefty nodded, pressing himself as tightly as possible against the hot steel cowling. 

Frank took a deep breath, the suffocating, stale air burning his lungs. He stepped up onto the heavy iron steering column. It wobbled slightly, but held his weight. 

He bent his knees, his eyes locked on the heavy oak beam eight feet above his head. 

"I'll be right back for you, Lefty," Frank promised. 

He pushed off the steering column with every ounce of explosive strength he had left, launching himself upward into the stifling heat of the barn.



Chapter Seven: The Hayloft

Frank hung suspended in the baking air of the barn, his entire existence distilled into the burning agony of his forearms. 

The leap from the unsteady steering column of the Fordson tractor had been explosive, driven by pure, adrenaline-fueled desperation. His calloused hands had slammed over the top of the massive oak support beam exactly as he had planned. 

But he hadn't accounted for the dust. 

Decades of fine Texas topsoil, mixed with the aerosolized grease and oil from the tractor below, had settled onto the flat top of the beam, creating a slick, powdery surface. The moment Frank's full body weight hit the apex of the jump, his sweaty palms slipped backward across the wood. 

He didn't fall. His thick fingers, hardened by years of gripping a plow handle, clamped down on the sharp right-angle edge of the oak beam. 

He swung violently, his boots kicking at the empty air ten feet above the dirt floor. 

Below him, the five infected surged forward, their milky eyes tracking his dangling legs. The man in the torn suit reached up, his snapping jaws missing the heel of Frank's boot by less than six inches. 

"Pull, Frank!" Lefty screamed from the engine cowling, his voice raw with terror. "Pull!"

Frank gritted his teeth, the muscles in his neck standing out like thick ropes. He didn't try to pull himself straight up; his grip was too compromised. Instead, he swung his legs forward, using the momentum to swing his lower body upward in a violent, jerking motion. 

He managed to hook the heel of his right boot over the top of the oak beam. 

With his leg providing a temporary anchor, Frank let out a guttural roar and hauled his upper body over the edge of the wood, collapsing onto his stomach. He lay flat against the heavy beam for three agonizing seconds, his chest heaving, listening to the furious, frustrated hissing of the infected below. 

He didn't have time to rest. He pushed himself up onto his hands and knees, scrambling like a frightened crab across the fifteen feet of exposed beam until he reached the solid, wooden floorboards of the hayloft. 

He rolled off the beam and crashed into a pile of dry, rotting hay. 

The heat in the loft was exponentially worse than on the ground floor. Trapped directly beneath the corrugated tin roof, the air temperature was easily over a hundred and twenty degrees. It smelled of dry rot, ancient dust, and the desiccated carcasses of dead mice. 

Frank scrambled to his feet, ignoring the sweat pouring into his eyes. He began to tear frantically through the piles of ruined hay and discarded farm equipment, searching for anything he could use as a hoist. A rope, a chain, a heavy cable. 

He found a tangle of thick leather straps buried beneath a rusted saddle. It was a heavy-duty driving harness, designed for a massive draft horse. The leather was dry and cracked, but the thick brass buckles and heavy stitching looked sturdy enough to hold a man's weight. 

Frank grabbed the heavy harness and sprinted back to the edge of the loft floorboards, looking down at the tractor. 

Lefty was still flat against the cowling, his eyes wide and panicked. The infected were pressing so hard against the side of the tractor that the massive cast-iron chassis was actually trembling slightly under their combined weight. 

"Catch this!" Frank yelled, throwing one end of the heavy leather harness down toward his brother. 

The brass buckle struck the engine block with a loud clatter. Lefty grabbed the thick leather strap with shaking hands. 

"Wrap it around your chest!" Frank ordered, wrapping his own end of the harness twice around his forearms to secure his grip. "Under your arms! Make sure it's tight!"

Lefty fumbled with the stiff leather, fighting through the wave of dizziness threatening to pull him under. He managed to loop the heavy strap under his armpits, crossing the leather over his chest and pulling it taut against his sweat-soaked shirt. 

"I'm secure!" Lefty yelled up, his voice cracking. 

Frank didn't brace himself against a beam; he didn't have time. He planted his boots wide on the wooden floorboards, bent his knees, and leaned his entire body weight backward. 

"Up!" Frank roared. 

Lefty was hauled brutally off the cast-iron cowling. He screamed as the heavy leather dug into his ribs and the sudden movement sent a fresh spike of blinding agony radiating from his gunshot wound. 

The sudden departure of their prey triggered a frantic, violent reaction from the dead. 

As Lefty swung helplessly in the air, slowly ascending toward the loft, the infected lunged upward. The young girl with the shattered skull scrambled onto the massive rear tire of the tractor, using the thick rubber treads as a makeshift ladder. 

She launched herself off the top of the tire, her bloody hands reaching for Lefty's dangling legs. 

Her rotting fingers closed around the cuff of Lefty's canvas trousers. 

The sudden, dead weight hit the harness with a terrifying jolt. Frank was pulled forward a half-step, his boots sliding across the dusty floorboards of the loft. 

"She's got me!" Lefty shrieked, kicking wildly with his good right leg. He struck the young girl in the shoulder, but she didn't let go. Her jaw unhinged, aiming for the exposed skin of his ankle. 

Frank let out a scream of pure, desperate exertion. He pulled backward, using every muscle in his back and legs, ignoring the burning tear of his own overstressed tendons. 

The horrific game of tug-of-war lasted for three agonizing seconds. 

The rotting flesh of the dead girl's fingers simply couldn't withstand the immense tension. With a sickening sound of tearing tissue, her fingers literally ripped apart. She fell backward, crashing heavily into the dirt beside the tractor. 

Frank hauled backward, pulling Lefty over the edge of the loft. The younger brother tumbled onto the wooden floorboards, gasping for air, clutching his bleeding thigh. 

Frank collapsed beside him, the heavy leather harness falling from his trembling hands. 

They lay there in the baking, suffocating heat of the loft, listening to the furious scratching and hissing of the infected from the floor below. 

"We made it," Lefty gasped, a weak, hysterical laugh escaping his lips. "We're up."

Frank pushed himself onto his elbows, wiping the stinging sweat from his eyes. He looked around the small, enclosed space of the hayloft. 

There was a large, square loading door built into the far wall of the barn, designed to pitch hay directly into wagons parked outside. It was their only way out. 

Frank crawled toward it, pushing a pile of dry hay out of the way. He reached for the wooden latch to throw the door open and let some air into the suffocating oven. 

The latch was engaged. But the door didn't move. 

Frank pushed harder, putting his shoulder against the heavy wood. It was solid. 

He leaned close, inspecting the edges of the doorframe. 

Through the narrow cracks in the warped wood, he could see the bright, glaring sunlight of the Texas afternoon. And he could see the thick, heavy steel nails driven through the exterior planks. 

"Frank?" Lefty asked, his voice filled with dread as he watched his brother's face drain of color. "What is it?"

"The door," Frank said, his voice dropping to a hollow whisper. "It's boarded shut. From the outside."

They had escaped the teeth of the dead, only to lock themselves inside a wooden oven.



Chapter Eight: The Oven

The heat inside the hayloft was not merely uncomfortable; it was a physical, aggressive entity. The corrugated tin roof absorbed the brutal radiation of the midday Texas sun, turning the enclosed space beneath it into a massive, suffocating oven. The air was thick with the choking dust of dry rot and pulverized manure, settling like a heavy blanket over Frank and Lefty. 

"We got to get out of here," Lefty mumbled, his head lolling against a bale of ancient, petrified hay. His skin was pale and dry. The profuse sweating that had soaked his clothes during the run to the barn had stopped completely—a terrifying symptom of advanced heatstroke. 

"I know," Frank said, his voice a dry rasp. His throat felt like it was packed with sand. 

He pushed himself up from the floorboards, staggering slightly as a wave of dizziness washed over him. He walked back to the large, square loading door. 

He ran his hands over the warped wood, desperately searching for a loose plank or a weakened joint. But the door was solid. The heavy steel nails driven through the exterior planks held the wood tight against the frame. 

Frank slammed his fists against the door, putting his entire body weight behind the blows. The wood barely vibrated. 

He turned away from the door, his eyes frantically scanning the gloom of the loft. He needed a tool. A crowbar, a heavy hammer, a rusted axle—anything with enough mass and leverage to smash through the two-inch oak planks. 

He dug through the piles of debris, kicking aside broken harnesses, rusted horseshoes, and the petrified carcasses of small animals. He found nothing. Whoever had boarded up the loft had been thorough; they had left no heavy tools behind. 

"Ma?" Lefty asked softly. 

Frank froze, turning slowly toward his brother. 

Lefty was staring blankly at the sloping tin roof. His eyes were unfocused, tracking something that wasn't there. 

"Ma, I told Frank we shouldn't have planted the west field," Lefty whispered, his voice conversational, devoid of the panic that had consumed him moments earlier. "The dirt's no good. It just blows away. We're gonna lose the bank note."

Frank felt a cold knot of pure dread form in his stomach. Lefty was hallucinating. The combination of massive blood loss from the gunshot wound and the lethal temperature in the loft was shutting down his brain. 

"Lefty, listen to me," Frank said, dropping to his knees beside his brother. He grabbed Lefty's face, his hands leaving streaks of clean skin through the layer of grime. "Look at me. You ain't on the farm. We're in a barn."

Lefty blinked slowly, his eyes drifting down to Frank's face. He smiled, a weak, terrifying expression. "I'm thirsty, Frank. The pump is dry again."

"I know," Frank whispered, a single tear cutting through the dust on his cheek. "I'm going to get you some water. You just hold on."

Frank stood up, his jaw setting into a hard, rigid line. He walked to the edge of the loft and looked down at the dirt floor fifteen feet below. 

The infected had not left. They were clustered around the base of the tractor, directly beneath the loft. There were eight of them now. Three more had wandered into the barn, drawn by the sound of Frank smashing his fists against the loading door. 

They stood there, a horrific congregation of the dead, their milky eyes staring upward, their jaws snapping with a relentless, mechanical rhythm. 

Frank looked at the heavy leather draft horse harness lying on the floorboards. 

He couldn't break out of the loft from the inside. He had no tools. But there were tools on the ground floor. The heavy iron pitchfork he had used to impale the farmer. The iron hand crank Lefty had used to crush the drifter's skull. Heavy wrenches sitting on the workbench in the shadows. 

If he could retrieve one of those tools, he could smash through the boarded door. 

But getting a tool meant going back down into the slaughterhouse. 

Frank picked up the heavy leather harness. He walked over to the massive oak support beam that ran across the ceiling of the loft. He looped the thick leather straps securely over the beam, pulling hard to test the weight limit. The ancient brass buckles groaned, but held. 

"What are you doing, Frank?" Lefty asked, his moments of lucidity flickering like a dying candle. 

"I'm going fishing," Frank lied, forcing a steady tone into his voice. "I'm going to get us a crowbar. Then we're going to break that door open, jump down onto the soft dirt outside, and walk out of here."

Lefty nodded slowly, his eyes closing again. "Okay, Frank. Don't be long."

Frank walked back to the edge of the loft. He grabbed the dangling end of the heavy leather harness, wrapping it tightly around his forearms. 

He didn't have a gun. He didn't have a knife. He was going to lower himself directly into a swarm of the walking dead, hoping he was fast enough to grab a heavy piece of iron before they tore him to pieces. 

It was a suicidal plan. But staying in the oven was a guaranteed death sentence for Lefty. 

Frank took a deep, burning breath of the dusty air, squeezed his eyes shut for a fraction of a second, and swung his legs over the edge of the loft.



Chapter Nine: The Drop

Frank wrapped the heavy leather draft-horse harness around his forearms, ignoring the abrasive friction that immediately rubbed his skin raw. He walked to the edge of the hayloft and looked down. 

The eight infected were clustered tightly around the base of the Fordson tractor, their milky eyes fixed upward, their hands raised in a grotesque, silent supplication. They were drawn to the sound of his movements and the smell of Lefty's blood. 

Across the dirt floor, leaning against the far wall of the barn, was a heavy wooden workbench. Scattered across its surface were various rusted tools: a pair of farrier's pincers, a ball-peen hammer, and exactly what Frank was looking for—a massive, two-foot-long iron pipe wrench. It weighed at least fifteen pounds. It was easily heavy enough to smash through the oak planks of the loading door. 

But the workbench was thirty feet away from the tractor. 

Frank had to drop down, sprint through the swarm of the dead, grab the wrench, sprint back, and climb the leather strap fast enough to avoid being pulled down. 

It was mathematically impossible. But it was the only math that mattered. 

"Lefty," Frank said softly, not looking back at his hallucinating brother. "When I tie the wrench to the strap, you pull it up. You understand? Don't wait for me. Just pull it up and smash that door."

Lefty didn't answer. He was murmuring softly about the spring planting, his eyes closed. 

Frank took a deep breath, filling his lungs with the scorching, dusty air of the loft. He grabbed the thick leather strap with both hands, stepped off the edge of the loft, and slid down fifteen feet of leather. 

He didn't try to slow his descent. He let gravity pull him down in a controlled freefall, the stiff leather burning a deep trench across the palms of his hands. 

He hit the dirt floor with a heavy thud, bending his knees to absorb the impact. 

The infected reacted instantly. The sound of his boots hitting the dirt drew their attention away from the loft. The eight corpses pivoted toward him with that jerky, unnatural speed, their jaws snapping wildly. 

Frank didn't hesitate. He launched himself forward, sprinting directly at the nearest infected—a tall man in a torn, grease-stained undershirt. 

Frank lowered his shoulder, driving all of his momentum into the dead man's chest. The impact sounded like a sack of wet meat hitting a brick wall. The infected man was thrown backward, crashing into two others and sending all three tumbling into the dirt. 

Frank hurdled the fallen bodies, his boots digging into the loose soil as he sprinted toward the workbench. 

A woman in a faded floral dress lunged at him from his blind spot. Her rotting fingers clamped onto the fabric of his trousers. Frank didn't stop running. He spun violently, tearing the fabric of his own pants to break her grip, and drove his elbow backward directly into her face. He felt her nose shatter against the bone of his arm, but she barely flinched, lunging forward again. 

Frank reached the workbench. He grabbed the massive iron pipe wrench with both hands. It was incredibly heavy, the rusted teeth of the adjustable jaw coated in old grease. 

He spun around to face the barn. 

The horde had recovered. They were fanning out, cutting off his direct path back to the dangling leather harness. They didn't run, but their relentless, shuffling advance quickly closed the distance. 

"Come on then!" Frank roared, swinging the fifteen-pound iron wrench in a massive, horizontal arc. 

The heavy iron connected squarely with the side of the floral-dress woman's head. The impact was devastating. Her skull caved inward with a sickening crunch, the kinetic force lifting her off her feet and throwing her against the side of the tractor. She didn't get back up. 

But the swing had carried Frank's momentum too far to the left, leaving his right flank entirely exposed. 

The older man in the bloodstained overalls—the farmer who had pushed himself through the pitchfork—lunged forward. 

His unhinged jaw snapped shut with the force of a steel trap. 

His rotting teeth sank deep into the thick muscle of Frank's right shoulder, tearing through the cotton of his shirt and biting clean down to the collarbone. 

Frank screamed, a raw, primal sound of absolute agony that echoed loudly through the sweltering barn. The pain was blinding, a sharp, tearing sensation accompanied instantly by the sickening, freezing burn of the black fluid entering his bloodstream. 

He knew instantly what it meant. There was no cure. There was no surviving a bite. He was a dead man. 

The realization didn't paralyze him; it liberated him from the fear of death. 

"Get off me!" Frank roared, his voice devoid of panic, replaced by a cold, homicidal fury. 

He dropped the heavy pipe wrench, reached up with his left hand, and grabbed the farmer by the throat. He squeezed with every ounce of his immense strength, feeling the cartilage of the dead man's windpipe crush beneath his fingers, and violently hurled the corpse backward into the dirt. 

Frank stood panting, his right arm hanging uselessly at his side, blood pouring from the horrific wound on his shoulder. Black fluid was already mixing with the bright red arterial blood. 

The remaining six infected closed in, drawn by the fresh blood. 

Frank looked up at the hayloft. Lefty was still lying on the floorboards, completely oblivious to the slaughter below him. 

Frank picked up the heavy iron pipe wrench with his left hand. He didn't have the strength to swing it again. The sickness was already burning in his veins, blurring the edges of his vision with a dark, freezing fog. 

He stumbled forward, shoving past a teenage boy, ignoring the rotting hands that clawed at his shirt and trousers. 

He reached the dangling leather harness. 

With his remaining strength, he quickly tied the heavy iron pipe wrench to the end of the leather strap, pulling the knot tight with his teeth. 

"Lefty!" Frank screamed, his voice cracking with exertion and the encroaching sickness. "Lefty, wake up! Pull the strap!"

Above him, Lefty stirred. He slowly dragged himself to the edge of the loft, looking down through glazed, unfocused eyes. 

"Frank?" Lefty whispered. 

"Pull the strap, kid!" Frank roared, pointing up at his brother. "Smash the door! Go!"

Lefty reached out with trembling hands and grabbed the leather strap. 

Frank didn't have time to say goodbye. The horde collapsed upon him. 

A rotting hand grabbed his hair, jerking his head backward. Another set of teeth sank deeply into the calf of his left leg, tearing away a massive chunk of muscle. 

Frank didn't scream this time. He simply let go of the leather strap. 

He fell backward into the dirt, instantly buried beneath a writhing mass of snapping jaws and tearing hands. The heavy iron pipe wrench swung upward, disappearing into the stifling heat of the hayloft, as the sharecropper was torn apart on the barn floor.



Chapter Ten: The Toll

The heavy iron pipe wrench struck the wooden floorboards of the hayloft with a dull, heavy thud. 

Lefty lay on his stomach, his chest heaving, the thick leather strap slipping from his exhausted fingers. He had pulled the fifteen-pound weight up fifteen feet, every inch an agonizing battle against the severe heatstroke shutting down his organs. 

From the dirt floor below, the sounds of his brother's death were loud and horrifyingly clear. 

There were no more shouts of defiance. There was only the wet, tearing sound of rending flesh, the snapping of bones, and the furious, guttural hissing of the feeding infected. 

"Frank," Lefty whispered, a single, bloody tear cutting a path through the dust on his cheek. 

The shock of what had just happened briefly pierced through the dense fog of his hallucinations. His mother and the farm were gone. The reality of the sweltering, blood-soaked barn crashed down upon him. Frank had sacrificed himself for a rusted piece of iron. 

Lefty reached out and grabbed the greasy, iron handle of the wrench. 

He didn't cry. He didn't have enough moisture left in his body for tears. He simply felt a cold, hollow emptiness settle into his chest, replacing the terror. 

He dragged himself across the floorboards, pulling his ruined left leg behind him, until he reached the boarded-up loading door. 

The Texas sun was still beating down mercilessly on the tin roof, the heat radiating off the wood like an open furnace. Lefty struggled to his knees, gripping the heavy iron wrench with both hands. 

He swung the fifteen-pound weight backward and drove it forward like a battering ram, striking the center of the lowest oak plank. 

The impact jarred his shoulders, sending a fresh wave of blinding pain down his spine, but the wood cracked. The rusted steel nails holding the plank to the exterior frame groaned in protest. 

Lefty didn't stop. He swung the wrench again, and again, and again. He swung it for the farm they had lost. He swung it for the seventy-two dollars sitting uselessly on the tractor. He swung it for Frank. 

*CRACK.*

With a final, desperate heave, the center plank shattered. The heavy iron wrench broke through the wood, tearing the nails free from the frame. 

Lefty dropped the wrench and grabbed the splintered edges of the hole with his bare hands, tearing the remaining wood away. He kicked at the upper planks with his good right boot until the entire structure gave way. 

The heavy, square loading door burst open on its rusted hinges. 

A wave of fresh, moving air swept into the loft. It was still over a hundred degrees outside, but compared to the stagnant, suffocating oven of the barn, it felt like a cool breeze. 

Lefty crawled to the edge of the open door and looked out. 

The drop was fifteen feet down to the hard-packed dirt of the farmyard. The rusted Ford Model T sat abandoned near the house. The skeletal wooden windmill stood motionless in the dead air. And beyond the farm, the cracked, barren earth of the Texas panhandle stretched out endlessly in every direction, shimmering under the brutal sun. 

Suddenly, a new sound echoed from the darkness of the barn floor behind him. 

It wasn't the sound of feeding. It was the sound of heavy boots dragging slowly across the dirt. 

Lefty slowly turned his head, looking back over his shoulder into the gloom. 

Standing beneath the oak support beam, covered entirely in blood and dirt, was Frank. 

His right shoulder was a ruined, mangled mess. A massive chunk of his left calf was missing. Yet, he stood completely upright, putting his full weight onto the shattered leg without a single flinch of pain. 

Frank looked up at the hayloft. His eyes, which had been a vibrant, piercing blue just ten minutes ago, were now a milky, dead white. His jaw hung slack, a thick string of black fluid dripping from his chin. 

He didn't recognize Lefty. He didn't remember the farm, or the bank robbery, or the sacrifice he had just made. He was simply a vessel driven by the sickness, staring up at the fresh meat in the loft. 

He raised his hands toward the oak beam, his jaws snapping mechanically at the air. He couldn't jump. He couldn't climb. But he would stand there until the end of time, waiting for Lefty to fall. 

Lefty stared at the thing that had been his older brother. The cold emptiness in his chest finally cracked, replaced by a profound, crushing sorrow. 

He looked back out the open loading door at the endless, dying desert. 

Frank had bought him a way out of the oven. But there was nowhere left to go. 

They were fifty miles from the nearest town. Their stolen Ford V8 had a cracked engine block. Lefty had a bullet in his thigh, severe blood loss, and advanced heatstroke. If he jumped out the window, he would shatter his good leg on the hardpan dirt. 

If the fall didn't kill him, the Texas sun would finish the job long before he could crawl a single mile. 

The universe had offered them a choice: die by the teeth of the dead, or die by the brutal indifference of the Dustbowl. 

Lefty gripped the splintered edge of the wooden doorframe. He looked down at the hard, cracked earth waiting below. 

"I'm sorry, Frank," Lefty whispered into the hot, dry wind. 

He didn't look back at the barn. He didn't look back at the monster wearing his brother's face. 

Lefty simply pushed himself forward, sliding off the edge of the wooden floorboards, and let gravity take him down into the blinding glare of the Texas afternoon. 

The dull, heavy crack of his body hitting the hardpan dirt echoed briefly across the dead farm. 

And then, the silence returned to the American wasteland, broken only by the buzzing of the flies and the distant, rhythmic snapping of jaws from the shadows of the barn.



Story Three: The Deep Seam

Chapter One: The Cage

The steel drop-cage smelled of ancient coal dust, chewing tobacco, and the sour sweat of terrified men. 

It was November 1936, in the heart of the Appalachian Mountains of West Virginia. The Blackwood Mining Company's Shaft No. 4 dropped exactly one thousand, two hundred feet straight down into the earth. The cage, a rusted iron box suspended by a single, terrifyingly thin steel cable, was currently descending at a stomach-churning forty miles per hour. 

Silas "Big Si" Vance stood perfectly still in the center of the cramped cage, his massive shoulders brushing against the damp iron grating. He was fifty years old, and he had spent thirty-five of those years underground. His face was a map of deep wrinkles, permanently stained with a fine layer of black soot that no amount of lye soap could ever completely wash away. 

He didn't hold onto the overhead rails. He simply rode the sickening drop with the calm, detached stoicism of a man who had accepted long ago that the mountain would eventually kill him. 

But today, the fear in the cage had nothing to do with cave-ins or pockets of explosive methane gas. 

"They were locking the company store, Si," whispered Tommy, a nineteen-year-old loader standing shivering next to the older man. "I saw 'em. The Pinkertons were dragging heavy chain across the double doors. And they had the riot guns out. The big repeating shotguns."

Silas didn't look at the boy. He kept his eyes fixed on the rusted iron plate of the cage floor. "Company business ain't our business, Tommy. Our business is in the seam."

"But the siren," Tommy insisted, his voice trembling over the deafening rattle of the descending cage. "The town siren was going off. It wasn't the shift whistle. It was the emergency wail. Like a flood."

"There ain't no flood on the mountain," a voice grunted from the dark corner of the cage. 

It was Miller, the shift boss. He was a hard, uncompromising man, holding a heavy brass carbide lamp in his calloused hand. "Whatever is happening up top stays up top. The company pays you to load the carts, not to worry about the company store."

A sudden, violent coughing fit interrupted Miller. 

The sound came from a man named O'Bannon, slumped heavily against the far wall of the cage. He was a broad-chested driller, a man usually full of loud jokes and boisterous energy. But right now, he looked like a ghost. 

His face was coated in a sheen of thick, greasy sweat. His hands were trembling violently, rattling the brass casing of his unlit carbide lamp against the iron grating. 

"You alright, O'Bannon?" Silas asked, his deep voice rumbling over the noise of the drop. 

O'Bannon didn't look up. He kept his chin tucked against his chest, his breathing shallow and rapid. "Just a fever, Si. Caught a chill in the rain this morning."

Silas narrowed his eyes, the dim light from Miller's lamp casting long, sinister shadows across the men's faces. 

"That ain't no chill," Tommy whispered, leaning closer to Silas. "Look at his hand."

Silas shifted his gaze. O'Bannon was clutching his right forearm tightly with his left hand. A piece of dirty, oil-stained rag was tied clumsily around his wrist. 

But the rag wasn't stopping the bleeding. And the blood wasn't right. 

Seeping through the frayed edges of the cloth, staining the man's pale skin, was a thick, viscous black fluid. It looked like crude oil, but it smelled like rotting meat. 

"What happened to your arm, O'Bannon?" Silas demanded, his voice suddenly sharp, cutting through the ambient noise of the cage. 

"Cut it," O'Bannon gasped, his whole body shaking violently. "Cut it on a piece of rusted tin behind the breaker house. It ain't nothing. Just... just needs a poultice."

"Let me see it," Silas said, taking a step forward. The cramped quarters of the cage meant he was instantly looming over the sick man. 

"Leave him be, Si," Miller barked from the corner. "If he can swing a pick, he works. We're already a man short today. I ain't sending him back up."

"He's bleeding black, Miller," Silas growled, not breaking his gaze from O'Bannon. "I ain't never seen a man bleed black from a piece of rusted tin."

Before Miller could argue, the drop-cage hit the bottom of the shaft. 

The deceleration was brutal. The heavy iron box slammed into the massive hydraulic shock absorbers at the base of the shaft with a deafening *CRASH*. The impact sent every man in the cage staggering to their knees. 

The heavy iron grate doors rolled open, revealing the "main heading" of the Blackwood mine. 

It was a cavernous, dark tunnel carved directly into the solid rock. The air down here was cold, damp, and utterly devoid of sunlight. The only illumination came from a few flickering electric bulbs strung along the ceiling, powered by a sputtering generator a mile away. 

"Out!" Miller shouted, recovering quickly from the drop. "Get your lamps lit and get down to Section Four! The coal ain't gonna dig itself!"

The men filed out of the cage, the heavy, silence of the deep earth immediately pressing in on them. They pulled the small brass carbide lamps from their belts, filling the lower reservoirs with calcium carbide pellets and adding a few drops of water from their canteens. 

The chemical reaction instantly produced acetylene gas. The miners struck the flint wheels on the side of the lamps, igniting the gas into bright, piercing white flames. 

The sudden illumination pushed the darkness back a few feet, revealing the jagged, wet walls of the coal seam. 

Silas lit his lamp and clipped it securely to the front of his hardened leather helmet. He turned back to look at O'Bannon. 

The driller had staggered out of the cage and collapsed onto a heavy wooden crate used to store blasting caps. His breathing was ragged, a wet, bubbling sound echoing in the silent tunnel. The black fluid was now dripping steadily from his wrapped arm, pooling on the dusty rock floor. 

"Miller," Silas said, his voice low and dangerous. "He ain't working. He's dying. You need to ring the bell and send the cage back up. Get him to the company doctor."

Miller adjusted his helmet, the flame of his lamp casting his angry face in sharp relief. "I told you, Si. We're a man short. The quota don't change just because O'Bannon caught a bug."

"It ain't a bug," Tommy said, his voice trembling as he stared at the black puddle forming beneath O'Bannon's boots. "Look at his eyes, Si. Look at his eyes."

Silas stepped closer to the sick man. 

O'Bannon slowly lifted his head. The greasy sweat on his face had dried into a pale, chalky film. But it was his eyes that made the breath catch in Silas's massive chest. 

The deep, familiar brown of O'Bannon's irises was gone. His eyes had clouded over entirely, turning a milky, dead white. 

"O'Bannon?" Silas whispered, his hand instinctively dropping to the heavy, iron-headed pickaxe slung over his shoulder. 

O'Bannon didn't answer. He didn't blink. He just stared through Silas, his jaw slowly falling open with a wet, popping sound. 

And then, deep in the absolute darkness of the Appalachian earth, one thousand feet away from the sun, the dead man began to rise.



Chapter Two: The Deep Dark

The silence of the Appalachian deep earth was absolute, heavy enough to crush the air out of a man's lungs. 

It was broken by the wet, sickening sound of O'Bannon's jaw snapping open. 

The driller pushed himself off the wooden crate of blasting caps with an unnatural, jerky motion. The milky white of his dead eyes reflected the harsh, bright flames of the carbide lamps strapped to the miners' helmets. 

"Get up, you lazy son of a bitch," Miller barked, entirely misreading the situation. The shift boss took a step toward O'Bannon, raising his heavy brass lamp as if to strike the man. "You ain't getting out of the shift that easy."

"Miller, step back!" Silas roared, the deep rumble of his voice echoing off the damp coal walls. He swung the heavy iron pickaxe off his shoulder, gripping the polished ash handle with both hands. 

Miller ignored him. He reached out and grabbed the collar of O'Bannon's canvas work shirt. 

O'Bannon didn't recoil. He didn't speak. Driven entirely by the sudden, overwhelming hunger of the sickness, he lunged forward with terrifying speed. 

His teeth sank deeply into the soft, exposed flesh of Miller's neck, right at the juncture of the collarbone. 

Miller's angry shout turned instantly into a high, piercing shriek of absolute agony. He dropped his carbide lamp, the brass casing clattering loudly against the rock floor. The flame flickered wildly as Miller staggered backward, his hands frantically clawing at the driller's face. 

But O'Bannon's jaw was locked in a death grip. He tore his head to the side, ripping a massive chunk of muscle and artery away from the shift boss's neck. 

A torrent of bright red arterial blood sprayed across the dark tunnel, hissing loudly as it struck the hot brass of the fallen lamps. 

The other six men in the heading froze in pure, unadulterated shock. They were hard men, used to the brutal violence of the coal face—crushed limbs, severed fingers, the sudden roar of a methane explosion. But they had never seen a man eaten alive by his friend. 

Silas didn't freeze. The rumors of the sickness sweeping through the Hoovervilles and the company towns had reached the deep hollows of West Virginia weeks ago. He hadn't believed them. Until now. 

He stepped forward, bringing the heavy iron pickaxe down in a short, brutal, overhead arc. 

The sharp, tempered steel point of the pickaxe penetrated the top of O'Bannon's skull with a sickening crunch. The kinetic force of the blow drove the driller straight down, pinning his ruined head against the rock floor of the tunnel. 

O'Bannon's body jerked violently for a fraction of a second, black fluid spraying from the wound, before going completely limp. 

Silas planted his heavy, steel-toed boot against the dead man's shoulder and wrenched the pickaxe free with a sickening squelch. 

"Jesus Christ!" Tommy screamed, backing away so fast he tripped over a section of rusted rail track, tumbling backward into the dust. "He ate him! Si, he ate him!"

Miller was on his knees, both hands clamped desperately over his torn neck. The blood was pulsing through his fingers in rapid, diminishing spurts. He looked up at Silas, his eyes wide with a terror that transcended the pain. He tried to speak, but only a wet, bubbling sound escaped his lips. 

And then, he saw it. Mixing with the bright red blood covering Miller's hands was a thick, dark streak of the viscous black fluid. 

The infection. 

"He's poisoned," Silas said, his voice grim and hollow. "The sickness is in him."

"We got to get out of here!" one of the other loaders yelled, his voice cracking with panic. He spun around and lunged for the heavy iron grate of the drop-cage. 

The panic was contagious. The remaining four men broke and ran for the cage, shoving each other aside to get into the cramped iron box. One of them frantically grabbed the heavy chain attached to the signal bell, yanking it three times in rapid succession—the emergency signal to the hoist operator on the surface. 

"Wait!" Silas roared, pointing a massive, calloused finger at Miller, who had just collapsed face-first onto the tunnel floor. "Don't get in that box! He's turning!"

But the men were deaf with terror. They piled into the cage, slamming the heavy iron grate shut behind them, desperate to put distance between themselves and the slaughter in the tunnel. 

"Si, come on!" Tommy yelled from the dirt, scrambling to his feet. He took a step toward the cage. 

Silas reached out, his massive hand clamping onto the collar of Tommy's shirt, hauling the teenager backward. 

"No," Silas said firmly. 

Inside the cage, the men were shouting at the ceiling, begging the unseen hoist operator to pull them up. 

On the tunnel floor, exactly ten seconds after his heart had stopped beating from massive blood loss, Miller's body jerked violently. 

The heavy shift boss pushed himself up off the rock floor. His hands were slick with his own blood. His neck was a torn, mangled ruin. But his eyes were a milky, dead white. 

"Miller?" one of the men in the cage asked, his voice trembling as he pressed his face against the iron grating. 

Miller turned his head toward the cage, his jaw unhinging with a wet crack. 

With terrifying speed, the dead shift boss threw himself at the iron grate. His bloodstained hands reached through the rusted bars, grabbing the shirt of the nearest miner. 

"Get him off me!" the miner screamed, desperately trying to push himself backward. But the cage was too cramped. There was nowhere to run. 

With a sickening screech of rusted metal, the heavy steel cable above the cage finally went taut. The massive hydraulic winch on the surface engaged, and the cage jerked violently upward, beginning its slow, agonizing ascent to the surface. 

But Miller was still clinging to the outside of the grate. And as the cage lifted off the floor, his unnatural strength allowed him to pull the rusted latch holding the iron doors shut. 

The doors swung open. Miller pulled himself inside. 

Silas and Tommy stood in the flickering light of their carbide lamps, watching in absolute horror as the drop-cage slowly disappeared up the dark, vertical shaft. 

The screams of the five men trapped inside the iron box with the monster echoed down the shaft, amplified by the smooth stone walls. The sound was a symphony of pure, concentrated terror, followed by the wet, tearing sounds of slaughter. 

The screams lasted for exactly two hundred feet of the ascent before silence finally fell. 

Silas stood frozen, his massive hands trembling around the handle of the pickaxe. 

They were one thousand, two hundred feet beneath the surface of the earth. The only functional elevator in the mine was now a flying coffin, heading toward a surface camp that was likely already overrun with the sickness. 

They were trapped. 

Tommy fell to his knees in the coal dust, his shoulders shaking with silent sobs. The reality of their situation was crushing. 

"We can't go up," Tommy whispered, his voice broken. "The cage is gone. The surface is dead. We're buried alive, Si."

Silas slowly turned his head, looking down the dark, cavernous tunnel of the main heading. The flickering light of his lamp barely penetrated the absolute, oppressive darkness of the deep seam. 

"We ain't dead yet, boy," Silas rumbled, his voice low and dangerous. "There's an old ventilation shaft in Section Nine. Hasn't been used since the explosion in '28. But it leads to the surface."

"Section Nine?" Tommy gasped, looking up in horror. "That's three miles deep. Into the old workings. There ain't no lights down there. No fresh air."

"It's either the dark," Silas said, tightening his grip on the iron pickaxe, "or we sit here and wait for whatever comes down that shaft next."

He turned and began walking into the oppressive darkness of the tunnel, the bright white flame of his carbide lamp cutting a narrow, solitary path through the gloom. Tommy scrambled to his feet, terrified of being left behind, and hurried after the massive miner into the deep dark.



Chapter Three: The Dead Air

The absolute darkness of the deep seam was a physical weight, pressing against the meager, flickering light of their carbide lamps. 

Silas and Tommy walked in silence, following the rusted steel rails of the narrow-gauge cart track deeper into the mountain. The main heading gradually narrowed, the smooth rock giving way to jagged, irregular cuts of pure black anthracite. The temperature dropped steadily, the damp air chilling the sweat on their skin. 

The only sounds were the rhythmic crunch of their heavy leather boots on the loose coal and the hollow, echoing drip of groundwater falling from the ceiling into stagnant puddles. 

"How far to Section Nine, Si?" Tommy asked, his voice barely a whisper. He kept looking back over his shoulder, terrified that the infected shift boss might have somehow survived the cage ascent and followed them into the dark. 

"Two miles," Silas rumbled, his massive frame navigating the low ceiling with practiced ease. "Keep your lamp low. We don't want to burn through the carbide before we find the shaft."

Tommy nodded nervously, adjusting the water drip valve on his brass lamp to lower the bright white acetylene flame. 

They walked for what felt like hours, descending deeper into the labyrinthine network of the Blackwood mine. The tunnels began to branch out, creating a terrifying maze of identical black corridors. But Silas didn't hesitate. He possessed the innate, instinctual compass of a man who had spent his entire adult life beneath the earth. 

Finally, the steel tracks abruptly ended. 

They stood before a massive barricade built of heavy, rough-hewn timbers. The wood was black with age and damp rot. Nailed to the center of the barricade was a rusted tin sign with faded white lettering: 

*DANGER. SECTION NINE. ABANDONED 1928. BAD AIR.*

"This is it," Silas said, stepping up to the timbers. 

"Bad air, Si," Tommy pointed out, taking a step back. "If it's blackdamp, we'll suffocate in ten minutes. If it's firedamp, our lamps will blow us to kingdom come the second we step through."

"The explosion in '28 burned most of the firedamp out," Silas replied, his face grim in the lamplight. "And the ventilation shaft we're looking for is at the far end of this section. It should be pulling enough fresh air down to keep the blackdamp from settling too thick. But you keep an eye on your flame. If it starts to turn blue, or if it shrinks, you tell me immediately."

Tommy swallowed hard, his throat dry with fear, but he nodded.
