Silas unslung the heavy iron pickaxe from his shoulder. He stepped back, swung the heavy ash handle in a wide arc, and drove the steel point directly into the center of the rotting timbers. 

The wood splintered with a loud crack, the sound echoing violently down the empty tunnel behind them. Silas swung again, and again, using his immense strength to literally tear the barricade apart. 

After five brutal minutes, he had created a gap wide enough for a man to slip through. 

A rush of cold, stagnant air immediately flowed out of the dark opening. It smelled of sulfur, ancient coal dust, and a faint, metallic odor that made the hairs on the back of Tommy's neck stand up. 

"Stay close," Silas ordered, ducking his head and stepping through the shattered timbers into Section Nine. 

Tommy followed, his hand instinctively gripping the handle of the small scaling hammer hooked to his belt. 

The tunnel beyond the barricade was different. The walls were uneven, scarred by the massive explosion that had collapsed the section eight years ago. Piles of fallen rock and heavy timber supports choked the narrow corridor, forcing them to climb over the debris. 

They walked for another twenty minutes, their lamps cutting narrow swaths of white light through the oppressive gloom. The silence here was even more profound than in the main heading. There was no dripping water. There was absolutely nothing. 

Until there was. 

Silas suddenly stopped dead in his tracks, raising a massive hand to signal Tommy. 

"What is it?" Tommy whispered, his heart hammering against his ribs. 

"Listen," Silas hissed, turning his head slightly, his ear angled down the dark tunnel ahead of them. 

Tommy held his breath, straining to hear past the rushing of his own blood. 

From deep within the black void of Section Nine, a sound emerged. 

*Clink.*

It was faint, but incredibly distinct in the total silence. 

*Clink.*

It was the unmistakable sound of tempered steel striking solid rock. It was the rhythmic, steady sound of a miner swinging a pickaxe into a coal seam. 

*Clink... Clink... Clink.*

"Someone's down there," Tommy breathed, a surge of relief washing over him. "Survivors from the explosion? Or maybe someone else from the camp found a way down?"

"Nobody survived the explosion in '28," Silas said, his voice dropping to a terrifying, hollow register. "And no living man swings a pick with that kind of rhythm in the pitch dark."

"Then what is it?" Tommy asked, the relief instantly vaporizing into cold terror. 

"The sickness," Silas said, his grip tightening on his own pickaxe until his knuckles turned white. "It makes them hungry. But sometimes, it makes them remember."

Silas knew the old stories. He had heard the whispers from the travelers passing through the company towns. The dead didn't just walk. Sometimes, the faint, lingering echoes of their past lives drove their rotting bodies to repeat the motions they knew in life. A dead mother rocking an empty crib. A dead farmer pushing a rusted plow. 

And in the deep earth, a dead miner, trapped in the darkness, swinging his pick at the rock face forever. 

*Clink.*

The sound was getting louder. They were getting closer to the source. 

"Turn your lamp down, Tommy," Silas whispered, reaching up and twisting the water valve on his own brass lamp. The bright white flame immediately shrank to a tiny, dim blue bead. "Don't let them see the light."

Tommy did the same, plunging them into near-total darkness. They were now navigating by memory and touch, feeling their way along the jagged coal wall. 

They rounded a sharp bend in the tunnel, stepping into a large, cavernous chamber that had once been the main hub of Section Nine. 

The sound of the pickaxe was loud now, echoing off the high ceiling. 

*Clink.*

Silas stopped, his hand resting on Tommy's shoulder to keep the boy from moving forward. 

In the center of the chamber, illuminated only by the faint, ghostly luminescence of a rare, phosphorescent fungus growing on the damp timber supports, was a figure. 

It was a man, wearing the canvas overalls and heavy leather boots of a Blackwood miner. But his clothes were shredded, his skin gray and desiccated. He was swinging a heavy iron pickaxe against the solid rock wall with a slow, mechanical rhythm. 

*Clink.*

He didn't need a light to see the rock. He didn't need air to breathe. He was simply a machine of rotting flesh and bone, driven by the lingering echo of his thirty years in the seam. 

But as Silas and Tommy stood frozen in the dark, a small piece of loose coal crunched softly beneath Tommy's heavy boot. 

The sound was tiny. But in the total silence of the cavern, it was like a gunshot. 

The dead miner instantly stopped swinging the pickaxe. 

He didn't turn around. He simply stood there, his back to them, the heavy iron tool hanging loosely in his rotting hands. 

And then, from the absolute blackness of the tunnels branching off from the main chamber, other sounds began to emerge. 

The soft, dragging shuffle of heavy leather boots. The wet, popping sound of unhinging jaws. And the guttural, rattling hiss of dead lungs drawing in the stagnant air. 

There wasn't just one dead miner in Section Nine. 

The explosion in '28 had killed forty men. And the sickness, seeping down through the groundwater and the earth, had found every single one of them.



Chapter Four: The Spark

The dead miner in the center of the cavern dropped his pickaxe. The heavy iron tool hit the stone floor with a loud clang, the sound immediately swallowed by the surrounding darkness. 

He didn't need the tool anymore. The faint echo of his past life was entirely overridden by the brutal, immediate hunger of the sickness. 

He lunged forward, his heavy leather boots dragging across the loose coal. His jaw, unhinged and dripping with thick black fluid, snapped wildly as he aimed for Tommy's throat. 

"Move!" Silas roared, stepping in front of the terrified teenager. 

Silas swung his heavy iron pickaxe, aiming for the dead miner's skull. But the dim, blue bead of light from his carbide lamp provided almost no depth perception in the cavernous space. The swing was a fraction of an inch too low. 

Instead of crushing the skull, the sharp steel point of the pickaxe drove directly into the center of the dead man's chest. 

The kinetic force halted the corpse's forward momentum, but it didn't kill him. The iron point buried itself deep in the desiccated ribcage, grating against the bone and lodging firmly in the spine. 

The dead miner didn't even flinch. He simply reached his rotting hands forward, grabbing the wooden handle of the pickaxe, and began to pull himself up the length of the ash wood toward Silas. 

Silas cursed, planting his heavy boots and trying to wrench the tool free. It wouldn't budge. The iron was wedged tight between the vertebrae. 

From the dark tunnels branching off the main cavern, the shuffling sounds grew louder. 

Five more figures emerged from the pitch black. They were all wearing the same tattered Blackwood Mining uniforms. Their skin was pulled tight over their skulls, preserved by the cold, dry air of the deep seam before the sickness had finally reanimated them. 

"Si, let it go!" Tommy screamed, backing away into the dark. "There's too many of them!"

Silas knew the boy was right. He couldn't hold the pickaxe and fight off six of the infected in near-total darkness. 

He released his grip on the wooden handle, stepping back just as the first dead miner's snapping jaws closed on the empty air where Silas's hands had been a second before. 

"Run!" Silas ordered, grabbing Tommy by the shoulder and shoving him toward the tunnel that led deeper into Section Nine. 

They sprinted blindly into the dark. 

The ground in the abandoned section was treacherous, littered with fallen rock, rotting timber supports, and twisted rails. Running with their carbide lamps turned down to a dim blue flicker was suicidal, but turning them up would paint a massive target on their backs for every dead thing in the mine. 

Tommy was running purely on adrenaline and terror. He didn't see the heavy iron rail tie jutting out from the dirt floor until his boot slammed hard against it. 

He cried out as his ankle twisted violently, sending him crashing face-first into the sharp coal. 

The impact was brutal. But worse than the pain in his ankle was the sound that followed. 

*Clatter. Hiss.*

Tommy's brass carbide lamp had been torn from his leather helmet during the fall. It bounced across the rocks, the impact knocking the flint wheel loose and immediately extinguishing the dim blue flame. 

Silas spun around at the sound of the fall, his own lamp casting a pathetic, narrow beam of light. 

"Tommy!" Silas yelled, scanning the darkness. 

"I'm here!" Tommy gasped, trying to push himself up from the floor. His ankle sent a shooting spike of agony up his leg. "My lamp is out, Si! I can't see anything!"

The absolute, crushing darkness of the deep earth swallowed Tommy entirely. He was less than ten feet away from Silas, but he might as well have been on the dark side of the moon. 

"Don't move," Silas commanded. 

From the cavern behind them, the guttural hissing of the infected was growing louder. They didn't need light to navigate. They were drawn to the sound of Tommy's fall and the fresh scent of living meat. 

Silas knew he had a fraction of a second to make a horrific choice. 

If he kept his lamp dim, he would never find Tommy in time. The infected would swarm the boy in the dark. 

If he turned his lamp up, he would blind himself temporarily, but he would find the boy. And he would draw every single dead miner in Section Nine directly to them. 

Silas didn't hesitate. 

He reached up to the brass lamp mounted on his helmet and twisted the water valve completely open. 

The water flooded the calcium carbide chamber below. The chemical reaction was instantaneous and massive. The tiny blue bead of flame erupted into a blinding, piercing white jet of burning acetylene gas. 

The sudden illumination was agonizing. Silas squinted, his eyes watering profusely against the harsh glare. 

But the light cut through the oppressive darkness like a knife. 

Silas saw Tommy sitting in the dirt, clutching his twisted ankle. 

And he saw what was coming behind him. 

The bright white flame illuminated the tunnel behind them for fifty yards. It was a terrifying, suffocating sight. 

There were at least twenty of the dead miners packed into the narrow corridor. They were shoulder-to-shoulder, a writhing, snapping mass of rotting flesh and tattered canvas. The sudden burst of bright light didn't blind them; it merely gave them a beacon. 

Their milky eyes locked onto the intense white flame of Silas's lamp. The entire horde surged forward with a renewed, frantic energy. 

"Up!" Silas roared, sprinting the ten feet to Tommy and hauling the teenager roughly to his feet. 

"My ankle!" Tommy screamed, his leg buckling instantly under his weight. 

"You run on it, or they eat it!" Silas yelled, throwing Tommy's arm over his massive shoulder. 

He didn't have a weapon. He was carrying a wounded man. And he was leading a horde of twenty infected deeper into a maze of abandoned, collapsed tunnels. 

The light of his carbide lamp bounced wildly off the jagged coal walls as Silas dragged Tommy forward, plunging deeper into the suffocating, terrifying depths of Section Nine.



Chapter Five: The Dead End

The intense white glare of the carbide lamp was both a lifeline and a beacon. It cut a brilliant path through the suffocating darkness of Section Nine, illuminating the jagged rock walls and the twisted, rusted iron tracks. But it also painted a massive, glowing target on Silas's back. 

Behind them, the darkness was alive. The horde of dead miners moved with a terrifying, jerky synchronization, their heavy leather boots dragging across the loose coal in a sound like a massive, breaking wave. They didn't run, but their relentless, untiring pace was rapidly closing the distance. 

Silas Vance was a giant of a man, his muscles built by thirty-five years of swinging a pickaxe into solid rock. But even his immense strength was failing. Carrying the dead weight of a terrified nineteen-year-old over uneven, debris-choked ground was rapidly draining his stamina. His chest heaved, drawing in huge gulps of the cold, stagnant air. 

"Put me down, Si!" Tommy gasped, his voice tight with the agonizing pain radiating from his twisted ankle. "You can outrun them if you drop me!"

"Shut up, boy," Silas grunted, tightening his massive arm around Tommy's waist and dragging him forward over a pile of fallen timber supports. "I ain't leaving nobody in the dark."

The tunnel banked sharply to the left. Silas rounded the corner, his lamp beam sweeping across the darkness ahead. 

The tunnel ended. 

They had run directly into a massive, solid wall of unmined coal. The seam had petered out, or the miners of 1928 had simply stopped digging in this direction. 

"It's a dead end!" Tommy screamed, staring at the sheer rock face. "Si, we're trapped!"

Silas didn't stop. His eyes were scanning the rock wall furiously. "There ain't no dead ends in a main heading, Tommy. They wouldn't lay track to a blank wall without a reason."

His intense white light swept across the left side of the cavern. The beam illuminated a heavy, rivet-studded steel door set flush into the rock face. The steel was rusted and covered in a thick layer of gray dust, making it almost indistinguishable from the surrounding coal. 

Above the door, barely visible beneath decades of grime, was a faded white stenciled sign: 

*REFUGE CHAMBER 4. CAPACITY: 15 MEN.*

"There," Silas rasped, his lungs burning. 

The refuge chamber was a heavily reinforced, airtight bunker designed to keep a crew alive in the event of a cave-in or a massive gas explosion. 

Silas hauled Tommy across the remaining thirty feet of tunnel, throwing the boy roughly against the solid steel door. 

"Get the latch!" Silas roared, spinning around to face the dark tunnel behind them. 

The horde rounded the corner. In the harsh glare of Silas's lamp, they were a horrifying, writhing mass of gray skin, milky eyes, and snapping jaws. The bright light illuminated the thick black fluid dripping from their unhinged mouths, splashing onto the rusted tracks. 

They were less than fifty feet away. 

Tommy grabbed the heavy iron wheel set into the center of the steel door. He pulled with all his might, ignoring the screaming pain in his ankle. The wheel was rusted solid, the gears fused by eight years of damp neglect. 

"It won't turn!" Tommy cried, his hands slipping on the greasy iron. 

"Move!" Silas bellowed. 

He didn't wait for the boy to get out of the way. He grabbed the iron wheel with both of his massive, calloused hands. He planted his boots against the steel frame, locked his broad shoulders, and twisted with every ounce of kinetic energy left in his exhausted body. 

The muscles in Silas's back and arms strained against the rusted iron. For a terrifying second, nothing happened. The dead were twenty feet away. Ten feet. 

With a loud, metallic shriek that echoed off the cavern walls, the rust gave way. The heavy iron wheel turned. 

Silas threw his shoulder against the steel door, pushing it inward. The heavy hinges groaned loudly, but the door swung open, revealing a pitch-black, narrow chamber carved into the rock. 

Silas grabbed Tommy by the collar and hurled him violently into the darkness of the chamber. 

He turned back just as the first infected miner—a man with a shattered jaw and a missing ear—lunged across the threshold. 

Silas didn't have a weapon. He didn't have time to swing his fists. He simply drove his heavy, steel-toed work boot directly into the dead man's chest. The brutal kick sent the corpse flying backward, crashing heavily into the two miners directly behind him. 

Silas threw his massive weight against the steel door, slamming it shut with a deafening, metallic *CLANG*. 

He grabbed the heavy iron locking bar on the inside of the door and dropped it into the heavy steel brackets just as the horde slammed against the outside. 

The solid steel door vibrated violently under the impact of twenty dead men throwing their weight against it. The thick iron bar rattled in its brackets, but it held. The refuge chamber was designed to withstand a million tons of falling rock; it wasn't going to break under the fists of rotting corpses. 

Silas collapsed back against the cold steel, his chest heaving violently, his entire body trembling from the massive adrenaline dump. 

"We're safe," Tommy gasped from the floor of the chamber, the harsh white light of Silas's lamp casting long, distorted shadows across the small room. "They can't get in."

Silas didn't answer immediately. He was staring at the bright white flame of his carbide lamp. 

The refuge chamber was tiny, barely ten feet wide and fifteen feet deep. Along the walls were rusted metal benches, a large iron water barrel, and a hand-cranked air purifier designed to filter out smoke. 

But as Silas watched, the bright, piercing white flame of his lamp began to change. 

The edges of the flame flickered wildly, the intense white color rapidly shrinking and fading into a sickly, pale blue. 

"Si?" Tommy asked, noticing the sudden drop in illumination. "Is your lamp running out of water?"

"No," Silas whispered, a cold, absolute dread settling into his stomach, entirely replacing the adrenaline. 

He reached up and tapped the brass water valve, ensuring it was fully open. The chemical reaction was still producing acetylene gas, but the flame continued to shrink, turning a deep, ghostly blue before stabilizing as a tiny, pathetic bead of light. 

"Then what's wrong with it?" Tommy asked, the panic returning to his voice. 

"The fire needs oxygen to burn, Tommy," Silas said, his deep voice sounding hollow in the cramped steel box. 

He looked up at the ceiling of the refuge chamber. In the corner was a massive iron pipe that was supposed to connect to the surface ventilation system, pumping fresh air down into the bunker. 

The iron pipe was completely sheared off at the ceiling, likely destroyed by the massive explosion of 1928. The chamber was completely cut off from the surface. 

"The explosion cut the air pipe," Silas explained, the terrible reality of their situation settling over them like a shroud. "This chamber is completely airtight. It was designed to keep the firedamp out."

"So?" Tommy asked, not fully understanding. 

"So, the air in here has been sitting stagnant for eight years," Silas said, slowly sliding down the steel door until he was sitting on the cold rock floor. "And every breath we take, every second this lamp burns, we're using up whatever oxygen is left."

He pointed a massive, trembling finger at the tiny, blue bead of flame. 

"That's blackdamp," Silas rumbled. "The air is mostly carbon dioxide and nitrogen. We've locked ourselves in a steel box, Tommy. The dead can't get in, but the air is going to kill us long before they ever could."

They had traded a quick, violent death at the hands of the horde for a slow, agonizing suffocation in the dark.



Chapter Six: The Blackdamp

"Turn it off, Si," Tommy whispered, his voice trembling in the small, enclosed space. 

Silas sat on the cold rock floor, his back pressed against the vibrating steel door. The twenty dead miners outside were relentlessly throwing their weight against the metal, a continuous, deafening rhythm of impacts that rattled the bones in Silas's jaw. 

But Tommy wasn't talking about the noise. He was staring at the tiny, flickering blue bead of flame on Silas's carbide lamp. 

"If I turn it off, we're in the dark," Silas rumbled, the heavy, stagnant air already making it difficult to draw a full breath. 

"The flame is eating the air," Tommy argued, his chest heaving as he began to hyperventilate. The panic was constricting his throat, compounding the effects of the blackdamp. "Every second it burns, that's a breath we don't get. Turn it off."

Silas knew the boy was right. A carbide lamp burned oxygen at roughly the same rate as a resting man. Leaving it on effectively halved their survival time. 

Silas reached up to his helmet. He twisted the brass water valve closed, starving the calcium carbide of moisture. 

The chemical reaction stopped. The tiny blue flame flickered once, twice, and died. 

The darkness that flooded the small refuge chamber was absolute. It wasn't the kind of darkness found in a closed room at night. It was the crushing, profound void of the deep earth, a sensory deprivation so complete it felt like a physical weight pressing against their eyeballs. 

"It's so heavy," Tommy gasped, the sound of his ragged breathing echoing loudly off the steel walls. 

"Slow your breathing, boy," Silas commanded, his voice a disembodied rumble in the void. "You're burning it up too fast. In through your nose, out through your mouth. Slow and steady."

Tommy tried to obey, but the terror was a living thing in the dark. Without visual input, his brain began to manufacture horrors. He imagined the dead miners outside somehow seeping through the edges of the steel door like black fluid. He imagined O'Bannon standing right in front of him, reaching out in the pitch black. 

The pounding on the steel door suddenly stopped. 

The silence that followed was even more terrifying than the noise. The infected hadn't left. They had simply realized that throwing their bodies against the thick steel was achieving nothing. They were standing out there in the tunnel, shoulder-to-shoulder, waiting with the infinite patience of the dead. 

"Why did they stop?" Tommy asked, his voice spiking an octave in panic. 

"They're waiting," Silas said, conserving his words. 

Ten minutes passed. Then twenty. The air in the chamber grew incredibly dense, the mixture of carbon dioxide and nitrogen slowly displacing the remaining oxygen. Silas felt a dull, throbbing ache forming behind his eyes—the first symptom of hypoxia. 

Tommy was faring much worse. The adrenaline of the chase had worn off, leaving only the crushing reality of their tomb. 

"Si," Tommy whispered, his words slurring slightly. "I can see them. The men from the explosion. They're in here with us."

"There ain't nobody in here but us, Tommy," Silas said, recognizing the onset of oxygen-deprivation hallucinations. 

"They're sitting on the benches," Tommy continued, a weak, hysterical giggle escaping his lips. "They're covered in soot. They're telling me to open the door, Si. They want to go home."

Silas pushed himself up from the floor, his massive limbs feeling leaden and sluggish. 

The refuge chamber had been stocked in 1928. Standard protocol dictated that every chamber had an emergency medical kit, a barrel of water, and an oxygen candle—a solid chemical generator that, when ignited, produced a slow, steady stream of pure oxygen. 

Silas dropped to his hands and knees, ignoring the throbbing in his head, and began to blindly sweep the cold rock floor with his calloused hands. 

He crawled past Tommy, who was now engaged in a full, whispered conversation with the empty air. 

Silas felt the rusted iron legs of the metal benches. He felt the curved wood of the water barrel. 

And then, his fingers brushed against a heavy, rectangular steel box bolted to the back wall. 

It was the emergency supplies locker. 

Silas felt for the latch. It was secured with a small brass padlock. 

He didn't have his pickaxe. He didn't have a hammer. But he had the immense, desperate strength of a man drowning in the dark. 

Silas grabbed the small brass padlock with both hands. He twisted violently, putting all of his weight behind the motion. The brittle, ancient brass snapped with a sharp crack. 

He threw the lid of the box open and frantically dug through the contents. He felt bandages, a tin of iodine, and finally, a heavy, cylindrical object wrapped in waxed canvas. 

The oxygen candle. 

He tore the canvas away, his fingers tracing the rough surface of the chemical block. To start the reaction, it required an open flame or a massive spark to ignite the primer cap at the top. 

Silas grabbed his helmet. He didn't turn the water valve—he didn't want the acetylene gas. He just needed the spark from the flint wheel on the side of the lamp. 

He held the flint wheel directly against the primer cap of the heavy candle and struck it hard with his thumb. 

A shower of bright orange sparks illuminated the small chamber for a fraction of a second. The sparks caught the primer. 

With a loud, sharp hiss, the chemical block ignited. 

It didn't produce a flame. It produced a harsh, chemical glow, a pale green light that pushed the darkness back into the corners of the steel room. 

And it produced oxygen. A steady, hissing stream of pure, life-giving air poured from the top of the candle, rapidly mixing with the stagnant blackdamp. 

Silas took a deep breath, the pure oxygen hitting his starving lungs like a physical shock. His vision cleared instantly, the throbbing behind his eyes receding. 

He looked over at Tommy. The teenager was slumped against the water barrel, his face deathly pale, his eyes wide and staring. 

Silas crawled over and dragged Tommy closer to the hissing candle. 

"Breathe, boy," Silas ordered. 

Tommy gasped, his chest heaving as the pure oxygen flooded his system. The hallucinations vanished, replaced by the stark, terrifying reality of the pale green steel box. 

"You did it, Si," Tommy coughed, tears streaming down his face. "We got air."

"We got time," Silas corrected him, his deep voice grim. He looked down at the chemical block. "This candle burns fast. It'll give us an hour. Maybe an hour and a half."

"And then what?" Tommy asked, staring at the locked steel door. 

"And then," Silas said, picking up the broken brass padlock from the floor, "we figure a way to kill twenty dead men in a tunnel with no weapons, or we open that door and let them do it for us."



Chapter Seven: The Hour

The oxygen candle burned with a soft, steady hiss, filling the small steel refuge chamber with a pale, ghostly green light. The air tasted slightly metallic, tinged with the sharp odor of the chemical reaction, but it was breathable. 

For the first thirty minutes, neither man spoke. The sheer relief of drawing a full breath without the crushing weight of the blackdamp kept them silent. 

Silas sat against the heavy steel door, his massive hands resting on his knees. The adrenaline had fully receded, leaving behind a bone-deep, aching exhaustion. 

Across from him, Tommy leaned against the iron water barrel. The boy was shivering, despite the stagnant warmth of the deep seam. His eyes, usually bright with youth, were hollow and dark in the green light. 

"They got her, Si," Tommy whispered, breaking the heavy silence. 

Silas looked up slowly. "Who?"

"Mary," Tommy said, his voice cracking. "The siren this morning. When the Pinkertons locked the company store. I saw the guards running toward the boarding houses. Mary's house is right next to the breaker."

Silas didn't offer empty reassurances. He didn't tell the boy she was probably fine. The brutal reality of their world didn't allow for such pleasantries. 

"She's a tough girl, Tommy," Silas said quietly. "If anyone could find a place to hide, it's her."

Tommy shook his head, burying his face in his soot-stained hands. "She ain't hiding. I know it. When O'Bannon turned in the tunnel... I saw it in his eyes. It ain't just a sickness. It's the end of the whole damn world."

Silas looked at his calloused, scarred hands. He had spent his entire life pulling coal out of the earth to feed a machine that didn't care if he lived or died. He had no wife, no children. The mine was his entire world. And now, the mine was his tomb. 

"Maybe," Silas rumbled, his deep voice vibrating in the small steel room. "But the world ending up there don't change the math down here."

Silas pushed himself up, his joints popping loudly in the quiet chamber. He walked back over to the heavy steel emergency locker and began to methodically empty the remaining contents onto the rock floor. 

He found a roll of thick canvas bandages, a rusted tin cup, and finally, wedged in the back corner of the box, a heavy iron pry bar. It was two feet long, meant for forcing open jammed machinery. 

Silas picked up the pry bar and tossed it across the room. It clattered loudly onto the floor right in front of Tommy's boots. 

"Pick it up," Silas ordered. 

Tommy stared at the iron bar. "For what? You said it yourself, Si. We can't fight twenty of 'em."

"We ain't going to fight 'em," Silas said, tearing the thick canvas bandages into long strips. He began to wrap the tough fabric tightly around his hands and forearms, creating crude, padded gauntlets. "We're going to push through 'em."

Tommy looked up, his eyes wide with disbelief. "Through them? Si, they're standing right outside the door! The second you open it, they're going to flood in here like water!"

"They ain't water," Silas corrected him, pulling the final knot tight with his teeth. "They're dead meat. And they don't know how to organize. They're just going to press against the opening."

Silas walked over to the heavy steel door and placed his canvas-wrapped hands flat against the cold metal. 

"Listen to me carefully, boy," Silas said, his voice dropping to a low, authoritative register that demanded absolute obedience. "This candle has maybe twenty minutes left before it burns out. When it dies, we're in the dark, and the blackdamp comes back."

Tommy swallowed hard, his grip tightening on the iron pry bar. 

"I'm going to grab the wheel," Silas continued, mapping out the suicidal logistics. "I'm going to throw the door open wide. They're going to surge forward. I'm going to step into the gap and use my shoulders to bottleneck the frame. I'm big enough to plug the hole for a few seconds."

"And what about me?" Tommy asked, his voice trembling. 

"You don't fight," Silas commanded, pointing a massive finger at the teenager. "You stay low. You use that iron bar to sweep their legs if they grab you. And you slip past my right hip, directly into the tunnel."

"I ain't leaving you, Si," Tommy protested, standing up, ignoring the shooting pain in his ankle. "We fight our way out together."

"This ain't a discussion, Tommy," Silas roared, the sudden volume making the boy flinch. "I'm fifty years old. I got coal dust in my lungs and arthritis in my knees. I can't outrun 'em. But you can."

Silas's expression softened slightly in the pale green light. "The old ventilation shaft is a half-mile straight down the main tunnel of Section Nine. You find the ladder, you climb until your hands bleed, and you don't look back."

Before Tommy could argue again, the hissing sound in the chamber suddenly changed pitch. 

It grew erratic, sputtering loudly. 

The pale green light illuminating the steel walls flickered, dimming significantly, before flaring up bright again. 

"The candle is dying," Silas said, turning his back to the boy and gripping the rusted iron wheel of the heavy steel door. 

"Si, please," Tommy begged, tears streaming down his face. "Don't do this."

"Get low, Tommy," Silas said, his voice perfectly calm, the stoicism of a man who had made his peace with the mountain. "And when you get to the surface, you find your girl."

The hissing of the chemical block suddenly stopped completely. 

The pale green light vanished in an instant, plunging the refuge chamber back into absolute, crushing darkness. 

The heavy, stagnant air of the dead seam immediately seemed to press against them, the oxygen vanishing like a ghost. 

In the pitch black, Silas tightened his grip on the rusted iron wheel, his muscles bunching as he prepared to open the door to hell.



Chapter Eight: The Breach

The heavy iron bar lifted from the steel brackets with a loud, ringing scrape that echoed through the pitch-black chamber. 

"Get ready, boy," Silas rumbled, his voice tight with the massive exertion of holding the rusted iron wheel. 

Tommy crouched low on the rock floor, gripping the iron pry bar so tightly his hands cramped. He couldn't see a single thing. He was entirely reliant on sound and touch. He felt the cold draft of the blackdamp brushing against his face as Silas began to pull the heavy steel door inward. 

The horde didn't hesitate. The second the door cracked open an inch, the sheer, crushing weight of twenty dead men slammed against it. 

Silas roared, a primal sound of absolute exertion, as he caught the full kinetic force of the horde with his shoulder. The steel door groaned violently, threatening to swing wide open and crush him against the rock wall. 

"Now!" Silas bellowed. 

He didn't try to close the door. He threw his massive bulk directly into the narrow, three-foot gap between the steel edge and the rock frame. He became a human cork in a bottle of unimaginable violence. 

The sensory deprivation of the absolute darkness made the ensuing chaos terrifyingly intimate. 

Silas couldn't see the snapping jaws or the milky eyes. He only felt them. Cold, hard hands grabbed at his heavy canvas work jacket. Rotted fingers clawed blindly at his face. 

He swung his canvas-wrapped fists like sledgehammers into the dark mass of bodies. Every time his fist connected, he felt the sickening crunch of brittle bone and dried flesh shattering under his immense strength. He punched, he shoved, he kicked, using his fifty years of mining muscle to hold the line. 

"Go, Tommy! Go!" Silas roared again, his voice cracking as a set of dead teeth sank deeply into the thick canvas wrapping around his left forearm. 

Tommy threw himself forward into the chaotic void. 

He crawled on his hands and knees, dragging his ruined ankle behind him. He felt the heavy, scuffed leather of Silas's right boot. He used it to orient himself, sliding past the giant miner's leg and out into the main tunnel. 

Instantly, cold hands grabbed him in the dark. 

The horde was pressing so tightly against Silas that some of the infected were being squeezed around the edges of the bottleneck. One of the dead miners grabbed Tommy by the collar of his shirt, yanking him upward into a terrifying, unhinged maw that Tommy couldn't see, but could smell—a horrific wave of rot and sulfur. 

Tommy didn't think. He swung the heavy iron pry bar upward in a desperate, blind arc. 

The heavy iron rod connected with a solid, wet thud. Tommy felt the vibration travel up his arms as the dead miner's jaw shattered. The grip on his collar loosened instantly. 

Tommy tore himself free, scrambling forward over the loose coal and jagged rocks of the tunnel floor. 

He was out of the bottleneck. He was in the main tunnel of Section Nine. 

"I'm out, Si!" Tommy screamed into the darkness, turning back toward the sound of the struggle. "I'm out!"

"Don't look back!" Silas's voice echoed from the breach, distorted by the wet, tearing sounds of the horde pressing against him. "Find the ladder!"

Tommy hesitated for a fraction of a second, the guilt threatening to root him to the floor. But the terrifying sounds coming from the refuge chamber door spurred him into motion. He turned and began to limp blindly down the tunnel, dragging his hand along the rough coal wall to guide him in the pitch black. 

Back at the door, the sheer mathematics of the situation finally caught up with Silas Vance. 

He had crushed four skulls with his canvas-wrapped fists. He had broken a dozen ribs with his heavy boots. But for every dead man he knocked down, three more simply climbed over the fallen body, their unnatural strength fueled by the relentless hunger of the sickness. 

The weight pressing against him was astronomical. It was like trying to hold up a collapsing roof support with his bare shoulders. 

The thick canvas wrapped around his left arm finally tore away. 

Instantly, three sets of cold, wet jaws clamped down onto his exposed forearm, bicep, and shoulder. The pain was blinding, a searing white heat that cut through the darkness. 

Silas didn't scream. He simply planted his boots firmer against the rock floor and swung his right fist in a final, devastating arc, crushing the face of the miner biting his shoulder. 

But the damage was done. The line was broken. 

The combined weight of the remaining fifteen dead men pushed forward, driving Silas backward into the small refuge chamber. The heavy steel door slammed wide open, rebounding off the rock wall with a deafening crash. 

The horde flooded into the tiny room, a tidal wave of rotting flesh and snapping teeth. 

They dragged the giant miner down to the cold rock floor in the absolute darkness. Silas fought to the very last second, his massive hands tearing at the corpses, snapping necks and crushing windpipes, refusing to give the sickness an inch. 

But the deep earth had finally claimed its due. 

The agonizing sounds of Silas Vance being torn apart echoed loudly down the main tunnel of Section Nine, chasing Tommy into the abyss.



Chapter Nine: The Blind Crawl

The darkness was a physical entity, a smothering blanket of absolute black that stripped Tommy of his most vital sense. 

He moved purely on terror, dragging his ruined ankle over the jagged floor of Section Nine. His right hand trailed along the rough, wet surface of the coal wall, using it as a lifeline to keep himself moving in a straight line. Every few steps, his heavy leather boot would snag on a twisted section of rusted rail track or a piece of fallen timber, sending him crashing hard into the dust. 

But he didn't stop. He couldn't stop. 

Behind him, echoing down the cavernous tunnel, the sounds of Silas Vance's death had finally ceased. The wet, tearing noises had been replaced by a silence that was infinitely more terrifying. 

And then, the silence was broken by the sound of heavy leather boots dragging across the loose coal. 

The horde was out of the refuge chamber. They were coming down the tunnel. And in the absolute, crushing darkness of the deep seam, they were infinitely better equipped to hunt than he was to run. 

Tommy pushed himself up from his latest fall, his breath coming in ragged, high-pitched gasps. The pain in his ankle was a constant, searing fire, but the adrenaline muted it just enough to let him walk. 

*Half a mile,* Silas had said. The ventilation shaft was half a mile down the main heading. 

In a well-lit tunnel with two good legs, it was a ten-minute walk. In the pitch black, with a severe sprain and twenty dead men on his heels, it was an eternity. 

"Come on," Tommy sobbed, his voice raw in the dead air. 

He swung his hand forward to find the wall again, but instead of the rough texture of the coal seam, his fingers brushed against nothing but empty space. 

He stumbled, losing his balance entirely, and pitched forward. 

His face slammed hard into a massive, wooden support beam. The impact was brutal, a blinding flash of pain that exploded behind his eyes. He collapsed backward into the coal dust, a warm, thick stream of blood instantly pouring from his shattered nose. 

The heavy, dragging footsteps behind him were getting louder. They were less than fifty feet away. 

Tommy lay on his back in the dark, his ears ringing violently from the impact. The world was spinning. He raised the heavy iron pry bar in his right hand, pointing it blindly into the black void above him. 

He didn't have to wait long. 

He felt the sudden, unnatural displacement of the air right above his face. He smelled the horrific, sulfurous stench of the rotting canvas and dried flesh. 

He didn't see the infected miner lunge. He just swung the heavy iron bar upward with a desperate, terrified scream. 

The iron connected with a sickening, wet crunch. 

The force of the blow shattered the dead man's skull, sending the heavy corpse crashing down directly on top of Tommy. The dead weight drove the breath from Tommy's lungs, pinning his arms to his sides. 

Tommy screamed, thrashing wildly under the corpse. The dead man wasn't moving, but the sheer weight of him was suffocating. Tommy managed to wrench his right arm free, but the iron pry bar was gone, lost somewhere in the darkness of the tunnel floor. 

He planted his boots and shoved, rolling the heavy corpse off his chest. 

He scrambled to his hands and knees, hyperventilating, the blood from his nose mixing with the thick, black dust on his face. 

More footsteps. Faster now. The horde had heard the struggle. 

Tommy crawled forward, abandoning his attempt to walk. It was slower, but it kept him low to the ground, allowing him to feel for the obstacles that were tearing him apart. 

As he dragged himself over a pile of loose slate, he felt a sudden, distinct change in the atmosphere. 

The heavy, stagnant weight of the blackdamp that had filled the tunnel began to thin. A faint, incredibly cold draft of moving air brushed against his bloodied face. 

It tasted clean. It tasted like the sky. 

"The shaft," Tommy whispered, a surge of renewed energy cutting through his absolute exhaustion. 

He followed the draft, crawling frantically over the uneven ground. The cold air grew stronger, a steady, vertical current pulling up from the depths and rushing toward the surface. 

His hands suddenly slipped forward, dropping off the edge of the coal floor into empty space. 

Tommy caught himself just in time, his chest resting heavily on the smooth, rounded edge of a concrete lip. 

He had found it. The old ventilation shaft. 

He reached his hand out over the pitch-black void. The shaft was massive, at least twenty feet across, bored straight up through the solid rock of the Appalachian Mountains. 

His fingers brushed against cold, rusted steel. 

It was the ladder. A vertical series of heavy iron rungs set directly into the concrete wall of the shaft. It led straight up. One thousand, two hundred feet to the surface. 

Tommy didn't hesitate. He swung his body over the edge of the concrete lip, his boots desperately searching for purchase in the dark. 

He found the first rung. He gripped the cold iron tightly, transferring his weight off the tunnel floor and onto the ladder. 

Right as his hands closed around the third rung, a set of cold, dead fingers clamped like a vice around his left ankle. 

The infected had reached the edge of the shaft. 

Tommy screamed, his grip slipping on the rusted iron as the dead man threw his entire weight backward, trying to drag Tommy back into the tunnel. 

"No!" Tommy roared, the terror sharpening into pure, blinding rage. 

He didn't have a weapon. He couldn't see his attacker. He simply kicked backward with his right boot, driving the heavy leather heel blindly into the darkness. 

His boot connected solidly with a face, the impact shattering the dead man's nose and cheekbone. The grip on his ankle loosened for a fraction of a second. 

It was all Tommy needed. He kicked again, tearing his leg free, and frantically pulled himself upward. 

He climbed three rungs, then five, then ten, his heart hammering violently against his ribs. 

Below him, the terrifying sounds of the horde gathering at the edge of the shaft echoed in the darkness. But they couldn't climb. They could only stand there, snapping their jaws at the cold air rushing up from the deep earth. 

Tommy didn't stop. He climbed into the absolute blackness of the vertical shaft, moving hand over hand, leaving the nightmare of Section Nine behind him, and heading toward a surface world that Silas had warned might be entirely dead.



Chapter Ten: The Surface

The climb was an agonizing exercise in sheer, stubborn survival. 

One thousand, two hundred feet of vertical, rusted iron. 

Tommy moved in a trance, a machine fueled by the adrenaline of terror and the phantom pain of Silas Vance's sacrifice. He didn't think about the drop below him. He didn't think about the darkness. He only focused on the next rung. 

Reach up with the right hand. Grip the cold, flaking iron. Pull. Push up with the good right leg. Drag the swollen, screaming left foot up to the next rung. Repeat. 

He climbed for hours. 

His muscles burned with a fiery lactic acid buildup that made his arms shake uncontrollably. The skin on his palms tore, rubbing raw against the sharp rust, leaving a sticky, slick trail of blood on the iron bars. The cold draft rushing up the shaft chilled his sweat-soaked canvas shirt, sending violent shivers through his exhausted frame. 

But as the hours bled into what felt like days, the absolute, crushing blackness of the deep earth began to soften. 

It wasn't a sudden burst of light. It was a slow, agonizing transition from pitch black to a deep, bruised purple, and finally, to a dull, slate gray. 

Tommy stopped climbing, his chest heaving as he wrapped his bleeding arms around a rung and rested his forehead against the cold concrete wall. 

He looked up. 

Fifty feet above him, the shaft ended in a heavy, crisscrossing iron grate. And beyond the grate was the pale, overcast sky of a West Virginia morning. 

"I made it," Tommy whispered, his voice a dry, rattling croak. "Si, I made it."

He didn't have the energy to cry. He just felt a hollow, hollowed-out sense of disbelief. He had beaten the deep seam. He had survived the blackdamp and the dead. 

Driven by the sight of the sky, Tommy forced his burning muscles to move again. He climbed the remaining fifty feet, the air growing colder and sweeter with every agonizing pull. He could smell the damp earth, the pine needles of the Appalachian forest, and the sharp, distinct scent of a wood fire. 

His head bumped against the underside of the heavy iron grate. 

Tommy reached up, gripping the thick iron bars, and pushed. 

The grate didn't move. 

A fresh spike of terror pierced his exhaustion. He pushed harder, putting the strength of his good leg behind the motion. The heavy iron vibrated, but it held firm. 

Tommy squinted against the gray daylight, examining the edge of the grate. 

A heavy, galvanized steel padlock, looped through a thick iron hasp, secured the grate from the outside. The company had locked the old ventilation shaft to keep children and animals from falling into the abyss. 

"No," Tommy sobbed, his raw hands tearing at the lock. "No, no, no."

He rattled the heavy grate violently, his screams echoing down the massive concrete shaft below him. He was trapped. He had climbed one thousand, two hundred feet out of hell, only to be stopped inches from the sky by a five-dollar piece of company hardware. 

Tommy sagged against the ladder, his arms trembling so violently he could barely hold his own weight. 

But as his head rested against the concrete wall of the shaft, his bloodied cheek brushed against something loose. 

He turned his head. Set into the concrete wall just below the lip of the shaft was a large, loose piece of aggregate stone, roughly the size of a grapefruit. The mortar around it had crumbled away over the decades. 

Tommy let go of the ladder with his right hand. He dug his raw, bleeding fingers into the crack around the stone. He pulled with the last, desperate ounce of strength in his body. 

The stone popped free, heavy and solid in his hand. 

Tommy didn't hesitate. He swung the rock upward, smashing it violently against the heavy steel padlock. 

The sound was a deafening *CRACK* that rang out across the quiet mountain woods. 

The galvanized steel held. 

Tommy swung again. And again. He didn't feel the skin tearing off his knuckles. He didn't feel the muscles in his shoulder tearing. He just hammered the heavy stone against the lock, fueled by the sheer, desperate will to live. 

On the fifth strike, the brittle, hardened steel of the hasp finally gave way. The lock shattered, raining sharp pieces of metal down the shaft. 

Tommy dropped the stone and threw both hands against the iron grate, shoving upward with a primal roar. 

The heavy grate swung open on its rusted hinges, crashing loudly against the forest floor. 

Tommy hauled his broken, bleeding body over the concrete lip and collapsed onto a bed of damp pine needles. 

He lay there for a long time, staring up at the gray, overcast sky, his chest heaving as he drew in huge, uninhibited breaths of the cold mountain air. The wind rustled the high branches of the pines. A crow cawed in the distance. 

It was the most beautiful sound he had ever heard. 

Eventually, the freezing cold forced him to move. He had to find shelter. He had to get down the mountain to the company town and find Mary. 

He pushed himself up onto his good leg, leaning heavily against the trunk of a massive oak tree for support. 

The ventilation shaft was located on a high ridge overlooking the narrow valley where the Blackwood Mining Company town was situated. Tommy limped to the edge of the ridge, pushing through the thick underbrush, until the valley floor came into view. 

Tommy stopped. His breath caught in his throat. 

The smell of wood fire he had noticed in the shaft hadn't been coming from a hearth. 

The massive, towering wooden structure of the coal breaker house was entirely engulfed in flames, a roaring inferno sending a thick column of black smoke high into the overcast sky. 

The company store, the heavily fortified heart of the town, had its heavy double doors smashed inward. The windows were shattered, the shelves inside completely gutted. 

But it was the streets that made Tommy's blood run cold. 

The dirt roads winding between the rows of identical, cramped boarding houses were packed tight with people. 

From the high ridge, it looked like a massive town meeting. But they weren't moving right. They were shuffling aimlessly, bumping into each other, a chaotic, writhing mass of humanity. 

The sickness hadn't just spread. It had consumed the entire valley. 

Tommy's eyes desperately scanned the horrific scene below, drawn magnetically toward the second row of boarding houses. Mary's house. 

The front porch was torn away. The windows were dark. 

And standing in the muddy street directly in front of the house, surrounded by a dozen other infected townspeople, was a figure wearing a distinctive, bright red wool coat. 

Tommy recognized the coat instantly. He had bought it for Mary at the company store last Christmas. 

He watched in numb, silent horror as the figure in the red coat turned her face up toward the gray sky, her jaw opening in a silent, unnatural snarl. 

The wind shifted, blowing the thick, black smoke from the burning breaker house up the ridge, carrying with it the faint, terrifying sound of thousands of snapping jaws. 

Tommy slowly slid down the rough bark of the oak tree until he was sitting in the damp dirt. 

He looked down at his raw, bleeding hands. He looked back at the rusted iron grate of the ventilation shaft he had just spent twelve hours climbing out of. 

Silas had died to give him a chance to live. But the giant miner had been wrong about one crucial detail. 

There was no world left to climb up to. 

Tommy pulled his knees to his chest, wrapping his arms around his legs to stop the violent shivering. He was alone on the mountain, a mile above a town of the dead, with a shattered ankle and a broken heart. 

The deep seam hadn't killed him. It had just spared him for the brutal indifference of the surface. 

He closed his eyes and listened to the wind howling through the pines, waiting for the long, cold winter to finish what the sickness had started.



Story Four: Dead Water

Chapter One: The Delta Star

The muddy waters of the Mississippi River were as black as crude oil beneath the starless, overcast sky. 

The *Delta Star*, a wooden, flat-bottomed paddlewheel steamboat built in 1918, pushed its way against the heavy current. The massive, iron-bound wooden buckets of the rear paddlewheel churned the water into a frothy, white wake that was instantly swallowed by the darkness behind them. 

High up in the glass-enclosed pilothouse, Captain Elias Thorne stood at the massive wooden spoked wheel. He was sixty years old, his face weathered into deep, permanent creases by decades of river wind and glaring sun. He wore a heavy wool peacoat over a faded canvas shirt, his eyes locked onto the narrow beam of the brass searchlight mounted on the roof above him. 

The beam swept back and forth across the dark water, illuminating massive, submerged logs and the muddy banks of the Louisiana shoreline. 

"Coffee, Cap," a voice said softly from the open doorway. 

Elias didn't take his eyes off the river. "Put it on the binnacle, Cora."

Cora, a tough, pragmatic twenty-two-year-old woman who served as the boat's cook and deckhand, stepped into the pilothouse. She set a chipped enamel mug of pitch-black, chicory coffee on the wooden housing of the compass. She was wearing men's canvas trousers and a thick woolen sweater, her dark hair tied back with a piece of twine. 

"Engine's running hot," Cora reported, leaning against the glass window and staring out into the dark. "Sully says the wet pine we loaded in Baton Rouge is burning too fast. We're losing steam pressure."

"Tell Sully to throw more of it in the firebox," Elias rumbled, his large hands gripping the wheel tightly. "We ain't stopping to cut dry wood. Not tonight."

Cora nodded, taking a sip from her own mug. 

They both looked out the port-side window. On the western bank of the river, perhaps five miles inland, a dull, angry orange glow painted the low-hanging clouds. It wasn't the glow of a rising sun. It was the glow of a town burning to the ground. 

It was the third fire they had seen since leaving New Orleans three days ago. 

"You think they made it out?" Cora asked quietly, her voice barely carrying over the rhythmic, deafening *thump-thump-thump* of the massive steam engine below decks. 

"Nobody made it out of New Orleans, Cora," Elias said, the brutal honesty a hallmark of a man who had seen the worst of the river. 

The memory of their escape was burned permanently into his mind. The *Delta Star* had been docked at the bustling wharves of the French Quarter when the sickness finally took the city. It hadn't been a slow, creeping panic. It had been a sudden, terrifying explosion of violence. 

Elias had watched from the hurricane deck as the dead flooded the streets, tearing into the screaming crowds. People had thrown themselves into the river in a desperate attempt to escape the teeth of the infected, only to drown in the treacherous currents of the Mississippi. 

Elias had ordered Sully to cast off the mooring lines without waiting for the harbor master. They had steamed out of the harbor with fifty screaming passengers packed onto the lower deck, leaving a city of the dead burning behind them. 

"We're safe on the water, Cap," Cora said, trying to convince herself as much as him. "The dead can't swim."

"They don't need to swim," Elias said darkly. "They just sink and walk across the bottom. The river is deep, but it ain't infinite."

He reached up and grabbed the brass handle of the searchlight, swinging the powerful beam across the water directly ahead of their bow. 

"Lord Almighty," Elias whispered. 

The bright white beam illuminated a small, wooden skiff floating dead in the water, caught in a massive eddy near the eastern bank. The skiff had no oars. It was drifting aimlessly, turning slow circles in the muddy current. 

And lying slumped over the wooden bench in the center of the small boat was a figure. 

"Is he alive?" Cora asked, leaning forward until her breath fogged the cold glass of the pilothouse window. 

"Can't tell," Elias said, his jaw tightening. 

In the brutal calculus of the new world, a survivor in the water was no longer a rescue mission. It was a massive, terrifying risk. They had fifty terrified passengers locked in the main salon on the boiler deck. They were already short on food and clean water. Bringing an unknown person aboard, especially someone who might have been bitten, was suicidal. 

But Elias Thorne was a riverman. And the oldest law of the river was that you never left a man to drown. 

"Ring the engine room," Elias ordered, his hand reaching for the brass telegraph lever. "Tell Sully to cut the steam to half ahead. We're pulling alongside."

"Cap, are you sure?" Cora asked, her hand hovering over the speaking tube that led down to the boiler room. "If he's got the sickness..."

"If he's got the sickness, he's dead," Elias said flatly. "And if he's dead, I'll put a bullet in his head and throw him back to the catfish. But if he's a living man, I ain't leaving him out here in the dark."

Cora hesitated for a fraction of a second, then pulled the brass whistle on the speaking tube. "Sully! Half ahead! Cap's bringing us alongside a drifter!"

The massive rhythmic thumping of the paddlewheel immediately slowed, the *Delta Star* losing speed as it pushed against the current. 

Elias expertly spun the heavy wooden wheel, angling the massive, flat-bottomed steamboat toward the eastern bank. The hull groaned loudly as the boat caught the edge of the eddy, drifting perfectly alongside the small wooden skiff. 

"Get down to the main deck," Elias ordered, pulling a heavy, long-barreled Colt revolver from the pocket of his peacoat and placing it on the binnacle. "Take a boat hook. Don't let that skiff hit the hull, or we'll splinter it."

Cora nodded, setting her coffee mug down and turning to leave the pilothouse. 

"And Cora?" Elias called out, his voice sharp and commanding. 

She stopped in the doorway, looking back at the old captain. 

"If he ain't breathing," Elias said, his hand resting on the wooden grip of the revolver, "don't pull him aboard. You push that skiff right back into the current."

Cora didn't reply. She just nodded grimly and disappeared down the narrow, steep stairs leading to the darkness of the main deck below.



Chapter Two: The Drifter

The main deck of the *Delta Star* sat incredibly low to the water, barely three feet above the muddy surface of the Mississippi. 

The heat down here was oppressive, radiating outward from the massive iron boiler room located amidships. The air smelled strongly of burning pine, hot grease, and the stagnant, rotting vegetation of the river. 

Cora stepped out of the narrow companionway onto the starboard guardrail, grabbing a ten-foot wooden boat hook from its brackets on the bulkhead. The massive, rhythmic *thud-thud-thud* of the paddlewheel was deafening here, even running at half-speed, vibrating through the solid oak deck planks beneath her boots. 

Above her, the intense white beam of Elias's searchlight cut through the darkness, locking onto the drifting wooden skiff. 

The *Delta Star* eased forward, the dark water rushing past the hull. Elias was a master of the river; he brought the massive steamboat alongside the tiny skiff with a gentle precision that barely created a ripple. 

Cora leaned over the low wooden railing, extending the heavy wooden pole of the boat hook over the water. 

"Hey!" Cora shouted, her voice easily swallowed by the noise of the steam engine. "Hey, in the boat!"

The figure slumped in the center of the skiff didn't move. 

It was a man, wearing a fine, tailored wool suit that was now caked in thick, drying mud. He was lying face-down across the wooden rowing bench, his arms dangling loosely toward the floorboards. 

Cora hooked the bronze, curved tip of the pole onto the wooden gunwale of the skiff and pulled sharply. 

The small boat slid smoothly through the black water, bumping gently against the heavy wooden rub rail of the *Delta Star*. 

Cora wrapped the thick mooring line attached to the skiff's bow around an iron cleat on the deck, securing it tight. She kept the wooden pole in her right hand, her knuckles white with tension. 

"Mister?" Cora called out again, leaning slightly further over the railing. 

She used the blunt end of the bronze hook to gently prod the man's shoulder. 

The reaction was instantaneous and terrifying. 

The man didn't wake up; he erupted. His body snapped upward with an unnatural, jerky violence that defied the mechanics of normal muscle and bone. 

He spun around to face Cora. The bright white light from the pilothouse illuminated a face that was a mask of pure horror. His skin was the color of dirty chalk, his lips pulled back in a permanent, rictus snarl. The tailored collar of his expensive wool suit was torn away, revealing a massive, ragged bite wound on the side of his neck that had completely severed his jugular vein. 

He had bled to death in the skiff, miles downriver. And now, the sickness was driving him. 

His milky, dead eyes locked onto Cora. Before she could react, he lunged forward, his rotting hands clamping down onto the wooden shaft of her boat hook with terrifying strength. 

"Let go!" Cora screamed, planting her boots on the wet deck and trying to pull the pole backward. 

But the dead man didn't let go. He used her leverage against her. He threw his entire weight backward into the skiff, pulling the wooden pole violently toward the water. 

Cora was instantly jerked forward, her stomach slamming hard against the low wooden railing. The sudden shift in momentum sent her upper body tumbling over the edge. She stared down at the black, rushing water of the Mississippi, her boots scrambling desperately against the wet deck to keep from going over. 

"Cora!" a rough voice shouted from the darkness behind her. 

It was Sully, the boat's chief engineer. He was a massive, barrel-chested Irishman covered in coal dust and grease, holding a heavy iron spanner wrench. 

Sully lunged forward, grabbing the heavy canvas collar of Cora's sweater and hauling her violently backward onto the safety of the deck. 

The sudden release of tension caused the dead man to lose his balance. He fell backward into the bottom of the skiff, the wooden boat rocking violently in the current. 

"He's dead, Sully!" Cora yelled, scrambling to her feet, her heart hammering against her ribs. "The sickness!"

"I got him," Sully growled, stepping up to the railing and raising the heavy iron wrench. 

But the dead man was terrifyingly fast. Driven by the primal hunger of the infection, he didn't try to stand up in the rocking boat. He simply reached out, grabbed the wooden railing of the *Delta Star*, and vaulted himself out of the skiff with raw, animalistic power. 

He cleared the railing and slammed directly into Sully's massive chest. 

The impact drove the heavy engineer backward. Sully swung the iron wrench, but the close quarters rendered the weapon useless. The wrench glanced harmlessly off the dead man's shoulder. 

They crashed onto the hard oak deck, the dead man landing on top, his jaws snapping furiously at Sully's face. 

"Get him off!" Sully roared, his thick, grease-stained hands desperately trying to hold the snapping, rotting teeth away from his throat. The dead man's strength was overwhelming, the thick black fluid dripping from his mouth onto Sully's face. 

Cora didn't freeze. She spun around, her eyes frantically scanning the bulkhead. 

Mounted on the wall next to the companionway was a heavy, red-painted fire axe, secured behind a thin pane of glass. 

Cora drove her elbow into the glass, shattering it completely. She grabbed the smooth ash handle of the axe, tearing it from its brackets, and spun back toward the struggle on the deck. 

Sully was losing. His arms were shaking under the immense pressure, the dead man's teeth snapping less than an inch from his nose. 

Cora raised the fire axe high over her head. She didn't hesitate. She didn't think about the tailored suit or the man he used to be. 

She brought the heavy steel blade down in a brutal, vertical arc. 

The sharp edge of the axe buried itself deep into the center of the dead man's skull, splitting the bone with a sickening crunch. The kinetic force of the blow drove the corpse's face instantly downward, burying it against Sully's shoulder. 

The dead man went completely limp, the unnatural tension leaving his body in a rush. 

Cora stood over them, her chest heaving, her hands gripping the axe handle so tightly her knuckles ached. 

"Sully," Cora gasped, reaching down to grab the dead man's tailored jacket and hauling the heavy corpse off the engineer. "Sully, are you alright?"

Sully lay on his back on the wet oak planks, staring up at the dark sky. His chest was rising and falling in rapid, shallow breaths. He didn't answer her. 

Cora dropped the axe and dropped to her knees beside him. "Sully?"

The massive engineer slowly turned his head to look at her. The grease and coal dust on his face couldn't hide the absolute, terrifying paleness of his skin. 

He slowly lifted his right hand, pulling his thick canvas work shirt away from his shoulder. 

Cora felt her stomach drop into the cold, black water of the river. 

Right below Sully's collarbone, where the dead man's face had been driven downward by the force of the axe blow, the canvas shirt was torn. And beneath the torn fabric, surrounded by a thick smear of viscous black fluid, was a deep, ragged bite wound. 

The drifter was dead. But the sickness was officially aboard the *Delta Star*.



Chapter Three: The Boiler Room

Sully didn't scream. He didn't cry. He simply stared at the deep, jagged teeth marks in his heavy shoulder, watching the thick black fluid mix with his own bright red blood. 

He was a man who had spent forty years understanding the brutal mathematics of steam pressure, heat, and failing metal. He understood cause and effect. He knew exactly what this meant. 

"Sully," Cora whispered, the heavy fire axe trembling in her hands. The adrenaline was draining out of her, replaced by a cold, paralyzing horror. "We got to get you to Cap. We got iodine in the pilothouse."

"Iodine ain't going to fix this, lass," Sully rumbled, his thick Irish brogue barely masking the sudden tightness in his chest. 

He reached out with his good arm and grabbed the tailored collar of the dead man's suit. With a massive heave of his broad shoulders, Sully dragged the corpse across the wet oak planks and shoved it under the low wooden railing. 

The body fell with a heavy splash, disappearing instantly into the churning, black wake of the paddlewheel. 

Sully kicked the small wooden skiff away from the hull, letting the current take it back out into the dark river. 

"Listen to me, Cora," Sully said, turning back to the terrified girl. The pale light from the pilothouse above cast his grease-stained face in sharp, harsh relief. "You go back up to Cap. You tell him the drifter was dead, and we sent him back to the catfish. You tell him the line broke. You tell him whatever you want. But you do not tell him about this."

He pointed a massive, trembling finger at the bite on his shoulder. 

"But Sully..." Cora began, tears welling in her eyes. 

"Cap is a good man," Sully interrupted, his voice hardening into a command. "He'll try to lock me in a cabin. He'll try to save me. And when I turn, I'll tear right through whatever wooden door he puts me behind, and I'll kill half the passengers on the boiler deck before he can stop me."

Cora stared at him, unable to argue with the brutal, undeniable logic. 

"I'm going down to the engine room," Sully said, turning toward the heavy iron door set into the bulkhead. "The pine is wet. We're losing pressure. I'm going to stoke the fires and get us moving as fast as this old tub can handle. And then I'm going to lock the door from the inside."

"Sully, no," Cora sobbed, dropping the axe to the deck. 

"Go to the pilothouse, Cora," Sully said softly. "And don't come down here. No matter what you hear."

He didn't wait for her to agree. He pulled open the heavy iron door and stepped into the suffocating heat of the companionway leading down into the hull of the boat, slamming the heavy steel behind him. 

The boiler room of the *Delta Star* was a vision of hell even on a good day. 

It was a cavernous space taking up the bottom third of the hull, dominated by two massive, cast-iron boilers that fed the massive steam cylinders driving the paddlewheel. The heat was a physical, crushing weight, regularly topping a hundred and thirty degrees. The air was thick with coal dust, wood smoke, and the deafening hiss of escaping steam. 

Sully walked down the metal grating of the stairs, his boots clanking loudly over the roar of the fires. 

He walked directly to the heavy iron bulkhead door that separated the boiler room from the rest of the lower deck. He grabbed the heavy steel wheel and spun it hard, engaging the thick locking bolts. 

He was sealed in. 

Sully turned and walked to the massive pile of wet pine logs stacked against the hull. The sickness was already moving through his veins. It didn't start like a normal fever. It felt like liquid ice pouring into his chest, while his skin burned with a terrifying, dry heat. His vision began to swim, the bright orange glow of the fireboxes leaving long, strobing trails across his vision. 

"Not yet," Sully grunted through gritted teeth. 

He grabbed a heavy steel pitchfork, scooped up three massive pine logs, and threw them violently into the open maw of the port-side firebox. He grabbed three more and threw them into the starboard box. 

He didn't care that the wood was wet. He didn't care about efficiency. He just needed volume. He stoked the fires with a frantic, desperate energy, ignoring the burning pain in his bitten shoulder. 

He watched the brass pressure gauges mounted on the massive iron pipes. 

*150 PSI... 160 PSI...*

The needle was climbing. The massive steam pistons above him pumped faster, the *thud-thud-thud* of the paddlewheel increasing to a frantic, violent rhythm. 

*180 PSI.*

The red line on the gauge was 200 PSI. Above that, the ancient iron boilers risked catastrophic failure. 

Sully dropped the pitchfork. The ice in his chest had reached his heart. His breathing became ragged, wet, and incredibly shallow. He staggered backward, his massive legs suddenly feeling like wet sand. 

He had stoked the fires. He had given the *Delta Star* enough speed to put miles between them and the burning town on the shore. 

Now, he had to ensure he didn't become a monster. 

Sully turned toward the massive, open iron door of the central boiler firebox. The roar of the flames inside was deafening, a blinding white-hot inferno that consumed everything it touched. 

He had to go in. It was the only way to completely destroy the brain and prevent reanimation. 

He took a step forward, the intense heat singing the hairs on his arms. 

But the sickness was faster than he had calculated. It didn't give him the dignity of a final choice. 

As Sully raised his foot for the final step, a massive aneurysm, caused by the thick, black fluid coagulating in his brain, ruptured. 

Sully's eyes rolled back in his head. He collapsed forward, his heavy body crashing onto the iron grating of the floor, inches away from the open door of the firebox. 

He was dead before his face hit the metal. 

For ten seconds, the boiler room was filled only with the roar of the raging fires and the frantic pumping of the over-pressurized steam pistons. 

Then, Sully's massive, calloused hand twitched. 

His body jerked violently, a horrific spasm that arched his back off the floor. 

He pushed himself up, kneeling on the iron grating. The grease and coal dust on his face were illuminated by the harsh, blinding light of the open firebox. 

His eyes opened. The deep, warm brown was gone, replaced entirely by a milky, dead white. 

He slowly turned his head away from the fire, the heat no longer registering on his dead nerves. He looked toward the heavy, locked iron door leading to the rest of the boat. 

The chief engineer was dead. But a massive, incredibly strong infected man was now locked inside the engine room, and the fires he had stoked were burning completely out of control.



Chapter Four: The Pressure

Cora climbed the steep, narrow wooden stairs to the pilothouse with legs that felt like lead. Her hands were shaking so violently she had to grip the handrail to keep from falling backward. 

She stepped into the small, glass-enclosed room. The air up here was cool, completely detached from the sweltering nightmare she had just left on the main deck. 

Captain Elias Thorne was standing exactly where she had left him, his hands resting lightly on the massive wooden spokes of the ship's wheel. He didn't turn around as she entered. 

"Well?" Elias asked, his eyes focused on the narrow beam of the searchlight cutting through the dark river ahead. 

"He was dead, Cap," Cora lied, her voice sounding incredibly small and fragile over the rhythmic thumping of the engine. "Must have been dead for a day. The mooring line... the mooring line snapped when I tried to pull him tight. The skiff drifted off."

Elias was silent for a long moment. He adjusted the wheel slightly to port, compensating for a strong cross-current. 

"Alright," Elias finally said, his voice betraying no emotion. "We tried. That's all the river asks of us."

Cora walked over to the binnacle and picked up her cold coffee mug, simply to give her shaking hands something to hold onto. She stared out the window, the guilt of lying to the captain warring with the absolute terror of what Sully had just done to protect them. 

But as the minutes ticked by, the rhythmic *thud-thud-thud* of the massive paddlewheel behind them began to change. 

It wasn't slowing down. It was accelerating. 

The wooden floorboards of the pilothouse began to vibrate with a harsh, high-frequency shudder that rattled the glass windows in their frames. The entire superstructure of the *Delta Star* groaned under the sudden, massive increase in kinetic energy. 

"What in God's name is Sully doing down there?" Elias muttered, his brow furrowing as he felt the heavy wooden wheel vibrating in his hands. 

He reached over and pulled the brass whistle on the speaking tube that led directly down to the boiler room. 

"Sully!" Elias shouted into the brass funnel. "Sully, ease off the fire! You're shaking the timbers apart!"

He pressed his ear to the funnel, waiting for the engineer's gruff, Irish reply. 

There was only silence, accompanied by the terrifying, high-pitched hiss of severely over-pressurized steam. 

Elias stood up straight, his face tight with concern. "He ain't answering."

Cora felt her stomach drop. She knew exactly why Sully wasn't answering. 

"Maybe the noise..." Cora started, her voice trembling. 

"Sully can hear that whistle over a hurricane," Elias said sharply. He stepped away from the wheel and grabbed the heavy Colt revolver from the binnacle, shoving it into the pocket of his peacoat. 

"Take the wheel, Cora," Elias commanded. "Keep her steady on the searchlight beam. Don't steer too close to the banks."

"Cap, wait!" Cora blurted out, her panic overriding her promise to the engineer. "Don't go down there!"

Elias stopped in the doorway, turning back to look at her with piercing blue eyes. "Why not, Cora?"

Cora opened her mouth, the truth right on the tip of her tongue, but the words wouldn't come. She saw the heavy revolver in Elias's pocket. If she told him Sully was infected, Elias would go down there to put a bullet in his friend's brain. And in the cramped, chaotic boiler room, a gunshot could easily sever a high-pressure steam line, killing them all instantly. 

"The... the heat, Cap," Cora stammered weakly. "It's dangerous down there."

Elias stared at her for a long second, his eyes narrowing slightly. He knew she was lying. But the violent shuddering of the deck beneath his boots demanded immediate action. 

"Keep her steady," Elias repeated, his voice hard. 

He turned and disappeared down the steep stairs, heading for the main deck. 

The heat hit Elias the moment he reached the bottom of the companionway. It was a suffocating, unnatural wall of hot air that smelled strongly of burning pine and scorching iron. The noise was deafening. The massive steam pistons were pumping with a frantic, suicidal speed. 

Elias marched down the narrow corridor leading to the boiler room. He reached the heavy iron bulkhead door. 

He grabbed the heavy steel wheel to open it, but the wheel wouldn't budge. The locking bolts were engaged from the inside. 

"Sully!" Elias roared, pounding his heavy fist against the solid iron door. "Open this damn door!"

Set into the iron door at eye level was a small, thick pane of wire-reinforced glass, designed to let the captain visually check the fires without letting the heat out into the corridor. 

Elias leaned forward and peered through the small, dirty window. 

The boiler room was an inferno. Both fireboxes were wide open, roaring with blinding, white-hot flames. The heat inside must have been pushing a hundred and fifty degrees. 

And kneeling on the iron grating of the floor, perfectly still amidst the chaos, was Sully. 

"Sully, what the hell are you doing?" Elias yelled, his voice muffled by the thick iron door. "You're going to blow the boilers!"

Sully slowly stood up. 

He didn't wipe the sweat from his brow. He didn't reach for a tool. He simply turned around and faced the small glass window in the door. 

Elias felt the breath catch in his throat. 

The engineer's face was a mask of grease and coal dust, but his eyes were completely white, reflecting the harsh light of the roaring fires. The thick canvas of his shirt was torn at the shoulder, revealing a massive, ragged bite wound dripping with black fluid. 

"Dear God," Elias whispered, instinctively taking a step backward. 

The dead engineer lunged. 

Sully's massive body slammed against the heavy iron door with terrifying force. The thick metal buckled slightly under the impact, the heavy locking bolts groaning in their brackets. 

Sully didn't stop. He began to pound his massive, calloused fists against the wire-reinforced glass of the window, his jaws snapping wildly, a thick string of black fluid splattering against the glass on the inside. 

Elias stumbled backward, pulling the Colt revolver from his pocket. His hands, which had held the wheel of the *Delta Star* steady through a dozen hurricanes, were shaking uncontrollably. 

He leveled the heavy barrel of the revolver at the small glass window. 

But as he raised the gun, his eyes caught sight of the large brass pressure gauge mounted on the main steam pipe running along the ceiling of the corridor, just outside the boiler room. 

The needle was vibrating wildly at 195 PSI. The red line was 200. 

Elias lowered the gun. 

The boiler room was locked. The only pressure release valves were located inside that room, mounted directly on top of the cast-iron boilers. 

If he shot the glass and tried to shoot Sully, the dead man would undoubtedly attack him before he could reach the valves. If he did nothing, the massive steam boilers would explode in less than five minutes. 

An explosion of that magnitude would instantly vaporize the entire midsection of the *Delta Star*. The fifty terrified passengers locked in the main salon directly above the boiler room would be boiled alive or blown into the river. 

Elias Thorne stood in the sweltering corridor, staring through the glass at the monster that used to be his friend. The math was brutal, and the time was almost up.



Chapter Five: The Release Valve
