The brass needle on the main steam pipe gauge twitched violently, kissing the thick red line that marked two hundred pounds per square inch. The heavy iron pipes running along the ceiling groaned, a terrifying, deep metallic sound of impending catastrophic failure. 

Captain Elias Thorne didn't have five minutes. He had sixty seconds. 

He stepped back from the thick iron bulkhead door. He couldn't shoot the locking mechanism; the massive steel bolts were set deep inside the reinforced frame. But the door opened outward into the corridor, which meant the heavy iron hinges were exposed on his side. 

Elias raised the heavy Colt revolver, gripping the wooden handle tightly with both hands to steady his aim over the violent shuddering of the deck. 

He aimed at the top hinge, taking a deep breath of the scorching air. 

*BANG.*

The deafening roar of the .45 caliber round in the narrow, enclosed corridor was absolute agony on his eardrums. The heavy lead slug shattered the brittle cast iron of the upper hinge, sending sharp fragments of shrapnel pinging off the steel walls. 

Behind the wire-reinforced glass, the dead engineer roared, slamming his rotting fists against the door in response to the noise. 

Elias cocked the hammer back and aimed at the bottom hinge. 

*BANG.*

The lower hinge shattered. The heavy iron door instantly shifted on its frame, sagging inward under the massive weight of the dead man pressing against it from the other side. 

Elias didn't wait for Sully to push his way out. He took a running start, launching his right boot directly into the center of the heavy iron door. 

The door collapsed inward with a deafening crash, falling flat onto the iron grating of the boiler room floor. 

The wall of heat that rushed out of the room was like a physical blow. The air was a blinding mix of white-hot glare from the open fireboxes, thick black coal dust, and swirling wood smoke. 

Sully had fallen with the door. But the infected engineer didn't feel the impact. He scrambled to his feet with terrifying speed, his milky eyes locking onto the captain. 

Elias raised the Colt. He aimed for the center of the dead man's grease-stained forehead. 

But as his finger tightened on the trigger, a sudden, violent hiss of escaping steam from a compromised pipe joint obscured his vision in a cloud of white vapor. 

*BANG.*

The heavy slug caught Sully high on the left shoulder, shattering his collarbone and spinning him violently to the side. 

But a shoulder wound didn't stop the sickness. Sully used the momentum of the spin to lunge forward, tackling Elias around the waist before the captain could cock the hammer for a second shot. 

They crashed backward into the narrow corridor. 

The heavy revolver flew from Elias's hand, skittering across the wet wooden deck and disappearing under a pile of coiled mooring lines. 

Sully's massive, grease-stained hands closed around Elias's throat. The dead man's strength was overwhelming, the thick fingers instantly crushing the captain's windpipe. Sully's jaw snapped wildly, the teeth missing Elias's nose by a fraction of an inch, thick black fluid splattering across the captain's cheeks. 

Elias didn't panic. He was a riverman, used to fighting the brutal currents of the Mississippi. 

He brought both of his knees up, planting his heavy boots squarely against Sully's chest. With a massive heave, utilizing the last of his oxygen-starved strength, Elias kicked upward. 

The dead engineer was thrown over Elias's head, crashing heavily back onto the fallen iron door inside the sweltering boiler room. 

Elias scrambled to his feet, gasping for air. He didn't look for the gun. The needle on the pressure gauge outside the door was now buried deep in the red. The entire hull of the *Delta Star* was vibrating with a high, screaming pitch. 

Elias threw himself into the boiler room. 

He ignored Sully, who was struggling to his feet on the iron grating. He ignored the blinding heat of the roaring fireboxes. He scrambled up the short iron ladder attached to the side of the massive starboard boiler. 

At the top of the boiler, obscured by swirling smoke and escaping steam, was the massive brass wheel of the primary pressure release valve. 

Elias grabbed the wheel with both hands. 

The brass was scalding hot, heated to over two hundred degrees by the superheated steam inside the iron tank. Elias felt the skin on his palms instantly blister and tear. He screamed, a raw, ragged sound of absolute agony, but he didn't let go. 

He locked his shoulders and twisted the heavy wheel. 

It groaned, resisting him for a terrifying second, before the threads finally gave way. 

The release was instantaneous and violent. 

A massive jet of superheated steam erupted from the top of the boiler, roaring up through the exhaust pipe that led directly to the roof of the *Delta Star*. The sound was an earth-shattering shriek, like a massive train whistle tied down and left to scream. 

The needle on the pressure gauge instantly dropped, saving the ancient iron boiler from blowing the ship to splinters. 

But the release valve was old, its gaskets dry-rotted. As the main jet vented to the roof, a secondary cloud of superheated steam exploded horizontally across the top of the boiler. 

The cloud enveloped Elias. 

The heat was unimaginable. It seared his face, singeing his eyebrows and boiling the moisture from his eyes. He collapsed backward, tumbling off the boiler and crashing heavily onto the iron grating of the floor. 

He lay there, blinded, his hands raw and bleeding, his face feeling like it was melting off his skull. 

Through the thick, swirling steam and the deafening roar of the venting pressure, he heard the heavy, dragging footsteps of the dead engineer approaching. 

Sully's left arm, the one closest to the open firebox when he had fallen, was actively on fire. The thick grease on his canvas shirt was burning brightly, but the dead man didn't care. He lumbered toward the fallen captain, his jaws snapping in anticipation. 

Elias blindly swept his blistered hands across the iron grating. 

His fingers brushed against the heavy steel pitchfork Sully had used to stoke the fires. 

Elias grabbed the wooden handle, the rough ash acting like sandpaper on his raw flesh. He pushed himself up onto his knees just as the burning silhouette of the dead engineer emerged from the white steam. 

Elias didn't aim. He didn't have the vision or the strength. He simply thrust the heavy steel pitchfork forward with everything he had left. 

The sharp, tempered steel tines of the pitchfork caught Sully directly under the chin. The kinetic force drove the tines up through the soft tissue of the jaw, piercing the roof of the mouth and burying themselves deep into the dead brain. 

Sully's forward momentum carried them both backward. 

Elias let go of the handle, collapsing onto his back. Sully's massive, burning body crashed into the open door of the port-side firebox. 

The dead engineer slumped forward, his head and shoulders disappearing into the roaring white-hot flames. The pitchfork pinned him there, the fire instantly consuming the infectious black fluid in his brain. 

The engine room was secure. The ship was saved from explosion. 

But as Elias lay on the iron grating, his body screaming in agony from the severe steam burns, the horrific shriek of the release valve echoing across the dark river, he knew the nightmare was far from over. 

The noise had been deafening. And directly above the boiler room, separated only by a layer of thick oak planks, were fifty terrified passengers locked in the main salon.



Chapter Six: The Panic

The ringing in Elias Thorne's ears was a high, sustained whine that drowned out the rhythmic thumping of the paddlewheel. 

He pushed himself up from the iron grating of the engine room floor. The flesh on the palms of his hands was completely gone, replaced by angry, weeping red blisters that burned with an intensity that threatened to drop him back to his knees. The left side of his face felt tight and raw, the skin severely scalded by the sudden burst of superheated steam. 

He didn't look at the firebox. He didn't want to see what was left of Sully. 

He stumbled out into the dark companionway, the cooler air of the corridor hitting his burned flesh like a thousand tiny needles. He sank to his knees, his raw hands fumbling blindly in the dark until his fingers brushed against the heavy steel frame of his Colt revolver. 

He picked it up, cradling the weapon against his chest, and leaned heavily against the wooden bulkhead. 

"Cap!" 

The voice cut through the ringing in his ears. Cora came scrambling down the steep, narrow wooden stairs from the main deck, holding a kerosene lantern high above her head. 

The yellow light illuminated the horrific burns on Elias's face. Cora stopped dead in her tracks, her hand flying to her mouth to stifle a scream. 

"The boat... is she steady?" Elias asked, his voice a hoarse, rattling croak. 

"I lashed the wheel, Cap," Cora said, rushing forward and kneeling beside him. She reached out, wanting to help him, but afraid to touch his burned skin. "It'll hold us in the center of the channel for now. Cap, your hands..."

"I'm fine," Elias lied, using the wooden bulkhead to push himself to his feet. He swayed dangerously, but he didn't fall. "The pressure is bled. We ain't going to explode."

"Where's Sully?" Cora asked, looking past him into the dark, smoke-filled corridor leading to the boiler room. 

"Sully's dead, Cora," Elias said, his voice completely devoid of emotion. It was the only way he could function. "The sickness took him. I put him in the fire."

Cora closed her eyes, a single tear cutting through the soot on her cheek. 

Before either of them could speak again, a new sound echoed down from the wooden ceiling directly above their heads. 

It wasn't the mechanical groan of the ship. It was the chaotic, muffled sound of fifty people screaming at the top of their lungs. 

"The salon," Elias said, looking up. 

When they had fled the burning docks of New Orleans, Elias had made a brutal, necessary decision. The fifty passengers who had scrambled aboard were terrified, hysterical, and entirely unpredictable. To keep them from interfering with the navigation of the boat or accidentally throwing themselves into the river in a panic, Elias had corralled them all into the massive, open ballroom of the boiler deck and locked the heavy double doors from the outside. 

"The gunshots," Cora said, her eyes wide with sudden realization. "And the steam release. They think the boat is going down."

"They're going to tear the salon apart," Elias grunted, forcing himself to move. He began to climb the steep stairs, every step sending a jolt of agony through his burned hands as he gripped the railing. 

They reached the promenade deck, a wide, covered wooden walkway that encircled the massive central cabin of the boat. The night air was freezing, biting sharply into Elias's scalded face. 

They ran toward the front of the boat, where the heavy, carved oak double doors of the main salon were located. 

The heavy iron chain Elias had wrapped around the brass door handles was pulled taut. From the inside, fifty people were throwing their weight against the wood, screaming in a chaotic chorus of pure panic. 

"Captain!" a man's voice roared from the other side of the thick, frosted glass panels set into the doors. "Open the doors! The ship is sinking! Open the damn doors!"

Fists and heavy shoes began to pound relentlessly against the wood and glass. 

"We ain't sinking!" Elias roared back, stepping up to the doors. His deep voice carried the absolute authority of the river. "The pressure valve blew! The ship is secure! Step away from the doors!"

The commanding tone worked for exactly three seconds. The pounding stopped. The chaotic shouting died down into a low, terrified murmur. 

But then, a different kind of scream shattered the silence. 

It didn't come from the people pressing against the doors. It came from the very back of the massive, dark salon. It was a high, piercing shriek of absolute, unimaginable pain. 

It was a sound Elias had heard echoing across the burning wharves of New Orleans three days ago. 

"No," Cora whispered, her face draining of color. 

The silence inside the salon was instantly replaced by a horrific, surging explosion of violence. 

The terrified murmurs turned into a deafening roar of pure, concentrated terror. The heavy thud of bodies crashing into the wooden walls and overturning heavy tables echoed through the night. 

"Help us!" a woman screamed, her voice right against the frosted glass of the double doors. "He's eating her! Oh God, he's eating her!"

Elias realized his terrible mistake. 

In the chaos of the escape from New Orleans, they hadn't checked the passengers. They had just hauled fifty desperate people aboard and locked them in a room together. 

Someone in that room had been hiding a bite. Someone had been sweating and shivering in the dark for three days, terrified of being thrown into the river if they admitted they were infected. 

And the sudden panic of the steam release had finally pushed their failing heart over the edge. 

The sickness was inside the locked room. 

A heavy, wet *thud* sounded against the right side of the double doors. 

A thick spray of bright, arterial blood suddenly splashed against the inside of the frosted glass panel, painting the opaque surface a horrific, brilliant red. 

The passengers at the front of the room began to throw themselves against the locked doors with the frantic, desperate strength of the doomed. The heavy brass handles strained against the iron chain. 

"Cap, the chain!" Cora yelled, reaching out to grab the heavy metal links. "We have to let them out!"

"No!" Elias roared, stepping between Cora and the doors. 

"They're being slaughtered in there!" Cora screamed, pointing at the blood smearing down the frosted glass. 

"If I open those doors," Elias said, his voice trembling with the crushing weight of the decision, "fifty terrified people are going to stampede onto this narrow deck in the pitch black. Half of 'em will go straight over the railing and drown in the river."

He looked at the blood on the glass. He heard the wet, tearing sounds echoing over the screams. 

"And the other half," Elias continued, his grip tightening on his heavy Colt revolver, "are already infected. If I open that door, we lose the whole boat."

Elias Thorne stood on the dark promenade deck, listening to fifty people being eaten alive in a locked room, forced to choose between a massacre he could see and a massacre he could prevent.



Chapter Seven: The Glass Breaks

The transition was agonizingly slow. 

For ten minutes, the dark promenade deck of the *Delta Star* was filled with the horrific, frantic screams of people being torn apart in close quarters. Elias Thorne and Cora stood frozen in the cold night air, the heavy iron chain in Elias's blistered hands the only thing keeping the massacre contained. 

Slowly, the screaming died down, replaced by the wet, rhythmic sounds of feeding. And then, as the sickness worked its terrifying magic on the freshly dead, the feeding stopped. 

A heavy, shuffling silence settled over the main salon. 

Elias lowered his head, the burned flesh on his face pulling tight with grief. Fifty souls. Men, women, and children he had sworn to protect, locked in a wooden box and fed to the slaughter. 

"Cap," Cora whispered, her voice trembling violently. She was staring past him, at the frosted glass panels of the double doors. 

The glass was completely opaque now, painted solid red on the inside. But behind the blood, dark, indistinct shapes were moving. 

The dead had finished feeding. And now, driven by the primal hunger of the infection, they were turning their attention outward. They had heard Elias's voice. They had heard Cora. 

A heavy thump echoed against the wood. Then another. 

Within seconds, the entire heavy oak frame of the double doors began to groan under the immense, combined weight of fifty infected passengers pressing against it from the inside. 

"They're trying to get out," Cora said, taking a slow step backward toward the railing. 

"The doors are solid oak," Elias said, though his voice lacked its usual commanding certainty. "The chain is heavy iron. It'll hold."

He looked out over the dark, rushing water of the Mississippi. The *Delta Star* was still moving steadily upriver, the massive paddlewheel pushing against the current. But without Sully in the engine room to continuously feed the massive fireboxes, the steam pressure was already beginning to drop. 

In an hour, the paddlewheel would stop turning. The massive, flat-bottomed steamboat would become a dead drift, at the complete mercy of the river's treacherous currents. They would eventually run aground on a mudbank, stranded in the middle of nowhere. 

"Cora," Elias said, turning away from the doors. "Get up to the pilothouse. Lock the heavy wooden door at the top of the stairs. Don't open it for anyone."

"What about you?" Cora asked, her eyes wide with fear. 

"I have to get back down to the boiler room," Elias said, ignoring the searing pain in his hands. "I have to stoke the fires. If we lose steam, we lose steering. We'll end up stuck in the mud, and they'll eventually break through those doors."

*CRACK.*

The sharp, terrifying sound of breaking glass echoed across the deck. 

Elias spun around. 

The heavy oak doors were holding, and the iron chain was tight. But the pressure of fifty bodies pressing against the center of the doors had focused immense stress on the frosted glass panels. 

A long, jagged crack had appeared in the right panel, splitting the smeared blood. 

"Go, Cora!" Elias roared, pulling the Colt revolver from his belt. "Now!"

Cora didn't hesitate this time. She turned and sprinted for the steep wooden stairs leading up to the hurricane deck and the pilothouse. 

Elias stepped up to the double doors. He didn't want to shoot into the crowd. He only had six rounds in the heavy cylinder, and the noise would only agitate them further. 

But he needed to reinforce the doors if he was going to leave them and go down to the engine room. 

He looked around the dark deck. Bolted to the bulkhead a few feet away was a heavy, brass-bound wooden bench used for passenger seating. 

Elias slung the heavy revolver back into his belt and rushed to the bench. He grabbed the thick wooden edge and pulled with all his might, ignoring the agony of his burned palms. The brass bolts tore free from the aged wood of the deck. 

He dragged the heavy bench toward the double doors, intending to wedge it under the brass handles to brace the oak frame. 

*CRASH.*

The right frosted glass panel completely shattered. 

A shower of sharp, bloody glass exploded outward onto the promenade deck. 

The pressure behind the glass was immense. The moment the pane shattered, a dozen rotting, bloody arms thrust through the empty wooden frame, grasping blindly at the cold night air. 

Elias dropped the bench. He was too close. 

A hand, belonging to a woman wearing a torn, blood-soaked silk dress, clamped onto the thick canvas collar of his peacoat. The grip was terrifyingly strong. 

"Get off!" Elias roared, throwing his weight backward. 

But a second hand grabbed his shoulder. Then a third grabbed his sleeve. 

They weren't trying to pull him into the salon; the broken glass frame was too narrow. They were simply trying to pull him close enough to reach him with their snapping jaws. 

Elias was dragged violently toward the broken window. He felt the jagged shards of glass still clinging to the frame bite into the heavy wool of his coat. 

A face appeared in the broken window, thrusting forward through the grasping arms. It was a man he had spoken to in New Orleans, a banker in a ruined suit. His face was a horrific mess of torn flesh, his jaw snapping wildly just inches from Elias's nose. 

Elias reached down, grabbing the grip of his Colt revolver. He didn't bother to aim. He simply shoved the heavy barrel directly into the banker's open mouth and pulled the trigger. 

The deafening roar of the .45 caliber round in such close quarters was disorienting. The back of the banker's skull exploded, showering the passengers behind him in a fine mist of blood and bone. 

The dead man went limp, but the other hands didn't release their grip on Elias's coat. The dead banker's body was instantly shoved aside by two more infected passengers pushing their way toward the broken window. 

Elias cocked the hammer and fired again, dropping a woman in a ruined hat. But for every one he killed, three more pushed forward. 

They were dragging him against the sharp glass. He felt the jagged edges cut through his coat, biting into the flesh of his shoulder. 

With a desperate roar, Elias let go of the revolver. He unbuttoned the heavy wool peacoat and violently twisted his body, slipping his arms out of the sleeves. 

The sudden release of tension sent him tumbling backward onto the wooden deck. 

The infected passengers pulled the empty, blood-soaked coat through the broken window, tearing it to shreds in a blind frenzy. 

Elias scrambled to his feet, gasping for air. His shoulder was bleeding freely from the glass cuts, and his hands were a ruined, blistered mess. 

He watched in horror as the first infected passenger—a young man with half his throat missing—pushed his head and shoulders through the broken glass panel. 

The jagged edges sliced deep into the young man's torso, tearing his flesh to ribbons, but the dead didn't care about pain. He wriggled through the narrow opening and tumbled heavily onto the promenade deck. 

The breach was open. 

Elias didn't try to stop them. He turned and sprinted for the steep stairs leading to the upper deck, the sound of the infected pouring through the broken glass echoing behind him into the dark Mississippi night.



Chapter Eight: The Hurricane Deck

The steep, narrow wooden stairs leading from the promenade deck to the hurricane deck were designed for a single crew member to climb at a time. 

Captain Elias Thorne took them three at a time, his heavy boots pounding against the aged oak. The pain in his severely burned hands was a white-hot blinding agony every time he grabbed the handrail to pull himself upward, but the terrifying sound of the horde flooding out of the broken salon doors provided all the motivation he needed. 

He reached the top of the stairs, stepping onto the wide, open expanse of the hurricane deck. The wind up here was fierce, whipping across the dark Mississippi River and biting into his sweat-soaked canvas shirt. 

Elias spun around and slammed the heavy wooden safety gate shut across the top of the stairwell, shoving the thick iron bolt into its bracket. 

A second later, the first of the infected slammed into the gate. 

It was the young man whose torso had been shredded by the broken glass. He didn't even try to unlatch the gate. He simply threw his bleeding, ruined body against the wooden slats, his dead eyes locked entirely on Elias, his jaws snapping wildly. 

Then a woman slammed into the young man's back, crushing him harder against the gate. Then another man. 

The horde was swarming up the narrow stairs, driven by a mindless, collective hunger. They didn't understand the bottleneck. The ones at the bottom simply pushed forward, crushing the ones at the top against the heavy wooden gate. 

Elias took a step back, the Colt revolver heavy in his blistering hand. He only had three rounds left. 

He looked up. 

Ten feet above him, perched on the roof of the hurricane deck, was the glass-enclosed pilothouse. Cora was standing at the window, her face pale in the glow of the binnacle lamp, looking down at him in absolute terror. 

"Keep the wheel lashed, Cora!" Elias roared over the sound of the wind and the groaning wood. 

The heavy *thud-thud-thud* of the massive paddlewheel was visibly changing. The rhythm was slowing down, the powerful thrust weakening. Without Sully feeding the raging fires in the boiler room, the steam pressure was bleeding off fast. 

Elias looked out over the port-side railing. The dark, muddy water was rushing past the hull, but the *Delta Star* was beginning to drift sideways, caught in the powerful cross-currents of the mighty river. 

A mile ahead, illuminated briefly by the sweeping beam of the pilothouse searchlight, Elias saw the danger. 

It was a massive, dark shape rising out of the water. A sandbar, built up over decades of river flooding, choked with the rotting trunks of massive willow trees. 

If they lost steering entirely, the current would drive the flat-bottomed steamboat directly into the mudbank. They would be permanently grounded, surrounded by deep water, trapped on a wooden island with fifty infected monsters. 

*CRACK.*

The sound snapped Elias's attention back to the stairwell. 

The sheer, collective weight of thirty bodies pressing up the narrow stairs was generating a terrifying amount of physical pressure. The iron bolt holding the gate was bending. The thick wooden hinges were beginning to splinter. 

The young man at the front of the crush was being literally squeezed to death a second time, his ribs snapping audibly under the pressure of the horde behind him, but he never stopped snapping his jaws at Elias. 

"Cap!" Cora screamed from the pilothouse window, pointing down at the stairs. 

With a deafening crack that sounded like a gunshot, the wooden hinges of the safety gate completely sheared off. 

The heavy wooden gate collapsed forward, and the horde spilled onto the hurricane deck like a wave of rotting meat. 

The ones at the front fell to the deck, instantly trampled by the ones behind them. They scrambled to their feet, their clothes torn, their bodies covered in the slick, black fluid of the infection, and charged Elias. 

Elias Thorne didn't run. There was nowhere left to run. 

He backed up until his spine hit the heavy wooden davit supporting one of the ship's lifeboats. He raised the heavy Colt revolver, gripping it tightly despite the agony in his burned hands. 

The first to reach him was a massive, heavy-set man wearing the tattered remains of a conductor's uniform. 

Elias waited until the man was three feet away, then pulled the trigger. 

The .45 caliber round caught the conductor squarely between the eyes. The back of his skull blew out in a shower of thick gore, and the massive body collapsed heavily onto the deck. 

Elias immediately swung the barrel to the right. 

A woman in a blood-soaked floral dress lunged at him, her arms outstretched. 

*BANG.*

The round entered her open mouth, severing her spinal cord and dropping her instantly. 

One bullet left. 

A dozen more infected were swarming onto the deck, slipping on the blood of the fallen, their dead eyes fixed on the captain. 

"Cora!" Elias roared, his voice cutting through the chaos. He didn't look up at the pilothouse. He kept his eyes locked on the approaching horde. "Don't open the door! Do not open the door!"

He raised the heavy revolver, aiming the final round at a tall, thin man leading the charge. 

Elias Thorne, a man who had survived hurricanes, boiler explosions, and the treacherous currents of the Mississippi River, prepared to make his final stand on the hurricane deck of the *Delta Star*. 

The dark river rushed past the hull, carrying the doomed steamboat relentlessly toward the black mudbanks ahead.



Chapter Nine: The Crash

The tall, thin man leading the charge wore the tattered remnants of a linen suit. His jaw was hanging loose, completely dislocated on the left side, exposing the pale bone of his cheek. 

Captain Elias Thorne leveled the heavy barrel of the Colt revolver directly at the center of the man's chest. He didn't want to waste the final bullet on a body shot, but his severely burned hands were shaking too violently to guarantee a headshot. 

He waited until the man was less than two feet away. 

*BANG.*

The heavy .45 caliber slug caught the thin man perfectly in the sternum. The kinetic force lifted the dead man completely off his feet, throwing him backward into the two infected passengers pushing up behind him. 

The hammer clicked down on an empty chamber. 

Elias didn't drop the gun. He grabbed the hot barrel with his blistered right hand, ignoring the searing pain, and raised the heavy steel handle like a club. 

"Come on then!" Elias roared, his voice cracking with exhaustion and fury. 

A young woman in a torn skirt lunged at him. Elias swung the heavy steel handle of the revolver in a brutal, horizontal arc, catching her squarely on the temple. The bone shattered with a sickening crunch, dropping her to the deck. 

But Elias Thorne was sixty years old. He was severely burned, bleeding from a dozen deep glass cuts, and completely exhausted. 

And there were forty-five of them left. 

The horde didn't hesitate. They simply poured over the fallen woman, a tidal wave of rotting flesh and snapping teeth. 

A heavy, dead hand clamped onto Elias's wrist. Another grabbed his belt. 

Elias swung the revolver again, but the sheer press of bodies in the tight space between the railing and the wooden lifeboat made it impossible to generate any force. An infected man buried his face in Elias's shoulder, his jaws clamping down hard on the heavy canvas shirt, tearing through the fabric and sinking deep into the muscle. 

Elias screamed, a raw, ragged sound, as the pain exploded through his arm. 

He tried to kick, but a dozen hands were pulling him downward. The collective weight of the dead was an unstoppable, crushing force. 

Elias was pulled violently to the hard oak deck. 

The last thing he saw before the horde completely covered him was the bright yellow glow of the kerosene lantern in the pilothouse window above. 

"Don't open the door," Elias whispered, the words bubbling through a mouthful of his own blood. 

And then, the teeth found his throat. 

Ten feet above the slaughter, safely enclosed behind the thick glass windows of the pilothouse, Cora watched the captain die. 

She pressed her hands over her ears, her eyes squeezed tightly shut, her entire body shaking uncontrollably. The wet, tearing sounds echoing up from the hurricane deck were infinitely worse than the screaming. 

She was completely alone. The man who had taught her the river, the man who had saved her from the burning docks of New Orleans, was gone. 

The rhythmic *thud-thud-thud* of the massive paddlewheel finally stopped entirely. 

The *Delta Star* was completely silent, save for the horrific sounds of feeding on the deck below. 

The massive steamboat became a dead drift. The powerful current of the Mississippi caught the flat-bottomed hull, slowly spinning the boat until it was moving sideways down the river. 

Cora opened her eyes. The bright beam of the searchlight swept across the dark water, illuminating the massive, dark shape of the mudbank rising directly in their path. 

"Oh God," Cora whispered, gripping the heavy wooden spokes of the ship's wheel. 

The impact was catastrophic. 

The heavy, flat-bottomed hull of the *Delta Star* didn't just bump into the mudbank; it drove itself deep into the thick, submerged clay with the immense, stored kinetic energy of a hundred-ton wooden ship. 

The sudden, violent stop sent a shockwave of kinetic energy ripping through the entire superstructure. 

Cora was thrown violently forward. Her chest slammed directly into the solid iron hub of the ship's wheel. She heard two of her ribs snap with a sharp, sickening crack, the air driven completely from her lungs. She collapsed onto the wooden floorboards of the pilothouse, gasping in agony. 

Down below, the ancient iron straps holding the massive rear paddlewheel instantly sheared off under the sudden stress. The massive wooden wheel tore free from the stern, crashing heavily into the dark water and sinking instantly. 

On the hurricane deck, the sudden impact acted like a giant broom. 

The horde of infected, hunched over the remains of Elias Thorne, were thrown violently off their feet. A dozen of them slid across the slick, blood-soaked oak planks, crashing through the low wooden railing and plummeting thirty feet down into the freezing black water of the Mississippi. 

The *Delta Star* groaned loudly, the wooden hull settling deep into the mud. The massive steamboat was listing heavily to the starboard side, permanently grounded in the center of the dark river. 

Cora lay on the floor of the pilothouse, clutching her broken ribs, fighting the overwhelming urge to pass out from the pain. 

Slowly, the sounds of feeding began again. But it was different this time. 

The heavy, dragging footsteps weren't moving around Elias's body. They were moving toward the front of the hurricane deck. 

Cora forced herself to sit up, peering over the edge of the binnacle. 

The infected passengers who hadn't been thrown overboard had regained their feet. The impact had distracted them from their meal. 

And now, thirty pairs of milky, dead eyes were staring up at the bright yellow glow of the pilothouse. 

The glass-enclosed room was the only place left on the *Delta Star* that contained living meat. 

Cora watched in absolute, paralyzing horror as the first infected passenger—a man wearing a ruined tuxedo—grabbed the iron railing of the short ladder leading up to the pilothouse roof. 

He began to climb. 

Cora was trapped in a glass box, a hundred miles from anywhere, and the sickness was coming for the door.



Chapter Ten: The Glass Box

The pilothouse of the *Delta Star* had been designed for maximum visibility. It was a square box perched at the absolute highest point of the steamboat, enclosed on all four sides by large panes of clear, heavy glass. 

It was a magnificent vantage point for navigating the treacherous bends of the Mississippi River. 

It was a perfectly terrible place to hide. 

Cora pushed herself up into a sitting position, her back resting against the solid iron column of the ship's wheel. Every breath felt like a jagged knife twisting in her chest. She spat a mouthful of warm blood onto the floorboards, a dark stain that was almost invisible in the dim yellow light of the binnacle lamp. 

The first infected passenger reached the roof of the hurricane deck. It was the man in the ruined tuxedo. 

He didn't walk toward the door. He walked directly to the front window of the pilothouse, his hands pressing flat against the cold glass. 

Cora stared at him, unable to look away. 

His face was pressed so hard against the pane that his nose flattened grotesquely. His milky, dead eyes stared blankly at her, devoid of any human recognition. His jaw snapped open and shut in a rapid, mechanical rhythm, his teeth clacking loudly against the glass. 

A thick smear of viscous black fluid dripped from his mouth, running down the windowpane like thick, dark tears. 

A second later, a woman in a torn floral dress joined him at the window. Then a young boy, barely ten years old, missing his left ear. Then the banker Elias had shot, his skull completely shattered in the back but his front jaw still functioning perfectly. 

Within two minutes, the entire front window of the pilothouse was completely obscured by a wall of dead, rotting faces pressing against the glass. 

The heavy, rhythmic thudding of fists and foreheads against the windows began. 

The noise was terrifying. It wasn't the chaotic, panicked pounding of people trying to escape. It was the relentless, unfeeling percussion of thirty bodies trying to breach an obstacle through sheer, blunt-force attrition. 

Cora looked to the port-side window. More faces. She looked to the starboard window. The same. 

She was surrounded. 

The *Delta Star* shifted slightly, the massive wooden hull groaning as it settled deeper into the thick mud of the sandbar. Outside the ring of dead faces, Cora could see the dark, rushing water of the Mississippi River flowing relentlessly past them. 

She was trapped on an island of wood and iron in the middle of a black ocean. Even if she managed to open the heavy oak door at the back of the pilothouse, where would she go? The hurricane deck below was swarming with the dead. If she jumped into the river, the freezing water and the powerful undertow would drown her in minutes. And the mudbank itself was likely crawling with the infected who had been thrown overboard during the crash. 

There was no escape. Captain Elias Thorne had fought to the death to give her a chance, but he hadn't realized that the river itself had already sealed their fate. 

*CRACK.*

A sharp, spiderweb fracture appeared in the center of the front window, radiating outward from the flattened nose of the man in the tuxedo. 

Cora closed her eyes. The pain in her broken ribs was suddenly eclipsed by a profound, hollow numbness. She wasn't terrified anymore. She was just incredibly, overwhelmingly tired. 

She reached out with her right hand, her fingers tracing the smooth, polished wood of the ship's wheel. She thought of New Orleans before the sickness. She thought of the smell of chicory coffee in the morning and the heavy, humid heat of a Louisiana summer. 

*CRACK.*

The fracture spread, the heavy glass groaning under the immense, concentrated pressure of the horde. 

Cora slowly opened her eyes. She reached into her canvas trousers and pulled out a small, folding pocketknife she used for cutting rope in the galley. It was a pathetic weapon, a two-inch steel blade against thirty monsters who couldn't feel pain. 

But Cora was a river woman. And river people didn't die quietly. 

She unfolded the small blade, her hand surprisingly steady. She pushed herself up, using the wheel for support, until she was standing, leaning heavily against the iron column. 

"Come on then," Cora whispered, her voice barely carrying over the sound of the cracking glass. 

The front window completely shattered. 

A thousand shards of sharp, heavy glass exploded inward, raining down on the wooden floorboards. 

The man in the tuxedo tumbled into the pilothouse, followed instantly by the woman in the floral dress and the ruined banker. They poured through the empty window frame, a chaotic tangle of rotting limbs and snapping teeth, slipping on the broken glass. 

Cora didn't scream. She didn't step back. 

As the man in the tuxedo scrambled to his feet and lunged at her, his jaws wide open, Cora drove the small steel blade of the pocketknife directly into his milky left eye. 

The blade sank to the hilt, burying itself in the dead brain. The man went limp, crashing heavily against the ship's wheel. 

But Cora didn't have time to pull the knife free. 

The woman in the floral dress tackled her around the waist, driving her backward into the binnacle. The sudden, violent impact sent a blinding flash of agony through Cora's broken ribs, driving the air from her lungs. 

Cora fell backward onto the floorboards, the heavy binnacle lamp crashing down beside her, shattering its glass chimney. 

The dead woman was instantly on top of her. 

Cora raised her forearms, trying desperately to hold the snapping jaws away from her face. The dead woman's strength was overwhelming, her cold, rotting fingers digging into Cora's shoulders. 

Cora looked past the dead woman's snarling face, up at the ceiling of the pilothouse. The yellow flame from the shattered binnacle lamp had caught the spilled kerosene, setting the wooden housing of the compass on fire. 

The flames flickered brightly, casting dancing, horrific shadows across the thirty dead faces crowding into the small room. 

The woman's teeth sank deep into the thick canvas of Cora's sweater, tearing through the wool and biting cleanly into her collarbone. 

Cora screamed, a raw, ragged sound that was quickly swallowed by the chaos in the tiny room. 

She didn't try to fight anymore. She just stared up at the flames dancing on the binnacle, watching the fire grow brighter as the dark, muddy waters of the Mississippi River rushed silently past the grounded hull of the *Delta Star*. 

The steamboat would burn, eventually. It would burn down to the waterline, leaving nothing but the heavy iron boilers rusting in the deep mud. A silent, forgotten tomb in the middle of the river, a monument to a world that was already dead. 

Cora closed her eyes, and let the darkness take her.



Story Five: The Sanctuary

Chapter One: The Winter Guest

The wind howling through the San Juan Mountains didn't sound like moving air. It sounded like a physical entity, a massive, enraged beast tearing at the heavy slate roof of the St. Jude Home for Children. 

Sister Margaret stood before the massive stone fireplace in the main hall, her dark wool habit heavy on her shoulders. She was fifty-five years old, her face lined with the deep exhaustion of raising thirty orphans during the hardest years of the Depression. She held a heavy iron poker, stirring the dying embers of the pine logs, her brow furrowed in deep concern. 

The blizzard had started three days ago, dumping four feet of snow across the isolated Colorado valley. The narrow dirt road leading down to the town of Silverton was completely impassable. The telephone lines had snapped on the first night under the weight of the ice. 

They were entirely cut off from the world. 

And the coal bin in the cellar was almost empty. 

"Sister?" a small voice called out from the arched doorway leading to the dormitories. 

Margaret turned. Leo, a scrawny twelve-year-old boy with a mop of unruly brown hair, stood shivering in the drafty hall. He was wearing an oversized wool sweater that had belonged to three other boys before him. 

"You should be in bed, Leo," Margaret said gently, setting the heavy iron poker against the stone hearth. "The fire is low. It's too cold out here."

"I can't sleep," Leo admitted, walking closer to the meager warmth of the hearth. "The wind is too loud. Do you think Father Thomas is going to make it before the coal runs out?"

"Father Thomas is a stubborn man, Leo," Margaret said, offering the boy a reassuring smile she didn't entirely feel. "He's been driving that old Ford truck up this mountain for ten years. A little snow won't stop him."

But as she said it, she knew it was a lie. This wasn't a little snow. It was a whiteout. If Father Thomas had tried to make the drive from Silverton today, he was likely buried in a snowdrift at the bottom of the pass. 

*THUD. THUD. THUD.*

The sudden, heavy pounding on the massive oak front doors echoed through the high-ceilinged hall, startling them both. 

Margaret and Leo exchanged a wide-eyed look. 

"Stay here," Margaret ordered, her voice firm. 

She turned and hurried across the cold flagstone floor of the hall toward the entryway. The pounding came again, weaker this time, accompanied by a muffled voice shouting against the wind. 

Margaret grabbed the heavy iron bolt and threw it back, hauling the heavy oak door open against the howling gale. 

A swirling vortex of white snow instantly invaded the hall, instantly extinguishing the few remaining candles in the wall sconces. 

A figure collapsed through the doorway, falling heavily onto the stone floor in a heap of snow-covered wool and leather. 

It was Father Thomas. 

Margaret slammed the heavy oak door shut against the storm, throwing her entire body weight against the wood to force it back into the frame, and slid the bolt home. 

The sudden silence in the hall was deafening, broken only by the ragged, wheezing gasps of the old priest on the floor. 

"Father!" Margaret cried, dropping to her knees beside him. 

The priest was in his late sixties. He was wearing a heavy leather driving coat and a thick wool scarf, but his face was an unnatural, terrifying shade of blue. Frost clung to his eyebrows and the white stubble on his cheeks. He was shivering so violently his teeth chattered like a telegraph key. 

"Leo!" Margaret shouted over her shoulder. "Get a blanket from the rectory! And fetch Sister Bernadette!"

Leo didn't hesitate. He turned and sprinted down the dark hallway. 

Margaret pulled the heavy leather driving gloves off the priest's hands. His fingers were stiff and white, showing the early signs of severe frostbite. 

"Thomas, can you hear me?" Margaret asked, gently slapping his frozen cheek. "How did you get here? Did the truck make it up the pass?"

The old priest's eyes fluttered open. They were bloodshot and wide with a terror that had nothing to do with the cold. 

"The truck..." Thomas gasped, his breath pluming in the freezing air of the hall. "Stalled... a mile down. Radiator froze solid."

"You walked a mile in this?" Margaret asked, her voice tight with disbelief. It was a miracle he was alive. 

"Had to," Thomas whispered, his trembling hands gripping the dark wool of Margaret's habit. "Had to warn you. They're on the road."

"Who's on the road?" Margaret asked, confused. "Nobody could be traveling in this storm."

"They don't feel the cold," Thomas rasped, his eyes darting frantically around the dark, empty hall. "I saw them. Walking in the snowdrifts. Barefoot. They tried to get in the truck."

Margaret felt a cold knot form in her stomach. The priest was delirious. The hypothermia was playing tricks on his mind. 

"Hush now," Margaret said soothingly, beginning to unbutton his stiff, frozen leather coat. "You're safe inside. We'll get you warm."

She pulled the heavy coat open, intending to help him out of it. 

Underneath the leather, Thomas was wearing his black clerical shirt and a thick grey wool sweater. But the sweater was torn. 

Right below his collarbone, the thick grey wool had been ripped away in a ragged, jagged hole. 

Margaret stared at the tear. It wasn't a clean cut from a branch or a piece of jagged metal on the stalled truck. The wool was frayed and pulled, as if something had grabbed him with incredible violence and simply bitten through the fabric. 

And beneath the torn wool, surrounded by the pale, frozen flesh of his shoulder, was a deep, ragged wound. 

It wasn't bleeding. The extreme cold had frozen the blood instantly, leaving a thick smear of dark, almost black fluid around the jagged edges of the broken skin. 

"Sister?" 

Margaret jumped. Leo had returned, carrying a heavy wool blanket. Sister Bernadette, a younger, nervous nun, was right behind him, carrying a kerosene lantern. 

The yellow light illuminated the horrific wound on the priest's shoulder. 

"Mother of God," Bernadette whispered, stopping dead in her tracks. "Did an animal attack him?"

"No," Thomas wheezed, his eyes rolling back in his head. The violent shivering suddenly stopped, his body going completely limp on the cold flagstone floor. 

"Father?" Margaret said, shaking his shoulder gently. 

The priest didn't answer. His chest stopped moving. The wheezing breath was gone. 

"Is he dead?" Leo asked, his voice trembling as he clutched the blanket to his chest. 

Margaret pressed two fingers against the side of the priest's freezing neck. There was no pulse. The brutal combination of hypothermia, exhaustion, and the horrific wound had been too much for his ancient heart. 

"Yes," Margaret said softly, making the sign of the cross over the priest's chest. "He is with the Lord now."

But outside, the blizzard continued to rage, burying the St. Jude Home for Children under four feet of snow, sealing the massive stone doors shut, and trapping thirty orphans inside with a corpse that was about to prove her wrong.



Chapter Two: The Infirmary

The dead weight of a full-grown man is a terrible thing to move, especially when the flesh is already freezing stiff. 

Sister Margaret, Sister Bernadette, and young Leo dragged the body of Father Thomas across the flagstone floor of the main hall, their breath pluming in the freezing air. The wind outside shrieked like a banshee, rattling the heavy iron latches on the massive oak doors. 

They hauled the priest into the small, sterile infirmary located just off the main corridor. The room smelled faintly of carbolic soap and iodine. It was freezing; the small cast-iron stove in the corner hadn't been lit in three days to conserve coal. 

With a final, desperate heave, they lifted the priest onto a narrow iron cot, his heavy leather boots hanging over the edge. 

Margaret pulled a crisp white sheet over the body, covering the horrific wound on his shoulder and the terrifyingly pale blue of his face. 

"Leo," Margaret said, turning to the trembling boy. She placed a firm, steady hand on his shoulder. "You've been a brave boy. I need you to go back to the boys' dormitory. Tell the others everything is fine, but nobody is to leave their beds until morning."

"Is he... is he going to wake up?" Leo asked, his wide eyes fixed on the white sheet. 

"He is with the angels now, Leo," Margaret said softly, though the words felt hollow in the freezing room. "Go on now. Keep the little ones quiet."

Leo nodded, pulling his oversized sweater tighter around his thin frame, and hurried out of the infirmary. 

Sister Bernadette, who was barely twenty-four and still deeply sheltered, stood near the unlit stove, wringing her pale hands. The yellow light from the kerosene lantern she held cast long, jittery shadows across the white walls. 

"Mother of God, Sister Margaret," Bernadette whispered, her voice cracking. "What manner of beast could do that to a man's shoulder?"

"A wolf, perhaps," Margaret lied smoothly. She hadn't forgotten the delirious words the priest had spoken before he died—*They don't feel the cold. Barefoot in the snow.* But she couldn't afford a panic. Not with thirty children depending on them. "A desperate animal driven mad by the blizzard."

"What are we going to do with him?" Bernadette asked, gesturing nervously toward the cot. "We can't bury him. The ground in the courtyard is frozen solid for three feet down."

"We will keep him here in the infirmary," Margaret decided. "The cold will preserve the body until the storm breaks and we can dig a proper grave. I want you to stay here, Sister. Say the rosary over him. Keep the vigil."

Bernadette looked terrified at the prospect of being left alone with the corpse, but obedience was ingrained in her soul. She nodded slowly, reaching into the pocket of her habit for her wooden beads. 

"I need to check the cellar," Margaret continued, picking up a heavy iron candlestick from a small table. "We need to know exactly how much coal we have left. If the storm lasts another three days, we'll have to start burning the pews in the chapel."

Margaret left the young nun kneeling beside the iron cot, the soft, rhythmic murmur of the Hail Mary following her out into the dark, freezing corridor. 

The door to the cellar was located at the far end of the kitchen. Margaret pulled it open, the rusted iron hinges groaning loudly. The air drifting up from the darkness below was dank and smelled of wet earth and old potatoes. 

She lit the candle, the small flame struggling against the freezing draft, and began the long descent down the steep, uneven stone steps. 

The cellar of the orphanage was massive, stretching beneath the entire main building. It was a labyrinth of heavy stone pillars and low arches. 

Margaret walked past the wooden crates of winter squash and the rows of dusty glass preserves, heading straight for the massive wooden bin that held the coal supply. 

She held the candle high, peering over the edge of the wooden slats. 

Her heart sank. 

The bin was essentially empty. A small, pathetic pile of black rocks sat in the very center of the massive enclosure. It was barely enough to keep the main hall fireplace burning for another twelve hours. After that, the massive stone orphanage would become a tomb of ice. 

They were going to freeze to death long before the roads cleared. 

Margaret lowered the candle, closing her eyes and pressing her forehead against the rough wood of the coal bin. She had to pray for guidance. She had to find a way to keep the children warm. 

*SCREEEEEEECH.*

The sound was sudden, sharp, and terrifying. 

It wasn't the wind. It came from directly above her, filtering down through the heavy floorboards of the kitchen. It was the sound of a heavy wooden chair being violently dragged across the floor. 

Margaret's eyes snapped open. 

Then came the scream. 

It was a sound of absolute, unadulterated horror. A high-pitched, tearing shriek that shredded the silence of the massive stone building. 

It was Sister Bernadette. 

Margaret didn't think. The heavy iron candlestick slipped from her grasp, crashing onto the dirt floor of the cellar and instantly extinguishing the flame. 

Plunged into absolute blackness, Margaret scrambled blindly for the stone stairs. She barked her shins against a wooden crate, the pain barely registering over the adrenaline surging through her veins. 

She found the first step and practically ran up the steep staircase in the dark, her heavy skirts tangling around her legs. 

She burst through the cellar door into the dim light of the kitchen. The screaming had stopped. The silence that replaced it was infinitely worse. 

Margaret ran down the corridor, her heavy black shoes slapping loudly against the flagstone. 

"Bernadette!" Margaret shouted, the panic finally breaking through her stoic exterior. 

She reached the open door of the infirmary. The kerosene lantern Bernadette had been holding was lying on its side on the floor, the glass shattered, a small pool of burning oil casting a flickering, demonic light across the room. 

Margaret stopped in the doorway, the breath freezing in her lungs. 

The white sheet had been thrown onto the floor. The iron cot was empty. 

Lying in the center of the room, illuminated by the burning oil, was Sister Bernadette. The young nun's hands were desperately clutching her own throat, but the effort was useless. Her white wimple was soaked in a massive, spreading pool of bright red arterial blood. Her throat had been completely ripped out. 

Standing over her, completely unaffected by the freezing temperature of the room, was Father Thomas. 

The old priest's heavy leather coat was hanging open. His head was tilted at an unnatural angle. 

He slowly turned to look at Margaret. 

The kind, gentle eyes that had smiled at her for a decade were gone. They were entirely white, a milky, dead void that reflected the flickering flames. His mouth hung open in a grotesque rictus snarl, his teeth and chin completely painted in Bernadette's blood. 

The wolf hadn't attacked him on the road. The sickness had. 

And now, the sickness was awake inside the sanctuary.



Chapter Three: The Heavy Door

For a terrible, paralyzing second, Sister Margaret's mind completely rejected what her eyes were seeing. 

Father Thomas was dead. She had felt the absolute stillness of his chest. She had made the sign of the cross over his frozen body. Dead men did not stand up. Dead men did not tear the throats out of young nuns. 

But as the horrific, guttural snarl rattled in the old priest's ruined throat, the terrifying words he had spoken in the hall suddenly made perfect, horrific sense. 

*They don't feel the cold.*

The dead priest lunged. 

He moved with a sudden, jerky violence that defied the severe frostbite in his joints. His heavy leather boots slipped slightly in the spreading pool of Bernadette's blood, but his momentum carried him rapidly toward the open doorway. 

Margaret's fifty-five years of quiet devotion were instantly overridden by a fierce, primal instinct to protect her flock. 

She didn't try to reason with him. She didn't freeze in prayer. She stepped backward into the corridor, her hands flying to the heavy iron ring of the solid oak infirmary door. 

She pulled the door toward her with all her might. 

*SLAM.*

The heavy oak slammed shut into its thick stone frame less than half a second before Father Thomas's dead weight crashed against the other side. 

The impact shuddered through the solid wood, rattling the hinges. 

Margaret didn't hesitate. She threw her entire body weight against the door, holding it closed, and desperately slid the heavy iron bolt across the latch with her right hand. 

The bolt locked into place with a sharp, metallic *clack*. 

Margaret collapsed against the wood, her chest heaving, her breath coming in ragged gasps that plumed in the freezing air of the corridor. 

Immediately, the pounding began. 

It wasn't a frantic, panicked knocking. It was the relentless, rhythmic thudding of heavy fists driven by an unfeeling, tireless machine. The noise echoed terribly through the high-ceilinged stone corridors of the massive orphanage, competing with the howling blizzard outside. 

"Mother Mary, pray for us," Margaret whispered, squeezing her eyes shut against the tears threatening to fall. Bernadette was dead. The sweet, nervous girl who had only taken her vows a year ago was gone. 

"Sister?" 

Margaret snapped her eyes open and turned her head. 

Standing at the far end of the long stone corridor, illuminated only by the faint, flickering light of a single candle, were five of the older orphan boys. Leo was at the front, holding the candle, his face pale and drawn. 

They had heard Bernadette's scream. And now they were listening to the heavy, violent pounding on the infirmary door. 

"Leo," Margaret said, pushing herself away from the door and forcing her voice to remain incredibly calm and steady. "I told you to keep the children in the dormitory."

"We heard Sister Bernadette," Leo said, his voice trembling as he took a hesitant step forward. He stared at the locked oak door. "What's in there, Sister? Is Father Thomas..."

"Father Thomas is having a violent seizure," Margaret lied, the sin falling easily from her lips in the face of absolute necessity. "The fever from the cold has taken his mind. He doesn't know what he's doing. Sister Bernadette was frightened, that's all."

"But the pounding..." one of the other boys whispered, clutching his thin arms around his chest. 

"He is a strong man, despite his age," Margaret said, stepping away from the door and walking slowly down the corridor toward the boys. "He needs to be locked in until the fever breaks. For his safety, and ours."

Leo looked at her, his twelve-year-old eyes entirely too sharp for the lie. He saw the tremor in her hands. He saw the profound, unnatural terror in her eyes. 

But he also knew that Sister Margaret was the only thing standing between them and the freezing dark. If she said it was a fever, he had to believe her. 

"What do we do?" Leo asked, his jaw setting with a forced bravery. 

"I need you to wake the rest of the children," Margaret instructed, her mind racing through the brutal logistics of their situation. "Quickly, but quietly. Do not panic the little ones. Tell them we are moving to the chapel."

"The chapel?" Leo asked, confused. The chapel was at the center of the massive building, but it was enormous and notoriously hard to heat. 

"Yes," Margaret said firmly. "The chapel has the heaviest doors in the building. It can be locked from the inside. And it's far away from the infirmary. Bring all the heavy blankets from the beds."

"Yes, Sister," Leo nodded, turning to the other boys and shooing them back down the hall toward the dormitories. 

Margaret watched them go, the heavy, rhythmic pounding on the infirmary door echoing behind her. 

She turned back to look at the thick oak wood. 

The lie had bought her time, but it wouldn't hold forever. Because Sister Margaret knew a terrifying truth that Leo didn't. 

Sister Bernadette was dead on the floor of that room. And whatever horrific sickness had reanimated Father Thomas was surely infectious. 

In a few minutes, Bernadette's blood would stop flowing. Her young, healthy heart would stop beating. And then, she would stand up. 

One dead person pounding on the door was terrifying. Two would double the physical pressure on the heavy iron bolt. 

The oak was strong, but the dead didn't need to sleep, or eat, or stay warm. They would pound on that door relentlessly, for days, until the iron hinges finally sheared off the stone frame. 

And long before that happened, the twelve hours of coal sitting in the cellar would burn to ash. 

Sister Margaret stood in the freezing stone corridor, realizing that the massive, isolated orphanage had just become a trap. They were locked in with the monsters, the temperature was dropping rapidly, and there was absolutely nowhere left to run.



Chapter Four: The Chapel

Sister Margaret stood by the open double doors of the chapel, her breath catching in her throat as the procession of shivering ghosts filed past her. 

There were thirty of them. The youngest was barely five years old, a tiny, terrified girl named Sarah, clutching a ragged wool blanket around her thin shoulders. The older boys carried the younger ones, their faces pale in the dim light of the single kerosene lantern Margaret had salvaged from the kitchen. 

They didn't speak. The rigid discipline of the orphanage held even in the face of profound terror. But their wide, frightened eyes constantly darted back down the long, dark corridor toward the rhythmic, thudding sound echoing from the infirmary. 

"Quickly now, children," Margaret whispered, placing a reassuring hand on Sarah's head as the little girl hurried past. "Into the pews. Keep your blankets wrapped tight."

Leo was the last one in. He stood in the doorway, looking back down the dark hall. The pounding was relentless, a heavy, unceasing drumbeat that seemed to vibrate through the very stone of the building. 

"Sister," Leo whispered, his voice cracking. "It sounds like... it sounds like he's trying to break the door down."

"He won't," Margaret said, placing her hands firmly on the heavy oak double doors and pulling them shut. "The iron is strong. Now help me with the bar."

Together, Margaret and the twelve-year-old boy lifted a massive, square-cut timber of solid oak and dropped it heavily into the thick iron brackets bolted to the inside of the doors. 

The chapel was officially sealed. 

Margaret turned to face the room. The chapel of the St. Jude Home for Children was a cavernous space with a high, vaulted stone ceiling and tall, narrow stained-glass windows. In the summer, it was breathtaking. Tonight, it was a magnificent icebox. 

The air was bitterly cold, dropping several degrees below freezing. The wind howled against the stained glass, rattling the leaded panes with a terrifying ferocity. The thirty children were huddled together in the front three pews, a tangled mass of thin limbs and wool blankets, shivering violently. 

"Leo," Margaret called out, her voice echoing sharply in the large, empty space. 

"Yes, Sister?" Leo replied, stepping forward. 

Margaret looked at the beautiful, hand-carved oak pews that filled the back half of the chapel. They had been donated by a wealthy parishioner in Denver twenty years ago. They were a source of immense pride for the isolated orphanage. 

"Take the older boys," Margaret ordered, her voice completely devoid of emotion. "Go to the back row. I want you to break the pews apart."

Leo stared at her, his eyes wide with shock. "Break them, Sister? But... it's the chapel."

"It's wood, Leo," Margaret said, her tone absolute. "And if we don't have a fire burning in the center aisle within ten minutes, little Sarah and the other young ones won't wake up tomorrow morning. God will forgive the sacrilege. He will not forgive us freezing to death."

Leo swallowed hard, but he didn't argue. He turned to four of the older boys. "You heard Sister Margaret. Come on."

The sound of splintering oak soon filled the chapel, a sharp, violent noise that temporarily drowned out the howling wind outside. The boys used their heavy boots to kick the intricately carved armrests off the benches, tearing the long planks of the seats free from the frames. 

Margaret directed them to pile the broken wood directly on the stone flagstones of the center aisle, well away from the remaining pews. She used the kerosene from the lantern to douse the wood, striking a match and dropping it onto the pile. 

The dry, aged oak caught instantly. A bright, warm orange light flared up, pushing back the oppressive darkness of the vaulted ceiling. The crackle of the flames was a beautiful, comforting sound. 

The children instinctively huddled closer to the fire, holding their freezing hands out to the flames. For a moment, the terror of the night seemed to recede. 

*THUD. THUD. THUD.*

The rhythmic pounding from the distant infirmary continued, muffled by the heavy chapel doors, but still undeniably present. 

And then, a new sound joined it. 

It wasn't a sudden change in rhythm. It was a second set of heavy fists hitting the oak door, slightly out of sync with the first. 

*THUD-thud. THUD-thud. THUD-thud.*

The volume of the noise doubled instantly. The force vibrating through the stone corridors intensified. 

Sister Bernadette had turned. 

Margaret closed her eyes, a wave of profound nausea washing over her. She gripped the back of a pew to steady herself. 

The children stopped shivering. They stared at the heavy double doors of the chapel, their eyes wide with renewed terror. The comforting warmth of the fire was completely eclipsed by the horrifying realization of what that new sound meant. 

"Sister?" Leo whispered, stepping close to her. "Why are there two of them?"

Margaret didn't answer him. She couldn't. 

She stared at the thick oak timber securing the chapel doors. It was strong. It would hold against a dozen men. 

But the infirmary door was different. It was secured only by a single iron bolt on the outside, and heavy iron hinges bolted into the stone frame. 

Two infected adults, throwing their combined, unfeeling weight against that single door, hour after hour, without pause, without exhaustion. The iron would eventually heat up from the friction. The stone around the bolts would begin to crack under the relentless vibration. 

It might take five hours. It might take ten. But eventually, that door was going to fail. 

And when it did, the sickness would be loose in the cold, dark corridors of the orphanage. 

Margaret looked down at the thirty terrified children huddled around the burning pews. They were trapped. They couldn't run into the blizzard. 

She had to go back out there. She had to open that door on her own terms and destroy the monsters inside before they could break out and hunt her flock. 

But as she looked down at her empty hands, the brutal reality of her situation settled over her like a shroud. She was a fifty-five-year-old nun in a heavy wool habit. She didn't have a gun. She didn't have a weapon. 

She had a fire poker, a kitchen knife, and an absolute, desperate faith. 

It was going to have to be enough.



Chapter Five: The Trap

Sister Margaret pulled her heavy wool shawl tighter over her habit, feeling the comforting, familiar weight of the rosary beads in her pocket. 

"Leo," Margaret said softly, turning to the twelve-year-old boy. The firelight flickered across his pale, frightened face. "I must leave the chapel for a little while."

"Where are you going?" Leo asked, his eyes darting toward the heavy double doors. The rhythmic *THUD-thud* from the infirmary was a constant, terrifying backdrop. "You said there are monsters out there."

"There are," Margaret said, placing her hands firmly on his shoulders. "And they must be dealt with before they grow strong enough to break down that door. You are the oldest here, Leo. You are in charge of this room."

She pointed to the massive oak timber resting in the iron brackets on the doors. 

"When I step out, you will put that bar back in place immediately," Margaret instructed, her voice absolute steel. "And you will not remove it for anyone. Not for the wind. Not for a voice. Not even if you think it's me. You do not open those doors until morning light breaks through the stained glass. Do you understand?"

Leo swallowed hard, tears welling in his eyes, but he nodded. "I understand, Sister."

Margaret turned, lifted the heavy timber, and pulled the right door open just enough to slip her body through. 

The immediate silence of the corridor was striking, amplifying the terrifying, rhythmic pounding echoing from the infirmary wing. The temperature out here was plunging rapidly. Margaret could see her breath pluming in the pitch-black air. 

She heard the heavy oak door close behind her, followed by the solid, reassuring *clack* of the wooden timber dropping into the iron brackets. The children were secure. 

Now, she had to become the hunter. 

Margaret walked quickly to the main hall, her heavy black shoes making no sound on the flagstones. She went straight to the massive stone fireplace. The fire had died completely, leaving only a bed of grey ash. 

She picked up the heavy iron fire poker. It was three feet long, made of solid, forged iron, ending in a sharp point designed for breaking apart heavy logs. She gripped it tightly in her right hand, testing its weight. 

It was a formidable weapon, but she knew it wasn't enough. 

Father Thomas had been a strong man in life. The sickness would have amplified that strength by stripping away his natural physical limiters. And Sister Bernadette, though small, would be just as relentless. She couldn't fight two of them in open combat. They would overwhelm her in seconds. 

She needed a trap. She needed to use the massive stone architecture of the St. Jude Home against them. 

Margaret's mind raced through the layout of the building. The cellar was too expansive; they could easily outflank her in the dark. The dormitories had too many beds to trip over. 

She needed a small, confined space with a heavy door. 

Her eyes widened in the dark. *The larder.*

Located at the very back of the kitchen, built directly into the bedrock of the mountain, was a small, square stone room used for storing salted meat and root vegetables during the summer months. It had a massive, iron-bound door with a heavy steel slide-bolt on the outside. 

It was essentially a stone vault. If she could lock them inside, they could pound on that door until the Second Coming, and they would never break it down. 

Margaret hurried down the dark corridor toward the kitchen, passing the archway that led to the infirmary wing. The pounding was incredibly loud here, the heavy oak door rattling violently in its frame. 

She entered the kitchen and went straight to the back. She found the larder door and slid the heavy steel bolt back, pulling the heavy door open. The air inside smelled of dried salt and old onions. She wedged the door open with a heavy sack of flour, ensuring it wouldn't swing shut on its own. 

The trap was set. Now, she had to play the bait. 

Margaret walked back down the corridor, her heart hammering against her ribs like a trapped bird. She stopped ten feet from the infirmary door. 

The noise was deafening. The oak door was visibly shuddering with every combined impact of the two infected adults. The iron bolt holding it shut was already beginning to bend under the relentless pressure. 

Margaret raised the heavy iron fire poker in her left hand. She placed her right hand on the iron bolt. 

She took a deep, freezing breath, closing her eyes for a fraction of a second. 

*Lord, give me the speed of the hare and the courage of the lion,* she prayed silently. 

Margaret grabbed the iron bolt and threw it back. 

The release of tension was explosive. The heavy oak door burst open violently, slamming hard against the stone wall of the corridor. 

Father Thomas and Sister Bernadette spilled out into the hallway like a flood of rotting meat. 

The old priest was in front, his heavy leather coat hanging open, his face a horrific mask of dried blood. Sister Bernadette tumbled out behind him. Her pristine white habit was soaked in a massive, spreading stain of bright red, her head lolling at a terrifying, unnatural angle due to the horrific damage to her throat. 

They scrambled on the flagstone floor, finding their footing with terrifying speed. 

Margaret didn't run immediately. She had to ensure they followed her toward the kitchen, and not down the opposite hall toward the chapel. 

She swung the heavy iron fire poker, striking it violently against the solid stone wall of the corridor. 

*CLANG.*

The sharp, ringing noise cut through the howling wind outside. 

Both dead figures instantly froze. They snapped their heads toward the sound, their milky, dead eyes locking onto the solitary figure of the nun standing in the dark. 

Their jaws snapped open in unison, a horrific, silent chorus of primal hunger. 

"May God have mercy on your souls," Margaret whispered. 

She turned and sprinted down the dark, freezing corridor toward the kitchen. 

The heavy, dragging footsteps of the dead instantly erupted behind her, the terrifying sounds of the hunt echoing through the empty halls of the sanctuary.



Chapter Six: The Larder

Sister Margaret had not run since she was a young novitiate in Denver, thirty years ago. 

Her heavy wool habit and the thick, stiff layers of her skirts were designed for modesty and warmth, not for sprinting. Every step was a battle against the heavy fabric, her breath burning in her lungs like swallowed glass. 

Behind her, the heavy, dragging footsteps of the dead were terrifyingly close. 

Father Thomas, despite his age and the severe frostbite stiffening his joints, moved with a relentless, mechanical efficiency. He didn't tire. He didn't pace himself. He simply propelled his dead weight forward, driven entirely by the primal hunger animating his corpse. 

Margaret burst through the arched doorway into the cavernous kitchen, her heavy black shoes skidding slightly on the smooth flagstone floor. 

The kitchen was dark, lit only by the faint, ghostly moonlight filtering through the high, frosted windows, illuminating the swirling snow of the blizzard outside. Long, heavy wooden prep tables ran down the center of the room, flanked by massive cast-iron stoves that were now entirely cold. 

Margaret didn't slow down. She ran past the tables, her eyes locked on the dark, open doorway of the stone larder at the very back of the room. 

The heavy sack of flour she had used as a wedge was still in place. The trap was open. 

She heard a loud crash behind her. Father Thomas had hit the doorframe entering the kitchen, his lack of coordination making him clumsy, but he didn't stop or slow down. 

Margaret reached the larder and ran directly inside the small, freezing stone room. The smell of old salt and dried onions was overpowering. 

She spun around instantly, her back against the cold stone wall, the heavy iron fire poker raised in her right hand. 

The plan was simple, brutal timing. She had to let them cross the threshold, then step around them, kick the flour sack away, and pull the heavy iron-bound door shut before they could turn around. 

A second later, Father Thomas filled the doorway. 

The old priest didn't hesitate. He saw Margaret standing in the back of the small stone room and lunged forward, his hands reaching for her throat, his jaws snapping wildly. 

Margaret dropped to her knees, ducking under his outstretched arms, and scrambled forward, squeezing past his heavy leather coat. 

She made it past him, bursting out of the larder and back into the main kitchen. She spun around, her boot lashing out to kick the heavy flour sack away from the door. 

But she had forgotten about Sister Bernadette. 

The young, dead nun had been directly behind the priest. As Margaret kicked the sack away and reached for the heavy iron ring on the outside of the door, Bernadette lunged. 

Cold, stiff fingers clamped onto the heavy wool fabric of Margaret's shawl. 

Margaret gasped as she was violently jerked backward, entirely losing her grip on the iron ring. 

Bernadette didn't try to strike her. The young woman simply pulled Margaret closer, her head still lolling at that terrifying angle, her jaws opening wide to reveal teeth stained bright red with her own arterial blood. 

Margaret swung the heavy iron fire poker, bringing the heavy iron shaft hard against Bernadette's ribs. 

The impact was solid, enough to break bone, but the dead nun didn't even flinch. She just kept pulling, drawing Margaret's face inches away from the snapping teeth. 

Inside the larder, Father Thomas had hit the back wall and was already turning around, his heavy boots scraping on the stone floor as he realized his prey had escaped the box. 

If he made it out the door, Margaret was dead. 

Margaret realized the heavy wool shawl, designed to keep her warm, had become a fatal tether. 

She didn't try to pull away. Instead, she unclasped the heavy brass pin at her throat, throwing her shoulders forward and letting the thick wool garment slip completely off her habit. 

The sudden release of tension sent Sister Bernadette tumbling backward onto the flagstone floor, her dead hands still clutching the empty wool shawl. 

Margaret spun around. Father Thomas was in the doorway, stepping out of the larder. 

With a desperate, guttural scream, Margaret swung the heavy iron fire poker like a baseball bat, driving the sharp, pointed tip directly into the center of the priest's chest. 

The iron pierced the heavy leather coat, sinking deep into the frozen flesh and bone beneath. The kinetic force of the blow halted the priest's forward momentum, driving him backward, a single step back into the stone room. 

Margaret didn't try to pull the poker free. She let go of the iron handle, grabbed the heavy iron ring on the outside of the door, and pulled with every ounce of strength left in her fifty-five-year-old body. 

The massive, iron-bound door swung shut on its rusted hinges. 

*SLAM.*

The heavy wood crashed into the stone frame, sealing the darkness inside. 

Margaret instantly threw her weight against the wood, her hands fumbling in the dark for the heavy steel slide-bolt. 

*THUD.*

Father Thomas hit the inside of the door, the impact shuddering through the wood, but Margaret held firm. She found the thick steel handle of the bolt and shoved it across the iron brackets. 

The bolt locked into place with a definitive, heavy metallic *clack*. 

Margaret collapsed backward, landing hard on the cold flagstone floor of the kitchen, her chest heaving, her entire body shaking uncontrollably. 

Immediately, the heavy, rhythmic pounding began on the inside of the larder door. But this door was different. It was three inches of solid oak, reinforced with iron bands, set deeply into solid bedrock, and secured by a massive steel bolt. They could pound on that door until the flesh rotted off their bones, and they would never break it down. 

Margaret lay on the floor, listening to the muffled thudding. She had done it. The monsters were caged. 

She turned her head. 

Sister Bernadette was still lying on the floor ten feet away, tangled in the heavy wool shawl. 

The young nun wasn't moving. 

Margaret frowned, pushing herself up onto her elbows. Why wasn't she moving? The fall shouldn't have stopped her. The dead didn't feel pain. 

Margaret crawled slowly across the cold floor, her heart pounding. 

When she reached the tangled wool, she understood. 

When Bernadette had fallen backward, her head, already hanging at a severe, unnatural angle due to the horrific damage to her throat, had struck the sharp, solid iron corner of the massive kitchen stove.
