The impact had completely severed her spinal cord at the base of the skull, completely disconnecting the reanimated brain from the rest of the nervous system. 

Sister Bernadette was, finally, truly dead. 

Margaret sat on the cold floor of the kitchen, surrounded by the dark, listening to the muffled pounding from the larder and the howling blizzard outside. 

She had survived the night. But the victory felt incredibly hollow. 

Because as the adrenaline slowly faded, the brutal reality of their situation returned. 

The fire in the chapel, fueled by the broken pews, would only burn for a few more hours. The cellar was completely empty of coal. The storm outside was still raging, burying the orphanage under feet of freezing snow. 

And Father Thomas's final, delirious words echoed in her mind with terrifying clarity. 

*They don't feel the cold. I saw them. Walking in the snowdrifts.*

The old priest hadn't been bitten by a wolf. He had been bitten by something on the road. Something that was still out there, walking through the blizzard, completely unaffected by the freezing temperatures. 

And the massive stone orphanage, standing alone in the dark, was the only place in the valley that still held the scent of living meat.



Chapter Seven: Morning Light

The silence was the first thing that registered. 

The howling, demonic shriek of the blizzard that had battered the stone walls of the orphanage for three days was finally gone. It had been replaced by a profound, heavy stillness that felt almost unnatural. 

Sister Margaret walked slowly down the long stone corridor toward the chapel, her heavy black shoes echoing loudly in the absolute quiet. Her entire body ached. Her left arm was bruised and trembling from the violent struggle in the kitchen, and her habit was stained with the dark, dried blood of the young nun she had failed to save. 

She reached the heavy double doors of the chapel and knocked softly, a specific, rhythmic pattern she used to signal the children during fire drills. 

"Leo," Margaret called out, her voice raspy with exhaustion. "It's me. Open the doors."

For a long moment, there was no response. Then, she heard the heavy scrape of wood against iron. 

The massive oak timber was slowly lifted away, and the right door cracked open. 

Leo stood in the gap, gripping a heavy piece of splintered oak from one of the broken pews like a club. When he saw Margaret standing alone in the dim morning light, his shoulders slumped, and he dropped the wood. 

"Sister," Leo whispered, his voice thick with relief. He stepped aside, pulling the heavy door open wide enough for her to enter. 

Margaret stepped into the freezing chapel. The air was thick with the sharp smell of woodsmoke and ash. In the center aisle, the fire they had built from the antique pews had completely burned down to a pile of dull red embers. 

The thirty orphans were still huddled together on the stone floor around the dying warmth, wrapped tightly in their wool blankets. They looked up at her with wide, terrified eyes. They had all survived the night, but the temperature in the massive, vaulted room was already plunging back toward zero. 

"Are they... are they gone, Sister?" little Sarah asked, clutching her ragged blanket to her chin. 

"Yes, Sarah," Margaret said softly, walking toward the center of the room. She looked at Leo and the older boys. Their expressions were grim. They understood the finality of that word. "Father Thomas and Sister Bernadette are gone."

Margaret didn't dwell on the horror. She couldn't afford to let grief paralyze them. They had a much more immediate problem. The coal was gone, the pews were burned, and if they couldn't find a way to heat the massive stone building by nightfall, they would all freeze to death in their sleep. 

Margaret turned and walked toward the tall, narrow stained-glass windows that lined the eastern wall of the chapel. 

The intricate glass depicted the life of St. Jude, the patron saint of lost causes. The colors were dark and muted, obscured by the heavy layer of frost clinging to the outside of the panes. 

Margaret used the sleeve of her habit to wipe a small circle of frost away from the clear glass border surrounding the saint's halo. 

She pressed her eye to the cold glass, looking out at the world for the first time in three days. 

The blizzard was truly over. The sky was a pale, washed-out grey, but the sun was attempting to burn through the thin layer of clouds. The San Juan Mountains were completely buried under a pristine, blindingly white blanket of snow. 

The courtyard of the orphanage, a wide expanse of frozen grass enclosed by a high wrought-iron fence, was covered in a perfectly smooth, undulating sea of white powder that had to be at least five feet deep. 

But as Margaret's eyes adjusted to the glare, she noticed something that made the breath catch in her throat. 

The pristine white surface of the courtyard was ruined. 

A massive, ragged trench had been plowed directly through the center of the deep snow. It started at the heavy iron gates at the edge of the property and led in a completely straight, chaotic line directly toward the massive oak front doors of the main hall. 

It wasn't a clean path made by a snowplow or a horse-drawn sleigh. It was a jagged, uneven furrow, characterized by deep, churning walls of compacted snow. 

It looked exactly like a trench made by dozens of bodies dragging themselves relentlessly through a five-foot snowdrift. 

Margaret's stomach plummeted. She pressed her face harder against the freezing glass, squinting through the small hole she had cleared. 

The trench wasn't empty. 

Standing in the courtyard, completely buried up to their waists in the freezing snowdrift, were dozens of figures. 

They were wearing the tattered remains of winter coats, woolen suits, and mining overalls. Their skin was a horrific shade of dark blue, their eyes milky and vacant. 

Father Thomas's delirious warning hadn't been an exaggeration. The sickness had followed the road. 

The infected were completely frozen. The heavy snowfall and the sub-zero temperatures had locked their joints in ice, temporarily immobilizing them in the deep drift. They stood like grotesque, macabre statues in the pristine white courtyard, their dead eyes fixed blindly on the massive stone building. 

Margaret watched them for a long, terrifying minute. None of them moved. The cold had done what she could not. It had stopped the horde. 

But as she watched, a thin ray of pale, watery sunlight finally broke through the grey clouds, striking the frosted stained glass of the chapel window. 

The sun was coming out. 

The temperature was going to rise. The ice locking their dead joints would slowly begin to thaw. 

*THUD.*

Margaret jumped, her hand flying to her chest. 

The sound didn't come from the infirmary or the kitchen. It was a heavy, muffled impact coming from the other side of the massive stone building. 

It was coming from the heavy oak front doors of the main hall. 

*THUD. THUD.*

Margaret realized with absolute, paralyzing clarity that not all of the infected had frozen in the courtyard. The ones at the front of the trench—the ones who had reached the sheltered, covered porch of the main entrance before the deep freeze set in—were already awake. 

And they were trying to get inside.



Chapter Eight: The Bell Tower

Sister Margaret backed away from the frosted glass, the rhythmic, heavy thudding from the front doors echoing down the stone corridor like a death knell. 

She turned back to the thirty children huddled in the freezing chapel. The red embers of the broken pews were fading fast, the temperature in the massive room dropping steadily. 

Even if the heavy oak doors held against the monsters on the porch, even if the dozens of frozen infected in the courtyard remained statues forever, the orphanage itself was going to kill them. They had no coal, no food that didn't require cooking, and the temperature would plunge back to thirty below zero once the sun went down. 

They couldn't fight. They couldn't run. They had to call for help. 

"Leo," Margaret said, her voice cutting through the silent terror of the room. 

The boy looked up, his lips blue from the cold. 

"The town of Silverton is three miles down the pass," Margaret said quickly, walking toward the heavy timber barring the chapel doors. "The miners there have plows. They have dynamite. If they know we are in trouble, they can clear the road."

"But the phone lines are down, Sister," Leo said, his voice shaking. "How can we tell them?"

Margaret pointed upward, toward the high, vaulted ceiling of the chapel. 

"The bell," she said. 

Mounted in the high stone tower directly above the main entrance was a massive, two-ton cast-iron bell. It was usually rung on Sundays to call the valley to mass, or in the summer to signal dinner for the children playing in the distant fields. 

A continuous, unbroken ringing of the bell was the universal mountain signal for extreme, immediate distress. It would echo for miles down the sheer rock walls of the valley. 

"I'm going to the vestibule," Margaret said, gripping the heavy oak timber. "I'm going to ring the bell until my arms fall off. You stay here. You keep this door barred."

"Sister, wait," Leo said, jumping to his feet. "The rope for the bell... it's in the main entryway."

"I know," Margaret said. 

"But the pounding..." Leo pointed toward the corridor. "The monsters are at the front doors."

"The oak is three inches thick, Leo," Margaret said, projecting a confidence she absolutely did not feel. "It will hold long enough for me to ring the bell. Pray for me."

Margaret lifted the heavy timber, slipped out of the chapel, and waited for Leo to drop the bar back into place. 

She turned and walked down the freezing stone corridor toward the main hall. 

As she reached the grand entryway, the sheer volume of the assault became terrifyingly clear. The massive, iron-bound double doors were shuddering violently in their heavy stone frames. It wasn't just one or two infected out there on the covered porch; it sounded like half a dozen heavy bodies throwing themselves relentlessly against the wood. 

The thick iron bolt holding the doors shut was groaning under the immense, repeated kinetic stress. 

Margaret didn't hesitate. She walked directly to the corner of the vestibule and grabbed the thick, braided hemp rope that dropped from a small hole in the ceiling. 

She wrapped the rough rope twice around her blistered hands, planted her heavy black shoes on the flagstone floor, and pulled with her entire body weight. 

High above her, in the freezing stone belfry, the massive cast-iron clapper swung hard against the brass-lined rim of the two-ton bell. 

*BONG.*

The sound was absolutely deafening. It rolled out of the tower like a physical shockwave, a deep, resonant peal that vibrated through the stone walls of the orphanage and echoed out across the pristine, snow-covered valley. 

Margaret pulled again. 

*BONG.*

And again. 

*BONG.*

She established a rapid, desperate rhythm, putting every ounce of her remaining strength into the heavy rope. She was ringing the bell as a frantic, screaming plea for salvation. 

But as the deep, metallic peals echoed down the mountain toward the mining town of Silverton, they also echoed across the courtyard of the St. Jude Home. 

The pale morning sun had been working on the frozen statues in the deep snowdrift for an hour. The thin layer of rime ice locking their dead joints was beginning to melt. 

And then, the bell began to ring. 

To the Romero infected, loud, continuous noise was an irresistible dinner bell. It triggered their primal, unthinking hunger, overriding whatever meager environmental obstacles held them back. 

In the courtyard, a man in a ruined woolen suit twitched. 

The ice crusting his shoulders cracked loudly. He didn't wait to thaw completely. Driven by the deafening sound of the bell, he simply tore his frozen muscles, snapping the ice binding his legs with a sickening crunch of tearing flesh and breaking bone. 

He didn't feel the massive structural damage he was inflicting on his own body. He simply began to drag his ruined legs through the deep snowdrift, churning the white powder into a muddy slush as he moved relentlessly toward the front doors. 

Beside him, a woman in a frozen fur coat began to move, her frozen jaw snapping open and shut in time with the ringing bell. 

Dozens of frozen "statues" were suddenly violently animating in the courtyard, tearing themselves free from the ice and joining the relentless push toward the main entrance. 

Inside the vestibule, Sister Margaret kept pulling the heavy rope, her muscles screaming in agony. 

*BONG. BONG. BONG.*

She was calling the miners for salvation. But she was also calling every single monster on the mountain directly to her front door. 

And the heavy iron bolt holding that door shut was beginning to slowly bend.



Chapter Nine: The Breach

The rough hemp rope tore the skin from Sister Margaret's palms, but she didn't stop pulling. The blood smeared across the coarse fibers, turning the rope a dark, slick crimson. 

*BONG. BONG. BONG.*

The massive cast-iron bell continued to toll, a desperate, deafening prayer sent out across the frozen San Juan Mountains. 

Ten feet in front of her, the massive oak double doors of the St. Jude Home for Children were losing their structural integrity. The thick, aged wood was groaning loudly, the joints splintering under the combined, relentless kinetic force of the half-dozen infected throwing themselves against the panels. 

Margaret watched the heavy iron bolt in the center of the doors. It was thick, forged iron, designed to withstand the brutal mountain winds, but it was bowing inward, forming a terrifying U-shape. 

She pulled the rope again. 

*CRACK.*

It sounded like a hunting rifle firing inside the stone vestibule. 

The heavy iron bolt sheared entirely off its brackets. The sudden release of tension was catastrophic. 

The massive oak double doors blew open, slamming violently against the interior stone walls with a deafening crash. 

A blast of freezing, icy wind rushed into the vestibule, bringing with it the heavy, coppery scent of old blood and rotting meat. 

The first wave of infected spilled over the threshold. 

They were the ones who had reached the porch before the deep freeze—a terrifying mix of miners in torn overalls and townspeople in ruined winter coats. They didn't pause to orient themselves. They scrambled over the broken wood of the doorframe and rushed directly toward the ringing bell. 

Margaret let go of the bloody rope. 

The heavy cast-iron clapper struck the brass rim one final time, the deep *BONG* fading slowly into the vaulted ceiling. 

The sudden silence in the vestibule was instantly replaced by a chorus of wet, guttural snarls and the heavy slapping of dead feet on the flagstone floor. 

Margaret turned and ran. 

Her initial instinct was to run back down the long corridor toward the chapel, to throw herself against the heavy oak doors and beg Leo to let her in. 

But as she reached the bottom of the grand staircase in the main hall, she stopped. 

She looked down the dark corridor toward the chapel. The children were safe behind those heavy doors. But if she ran to them, the horde would follow. They would swarm the chapel doors just as they had swarmed the front entrance. And the oak timber barring the chapel, while strong, would eventually splinter and break under the sheer weight of dozens of bodies. 

If she led the horde to the chapel, the children would die. 

Margaret looked over her shoulder. The first infected miner was entering the main hall, his pale, dead eyes locking onto her black habit. Behind him, more figures were pouring through the broken front doors. The courtyard "statues" had thawed enough to join the assault. 

There were too many of them. 

Sister Margaret, fifty-five years old, exhausted, and bleeding, made a decision that only a mother could make. 

She did not run toward the chapel. 

Instead, she turned and placed her heavy black shoe on the first wooden step of the grand, sweeping staircase that led to the second-floor dormitories. 

"Here!" Margaret screamed, her voice cracking with terror and absolute resolve. She clapped her bleeding hands together, the sharp sound echoing in the massive hall. "I am here!"

The infected miner snapped his jaws and lunged toward the stairs. The rest of the horde followed him, drawn by her voice and her movement, completely ignoring the dark corridor leading to the chapel. 

Margaret turned and began to climb the steep, sweeping staircase, her heavy skirts dragging on the wood. 

She was intentionally drawing the monsters up. She was pulling the entire horde away from the ground floor, leading them up to the empty, freezing dormitories. 

She reached the top landing, leaning heavily against the carved wooden banister. She looked down at the sea of rotting faces surging up the stairs behind her. 

They were climbing over each other, a mindless, hungry tide of dead flesh, snapping their jaws and reaching upward. 

Margaret didn't have a fire poker. She didn't have a knife. She was completely unarmed. 

She backed away from the landing, walking slowly down the center of the second-floor hallway, leading the horde deeper into the massive stone building, away from her flock. 

She reached into the deep pocket of her wool habit and pulled out her wooden rosary beads. She wrapped them tightly around her bleeding right hand. 

"Hail Mary, full of grace," Margaret whispered, her voice steady in the freezing dark, as the first of the dead crested the top of the stairs. 

She had sacrificed herself. Now, she just had to survive long enough for the miners of Silverton to hear the bell.



Chapter Ten: The Miners

The boys' dormitory on the second floor was a long, rectangular room filled with two dozen simple iron bedsteads. The mattresses were thin, the wool blankets neatly folded at the foot of each frame, a testament to the rigorous discipline of the orphanage. 

Sister Margaret walked backward down the center aisle of the dormitory, her eyes locked on the dark archway that served as the entrance. 

She reached the very back of the room, her shoulders pressing against the cold, frosted glass of the large window overlooking the rear courtyard. 

There was nowhere left to go. 

A moment later, the first of the infected spilled into the dormitory. It was the man in the ruined woolen suit. 

He didn't walk around the iron beds. Driven entirely by the sight of Margaret standing at the back of the room, he simply walked directly into the first iron frame, tripping violently over the footboard and crashing onto the hardwood floor. 

He didn't even pause. He immediately began to crawl over the tangled blankets, snapping his jaws. 

Behind him, a dozen more infected poured through the archway. They tripped over the fallen, tangled in the iron bedframes, a chaotic, writhing mass of dead flesh filling the pristine dormitory with the horrific, wet sounds of their hunger. 

Margaret closed her eyes, clutching the wooden rosary beads so tightly the edges cut into her raw, bleeding palms. 

She didn't pray for a miracle. She didn't pray for salvation. 

"Holy Mary, Mother of God," Margaret whispered, her voice barely audible over the snarling of the horde. "Protect the innocent. Keep the chapel doors barred. Let the iron hold."

A cold, stiff hand clamped onto the thick wool fabric of her habit, near her ankle. 

Margaret didn't open her eyes. 

She felt the sharp, brutal bite tear through the heavy fabric and sink deep into the calf of her leg. The pain was blinding, a white-hot spike that instantly shattered her stoic composure. 

Margaret screamed, her voice echoing off the high ceiling of the dormitory. 

She was violently pulled forward, her heavy black shoes slipping on the hardwood floor. She crashed onto her knees, surrounded instantly by a dozen snapping jaws and cold, tearing hands. 

The last thing Sister Margaret saw before the darkness completely overtook her was the pale, watery winter sun breaking through the frost on the windowpane, casting a faint, beautiful rainbow across the floorboards of the empty dormitory. 

Six hours later, the silence of the San Juan Mountains was shattered by the deafening roar of a heavy diesel engine. 

A massive, steam-powered rotary snowplow, painted a bright, industrial yellow, chewed violently through the final, massive snowdrift blocking the narrow mountain pass leading to the St. Jude Home. 

Behind the plow, three heavy Ford flatbed trucks ground their gears, their tires chained for traction. The beds of the trucks were packed with twenty men from the Silverton silver mines. They were rough, hardened men, armed with 12-gauge shotguns, heavy steel pickaxes, and canvas satchels filled with dynamite. 

They had heard the frantic, continuous peal of the church bell echoing down the valley. 

The convoy rumbled onto the orphanage grounds, the massive plow easily pushing aside the deep trench the infected had dragged themselves through earlier that morning. 

The foreman of the Silverton mine, a massive, bearded man named Harris, jumped down from the lead truck before it had completely stopped. He racked the pump of his shotgun, his eyes narrowing as he took in the scene. 

The massive oak front doors of the orphanage were completely shattered, the heavy iron bolt sheared entirely off its brackets. The vestibule was open to the freezing wind. 

"Spread out!" Harris roared, waving the men forward. "Check the perimeter! Nobody goes inside alone!"

Harris, flanked by four heavily armed miners, walked cautiously up the stone steps of the porch, their heavy boots crunching loudly in the snow. They stepped over the broken oak panels and entered the grand hallway. 

It was completely empty, and bitterly cold. The air smelled foul, a heavy, metallic tang that made the miners tighten their grips on their weapons. 

"Hello!" Harris shouted, his voice echoing loudly in the massive space. "Is anyone here? We heard the bell!"

There was no answer from the dark, freezing corridors. 

Harris motioned for the men to fan out. They moved slowly down the corridor leading to the chapel, the beams of their heavy electric flashlights cutting through the gloom. 

They reached the heavy double doors of the chapel. 

"Foreman," one of the miners whispered, pointing his flashlight at the floor. 

The stone flagstones in front of the chapel doors were pristine. There was no blood, no signs of a struggle. 

Harris stepped up to the doors and tried the heavy iron ring. The doors were locked from the inside. 

He knocked hard with the butt of his shotgun. 

"Open up!" Harris shouted. "It's the Silverton miners! We're here to help!"

For a long moment, there was nothing. Then, a small, trembling voice echoed through the heavy wood. 

"Are you... are you really from the town?" 

"Yes, son," Harris said, his voice softening slightly. "We heard your bell. Open the door. It's safe."

The heavy scrape of wood against iron echoed loudly from inside the chapel. 

The massive oak doors slowly opened inward. 

Harris stepped back, lowering his shotgun. 

Standing in the doorway, clutching a piece of splintered oak in his freezing hands, was a twelve-year-old boy. Behind him, huddled in the freezing chapel around the completely cold, grey ashes of a fire built on the stone floor, were twenty-nine other children. They were shivering violently, their faces pale and drawn, but they were alive. 

Harris felt a profound wave of relief wash over him. "Jesus Christ," he whispered, stepping into the chapel and kneeling down in front of the boy. "Are you alright, son? Are there any adults here?"

"Sister Margaret," Leo said, his voice a hoarse, ragged croak. He pointed back down the dark corridor toward the main hall. "She went to the vestibule to ring the bell. We haven't heard her since this morning."

Harris frowned. He stood up, turning to look back down the corridor. "Wait here," he told his men. 

He walked slowly back toward the grand hallway, his shotgun raised. If the nun had been ringing the bell when the doors broke, she should have been in the vestibule. 

He reached the main hall and stopped, listening intently. 

The orphanage was completely silent. 

Then, a soft, shuffling sound echoed from above. 

Harris raised his flashlight, sweeping the bright beam up the grand, sweeping staircase leading to the second floor. 

Standing at the very top landing, illuminated perfectly by the beam of the flashlight, was a solitary figure. 

Harris felt the blood drain from his face, his heart freezing in his chest. 

It was Sister Margaret. 

But her heavy wool habit was shredded, hanging in bloody tatters around her legs. Her white wimple was soaked entirely in crimson. Her throat was a horrific, ragged ruin of torn flesh and exposed bone. 

She didn't speak. She didn't call for help. 

The nun slowly turned her head, looking down at the massive miner standing in the hall. Her eyes, caught in the beam of the flashlight, were a milky, completely vacant white. 

A guttural, wet snarl rattled in her ruined throat, and she took a slow, heavy step down the stairs. 

Behind Harris, the children began to scream. 

Sister Margaret, the woman who had sacrificed everything to protect her flock from the wolves, had simply become one of them. 

The foreman racked his shotgun, the loud, metallic *clack* echoing sharply through the freezing sanctuary, signaling the absolute end of the St. Jude Home for Children.



Story Six: The Lighthouse

Chapter One: The Wreck

The sound of a Nor'easter hitting the Maine coast was not a roar; it was a physical pressure, a constant, deafening vibration that threatened to rattle the teeth out of a man's skull. 

Silas Thorne stood in the center of the lantern room at the absolute top of the Blackwood Shoals Lighthouse, his hands resting on the heavy brass handrail surrounding the massive Fresnel lens. He was a man carved from the same coastal granite the lighthouse was built upon—weathered, grey, and entirely immovable. 

At sixty years old, Silas had spent thirty years keeping the light burning on this isolated rock, ten miles off the coast. He had seen storms that had swallowed schooners whole. 

But this one felt different. The barometric pressure had plummeted so fast that Silas's ears had popped twice since sunset. 

"Keep her wound, Arthur!" Silas shouted over the deafening shriek of the wind outside the heavy glass panes. 

Arthur, his twenty-year-old assistant, was terrified. He was a city boy from Portland, on his first major assignment, and the isolation was already getting to him. He was currently hanging off the heavy iron crank handle of the clockwork mechanism that rotated the massive, three-ton glass lens. 

"I'm trying, Mr. Thorne!" Arthur yelled back, his face pale and slick with sweat despite the freezing temperature in the lantern room. "The gears are stiff!"

"The cold is thickening the oil!" Silas barked, stepping over to the heavy brass pedestal housing the kerosene vapor lamp. "Use your back, boy! If that light stops turning, ships die!"

Silas carefully adjusted the flow valve, ensuring the bright, blinding white light burning in the center of the intricate glass prisms remained steady. The technology was a marvel of the modern age, but it required constant, exhausting human labor to maintain. 

The heavy iron weights of the clockwork mechanism, suspended down the center of the lighthouse's massive stone column, had to be cranked back up to the top every four hours to keep the lens rotating. 

Silas wiped the condensation from the thick glass pane facing east and stared out into the absolute blackness of the Atlantic Ocean. 

The wind was whipping the crests off massive, forty-foot swells, throwing freezing salt spray completely over the top of the hundred-foot lighthouse. The entire stone structure shuddered with every massive wave that slammed into the jagged rocks of the small island below. 

Then, he saw it. 

A brief flash of light, swallowed almost instantly by the trough of a massive wave. 

Silas pressed his face against the cold glass, his grey eyes narrowing. 

It was a ship. A small vessel, maybe a sixty-foot Coast Guard cutter. It was entirely dark, its engines dead, pitching wildly in the chaotic surf. 

It was less than half a mile away, and the storm surge was driving it relentlessly toward the deadly, jagged reef known as the Devil's Teeth, which surrounded the Blackwood Shoals. 

"God almighty," Silas whispered. 

"What is it?" Arthur asked, pausing his frantic cranking. 

"Ship in distress!" Silas yelled, already moving toward the heavy iron trapdoor that led down to the spiral stairs. "Engines are out! She's drifting right onto the Teeth!"

Arthur let go of the crank, his eyes wide. "We have to radio the mainland!"

"Radio's been dead since the storm hit!" Silas snapped, pulling on a heavy, yellow oilskin coat that hung near the trapdoor. "And they're too close anyway. They'll be on the rocks in five minutes."

"What can we do?" Arthur asked, panic rising in his voice. 

"I'm going down," Silas said, grabbing a heavy coil of thick hemp rope and a long, iron-tipped boathook from the equipment rack. "There's a flat ledge on the leeward side of the rocks. If the current pulls them around the point, they might wash up there. I might be able to pull a few of them from the surf."

"You can't go out there!" Arthur yelled, grabbing Silas's arm. "The waves are breaking over the island! You'll be washed away!"

"I am the keeper of this light, Arthur," Silas said, his voice dropping to a low, absolute growl that cut through the sound of the storm. "I do not watch men drown if I have a rope to throw them. You stay here. You keep that lens turning. Do not let the light die."

Silas pulled his arm free, threw open the heavy iron trapdoor, and began the long, dizzying descent down the spiral iron staircase that wrapped around the inside of the stone tower. 

It took him two minutes to reach the bottom. 

He unbolted the heavy oak door at the base of the lighthouse and pushed it open. The storm hit him like a physical blow, driving him backward a step. The roar of the ocean was deafening, a chaotic, violent explosion of water and wind. 

Silas lowered his head, tightened the heavy rope across his chest, and stepped out into the tempest. 

The small, rocky island was constantly battered by the freezing surf. Silas moved slowly, using the iron boathook like a walking stick, jamming it into the deep crevices of the granite to keep himself anchored against the violent gusts of wind. 

He carefully navigated the treacherous, slippery rocks, moving toward the leeward side of the island where a relatively flat, low-lying shelf of rock met the churning water. 

As he reached the ledge, a massive wave crashed over the far side of the reef, sending a shower of freezing spray high into the air. 

Through the chaos, Silas saw the wreck. 

The small cutter had hit the Devil's Teeth. The massive, jagged rocks had completely torn the wooden hull apart. The ship was in pieces, the wreckage tossing violently in the churning white water. 

Silas braced himself against a large boulder, peering into the dark surf. The light from the lighthouse sweeping over head provided brief, blinding flashes of illumination every ten seconds. 

He saw a bright orange shape bobbing in the water, caught in the powerful current sweeping around the point. 

It was a life vest. 

Silas waited for the next sweep of the light. When the beam hit the water, he saw the man. 

A sailor, face down in the freezing water, was being driven toward the flat rock ledge by the massive swells. 

Silas didn't hesitate. He tied one end of the heavy hemp rope around the large boulder he was bracing against, quickly wrapped the other end around his waist, and stepped down onto the lower, more dangerous shelf of rock. 

The freezing water instantly washed over his heavy rubber boots, the powerful undertow pulling at his legs. 

He waited for the timing of the waves. As a massive swell lifted the body and drove it toward the ledge, Silas lunged forward, risking his own life to reach into the freezing, chaotic surf. 

His thick fingers clamped onto the heavy canvas straps of the life vest. 

"Gotcha!" Silas roared, though the sound was completely lost in the storm. 

He planted his boots on the slippery rock and pulled with all his might, fighting the massive power of the retreating wave. Slowly, agonizingly, he dragged the heavy, waterlogged body up onto the flat stone shelf, out of the immediate reach of the surf. 

Silas collapsed onto his knees beside the body, completely exhausted, his chest heaving as he gasped for air. 

He had saved one. It was a miracle. 

He reached out and grabbed the sailor's shoulder, rolling the man over onto his back to check for a pulse. 

The man was young, maybe thirty, wearing the dark uniform of a merchant marine under the orange vest. He was incredibly pale, his lips a dark, bruised purple from the freezing water. There was a massive, ragged laceration across his forehead, likely from hitting the rocks when the ship went down. 

Silas pressed two fingers against the man's freezing neck. 

Nothing. The man had drowned. 

Silas closed his eyes, a profound sense of failure washing over him. He had risked his life for a corpse. 

Then, Silas felt the cold, wet hand clamp onto his wrist with terrifying strength. 

Silas's eyes snapped open. 

The drowned sailor's eyes were wide open. But they were wrong. They weren't the panicked, desperate eyes of a man who had nearly drowned. 

They were completely milky white, devoid of any pupil or iris. A dark, black fluid began to leak from the jagged wound on the man's forehead, mixing with the seawater streaming down his face. 

The drowned man's jaw unhinged with a loud crack, exposing teeth that were immediately snapping wildly, hungry for the living flesh just inches away. 

Silas Thorne realized with horrifying clarity that the storm had not just brought a shipwreck to the Blackwood Shoals. 

It had brought the sickness from the sea.



Chapter Two: The Surf

The dead sailor's grip was terrifying. 

It wasn't the desperate, frantic clutch of a drowning man fighting for a last gasp of air. It was a cold, mechanical lock, driven by an unthinking, predatory instinct. His stiff fingers dug painfully through the thick rubberized canvas of Silas Thorne's yellow oilskin coat, pulling the sixty-year-old lighthouse keeper violently forward. 

Silas lost his footing on the slick, black granite ledge. He crashed heavily onto his knees, the jagged stone tearing through his heavy woolen trousers. 

The dead sailor surged upward, his jaw unhinging grotesquely. The thick, dark fluid leaking from his shattered skull mixed with the freezing sea spray blowing sideways across the rocks. 

"Get off me!" Silas roared, the sound completely swallowed by the deafening shriek of the Nor'easter. 

He didn't freeze. Thirty years on the Blackwood Shoals had taught him that hesitation on these rocks was an automatic death sentence. 

Silas brought his right arm up, driving the thick wooden handle of his iron-tipped boathook directly across the dead man's throat. 

The impact was brutal. It crushed the sailor's windpipe, forcing his snapping jaw backward. But the dead man's milky white eyes didn't even blink. He didn't register the pain or the catastrophic physical damage. He simply continued to pull, his teeth clacking wildly against the wooden shaft of the boathook, inches from Silas's face. 

The man was dead. Silas knew the sea. He knew what a drowned man looked like. This man had no pulse, no breath, and a wound that should have killed him instantly. Yet he was fighting with the strength of a panicked horse. 

The ocean roared. 

Silas looked up, his grey eyes widening in terror. 

A massive swell, a thirty-foot wall of black water crested with foaming white fury, was rising behind the broken wreckage of the Coast Guard cutter. It was moving incredibly fast, completely blotting out the horizon. 

Silas let go of the boathook entirely. 

He threw himself backward, grabbing the thick hemp rope he had tied around his waist with both hands, and braced his heavy rubber boots against a deep fissure in the granite ledge. 

The dead sailor, suddenly free of the boathook, lunged forward with his jaws wide open. 

The wave hit them before the teeth could find flesh. 

It was like being struck by a moving freight train. The freezing water slammed into the rock shelf with thousands of pounds of kinetic force. Silas was instantly submerged, his breath driven completely from his lungs by the brutal impact. 

He gripped the hemp rope blindly, his muscles screaming as the massive undertow tried to drag him off the rocks and into the churning chaos of the Devil's Teeth. 

The water tore the dead sailor away. Without a lifeline anchored to the stone, the heavy, waterlogged body was instantly swept backward, disappearing into the foaming white surf. 

The wave broke, the water receding rapidly over the jagged rocks. 

Silas was left gasping for air on the ledge, coughing up burning seawater, his hands completely numb from the freezing temperature. The boathook was gone. The dead sailor was gone. 

He had to get off the lower shelf before the next swell hit. 

Silas scrambled backward, pulling himself up the slippery, uneven granite using the hemp rope. He climbed ten feet higher, reaching the relatively safe, flat plateau at the base of the towering stone lighthouse. 

He collapsed against the heavy oak door of the tower, his chest heaving, his heart hammering against his ribs like a trapped bird. 

He wiped the stinging salt water from his eyes and turned back toward the ocean, waiting for the massive Fresnel lens at the top of the tower to sweep its blinding white beam across the water. 

Ten seconds later, the light hit the surf. 

The Coast Guard cutter was completely gone, reduced to a field of splintered wood and twisted metal bobbing in the chaotic water. 

But there were other things bobbing in the water. 

Bright orange shapes. 

Silas squinted through the driving rain. 

There were at least a dozen life vests floating in the shallow water surrounding the base of the island. The men wearing them were being violently battered against the jagged granite rocks of the Devil's Teeth by the relentless swells. 

Silas watched as a massive wave picked up three of the sailors and threw them violently onto the same flat ledge he had just escaped. 

The impact was horrific. One of the men hit the granite head-first, his skull audibly cracking over the sound of the wind. Another was thrown against a sharp outcropping that tore his side completely open. 

They were all dead. The impact alone would have killed them, let alone the freezing water and the drowning. 

But as the wave receded, leaving the three broken bodies on the rocks, Silas saw something that froze the blood in his veins. 

The man with the shattered skull slowly pushed himself up onto his hands and knees. 

The man with his side torn open began to crawl over the slippery rocks, dragging his ruined body forward with terrifying, mechanical determination. 

They were all moving. 

In the water below, the other orange life vests were slowly, relentlessly fighting the undertow, swimming toward the island with a unified, unthinking purpose. 

The crew of the cutter had all drowned. And now, the entire dead crew was climbing onto the Blackwood Shoals. 

Silas spun around, grabbed the heavy iron handle of the oak door at the base of the tower, and hauled it open. He threw himself inside the pitch-black, circular room at the bottom of the lighthouse, slamming the door shut behind him with a deafening crash. 

He grabbed the massive iron deadbolt and slid it into the stone wall, sealing the base of the tower. 

"Arthur!" Silas roared, his voice echoing up the dark, hollow core of the hundred-foot stone tower. "Arthur!"

He grabbed a heavy iron wrench from a workbench near the door and ran toward the steep, spiral iron staircase that wrapped around the inside of the stone walls. 

The storm was raging outside, but the true nightmare had just washed ashore. And Silas Thorne was locked in a stone tube with it.



Chapter Three: The Heavy Iron Door

The base of the Blackwood Shoals Lighthouse was a perfectly circular stone room, thirty feet in diameter. It served as the living quarters for the keepers: a small coal stove, a heavy wooden table, a wall of iron shelving holding canned provisions, and two narrow cots. 

Silas stood with his back pressed against the heavy oak entry door, his heavy yellow oilskins dripping freezing seawater onto the flagstones. He gripped the heavy iron wrench in his right hand so tightly his knuckles were white. 

"Mr. Thorne!" 

The frantic shout echoed down the central shaft of the tower. Arthur came clattering down the spiral iron stairs, his boots ringing loudly against the metal grates. He stopped on the lowest landing, gripping the handrail, his eyes wide as he took in the sight of the veteran keeper soaked in seawater and brandishing a wrench. 

"Did you find them?" Arthur asked, his chest heaving. "Did you pull anyone from the wreck?"

"I pulled one," Silas said, his voice a low, ragged rasp that barely carried over the roar of the storm outside. 

"Where is he?" Arthur asked, looking around the empty, dimly lit room. "We need to get him by the stove."

"He's back in the sea," Silas said, his grey eyes locked on the thick oak wood of the door. "And we need to pray he stays there."

Arthur frowned, stepping off the landing onto the flagstones. "I don't understand. If you pulled him out, why did you let him go back in?"

*THUD.*

The heavy impact against the outside of the oak door made Arthur jump nearly a foot in the air. 

*THUD. THUD.*

It wasn't the wind. It wasn't the sea throwing driftwood against the base of the tower. It was the distinct, heavy, rhythmic pounding of fists and shoulders hitting the solid wood. 

"Mr. Thorne," Arthur whispered, taking a slow step backward. "Are they... did they climb the rocks?"

"They did," Silas said, stepping away from the door. "But they aren't men anymore, Arthur. They drowned. Every last one of them on that cutter drowned when she hit the Teeth. And then they woke up."

*THUD. THUD. THUD.*

The pounding increased in volume and frequency as more of the dead sailors reached the base of the tower and joined the assault. 

"The sickness," Arthur said, the color draining entirely from his young face. He had heard the stories from the mainland, the terrifying rumors whispered in the taverns of Portland about the dead rising in the Dustbowl. But no one thought it could cross the water. 

"Yes," Silas said simply. 

He walked over to the heavy iron workbench bolted to the floor near the stove. He grabbed a massive iron crowbar and tossed it to Arthur. The young man fumbled but caught the heavy metal shaft. 

"That door is three inches of solid, seasoned oak," Silas said, his voice entirely calm, betraying none of the terror he felt. "But the hinges are forty years old, and the salt has been eating at the iron since before you were born. It won't hold forever against a dozen dead men who don't know how to stop."

*THUD-thud. THUD-thud.*

The door was visibly shuddering in its stone frame now. 

"We need to barricade it," Arthur said, his voice shaking. He pointed his crowbar toward the heavy wooden dining table. 

"No," Silas said sharply. "A wooden table won't stop them if the hinges break. And we can't stay down here anyway. If they breach this room, we're trapped against a wall with no way out."

"Then what do we do?" 

Silas pointed up the dark, hollow core of the stone tower. 

Hanging from the very top of the lighthouse, descending all the way down to a deep pit in the center of the flagstone floor, were thick, heavy hemp ropes supporting massive cast-iron weights. These weights drove the clockwork mechanism in the lantern room, providing the slow, steady power required to rotate the three-ton Fresnel lens. 

"If they get in here," Silas said, his eyes tracing the thick ropes upward, "they'll foul the ropes. They'll tangle in the weights. The clockwork will jam, and the light will stop turning."

Arthur stared at him, uncomprehending. "Mr. Thorne, there are dead men trying to break down the door. Who cares if the light stops turning?"

Silas turned on the boy, his grey eyes flashing with a sudden, terrifying intensity. 

"I care, Arthur!" Silas roared, the command echoing loudly up the stone walls. "If that light stops turning, it looks like a stationary beacon! Ships will think it's a channel marker, not a warning! They'll steer right into the Shoals! More ships will crash on the Devil's Teeth. And every drowned sailor will wash up on this rock and join the horde trying to kill us!"

Arthur swallowed hard, gripping the crowbar tightly. "So... what do we do?"

"We protect the weights," Silas said, turning back to the spiral staircase. "We retreat up the tower, one level at a time. We lock the iron grates behind us. And we fight them on the stairs."

*CRACK.*

A sharp, splintering sound came from the heavy oak door. The rusted iron hinges were beginning to give way under the relentless pressure of the dead sea. 

"Up the stairs, Arthur," Silas ordered, his voice dropping back to that calm, deadly rasp. "Now."



Chapter Four: The Spiral

The spiral iron staircase of the Blackwood Shoals Lighthouse wound upward like the internal threads of a massive screw, hugging the curved inner wall of the stone tower. It was a dizzying, open-tread design, allowing light to filter down from the lantern room, but offering no protection from a fall. 

Silas and Arthur scrambled up the first flight, their boots ringing loudly against the iron grating. They reached the first maintenance landing, a small semi-circular platform located thirty feet above the flagstone floor. 

"The grate!" Silas barked, grabbing the heavy iron trapdoor that sat flush with the landing floor. 

Arthur dropped to his knees, grabbing the opposite handle. Together, they hauled the heavy iron grate upward on its thick hinges, slamming it down over the open stairwell they had just climbed through. Silas instantly kicked the heavy sliding bolt into the stone wall, locking the grate in place. 

They were now standing on an iron grid, looking directly down at the base of the tower. 

*CRACK.*

The sound from below was sharp and definitive. The bottom iron hinge of the heavy oak door finally sheared completely off its stone mounting. 

The heavy wood canted inward at a severe angle, the massive deadbolt holding the top corner, but the structural integrity was gone. The horde outside didn't pause. Three dead sailors hit the angled wood simultaneously. 

The top hinge snapped. The deadbolt tore free from the stone. 

The massive oak door crashed violently onto the flagstone floor, bringing a blast of freezing wind, salt spray, and the deafening roar of the ocean into the base of the tower. 

And then, the sea gave up its dead. 

They poured over the broken oak door, a chaotic, writhing mass of waterlogged corpses. They were wearing torn merchant marine uniforms, heavy woolen sweaters, and bright orange life vests. Some were missing limbs torn away by the jagged rocks of the Devil's Teeth. Others had skulls that were visibly crushed or caved in. 

But they all moved with the same terrifying, unthinking urgency. 

They flooded the small circular living quarters, stumbling over the cots and crashing into the coal stove. 

"Dear God," Arthur whispered, his knuckles white as he gripped the iron crowbar. "There are so many of them."

Down below, a dead sailor with a missing lower jaw looked up. His milky white eyes locked onto the two figures standing on the iron grate thirty feet above him. 

He didn't scream or shout. He simply walked toward the base of the spiral staircase and began to climb. 

The rest of the horde instantly followed his movement. They crowded onto the narrow iron steps, a mindless procession of rotting flesh ascending the spiral. 

The climb was treacherous even for a living man. The open-tread design provided poor footing, and the dead were clumsy. A sailor near the front tripped on the iron lip of a stair, falling backward. He crashed into the two infected men behind him, sending all three tumbling over the low handrail. 

They plummeted thirty feet, crashing heavily onto the flagstone floor below with the sickening sound of breaking bones. 

But unless their skulls were completely pulverized by the fall, they simply dragged their ruined bodies back to the base of the stairs and began to climb again. 

"Here they come," Silas warned, gripping his heavy iron wrench tightly in his right hand. 

The first infected sailor reached the underside of the locked iron grate. He didn't try to unbolt it. He simply shoved his cold, waterlogged hands through the gaps in the iron grid, grabbing blindly at Silas's boots, his jaw snapping wildly against the metal bars. 

Silas brought the heavy iron wrench down with brutal force, smashing the dead man's fingers against the iron grate. 

The sailor didn't pull his hand back. He didn't feel the shattered bone. He simply pushed his face harder against the grate, trying to force his head through the narrow gaps. 

Arthur swung his crowbar, driving the pointed end through the grate and directly into the dead man's eye socket. The kinetic force pushed the sailor backward, and he tumbled down the stairs, taking two others with him. 

But three more instantly took his place. 

They were crowding the underside of the grate, dozens of dead hands reaching through the bars, grabbing at the keepers' ankles. The air was thick with the smell of rotting kelp and cold, dead blood. 

Silas and Arthur fought desperately, smashing fingers, breaking jaws, and driving the monsters back down the stairs. The claustrophobia was absolute. They were fighting in a vertical stone tube, with nowhere to retreat but higher up the spiral. 

Suddenly, the massive stone tower groaned. 

It wasn't the wind. It was a deep, mechanical grinding sound echoing down from the lantern room high above them. 

"The light!" Arthur yelled in panic. 

Silas looked down through the iron grate. 

Passing directly through the center of the maintenance landing, dropping completely unobstructed from the lantern room down into a deep pit in the flagstone floor, were the thick hemp ropes that supported the massive cast-iron weights of the clockwork mechanism. 

Down below, an infected sailor wearing an orange life vest had been pushed off the crowded stairs. He had tumbled into the center of the room and landed directly against one of the thick hemp ropes. 

He didn't know what it was. He was just grabbing at anything to pull himself up toward the living meat above. His dead, stiff fingers had clamped tightly around the thick hemp. 

The dead weight of the waterlogged corpse was pulling down hard on the rope. 

High above, the delicate, perfectly balanced brass gears of the clockwork mechanism screamed in protest against the sudden, massive strain. The rotation of the three-ton Fresnel lens visibly shuddered and slowed. 

"He's got the rope!" Arthur yelled, pointing his crowbar down at the sailor. 

"If he pulls the gears out of alignment, the light dies!" Silas roared, dropping to his knees on the grate. 

He swung the heavy iron wrench desperately at the hands reaching through the bars, trying to clear a path so he could reach the thick hemp rope passing through the center of the landing. 

But there were too many of them. The underside of the grate was completely covered in a writhing mass of dead flesh. 

Down below, two more infected sailors, blindly following the movement of the first, grabbed onto the thick hemp rope. 

The combined weight of three dead men yanked the rope downward with terrifying force. 

A loud, metallic *SNAP* echoed violently down the stone shaft. 

The massive clockwork mechanism in the lantern room had broken. The heavy, rhythmic ticking stopped entirely. 

The massive Fresnel lens groaned to a complete halt. 

Silas Thorne stared down through the iron grate, his grey eyes wide with horror as the beam of the lighthouse stopped sweeping across the ocean, locking into a stationary, deadly glare out over the churning black water of the Atlantic. 

The beacon was dead.



Chapter Five: Manual Labor

The sudden stillness in the lantern room high above them was more terrifying than the roar of the storm. 

The heavy, rhythmic ticking of the clockwork mechanism—the heartbeat of the Blackwood Shoals Lighthouse for fifty years—was dead. 

Silas Thorne stared down through the iron grate at the horde of dead sailors pressing against the bars. He could still see the blinding white beam of the kerosene vapor lamp reflecting off the churned black water of the Atlantic, but the beam wasn't sweeping. It was a stationary, unblinking eye staring directly out to sea. 

"God damn it," Silas hissed, the curse tearing from his throat. 

"It's broken!" Arthur yelled, his voice bordering on hysteria as he gripped his crowbar tightly. "The gears are stripped! What do we do?"

"We leave the grate," Silas ordered, turning away from the sea of dead faces and looking up the spiral staircase. "We have to get to the lantern room."

Arthur stared at him in utter disbelief. "Leave the grate? Mr. Thorne, if they break through—"

"The grate is locked, Arthur," Silas interrupted, his voice a sharp, commanding bark. "The iron will hold them for a while. But that stationary light won't hold anything. A stationary white light indicates a safe channel. Every freighter and fishing trawler within thirty miles is going to see that light and steer directly toward it, thinking it's the entrance to Portland Harbor. They'll sail right into the Devil's Teeth."

Arthur swallowed hard, looking down at the dead hands still reaching through the iron grid. 

"We have to turn the lens," Silas said, already moving up the iron stairs, his heavy rubber boots ringing loudly on the metal treads. "Come on!"

Arthur didn't argue. He turned and followed the veteran keeper, scrambling up the narrow, dizzying spiral. 

They climbed sixty feet in near-absolute darkness, the only light filtering down from the massive glass room above them. The circular stone walls of the tower felt impossibly tight, closing in around them as the howling wind outside battered the lighthouse. 

They reached the top landing and pushed through the heavy wooden trapdoor that led into the lantern room. 

The heat inside was stifling, radiating from the massively powerful kerosene vapor lamp burning in the center of the room. The wind screaming against the curved glass panes was deafening, a constant, physical pressure against the eardrums. 

Silas ran straight to the heavy brass pedestal that housed the clockwork mechanism. 

It was a total loss. 

The massive strain caused by the dead sailor pulling on the hemp rope had violently snapped the main drive shaft. The delicate, intricately machined brass gears were stripped bare, teeth sheared off and scattered across the metal floor. The heavy counterweights were jammed at the bottom of the tower, and the entire system was completely useless. 

"It's ruined," Arthur gasped, leaning against the cold glass of the window, his chest heaving from the climb. "We can't fix that."

"We aren't going to fix it," Silas said. 

He walked past the broken clockwork and approached the massive Fresnel lens itself. 

It was an architectural marvel, ten feet tall, composed of hundreds of perfectly cut glass prisms designed to bend and magnify the light of the kerosene lamp into a beam that could be seen twenty miles away. The entire three-ton glass and brass assembly rested on a heavy brass chariot that rolled on a track of polished steel ball bearings. 

Under normal circumstances, the clockwork mechanism turned the massive lens smoothly and effortlessly. 

"Grab the rail, Arthur," Silas commanded, placing his large, calloused hands on the thick brass handrail that encircled the bottom of the lens chariot. 

Arthur blinked. "You want us to push it?"

"It has to turn," Silas said, his grey eyes locking onto the young man. "One full revolution every two minutes. That is the signature of the Blackwood Shoals. That is how ships know where the rocks are."

"Mr. Thorne, it weighs three tons!" Arthur protested, but he stepped forward anyway, placing his hands on the brass rail beside Silas. 

"Then push with your legs," Silas growled. "On three. One. Two. Three!"

Both men leaned heavily into the brass rail, driving their boots against the metal floorplates of the lantern room. 

For a terrifying second, nothing happened. The massive glass lens felt like it was welded to the pedestal. The static friction was immense. 

Then, slowly, agonizingly, the heavy brass chariot groaned. 

The steel ball bearings protested loudly, grinding against the track without the smooth, continuous torque of the clockwork, but the lens began to inch forward. 

Silas and Arthur pushed with everything they had, their muscles burning, the sweat pouring down their faces in the stifling heat of the vapor lamp. 

"Keep it moving!" Silas shouted over the storm. "Once it's rolling, the momentum helps!"

It was true, but only slightly. Pushing three tons of glass and brass in a continuous circle was an act of brutal, exhausting manual labor. 

But as they pushed, the blinding white beam of the lighthouse slowly began to sweep across the black, churning water of the Atlantic once again. The warning signal was back online. 

"We can't... we can't do this until morning," Arthur gasped, his arms already shaking as they completed the first quarter-turn. "It's midnight, Mr. Thorne. It's six hours until dawn."

"We do it until we die, Arthur," Silas said, his jaw set in a grim, unwavering line. "We don't let the light fail."

They completed a half-turn, the massive beam sweeping across the jagged rocks of the Devil's Teeth on the leeward side of the island. 

*CLANG. CLANG. CLANG.*

The sharp, metallic ringing echoed up the hollow core of the stone tower, cutting clearly through the roar of the wind. 

Arthur stopped pushing for a fraction of a second, his head jerking toward the open trapdoor leading to the stairs. 

"They're hitting the grate," Arthur whispered, terror lacing his voice. 

Seventy feet below them, the horde of dead sailors had realized the meat had moved higher. Dozens of dead fists were now rhythmically, relentlessly pounding on the iron grating locking the first landing. 

"Keep pushing!" Silas roared, straining against the heavy brass rail. 

The lighthouse keepers began their grueling, circular march around the lantern room, manually turning the massive three-ton lens. The storm raged outside, the blinding white beam swept across the ocean, and below them, the dead beat a terrifying, unceasing drum on the iron bars. 

The true siege of the Blackwood Shoals had begun.



Chapter Six: The Toll

By three o'clock in the morning, the lantern room felt less like a sanctuary and more like a brass-and-glass purgatory. 

The heat radiating from the massive kerosene vapor lamp was oppressive, baking the sweat into their clothes. The physical toll of manually pushing three tons of glass and metal in a continuous, unyielding circle for three hours had reduced Arthur to a stumbling, trembling wreck. 

His leather work gloves were completely worn through, his palms blistering and bleeding against the heavy brass handrail of the chariot. He was hyperventilating, his breath coming in ragged, painful gasps over the deafening roar of the Nor'easter outside. 

"Keep... moving..." Silas rasped, his own voice sounding thin and weak. The veteran keeper's face was a mask of grey exhaustion, his eyes sunken, but his arms remained locked against the rail, driving the massive lens forward inch by agonizing inch. 

Arthur's boot slipped on a patch of condensed sweat on the iron floorplates. 

He didn't have the strength to catch himself. His hands peeled off the brass rail, and he collapsed heavily onto his hands and knees. 

Instantly, the massive Fresnel lens groaned in protest. Deprived of half its motive force, the heavy steel ball bearings ground harshly against the track, the momentum dying rapidly. 

"Get up!" Silas roared, straining with every fiber of his sixty-year-old body to keep the chariot moving. "It's slowing down!"

"I can't," Arthur sobbed, his forehead resting against the cold iron floor. "I can't push anymore, Mr. Thorne. My arms are dead. My chest is burning."

"Get on your feet, boy!" 

"Why?" Arthur yelled, looking up with tears streaming through the grime on his face. "Why are we doing this? No ship is out in this storm! No captain would be crazy enough to sail in this! We're killing ourselves for nothing!"

Silas stopped pushing. 

The massive lens ground to a horrific, screeching halt. The blinding white beam stopped its sweep, locking into a stationary glare. 

Silas turned, grabbed Arthur by the heavy collar of his yellow oilskin coat, and hauled the young man violently to his feet. He dragged Arthur over to the thick glass panes on the leeward side of the lantern room and slammed the boy's forehead against the cold glass. 

"Look!" Silas barked, pointing a thick, trembling finger out into the absolute darkness of the Atlantic. 

Arthur squinted through the driving rain and the salt spray. 

For a moment, he saw nothing but the chaotic, towering waves. But then, as the storm surge dipped into a deep trough, he saw it. 

A tiny, faint pinprick of light, maybe five miles out. It was bobbing wildly, flashing intermittently as the waves obscured it. 

"A freighter," Silas said, his voice hard. "Caught out deep when the barometric pressure dropped. They're trying to make Portland Harbor. If they see a stationary white light here, they'll think they've found the deep channel. They'll adjust their heading five degrees starboard. And in twenty minutes, they'll rip their hull wide open on the Devil's Teeth."

Arthur stared at the tiny, distant light, a profound wave of nausea washing over him. 

"And when they sink," Silas continued, his grip tightening on Arthur's collar, "every single man on that freighter will drown. The sea will take them, the sickness will claim them, and they will wash up on our rocks and join the horde trying to break down our door. We push this lens, Arthur, because if we don't, we are recruiting our own executioners."

Arthur closed his eyes, a ragged sob tearing from his throat. He understood. The logic was brutal, absolute, and inescapable. 

He nodded slowly. Silas released his collar. 

They walked back to the brass handrail, leaned their shoulders into the heavy metal, and began the agonizing process of overcoming the static friction all over again. 

*SCREEECH.*

The lens groaned, the bearings protested, but it slowly began to turn. The beam swept across the ocean, a warning in the dark. 

As they settled back into their torturous, circular rhythm, Arthur realized something had changed. 

The rhythmic, metallic clanging that had echoed up the stone shaft for the past three hours—the sound of the dead pounding on the iron grate seventy feet below—had stopped. 

"Mr. Thorne," Arthur gasped, leaning into the rail. "The pounding... did they give up?"

Silas didn't stop pushing, but his eyes darted toward the open wooden trapdoor leading to the stairs. "The dead don't get tired, Arthur. And they don't give up."

A new sound echoed up the hollow core of the lighthouse. 

It was a wet, heavy, tearing sound. It sounded like thick fabric being ripped apart, accompanied by the sickening, sharp cracks of breaking bone. 

Silas recognized the sound. His stomach twisted into a cold knot. 

"What is that?" Arthur asked, panic rising in his voice again. 

"They aren't pounding on the grate anymore," Silas said grimly. "There are so many of them crowded onto that narrow staircase... the ones in the back are pushing the ones in the front."

"Pushing them where?" 

"Through," Silas whispered. 

The horde was completely unthinking. They were driven only by the primal urge to reach the living meat at the top of the tower. The dead sailors at the back of the pack were surging upward with relentless force, pressing the sailors at the front directly against the locked iron grating. 

The pressure was immense. The iron bars were three inches apart. 

The horde was literally forcing dead flesh through the gaps in the iron grate like meat through a grinder. The wet tearing was skin and muscle shearing off the bone. The cracking was ribs and skulls fracturing under the massive kinetic pressure from below. 

The dead were destroying themselves against the iron bars. But Silas knew that eventually, enough ruined, broken flesh would pile up on the landing above the grate to form a new, writhing mass that could drag itself up the next flight of stairs. 

The physical toll of keeping the light turning was slowly killing the keepers. 

And the dead were perfectly willing to tear themselves to pieces to reach them.



Chapter Seven: The Long Night

The passage of time inside the lantern room had warped into a continuous, torturous loop. 

Push. Breathe. Push. Bleed. 

By five o'clock in the morning, the Blackwood Shoals Lighthouse was a monument to human endurance. Silas and Arthur had been manually rotating the three-ton Fresnel lens for five continuous hours. The stifling heat of the kerosene vapor lamp had dehydrated them completely, their heavy woolen clothes stiff with dried sweat and sea salt. 

Arthur was no longer fully conscious. He was leaning heavily against the brass chariot, his eyes half-closed, his blistered hands locked in a death grip on the rail. He was pushing purely on muscle memory and the terrifying, commanding rhythm set by the veteran keeper beside him. 

Silas Thorne was a ghost. His grey skin was pulled tight over his cheekbones, his jaw locked. He had seen storms that lasted for days, but he had never fought a war of attrition like this. The sheer physical output required to overcome the static friction of the heavy steel ball bearings every single second was tearing his muscles apart. 

But out in the pitch-black chaos of the Atlantic, the white beam of the lighthouse continued to sweep exactly once every two minutes. 

*Clack. Draaaag. Clack.*

The sound echoed up the hollow stone shaft from below. 

Silas’s grey eyes snapped wide open. 

It wasn't the wet tearing of flesh being forced through the iron grating. It was the distinct sound of a heavy, waterlogged boot dragging itself onto the iron tread of a spiral stair. 

And the sound was coming from *above* the first maintenance landing. 

"They're through," Silas rasped, his voice barely a whisper in the roaring heat of the lantern room. 

The horrific meat-grinder strategy had worked. The relentless pressure from the horde below had forced enough ruined flesh and shattered bone through the three-inch gaps in the iron grate to build a macabre ramp. The dead sailors in the middle of the pack had simply crawled over the pulverized remains of their crewmates, bypassing the locked barrier entirely. 

Now, the vanguard of the horde was climbing the remaining sixty feet of the spiral staircase, completely unimpeded, moving steadily toward the wooden trapdoor of the lantern room. 

Arthur heard the dragging footsteps. The sound shattered his delirium. 

He let go of the brass handrail, stumbling backward. 

"They're coming!" Arthur screamed, his voice cracking with absolute terror. "They're on the stairs!"

The massive Fresnel lens groaned immediately, slowing as Silas struggled to maintain the rotation by himself. 

"Get back on the rail!" Silas roared, his boots slipping on the iron floorplates. 

Arthur ignored him. He scrambled toward the heavy wooden trapdoor set into the floor. It was made of thick pine, but compared to the iron grate below, it was nothing. It was designed to keep the cold drafts out of the lantern room, not to stop a horde of the living dead. 

Arthur slammed his bloody hands against the heavy iron slide-bolt, locking the trapdoor. 

"It won't hold them, Mr. Thorne!" Arthur yelled, backing away from the trapdoor. "It's just wood!"

"It doesn't matter!" Silas shouted, his muscles screaming as he fought to keep the lens moving. "The light must turn! We don't stop until they are in the room!"

"I'm not dying in this oven!" Arthur cried, panic entirely consuming his rational thought. 

He turned away from the trapdoor and ran toward the heavy brass door set into the curved glass wall of the lantern room. It led out onto the gallery deck—a narrow, circular iron balcony that surrounded the absolute top of the lighthouse, completely exposed to the elements. 

"Arthur, no!" Silas yelled, letting go of the lens with one hand to reach for the boy. "The wind!"

Arthur grabbed the brass handle and threw the heavy door open. 

He had forgotten the storm. 

The Nor'easter didn't just enter the room; it exploded inward. A terrifying, hundred-mile-an-hour gust of wind ripped the heavy brass door completely out of Arthur's grip, bending the thick hinges backward until they screamed. 

The sudden, violent change in air pressure within the lantern room was catastrophic. The flame of the massive kerosene vapor lamp flickered wildly, the glass chimney rattling violently in its brass housing. 

Arthur stepped out onto the narrow iron gallery deck, instantly blinded by the freezing rain and salt spray. 

At that exact second, a heavy, sickening *THUD* echoed from the floor of the lantern room. 

The first of the infected sailors had reached the top of the spiral stairs and slammed its shattered, waterlogged skull violently against the underside of the wooden trapdoor.



Chapter Eight: The Gallery Deck

Stepping onto the narrow, circular iron balcony surrounding the top of the Blackwood Shoals Lighthouse during a Nor'easter was like stepping into the exhaust of a jet engine. 

The wind didn't just blow; it battered. It was a solid, continuous physical force that immediately pinned Arthur against the curved glass wall of the lantern room. He couldn't breathe. The air was moving so fast it sucked the oxygen straight from his lungs. The freezing rain felt like buckshot against his exposed skin. 

He gripped the low iron railing with both hands, his body completely rigid with terror, staring out into the chaotic, churning blackness of the Atlantic Ocean a hundred feet below him. 

Inside the lantern room, Silas Thorne was fighting a losing battle. 

He had abandoned the massive Fresnel lens the moment Arthur opened the door. The veteran keeper threw his heavy, sixty-year-old body against the thick brass door, fighting the massive air pressure trying to keep it open. 

"Help me!" Silas roared, the sound barely penetrating the howling wind outside. 

He managed to force the brass door shut, engaging the heavy rotary latch. He leaned against the cold metal, gasping for air. 

*CRACK.*

The sound was sharp and immediate. 

Silas spun around. The heavy pine wood of the trapdoor in the floor was splintering upward. The iron slide-bolt was bending under the immense pressure from the horde crowded onto the spiral stairs beneath it. 

Silas grabbed his heavy iron wrench from the floorplates. He didn't run. He didn't try to hide. He simply walked over to the trapdoor and raised the heavy iron tool high above his head. 

*CRUNCH.*

The trapdoor shattered entirely. 

A waterlogged, rotting head burst through the splintered wood, its jaw snapping wildly at the empty air. 

Silas brought the wrench down with bone-crushing force, caving the dead sailor's skull in. The body went limp, blocking the narrow opening. 

But it was only a momentary reprieve. 

Two more sets of dead hands reached up, grabbing the collar of the dead sailor and violently hauling the corpse backward down the stairs. The opening was clear again. 

And they poured through. 

Outside on the gallery deck, Arthur watched through the rain-streaked glass panes. The interior of the lantern room was brightly lit by the massive kerosene vapor lamp, casting stark, chaotic shadows across the curved walls. 

Arthur watched Silas Thorne fight like a demon. 

The veteran keeper swung the heavy iron wrench with brutal, practiced efficiency, crushing skulls and shattering knees as the dead spilled onto the iron floorplates. But there were too many of them, and the confined space of the lantern room offered no room to maneuver. 

A dead merchant marine in a torn orange life vest tackled Silas at the knees. The old man went down hard, his head striking the heavy brass pedestal of the clockwork mechanism. 

The horde swarmed him. 

Arthur screamed, the sound entirely lost to the storm. He pounded his bloody fists against the thick glass, watching the horrific, chaotic struggle. 

Then, the unthinkable happened. 

During the violent melee, one of the infected sailors was thrown violently backward. He crashed heavily into the brass housing of the kerosene vapor lamp. 

The delicate, pressurized mantle shattered. 

The blinding white light of the Blackwood Shoals Lighthouse, the beacon that had saved thousands of lives for half a century, was extinguished instantly. 

The lantern room plunged into pitch darkness, the only illumination coming from the faint, grey pre-dawn light filtering through the storm clouds outside. 

Arthur pressed his face against the glass, his breath fogging the pane. He couldn't see anything. The silence inside the room was terrifying, replaced only by the deafening howl of the wind on the balcony. 

Suddenly, the heavy brass door leading to the gallery deck was thrown violently open. 

The wind howled into the dark room. 

A massive figure stumbled out onto the narrow iron balcony, slipping on the slick, freezing rain. 

It was Silas. 

He was missing his yellow oilskin coat. His heavy woolen sweater was soaked in dark blood. A massive, ragged bite mark tore through the heavy fabric on his left shoulder, exposing the ruined muscle and bone beneath. 

He collapsed against the curved glass wall, coughing violently. 

Arthur crawled toward him along the narrow deck, keeping his head down against the brutal wind. 

"Mr. Thorne!" Arthur yelled, reaching out for the old man. 

Silas looked up. His grey eyes were clear, but his face was deathly pale. He looked at the massive bite on his shoulder, and then back up at Arthur. 

He didn't speak. He simply shook his head. He knew what the bite meant. He knew the sickness was already in his blood. 

Behind Silas, the dark doorway of the lantern room filled with movement. The horde, drawn by the open door and the movement on the balcony, began to spill out onto the narrow gallery deck. 

But the dead were unthinking. They were uncoordinated. And they didn't understand the physical reality of a hundred-mile-an-hour Nor'easter. 

As the first three infected sailors stepped out onto the narrow iron deck, a massive, shrieking gust of wind slammed into the side of the lighthouse. 

The dead had no concept of bracing themselves. The wind caught their waterlogged bodies like sails. 

Arthur watched in stunned horror as the three infected sailors were literally blown sideways, tumbling violently over the low iron railing of the balcony. They didn't even try to grab the rail. They simply plummeted a hundred feet through the storm, crashing onto the jagged granite of the Devil's Teeth below. 

Silas Thorne watched them fall. 

He looked back at the doorway. Dozens more were crowding the exit, completely undeterred by the wind or the fall. 

Silas looked at Arthur one last time. The veteran keeper didn't offer a dramatic speech or a final piece of advice. The time for that was gone. 

He simply turned his back to the boy, grabbed the low iron railing with both hands, and hoisted his heavy, infected body over the side. 

He plummeted into the grey storm, taking the sickness with him into the churning black water of the Atlantic, leaving Arthur entirely alone on a narrow iron ledge, a hundred feet in the air, with the dead pouring out the door.



Chapter Nine: The Wind and the Glass

The isolation of the gallery deck was absolute. 

A hundred feet below Arthur, the ocean was a churning, invisible void of roaring sound. A hundred feet above him, the heavy grey storm clouds were a solid ceiling pressing down on the lighthouse. 

And ten feet away, the heavy brass door leading back into the lantern room was completely open, a dark rectangle vomiting monsters into the freezing rain. 

Arthur lay flat on his stomach against the cold iron grating of the narrow balcony. The wind screamed over him, tearing at the heavy canvas of his yellow oilskin coat. 

He watched in a state of numb, frozen shock as another dead sailor stepped out of the darkness and onto the deck. 

The man was wearing a heavy woolen sweater. He stood completely upright, his milky white eyes scanning the narrow balcony. He didn't lean into the gale. He didn't bend his knees or grab the heavy iron handrail. 

A massive gust of wind, a solid wall of moving air, slammed into the lighthouse tower. 

The dead sailor was simply swept off his feet. He didn't cry out. He just tumbled violently backward over the low railing, disappearing into the chaotic void below. 

Arthur realized the horrific geometry of his survival. The dead were unthinking. They couldn't adapt to the physical reality of a hurricane. As long as the wind kept blowing, the gallery deck was a lethal funnel for the horde. 

He stayed low, pressing his face against the iron grating, watching the doorway. 

Two more infected sailors stepped out. The wind caught them immediately. One was thrown violently against the curved glass wall of the lantern room, his skull cracking audibly against the thick pane before he slid over the edge. The other simply cartwheeled over the railing. 

It was a macabre, terrifying assembly line of death. 

But a Nor'easter is not a constant, steady pressure. It breathes. It has lulls between the massive, shrieking gusts. 

As Arthur watched, the howling wind suddenly dropped, the deafening roar briefly subsiding into a low, heavy hum. 

In that moment of relative calm, three more infected sailors pushed through the heavy brass door. 

They didn't blow away. 

They stood on the wet iron grating, their waterlogged boots finding traction. They turned their heads, their dead eyes locking onto the young man lying flat against the deck just ten feet away. 

Their jaws snapped open, a chorus of wet, guttural clicks that cut right through the hum of the storm. 

They lunged forward. 

Arthur scrambled backward, crab-walking along the curved iron balcony. He had left his crowbar inside. He was completely unarmed, his muscles burning with the exhaustion of pushing the three-ton lens for five hours. 

The first dead sailor reached him, dropping heavily onto its knees and lunging for Arthur's throat. 

Arthur rolled to his left, kicking out wildly. The heavy heel of his rubber boot caught the dead man directly in the chest, driving him backward, but the other two were already closing in.
