Arthur's back slammed hard against the curved glass wall of the lantern room. He was trapped between the infected and the hundred-foot drop. 

He needed to get back inside. 

He looked toward the open brass door. It was completely blocked by the horde still pouring out from the stairs. 

"Help!" Arthur screamed, a pointless, desperate plea lost to the storm. 

He didn't have a weapon, but he had a desperate, wild idea. 

Arthur ripped his heavy rubber boot off his right foot. He gripped the thick leather ankle violently, holding the heavy, solid rubber heel like a hammer. 

He twisted his body, swung his arm back, and smashed the heavy rubber heel directly into the thick glass pane behind him. 

The glass of the lantern room was designed to withstand hurricane-force winds, but it wasn't designed to withstand a concentrated, blunt-force impact from the inside edge. 

The thick pane spider-webbed violently with a loud *CRACK*. 

The dead sailors lunged again, cold, stiff hands clamping onto Arthur's oilskin coat. 

Arthur swung the boot again, hitting the exact center of the spider-web. 

The massive pane of glass shattered completely, exploding inward into the dark lantern room. 

A terrifying vacuum of air immediately rushed into the hole, the storm violently asserting its dominance over the interior of the tower. 

Arthur didn't hesitate. He threw himself backward through the jagged opening, tumbling into the pitch-black void of the lantern room. 

He landed hard on the iron floorplates, crying out as a large shard of broken glass sliced deep into the palm of his left hand. 

He scrambled away from the window, bleeding heavily into the darkness, his breath coming in ragged, painful gasps. 

But the horror didn't stop at the window. 

The three infected sailors outside simply followed him. They didn't care about the jagged, broken glass lining the window frame. They dragged their heavy, waterlogged bodies directly over the sharp shards. 

The glass sliced through their woolen sweaters and tore their dead flesh to ribbons, but they didn't feel it. They just kept pulling themselves forward, tumbling through the broken window and landing heavily on the floorplates. 

Arthur was back inside the dark, sweltering oven of the lantern room. He was bleeding. And he was trapped in the pitch black with three monsters that couldn't feel pain.



Chapter Ten: The Deep Channel

The darkness inside the lantern room was absolute, a suffocating, heavy blackness broken only by the chaotic roar of the storm howling through the shattered window pane. 

Arthur lay perfectly still on the cold iron floorplates, his left hand clamped tightly over the deep gash in his right palm. The blood was warm and sticky, seeping rapidly between his fingers. 

He couldn't see the three infected sailors who had followed him through the broken glass, but he could hear them. 

*Slap. Drag. Click-click-click.*

Their waterlogged boots slapped against the metal floor. Their ruined flesh, torn to ribbons by the jagged shards of the window frame, dragged wetly. And their jaws snapped in a rapid, unceasing staccato rhythm, driven by the scent of the fresh blood pooling under Arthur's hand. 

They were searching for him in the dark. 

Arthur slowly, silently, rolled onto his stomach. He reached out with his uninjured hand, his fingertips tracing the cold iron floor. He found the smooth, curved base of the massive brass pedestal supporting the three-ton Fresnel lens. 

He pulled himself forward, sliding his thin frame into the narrow, two-foot clearance between the iron floor and the heavy brass chariot of the lens. 

It was a coffin of brass and iron, but it was the only hiding place in the circular room. 

As his boot scraped against the metal, the clicking sound instantly stopped. 

The three dead sailors had heard him. 

Heavy, uneven footsteps moved rapidly toward the center of the room. A pair of heavy rubber boots stopped inches from Arthur's face. 

A dead hand slammed against the iron floorplate, followed immediately by a wet, ruined face sliding under the brass chariot. 

The milky white eyes were invisible in the dark, but the smell of rotting kelp and old blood washed over Arthur like a physical wave. The dead sailor snapped its jaw, lunging forward into the narrow space. 

Arthur threw his left arm up defensively, his hand brushing against something cold, heavy, and metallic lying on the floor under the lens. 

It was Silas's heavy iron wrench. The veteran keeper had dropped it when the horde swarmed him. 

Arthur grabbed the thick iron handle. As the dead sailor's teeth found the heavy canvas of his oilskin coat, Arthur swung the wrench horizontally in the confined space. 

The heavy iron struck the side of the dead man's skull with a sickening crunch. The skull caved inward, and the body went instantly limp, pinning Arthur's legs under its dead weight. 

But Arthur didn't have time to push it off. 

Two more sets of hands reached under the brass chariot. They grabbed Arthur's ankles with terrifying, mechanical strength and violently hauled him backward, dragging him out from under the lens and into the center of the dark room. 

Arthur swung the wrench wildly, striking shoulders and arms, but the blows had absolutely no effect. The dead didn't register the pain or the shattered bones. 

They swarmed him in the darkness. 

A cold, stiff hand clamped over his mouth, stifling his scream. A second hand tore the heavy oilskin coat away from his neck. 

Arthur felt the sharp, brutal tear of teeth sinking deep into the muscle of his shoulder. 

The pain was a blinding white flare in the absolute darkness. He knew instantly, with terrifying certainty, that he was lost. The sickness of the sea was in his blood. 

He stopped swinging the wrench. He let it drop from his trembling fingers, the heavy iron clattering loudly against the floorplates. 

The storm continued to howl through the broken glass, a deafening requiem for the Blackwood Shoals. 

Ten hours later, the Nor'easter broke. 

The heavy grey clouds shattered, giving way to a brilliant, blindingly clear blue sky. The Atlantic Ocean, so violent and chaotic the night before, had settled into a gentle, rolling swell that glittered like diamonds in the afternoon sun. 

A heavy, eighty-foot Coast Guard rescue cutter cut through the calm water, its twin diesel engines humming a steady, powerful rhythm. 

The captain of the cutter stood on the bridge, a heavy pair of brass binoculars pressed to his eyes. He was scanning the horizon, looking for the familiar, sweeping white beam of the Blackwood Shoals Lighthouse. 

"I have the tower, Captain," the first mate said, pointing off the starboard bow. 

The captain tracked his binoculars to the massive stone column rising from the jagged rocks. 

His stomach plummeted. 

The massive Fresnel lens at the top of the tower was completely stationary. One of the thick, curved glass panes of the lantern room was completely shattered. 

But that wasn't the worst part. 

The stationary white light of the broken lens had acted exactly as Silas Thorne had predicted. It had beamed a false signal into the heart of the storm, indicating a safe, deep-water channel where none existed. 

Impaled brutally upon the jagged, black granite of the Devil's Teeth, less than a quarter-mile from the base of the lighthouse, was a massive, three-hundred-foot commercial freighter. Its steel hull was torn completely open, its cargo holds flooded. 

"Jesus," the captain whispered, lowering the binoculars. "She followed the dead light right onto the rocks."

"Survivors, sir?" the first mate asked urgently. 

The captain raised the binoculars again, scanning the calm, glittering water surrounding the massive wreck. 

There were hundreds of bright orange life vests bobbing in the gentle swells. The entire crew of the massive freighter had abandoned ship. 

"I see them," the captain said, a wave of relief washing over him. "They're in the water. They're swimming toward the base of the lighthouse."

"We'll deploy the launch," the first mate said, turning toward the door. 

"Wait," the captain said, his voice suddenly sharp. He adjusted the focus on the binoculars, zooming in on the men in the water. 

They weren't swimming frantically. They weren't calling for help. They were moving with a slow, synchronized, terrifying mechanical rhythm, their pale faces completely devoid of expression, their eyes fixed unwaveringly on the stone tower. 

The captain slowly panned the binoculars up the hundred-foot length of the stone lighthouse, past the open, broken oak door at the base, past the narrow iron balcony, until he reached the shattered window of the lantern room. 

Standing in the jagged hole, perfectly framed by the broken glass, was a young man in a torn, bloody yellow oilskin coat. 

He was completely still. His skin was a horrific, bruised blue. His eyes were milky white, staring down at the hundreds of fresh bodies dragging themselves out of the sea and onto the rocks below. 

The Blackwood Shoals Lighthouse was no longer a beacon of salvation. 

It was a monument to the dead. 



Story Seven: The Midnight Circus

Chapter One: The Derailment

"The Moonlight Express" was not a train of elegance, despite the heavy brass lettering bolted to the side of the engine. It was a rolling monument to cheap thrills and desperate entertainment, a twenty-car traveling circus that ground its way across the American Midwest, wringing dimes from Dustbowl farmers who needed a distraction from the dying crops. 

In the absolute center of the consist, insulated from the smell of the animal cars and the noise of the cheap sleeper coaches, was the private Pullman car of Bartholomew "The Great" Vance. 

Bart was a man who understood the value of an illusion. He was currently sitting in a plush red velvet armchair, wearing a crisp, tailored tuxedo, swirling a heavy crystal glass of Kentucky bourbon. The rain lashing against the thick glass windows of his private car was deafening, the result of a massive mid-summer thunderstorm rolling across the flat plains of Ohio, but Bart paid it no mind. 

He was counting the take from their three-day run in Columbus. 

"Not bad, Elias," Bart said, his voice a smooth, practiced baritone. He flicked a thick stack of greasy dollar bills onto the mahogany table. "The rubes were feeling generous. Must be the rain making them think the drought is breaking."

Sitting on a small, heavy wooden stool in the corner of the car was Elias. He was the circus strongman, a massive, towering slab of a human being with arms thicker than most men's waists. He was meticulously polishing a massive iron dumbbell with a rag. 

"They liked the lions, boss," Elias rumbled, his voice a deep, slow bass. 

"They always like the lions," Bart said, taking a sip of his bourbon. "They like the implication of danger. The illusion that the beast might break the cage and eat the tamer. It's the thrill of proximity to death, Elias. It's what we sell."

The train surged forward, the heavy steel wheels clacking loudly over a rough section of track. 

"Engineer's pushing her hard," Elias noted, pausing his polishing. 

"We have a schedule to keep," Bart said dismissively. "We open in Indianapolis on Tuesday. And time, my large friend, is money."

It was the last sentence Bartholomew Vance ever spoke in the comfort of his luxurious illusion. 

The catastrophic failure didn't begin with a sound; it began with a physical sensation of absolute weightlessness. The plush red velvet armchair dropped out from under Bart. 

Then came the noise. 

It was an apocalyptic scream of tearing metal, grinding steel, and exploding wood. The massive steam locomotive at the front of the consist had hit a washed-out section of track, the heavy rails completely undermined by the torrential flash flood. 

The engine plowed directly into the muddy embankment, stopping dead from sixty miles an hour in less than five seconds. 

The physics of a heavy train derailment are brutally unforgiving. The cars behind the engine, carrying thousands of tons of momentum, had nowhere to go but forward. 

Bart's private Pullman car was violently launched off the rails. It spun horizontally, the mahogany table shattering instantly, the crystal glasses exploding into shrapnel. 

Bart was thrown through the air, crashing heavily into the ornate wooden paneling of the ceiling as the car rolled completely over, tumbling down the steep, muddy embankment beside the tracks. 

When the violent, churning motion finally stopped, the silence that followed was terrifying. 

Bart groaned, opening his eyes. It was pitch black. The oil lamps had shattered. The car was resting on its side, the floor now a vertical wall. He was bruised and bleeding from a dozen minor cuts, but miraculously, nothing felt broken. 

"Elias?" Bart gasped, tasting copper in his mouth. 

"Here, boss," a deep voice rumbled from the darkness near what used to be the ceiling. 

A heavy, metallic clang echoed through the overturned car, followed by the sound of splintering wood. A sliver of pale, stormy light cut through the gloom. Elias had simply kicked the heavy oak door completely off its hinges. 

"We're upside down," Elias noted simply. He reached down into the darkness, his massive hand closing around Bart's tailored lapel, and hauled the ringmaster up and out of the wreckage with terrifying ease. 

They stood in the pouring rain, sinking ankle-deep into the thick, churning Ohio mud. 

The scene illuminated by the lightning strikes was a vision of absolute hell. 

"The Moonlight Express" was gone. The twenty cars were a twisted, smoking zig-zag of crushed steel and splintered wood, scattered across the flooded plains like discarded toys. 

Over the deafening roar of the thunderstorm, Bart heard something that made the blood freeze in his veins. 

It was a deep, guttural roar, followed by the terrifying sound of heavy claws tearing at wood. 

"The menagerie cars," Bart said, panic finally bleeding into his smooth voice. "They're broken open."

"Lions are out, boss," Elias said, looking toward the front of the train where the heavy wooden boxcars were completely shattered. 

"Forget the animals," Bart snapped, his ruthless pragmatism instantly overriding his fear. He pointed toward the rear of the train. "We need the roustabouts. We need the crew. If we don't secure the equipment, the bank will take everything!"

They waded through the deep mud, the freezing rain matting Bart's tailored tuxedo to his skin. Elias grabbed a kerosene storm lantern from a piece of debris, striking a match and illuminating a small circle of the chaotic darkness. 

They reached the last three cars of the consist. These were the cheap, wooden sleeper coaches where the dozens of roustabouts, acrobats, and sideshow performers lived stacked in narrow bunks. 

The kinetic force of the crash had been focused here. The heavy steel flatbeds carrying the circus tents had slammed into the back of the wooden coaches, telescoping them. The three sleeper cars had literally been crushed into the space of one, the wood splintered into millions of jagged toothpicks. 

Elias held the lantern up. The light washed over the horrific, compacted wreckage. 

"No one survived that, boss," Elias said softly. "They're all gone."

Bart stared at the ruined wood, a cold fury settling over him. He wasn't grieving the loss of human life; he was calculating the devastating loss of cheap labor. 

"Damn it," Bart hissed. "Every single one of them. Do you know how hard it is to find a good contortionist who doesn't drink?"

"We should go, boss," Elias said, looking nervously over his shoulder toward the roaring in the dark. "The big cats are hunting."

"Wait," Bart said, holding up a hand. 

He stepped closer to the compacted wreckage of the sleeper cars. Over the sound of the rain, he heard something. It wasn't the roar of a lion. 

It was a scraping sound. Wood sliding against wood. 

A pale, white hand suddenly thrust out from a gap in the splintered debris, the fingers grasping blindly at the mud. 

"Someone's alive in there!" Bart said, a surge of greedy hope washing over him. "Get him out, Elias!"

The strongman stepped forward, wrapping his massive hands around a thick wooden beam trapping the arm. He strained, his muscles bulging, and tore the heavy timber away. 

The survivor pulled himself out of the wreckage and tumbled into the mud at their feet. 

Elias lowered the lantern. 

It was "Jolly" Jack, the lead clown. He was still wearing his oversized polka-dot suit, soaked in rain and blood. His face was painted in a permanent, exaggerated grin of thick white greasepaint and red lipstick. 

But the smile was wrong. 

The crash had completely shattered Jack's jaw. The bone was hanging at a terrifying, impossible angle, tearing the greasepaint smile into a grotesque, jagged slash. 

"Jack?" Bart asked, stepping back. "Are you alright?"

The clown didn't answer. He didn't scream in agony or beg for help. He slowly pushed himself up onto his hands and knees in the thick mud. 

When he looked up, the light of the kerosene lantern reflected off his eyes. 

They were completely milky white. 

"Jolly" Jack snapped his broken, dangling jaw with a wet, horrific clicking sound, and lunged for the ringmaster's tailored leg. 

The performers of "The Moonlight Express" were dead. But the show wasn't over.



Chapter Two: The Strongman

The attack was clumsy, but the sheer, unthinking ferocity of it caught Bartholomew Vance completely off guard. 

"Jolly" Jack the clown, his polka-dot suit heavy with freezing mud, threw himself forward. His stiff, dead hands clamped onto the crisp lapels of Bart's ruined tuxedo. 

Bart stumbled backward, his polished leather shoes slipping in the deep Ohio mud. He fell hard onto his back, the breath driven from his lungs. Jack fell with him, the clown's shattered jaw swinging wildly, exposing a horrific, jagged edge of broken bone as he snapped at the ringmaster's face. 

The smell was atrocious—a mix of cheap greasepaint, wet wool, and the heavy, coppery tang of fresh blood. 

Bart threw his arms up, desperately trying to keep the snapping teeth away from his throat. "Get him off me!" Bart screamed, the pristine facade of "The Great" Vance completely shattered. 

Elias didn't hesitate. 

The strongman was not a complex thinker, but his loyalty was absolute, and his physical power was terrifying. He stepped forward, the heavy kerosene lantern swinging from his left hand. 

With his right hand, Elias simply reached down and grabbed the clown by the back of his thick, ruffled collar. 

Elias lifted Jack entirely off the ground with one arm. 

The clown weighed over two hundred pounds, his clothes completely waterlogged, but Elias held him suspended in the air as easily as a man might hold a misbehaving dog. 

Jack thrashed violently, his heavy, oversized clown shoes kicking at the air. He didn't try to break Elias's grip. He simply twisted his ruined neck, his milky white eyes locking onto the massive bicep currently holding him aloft. He snapped his broken jaw, trying to tear a chunk of flesh from the strongman's arm. 

"Boss, he's gone crazy!" Elias rumbled, holding the thrashing clown at arm's length. "The crash must have scrambled his brains!"

Bart scrambled backward in the mud, pushing himself up onto his knees. He wiped a smear of Jack's greasepaint and blood off his own cheek, his heart hammering against his ribs. 

He looked at the clown suspended in the lantern light. 

"He's not crazy, Elias," Bart breathed, his eyes wide. He pointed a trembling finger at the clown's neck. "Look at him. His neck is broken. The bone is sticking through the skin. He's dead."

"He's fighting real hard for a dead man," Elias noted, dodging another wild snap of the broken jaw. 

"Put him down!" Bart ordered. 

Elias opened his massive hand. 

Jack dropped heavily into the mud. The impact was brutal; he landed directly on his shattered jaw, completely tearing the remaining skin and muscle holding it in place. The lower jaw detached entirely, falling into the muck. 

The pain should have induced immediate, catastrophic shock. 

Instead, the clown simply pushed himself back up onto his hands and knees. Blood poured freely from his ruined face, a dark, pulsing stream that washed away the white greasepaint. He didn't even pause to orient himself. Driven purely by the scent of living meat, he turned toward Elias and lunged again, a guttural, wet clicking sound coming from his exposed throat. 

Elias frowned. The strongman stepped back, pivoting his massive hips, and drove his heavy work boot directly into the clown's chest. 

The kinetic force of the kick was immense. Jack was thrown backward through the air, crashing violently into the twisted steel siding of the derailed sleeper coach. Ribs snapped audibly over the roar of the storm. 

Jack hit the mud and immediately began to drag himself forward again. 

"Boss," Elias said, his deep voice finally tinged with real unease. "He don't feel it."

Before Bart could answer, a horrific chorus of sounds echoed from the crushed wreckage of the three sleeper coaches. 

*Crack. Splinter. Drag.*

Elias raised the kerosene lantern higher. 

The pale, flickering light washed over the ruined wooden cars. 

Jack wasn't the only survivor. 

Pushing themselves out of the splintered debris, clawing through the mud and the twisted metal, were the rest of the performers and roustabouts. 

A contortionist, her spine visibly snapped in the center of her back, was dragging her torso through the mud with her arms, her legs trailing uselessly behind her. The "Bearded Lady," her chest caved in by a heavy iron beam, stumbled forward, her milky white eyes fixed on the lantern light. Dozens of roustabouts, crushed and broken, were rising from the mud. 

"The rumors," Bart whispered, the rain washing over his horrified face. "The stories from the Dustbowl... they're true."

"What do we do, boss?" Elias asked, stepping back as the vanguard of the dead circus began to close the distance. 

"You put him down, Elias," Bart said, his voice hardening back into the ruthless tone of a ringmaster. He pointed at Jack, who was dragging himself relentlessly toward them. "You hit him until he stops moving. Destroy the head."

Elias looked around the wreckage. He walked over to a heavy section of steel rail that had been torn from the tracks during the derailment. It was four feet long and weighed at least sixty pounds. 

Elias picked it up with both hands, hefting it like a baseball bat. 

He stepped toward the crawling clown. 

Jack snapped his throat at the strongman. 

Elias swung the heavy steel rail in a massive, horizontal arc. 

The impact was devastating. The steel rail caught Jack directly in the side of his skull. The bone shattered instantly, the horrific crunch cutting clearly through the thunderstorm. 

The clown was thrown sideways, his ruined head caved completely in. He hit the mud and finally, mercifully, stopped moving. 

Elias stood over the body, breathing heavily, the rain washing the dark blood off the steel rail. 

"Good," Bart said, scrambling to his feet. He looked at the dozens of infected performers dragging themselves out of the wreckage. "Now, we run."

"Where?" Elias asked, keeping a tight grip on the heavy steel rail. 

"To the center of the train," Bart said, turning his back on the horrific scene. "The equipment cars. We need weapons. And we need a place to hide before the animals figure out they're free."



Chapter Three: The Props Car

The mud of the Ohio plains was a thick, sucking trap. It clung to Bart’s expensive patent leather shoes, pulling them completely off his feet as he scrambled blindly through the dark alongside Elias. 

Behind them, the macabre parade of ruined performers dragged themselves through the muck, their milky white eyes locked onto the faint glow of the strongman's kerosene lantern. 

"Keep moving!" Bart yelled over the deafening crack of thunder, abandoning his ruined shoes and running in his silk socks. 

They reached the middle section of the derailed consist. Several of the heavy steel boxcars carrying the circus equipment had managed to stay relatively upright, their massive weight digging deep into the flooded earth rather than rolling down the embankment. 

"The prop car!" Bart shouted, pointing to a dark, rust-colored boxcar tilted at a thirty-degree angle. "Open it!"

Elias handed the lantern to Bart, stepped up to the heavy sliding steel door, and dug his massive fingers into the recessed handle. He braced his boots in the mud and pulled with a terrifying display of raw physical power. 

The rusted steel wheels screamed against the track, but the heavy door slid open two feet. 

Bart didn't hesitate. He scrambled up the wet steel frame and threw himself into the dark interior of the boxcar, the strongman following immediately behind him. 

Elias grabbed the inside handle and slammed the heavy door shut, dropping the thick iron latch into place. 

The sudden silence inside the steel boxcar was jarring, the deafening roar of the thunderstorm reduced to a heavy, rhythmic drumming on the roof. 

Bart held the kerosene lantern up. 

The prop car was a chaotic, colorful treasure trove of illusions, all violently thrown toward the downhill side of the tilted boxcar. There were crates of bright juggling pins, a tangled mass of unicycles, shattered mirrors from the funhouse, and massive canvas trunks overflowing with sequined costumes and fake velvet capes. 

"Boss, we can't stay in here," Elias said, his chest heaving. "They'll surround the car."

"I know," Bart said, his eyes scanning the chaotic pile of debris. "We're not hiding. We're arming ourselves. We can't fight dozens of them with one piece of track."

Bart waded into the debris, frantically tearing open a wooden crate marked *The Great Saladin*. 

Inside, wrapped in cheap silk, were a dozen long, polished scimitars used for the sword-swallowing act. Bart grabbed one and struck the blade hard against the side of the boxcar. The metal chimed dully, but it didn't dent the steel. 

"Useless," Bart cursed, throwing the prop sword aside. "They're dull. They won't crush bone."

"Boss," Elias called out from the far side of the tilted car. 

The strongman was standing over a heavy, iron-bound trunk that had smashed through a pile of unicycles. The stenciled lettering on the side read *Valentin's Flying Blades*. 

Elias kicked the heavy brass latch open and threw back the lid. 

Inside, perfectly organized in custom velvet slots, were the tools of the circus's master knife-thrower. There were dozens of perfectly balanced, razor-sharp throwing knives, but more importantly, nestled in the bottom of the trunk were three massive, heavy-bladed machetes used for the dramatic finale of Valentin's act. 

"Real steel," Elias rumbled, pulling two of the massive machetes from the trunk. The blades were two feet long, thick, and brutally sharp, designed to easily sever a thick rope from twenty feet away. 

Elias handed one of the machetes to Bart. 

Bart gripped the leather-wrapped handle. The weight was perfect. It was a weapon designed for chopping, exactly what they needed to sever spinal cords or split skulls. 

"Alright," Bart said, a cold, calculating light returning to his eyes. "We have steel. We wait for the first wave to reach the door. We open it, we take off their heads, and we push through to the engine. The engineer had a Winchester in the cab. If we can reach the cab, we—"

*THUD.*

The massive impact didn't come from the sliding steel door. 

It came from the roof of the boxcar. 

The heavy steel ceiling bowed inward slightly, accompanied by the loud, scraping sound of thick claws finding purchase on wet metal. 

Bart froze, the machete held tight in his fist. 

The dead performers were crawling through the thick mud. They couldn't jump. They couldn't climb the sheer steel sides of the boxcar in the pouring rain. 

Whatever had just landed on the roof was massive, agile, and very much alive. 

The heavy scraping moved slowly across the roof, toward the sliding steel door. 

Then, a sound cut through the drumming rain that made the hair on the back of Bart's neck stand up. It was a deep, wet, rhythmic sniffing. 

A shadow fell over the two-inch gap between the sliding steel door and the frame. 

A massive, golden paw, as wide as a dinner plate, shoved its way into the gap. The claws, thick, curved, and as long as railroad spikes, scraped loudly against the steel. 

It was Sultan, the five-hundred-pound male African lion from the menagerie car. 

The beast roared, a deafening, terrifying sound that vibrated the steel walls of the boxcar, as it tried to pry the heavy door open. 

Bartholomew Vance gripped his machete, realizing with absolute horror that the dead clowns weren't the only predators hunting in the dark tonight.



Chapter Four: The Lion's Share

The sheer physical power of a full-grown African lion is something most men cannot truly comprehend until it is applied directly against them. 

The heavy iron latch holding the sliding steel door of the boxcar groaned, the thick metal visibly bending under the immense pressure of Sultan's massive paw. 

"Elias!" Bart yelled, stepping back into the shadows of the tilted car, gripping his machete tightly. "Hold it shut!"

The strongman dropped his own machetes and threw his massive weight against the inside of the steel door. He planted his boots on the tilted floor and pushed with everything he had, his massive biceps bulging under his soaked shirt. 

For a terrifying five seconds, it was a pure contest of strength between the strongest man in the circus and its apex predator. 

The steel door screamed. 

Elias roared in exertion, the veins in his neck standing out like thick ropes. 

But Sultan was pulling from the outside, using his massive shoulder muscles and the terrifying leverage of his claws hooked into the door frame. The two-inch gap slowly, agonizingly widened to four inches. 

"Boss," Elias gasped, his boots slipping on the wet steel floor. "He's too strong!"

Bart raised his machete, calculating the exact angle to strike the massive paw if the lion managed to force its head through the gap. He knew it was a losing battle. A machete might wound the beast, but it wouldn't stop a five-hundred-pound lion in an enclosed space. If the door opened, they were dead. 

Then, the terrifying, deafening roar of the lion suddenly stopped. 

Sultan yanked his massive paw out of the gap. The sudden release of tension sent Elias crashing heavily to the floor. 

The heavy scratching on the steel roof ceased immediately. The lion had jumped down. 

Bart didn't relax. He grabbed the kerosene lantern, turned the wick down to a faint, barely visible glow, and crept cautiously toward the four-inch gap in the steel door. 

He pressed his face to the cold metal and looked out into the pouring rain. 

Sultan was standing in the deep mud, twenty feet from the boxcar. The lion was a magnificent, terrifying silhouette in the flashes of lightning, his massive mane soaked and plastered to his thick neck. 

But the lion wasn't looking at the boxcar. He was facing the darkness at the rear of the train. 

The dead had arrived. 

The horde of ruined performers and crushed roustabouts dragged themselves out of the shadows, their milky white eyes tracking the movement of the massive animal. There were at least forty of them, a slow, relentless tide of dead flesh moving through the rain. 

Sultan dropped into a low crouch, his tail twitching aggressively. To the lion, the dead were simply a rival pack entering his territory. He let out a low, vibrating growl that Bart could feel in the soles of his feet. 

The dead didn't pause. They didn't exhibit the instinctual, paralyzing terror that any living creature would feel in the presence of an angry lion. They simply continued their slow, uncoordinated march forward, their jaws snapping wetly. 

Sultan attacked. 

It was an explosion of golden muscle and raw, primal violence. The massive lion launched himself through the air, covering thirty feet in a single bound. He landed directly in the center of the horde. 

With a single, brutal swipe of his massive paw, Sultan decapitated a dead acrobat. The claws tore entirely through the dead man's neck, sending the skull spinning into the mud. 

Sultan spun, his massive jaws clamping down on the torso of a ruined roustabout. He bit cleanly through the man's ribs, snapping the spine in half and tossing the severed upper body aside like a ragdoll. 

"Good God," Bart whispered, his breath fogging the steel door. 

It was an absolute massacre. The apex predator was a machine of death, tearing the dead performers to pieces with terrifying efficiency. Bones snapped, ruined flesh flew through the rain, and the mud turned a deep, horrific crimson. 

But the dead did not retreat. 

Sultan killed five of them in the first ten seconds. But there were thirty-five left. 

As the lion crushed the skull of a bearded woman in his jaws, three dead roustabouts lunged onto his massive back. 

Sultan roared in surprise, bucking violently to throw them off. 

But the dead hands clamped tightly into his thick mane and the loose skin of his flanks. They didn't try to wrestle him. They simply opened their jaws and sank their teeth deeply into the lion's flesh. 

Sultan let out a deafening roar of pain and absolute fury. He rolled violently in the mud, crushing two of the dead under his massive weight. 

But four more instantly took their place. 

They swarmed him like fire ants. They grabbed his thick legs, they bit into his flanks, they tore at his massive mane. 

The lion fought with the desperate, apocalyptic fury of a king being dragged from his throne. He severed arms, crushed skulls, and ripped bodies in half, his roars shaking the ground. 

But the sheer, relentless weight of the dead was overwhelming. They didn't care if their arms were torn off. They didn't care if their legs were crushed. As long as their heads were intact, they kept biting. 

Slowly, agonizingly, the massive, five-hundred-pound beast was dragged down into the bloody Ohio mud. 

The golden coat disappeared beneath a writhing, snapping mass of rotting flesh. Sultan's roars turned into wet, ragged gasps, and then, finally, to silence. 

The apex predator had fallen to the unthinking horde. 

Inside the boxcar, Bart lowered the lantern, his face ashen. 

The dead had lost over a dozen of their number to the lion, their bodies completely pulverized. But there were still nearly thirty of them left. 

And as the last of Sultan's blood soaked into the mud, the remaining dead performers slowly stood up, turned their milky white eyes back toward the steel boxcar, and began to walk toward the four-inch gap in the door.



Chapter Five: The Funhouse Mirrors

The sound of thirty bodies pressing against the heavy steel sliding door was a terrifying, wet compression of shifting bone and rotting meat. 

Elias threw his massive shoulder against the inside of the door, his boots slipping on the tilted steel floorplates. He could hold back the strength of two men, perhaps three, but the horde outside wasn't trying to pry the door open. They were simply marching relentlessly forward, their combined, dead weight pressing against the metal with hydraulic force. 

The heavy iron latch began to grind against its housing. 

"Boss," Elias grunted, the sweat pouring down his face despite the freezing temperature of the boxcar. "I can't hold them all. The latch is going to break."

Bart didn't answer immediately. He was staring at the chaotic pile of circus props, his ruthless mind calculating angles and probabilities. They couldn't fight thirty infected in a head-on clash, even with the heavy machetes. They would be overwhelmed by sheer numbers. 

They needed an illusion. 

"Elias, let them push," Bart said, his voice cold and steady. "Step back from the door."

"They'll flood the car, boss!"

"I know," Bart said. He picked up the heavy kerosene lantern and walked toward the back of the tilted boxcar, where a massive wooden crate had shattered during the derailment. 

Spilling out of the crate were dozens of thick, heavy glass panels—the warped, distorted mirrors from the circus funhouse. 

"Help me move these," Bart ordered. 

Elias stepped back from the door. The steel immediately bowed inward, the latch screaming in protest. The strongman hurried to the back of the car, grabbing the heavy mirrors with his massive hands. 

"Angle them," Bart directed, working with frantic speed. "Create a V-shape facing the door. Lean them against the unicycles and the costume trunks."

Within seconds, they had constructed a crude, jagged funnel of warped mirrors in the center of the boxcar. The glass distorted their reflections, stretching their bodies into horrific, elongated shapes. 

Bart took the kerosene lantern and placed it on the floor, perfectly positioned behind the apex of the mirrored V-shape. 

"Alright," Bart said, picking up his machete. He stood behind the mirrors, completely hidden from the doorway, his silhouette cast wildly across the warped glass. "Elias, open the door. Pull it wide. Then get behind the glass with me."

Elias didn't question the ringmaster. He stepped up to the groaning steel door, gripped the recessed handle, and pulled the iron latch free. 

He threw the heavy door wide open. 

The horde poured into the boxcar like a wave of rotting meat. The vanguard stumbled over the lip of the sliding door, falling onto the tilted steel floor, only to be immediately trampled by the relentless press of the bodies behind them. 

Elias scrambled backward, diving behind the barricade of warped mirrors just as the first wave of infected regained their footing. 

The dead stood in the dark boxcar, waterlogged and ruined, their milky white eyes tracking the only source of light. 

They looked at the funhouse mirrors. 

The Romero rules of the sickness dictated that the infected were completely unthinking. They had no cognitive reasoning, no memory, and absolutely no concept of self-awareness. 

When the dead performers looked into the warped glass, they didn't see distorted reflections of themselves. 

They saw movement. They saw figures in the dark. 

Driven entirely by the primal urge to feed on the living, the infected vanguard lunged violently forward, directly into the funhouse mirrors. 

The sound of shattering glass was deafening. 

The thick, heavy panes exploded inward under the weight of the dead bodies, slicing through their rotting flesh and snapping their collarbones. The horde was completely disoriented, attacking their own reflections, tearing at the shards of glass and the wooden frames of the mirrors. 

"Now!" Bart yelled over the chaos. 

Bart and Elias stepped out from behind the shattered barricade, bringing the heavy, razor-sharp machetes down with brutal, calculated efficiency. 

The illusion had worked perfectly. The vanguard was tangled in the broken glass and the debris of the circus props, their backs turned to the real threat. 

Elias swung his machete like a scythe. The heavy blade cleaved cleanly through the neck of a dead roustabout, the severed head rolling into a pile of juggling pins. Bart stepped forward, driving his blade into the top of a dead acrobat's skull with a sickening crunch. 

They hacked their way through the confusion, using the weight of the machetes to shatter skulls and sever spinal cords. The tight confines of the boxcar became a slaughterhouse of dark blood and broken glass. 

In less than thirty seconds, they had cleared a path through the vanguard. 

"Out!" Bart yelled, kicking a crawling torso out of the way. "Through the door!"

Elias led the charge, his massive frame barreling through the remaining infected at the doorway, throwing them aside like ragdolls. 

They leaped out of the boxcar and back into the freezing rain and deep mud of the Ohio plains. 

They had survived the ambush. But the illusion was broken. 

The remaining fifteen infected performers inside the boxcar turned away from the shattered mirrors. They heard the splashing in the mud. They smelled the fresh blood on the machetes. 

They stumbled out of the boxcar, their milky white eyes locking onto the two men, and continued their relentless, unthinking march forward.



Chapter Six: The High Wire

The thunderstorm over the Ohio plains showed no sign of breaking. The rain fell in solid, freezing sheets, turning the ground around the derailed circus train into a treacherous swamp of deep mud, twisted steel, and shattered wood. 

Bartholomew Vance ran blindly through the deluge, his expensive silk socks offering absolutely no traction in the muck. 

"Keep pushing, boss!" Elias yelled over the roar of the storm, his massive frame easily bulldozing through the debris ahead. "The engine is just past the flatbeds!"

Bart didn't answer. His chest burned, and his arms ached from the weight of the heavy machete. Behind them, the fifteen remaining infected performers were a slow, relentless nightmare, dragging their ruined bodies through the mud with terrifying, single-minded focus. 

Suddenly, Elias stopped dead in his tracks. 

"Boss," the strongman said, his voice grim. 

Bart stumbled up beside him and looked forward. The path to the engine was completely blocked. 

One of the massive steel flatbed cars, tasked with carrying the enormous, eighty-foot wooden center poles for the main circus tent, had violently jackknifed during the derailment. The heavy iron chains holding the load had snapped, spilling the massive wooden logs across the tracks and into the deep mud. 

It was a chaotic, thirty-foot-high barricade of rain-slicked, stripped pine logs. Going around the massive spill would mean wading chest-deep into the flooded plains, a detour that would cost them precious minutes and leave them completely exposed. 

"We have to go over," Bart gasped, leaning heavily on his machete. 

Elias didn't hesitate. He jammed his own machete through the thick leather belt of his trousers, reached up, and grabbed the thick end of a lower tent pole. His massive biceps flexed, and he pulled his heavy body up onto the chaotic pile of logs with shocking agility. 

Bart followed, but he was a man of luxury, not labor. He grabbed a wet log and pulled, but his silk socks immediately slipped on the rain-slicked bark. He fell hard, his shin cracking painfully against a lower log. 

"Come on, boss!" Elias yelled, already halfway up the barricade, his boots finding secure footholds in the gaps between the massive poles. 

Bart scrambled frantically, using the heavy handle of the machete like an ice pick to pull himself up. He made it ten feet off the ground, crawling over the wet, shifting logs. 

Below him, the horde arrived. 

The infected didn't pause at the obstacle. They simply began to crawl up the massive wooden tent poles, their dead fingers digging into the wet bark. They were clumsy, but there were fifteen of them, and they were relentless. 

Bart felt the massive log beneath him shift suddenly. 

A dead roustabout, his chest crushed inward, had grabbed the other end of the log, trying to pull himself up. The sudden shift in weight was catastrophic. 

The heavy pine pole rolled outward. 

Bart slipped, tumbling backward down the pile. His right leg slid cleanly into a narrow V-shaped gap between two massive, intersecting tent poles. 

As he fell, the heavy top log shifted again, rolling heavily back into place. 

*CRUNCH.*

Bart screamed as the sheer weight of the heavy wooden pole pinned his right leg just below the knee, crushing the muscle against the lower log. He was trapped, dangling upside down ten feet in the air, his leg locked in a massive wooden vise. 

"Elias!" Bart shrieked, panic finally overwhelming his ruthless composure. 

Elias looked down from the top of the pile. He immediately began scrambling back down the treacherous logs, ignoring his own safety. 

But the horde was already there. 

A dead acrobat, his sequined costume covered in mud, crawled rapidly up the log directly beneath Bart. The acrobat's jaw snapped wetly, his milky white eyes locked onto the exposed flesh of Bart's ankle. 

Bart swung the heavy machete wildly downward, striking the acrobat in the shoulder. The sharp blade bit deep, severing the collarbone, but the dead man didn't even flinch. He just kept crawling upward. 

"Elias, hurry!" Bart screamed, swinging again. 

Elias reached the intersecting logs. He didn't try to pull Bart. He knew the pressure would break the ringmaster's leg. 

Instead, the strongman wedged his massive shoulder under the heavy top log that was pinning Bart. He braced his boots against the lower pole, took a deep breath, and unleashed every ounce of his terrifying physical strength. 

The veins in Elias's neck bulged. The heavy wooden log groaned in protest. 

Bart watched in horror as the dead acrobat reached his trapped leg. Cold, stiff fingers wrapped around Bart's calf. The acrobat opened his ruined jaw and lunged forward. 

*CRACK.*

Elias roared, his muscles straining past their limit. The heavy top log shifted violently upward, lifting just enough to relieve the pressure. 

Bart violently yanked his trapped leg free from the wooden vise. 

But he was a fraction of a second too late. 

As Bart's foot jerked upward, the dead acrobat's teeth clamped down hard on the heel of Bart's foot. The teeth easily tore through the thin, expensive silk sock, sinking deeply into the soft flesh of Bart's heel. 

Bart screamed, a sharp, agonizing cry that cut through the storm, and kicked violently outward with his free leg, catching the acrobat in the face and dislodging the bite. 

The acrobat fell backward, tumbling down the logs and into the mud. 

Elias didn't see the bite. The strongman let the heavy log crash back into place, reached down, and hauled the ringmaster roughly up the rest of the barricade by the collar of his ruined tuxedo. 

"I got you, boss," Elias rumbled, practically carrying Bart over the top of the massive pile of tent poles. 

They scrambled down the other side of the logjam, hitting the mud near the front of the derailed train. The horde was trapped on the other side, struggling clumsily over the wet wood. 

"Are you okay, boss? Your leg?" Elias asked, looking back at the barricade. 

Bart leaned heavily against a piece of twisted steel, gasping for air. The pain in his heel was a white-hot, agonizing throb. He looked down at his ruined silk sock. The dark blood was already washing away in the freezing rain. 

He had survived the derailment. He had survived the lion. 

But he knew the rules of the sickness. He had seen what it did to the people in the Dustbowl. 

"My leg is fine, Elias," Bart lied, his voice cold and terrifyingly calm. "Just a bruise. Let's get to the engine."

The ruthless ringmaster of the Midnight Circus was infected, and he was keeping it a secret from the strongest man on the train.



Chapter Seven: The Engineer's Cab

The massive steam locomotive at the front of "The Moonlight Express" looked like a dying iron beast. It had plowed deep into the muddy embankment, the heavy steel cowcatcher buried entirely in the Ohio dirt. 

The massive boiler was hissing violently, venting thick plumes of white steam into the freezing rain as the internal pressure fought a losing battle against the ruptured pipes. 

Bart leaned heavily against the iron ladder leading up to the engineer's cab. His right leg felt like it was on fire, a deep, pulsing agony radiating upward from his heel. He was sweating profusely despite the freezing rain, and a cold shiver racked his body. 

"Boss, you look pale," Elias said, the strongman's deep voice rumbling with genuine concern. 

"I'm fine," Bart snapped, the words slurring slightly. He gripped the cold iron rungs of the ladder and began to pull himself up. 

It took all of his remaining strength, and Elias had to place a massive hand firmly on his lower back to push him the rest of the way. 

They hauled themselves over the threshold and into the cramped, sweltering heat of the engineer's cab. 

The air inside was thick with steam and the metallic smell of hot iron. It was also thick with the copper scent of fresh blood. 

The engineer and the fireman were dead. 

When the locomotive hit the washout, the sudden deceleration had thrown both men violently forward. They had been crushed instantly against the heavy iron face of the boiler. 

But Bart didn't feel the rising panic he had felt when he saw the crushed roustabouts in the sleeper cars. He stared at the two dead men in the cab with a cold, detached relief. 

Their heads were completely destroyed, pulverized against the hot iron. 

"They won't get up," Bart whispered, his breath shallow. "The brains are gone. The Romero rules."

"What rules, boss?" Elias asked, stepping carefully over the ruined bodies. 

"Nothing," Bart muttered, stumbling toward a heavy wooden lockbox bolted to the side of the cab. He swung his machete, shattering the cheap brass padlock with a single strike. 

He threw the lid open. Inside, wrapped in an oiled rag, was a pristine Winchester lever-action rifle and a heavy cardboard box of .44-40 ammunition. 

Bart grabbed the rifle, his trembling hands frantically loading the brass cartridges into the side gate. 

A sharp, violent spasm wracked his right leg. Bart cried out, collapsing heavily into the engineer's padded leather seat. The rifle clattered onto his lap. 

His vision blurred, the edge of the cab melting into the steam. The infection was moving with terrifying speed. The venom of the dead was pumping through his heart, systematically shutting down his nervous system. 

Elias stepped to the rear of the cab, looking out over the coal tender and into the rainy darkness. 

"Boss," Elias rumbled, his grip tightening on his machete. "They made it over the logs."

Through the hazy steam, Bart could hear the wet, dragging footsteps. The fifteen remaining infected performers had finally navigated the chaotic pile of tent poles and were now splashing through the mud directly toward the locomotive. 

"Give me the rifle, boss," Elias said, holding his massive hand out without looking back. "I can pick them off before they reach the ladders."

Bart looked at the strongman's outstretched hand. 

Then he looked down at the Winchester rifle in his lap. 

The infection was burning through Bart's brain, tearing away the last shreds of his civilized facade, leaving only the naked, ruthless survival instinct that had made him a successful ringmaster. 

But it was a twisted instinct. 

Bart knew he was dying. He knew that in a few minutes, his heart would stop, and he would rise again with a singular, unthinking hunger. 

And when he rose, the first thing he would try to eat was Elias. 

If he gave Elias the rifle, the strongman would easily shoot him in the head the moment he turned. The logic was sound. It was the right thing to do. 

But Bartholomew "The Great" Vance was not a good man. He was a vain, selfish creature who could not stomach the idea of his own destruction, even if it meant saving someone else. He didn't want to die, and he certainly didn't want to be put down like a rabid dog by his own hired muscle. 

Bart's fingers wrapped tightly around the wooden stock of the Winchester. 

"No," Bart rasped, his voice sounding thick and wet. 

Elias turned around, his brow furrowed in confusion. "Boss? There's fifteen of them. I can't chop them all before they get up here. Give me the gun."

"I said no," Bart hissed, raising the barrel of the rifle and pointing it directly at Elias's massive chest. "It's mine."

Elias stared at the gun, his slow mind struggling to process the betrayal. "I'm right here, boss. I'll protect you. Just like always."

"You can't protect me from this, you stupid ape!" Bart screamed, a line of dark saliva running down his chin. He pulled his right leg up, tearing the ruined silk sock away to reveal the deep, jagged bite mark on his heel. The flesh around the wound was already turning a horrific, bruised black. 

Elias stepped back, his eyes widening. He finally understood. 

"You're bit," Elias whispered. 

"And I'm keeping the gun," Bart smiled, a terrifying, manic expression. "I won't let you shoot me. I won't let you."

A heavy, metallic clank echoed from the side of the cab. 

The first dead performer had reached the locomotive and grabbed the iron rungs of the ladder. 

Elias looked at the ladder, and then back at the dying ringmaster pointing a rifle at his chest. The strongest man in the circus was trapped between a horde of monsters and the only man he had ever trusted.



Chapter Eight: The Boiler

The standoff in the sweltering, steam-filled cab of the locomotive was broken by the wet, slapping sound of a dead hand grabbing the iron threshold of the doorway. 

A dead roustabout, his face completely caved in from a blow to the jaw, pulled his ruined body up into the cab. He didn't hesitate. He lunged directly toward Elias, his teeth snapping. 

Elias had dropped his machetes when he held the boxcar door shut, and he hadn't had time to retrieve them. He was completely unarmed. 

But Elias was the strongest man in the Midnight Circus. 

He didn't back away. He stepped into the lunge, pivoting his massive hips, and drove a right cross directly into the dead man's chest. The impact sounded like a sledgehammer hitting a side of beef. The dead roustabout was lifted completely off his feet and thrown backward out the open doorway, plummeting down into the mud. 

"Boss!" Elias roared, not taking his eyes off the doorway. "Shoot them! They're coming up both sides!"

Bartholomew Vance sat slumped in the engineer's padded leather chair, the Winchester rifle resting across his knees. The dark poison in his blood had reached his brain. He was sweating profusely, his skin a horrific, waxy grey, his breath coming in shallow, ragged gasps. 

He looked at Elias, but he didn't see the loyal strongman who had saved his life twice tonight. He saw an audience member. A rube. 

"The show..." Bart mumbled, his voice a wet rasp. "The show must have a finale."

He slowly raised the barrel of the Winchester. 

"Boss, shoot the door!" Elias yelled, grappling with a dead acrobat who had just hauled himself into the cab. Elias wrapped his massive hands around the acrobat's throat, struggling to keep the snapping teeth away from his face. 

Bart didn't point the rifle at the door. 

He pointed the barrel directly at the massive, cast-iron face of the locomotive's boiler. Specifically, he aimed at the primary pressure relief valve, a thick brass fixture currently vibrating under the immense strain of the trapped, superheated steam. 

"For my final trick," Bart whispered, a line of dark blood trickling from his nose. 

He pulled the trigger. 

The deafening crack of the .44-40 cartridge in the confined space of the cab was absolute agony. 

The heavy lead bullet struck the brass pressure valve dead center. The brass shattered instantly. 

The result was catastrophic. 

A massive, horizontal geyser of superheated, high-pressure steam erupted from the boiler, screaming into the small cab like a physical entity. The temperature inside the iron room skyrocketed instantly. 

Elias cried out as the scalding steam washed over his back, blistering his skin through his soaked shirt. He threw the dead acrobat backward into the steam cloud. The dead man's flesh cooked instantly, peeling away from the bone in wet, grey strips, but the zombie didn't scream. It just kept crawling forward, its ruined hands reaching for Elias's legs. 

Two more infected performers pulled themselves into the cab from the other side, completely ignoring the scalding heat. 

Elias was trapped. The steam was blinding him, cooking him alive, and the dead were closing in. He grabbed a heavy iron coal shovel from the floor and swung it wildly, catching one of the infected in the side of the head and knocking it back out the door. 

Bart didn't react to the steam. 

He sat in the chair, the rifle slipping from his fingers and clattering to the floorplates. His chest stopped heaving. His breathing simply ceased. 

The ringmaster's head rolled backward, his eyes staring blankly at the iron ceiling. 

For five terrifying seconds, Bartholomew "The Great" Vance was truly dead. 

Then, his spine snapped violently straight. 

His eyes snapped open. The dark, calculating intelligence that had defined his life was completely gone, replaced by a vacant, milky white void. 

He didn't look at the rifle. He didn't comprehend the concept of a weapon anymore. The sickness had erased the man and left only the instinct. 

He smelled the blood. He heard the struggle. 

Zombie-Bart launched himself from the engineer's chair, flying through the blinding, scalding steam, his jaws snapping wildly as he lunged directly toward Elias's exposed back.



Chapter Nine: The Iron Grate

The blinding, superheated steam screaming from the ruptured boiler turned the cramped engineer's cab into a physical manifestation of hell. 

Elias was fighting blind. The scalding vapor was searing his lungs with every desperate breath. The skin on his massive forearms was turning a terrifying, angry red, blistering rapidly as he threw his heavy fists into the ruined faces of the two dead performers trying to drag him down. 

The infected didn't care about the heat. The steam was literally cooking the flesh off their cheekbones, exposing the yellowed teeth and grey bone beneath, but they simply snapped their jaws and continued to press forward. 

Elias shoved a boiled acrobat backward, his heavy boot slipping on the wet, bloody iron floorplates. 

He didn't see the attack coming from behind. 

He just felt the sudden, crushing impact as Bartholomew "The Great" Vance launched himself through the steam and slammed heavily into the strongman's broad back. 

Elias stumbled forward, hitting the edge of the coal tender. 

He felt cold, stiff hands clamp onto his shoulders. Then, he felt the sharp, brutal tear of teeth sinking deeply into the thick muscle of his neck. The bite tore through the steam-softened skin with terrifying ease. 

Elias roared, a sound of absolute, heart-rending betrayal. 

He spun violently around, the massive centrifugal force throwing Zombie-Bart off his back and sending the dead ringmaster crashing into the side of the boiler. 

Elias pressed his massive hand to his neck. The blood was pumping hot and fast through his fingers. He looked through the swirling white steam. 

Bart was pushing himself up from the iron floor. The ringmaster's tailored tuxedo was ruined, his face pale and dead, his eyes a vacant, milky white. He snapped his jaw, a piece of Elias's flesh still caught between his teeth. 

The strongman understood. The man he had protected, the man he had trusted to lead them through the storm, had murdered him in a fit of selfish paranoia. 

Elias was infected. The poison was already in his blood. 

A cold, terrifying calm settled over the strongman. The pain of the scalding steam and the brutal bite faded into the background. He didn't have to protect anyone anymore. He didn't have to follow orders. 

He just had to finish the show. 

The two dead acrobats lunged at him again from the doorway. 

Elias didn't punch them. He simply stepped forward, grabbed both of them by the throats with his massive hands, and threw them violently backward out the open door of the cab. They plummeted into the darkness, crashing heavily into the mud below. 

Elias turned slowly back to the dead ringmaster. 

Zombie-Bart lunged, his jaw snapping wetly. 

Elias reached out and caught Bart by the throat with one hand, stopping the dead man's forward momentum instantly. He squeezed, his massive fingers easily crushing the ringmaster's windpipe with a sickening crunch. 

But Elias knew the rules now. Crushing the throat didn't stop them. The head had to be destroyed. 

He didn't have a machete. He didn't have a heavy steel rail. 

But he had the boiler. 

Elias kept his left hand clamped tightly around Bart's throat, holding the thrashing, snapping zombie at arm's length. With his right hand, the strongman reached for the heavy iron handle of the locomotive's firebox door. 

The iron was burning hot, but Elias didn't flinch. He pulled the heavy latch up and threw the thick iron door wide open. 

The heat that rolled out of the massive firebox was apocalyptic. Inside, a mountain of high-grade coal was burning at two thousand degrees, a blinding, white-hot inferno that instantly vaporized the steam in the immediate area. 

Zombie-Bart didn't pull away from the heat. He just kept thrashing against Elias's grip, trying to reach the strongman's neck. 

"You always wanted to be the star, boss," Elias rumbled, his deep voice carrying over the roar of the fire and the hissing steam. 

Elias stepped forward, his massive muscles bunching with terrifying power. 

He lifted Bartholomew Vance off the iron floorplates and drove the ringmaster forward, shoving the zombie headfirst directly into the roaring white-hot coals of the firebox. 

The destruction was instantaneous and absolute. 

The intense heat incinerated Bart's face, boiling the fluid in his skull and turning his brain to ash in a fraction of a second. The flesh caught fire, the tailored tuxedo erupting into a violent burst of flame. 

Elias held the burning body in the firebox for three terrifying seconds, ensuring the head was completely destroyed, enforcing the Romero rule with brutal, elemental force. 

When he pulled his arm back, Bart's body went completely limp, dropping heavily to the floorplates, the head entirely gone, leaving only a smoking, blackened stump of a neck. 

Elias stood over the burning corpse, breathing heavily. 

But the victory was hollow. 

More heavy footsteps sounded on the iron ladders. The rest of the horde had arrived. 

Elias looked at his bleeding neck. He could feel the cold numbness spreading outward from the bite. His vision was beginning to blur. 

He looked back at the roaring firebox, and then out at the dozen ruined performers pulling themselves into the steam-filled cab. 

Elias didn't fight them. He didn't pick up the heavy iron shovel. 

The strongest man in the circus simply turned his back to the door, sat down heavily on the iron floor next to the open, roaring firebox, and waited for the fever to take him.



Chapter Ten: The Finale

The fever hit Elias like a physical blow. 

It was a cold, terrifying numbness that started at the deep bite mark on his neck and rapidly spread down his massive shoulders, paralyzing his muscles and clouding his mind. The scalding steam hissing from the ruptured pressure valve no longer burned him; it just felt like a heavy, wet blanket pressing him into the iron floorplates. 

He slumped against the side of the open firebox, the roaring, white-hot heat washing over his back. 

He didn't move as the first of the remaining infected performers hauled itself through the open doorway of the cab. It was a contortionist, her body a ruined, twisted knot of broken bones and torn velvet. She dragged herself across the wet iron and clamped her ruined jaw onto Elias's massive bicep. 

Elias barely felt the teeth break the skin. 

Two more pulled themselves into the cab. A juggler missing half his face, and a roustabout with a caved-in chest. They swarmed the sitting strongman, their cold hands grabbing his clothes, their teeth tearing at his shoulders and chest. 

Elias watched them with a strange, detached calm. 

He was the strongest man in the Midnight Circus. He could have grabbed them by their skulls and crushed them like walnuts. He could have fought until the very last drop of blood left his body. 

But Elias understood the brutal mathematics of the sickness. He was already dead. Fighting them would only prolong the agony of the transition. And when the transition was complete, when his heart finally stopped and restarted, he would become a monster with the physical strength to rip a man in half with his bare hands. 

He couldn't allow that to happen. 

Elias looked slowly to his right, toward the open, roaring inferno of the locomotive's firebox. 

Without a pressure relief valve, the massive steel boiler was a ticking bomb. The white-hot coals were superheating thousands of gallons of water, and the steam had nowhere to go but out the small hole Bart had shot in the brass valve. The iron walls of the boiler were already beginning to groan and buckle under the astronomical internal pressure. 

It just needed a little push. 

Elias reached his massive right arm out, ignoring the three dead performers tearing at his flesh. His thick fingers closed around the heavy iron handle of the coal shovel lying on the floorplates. 

He gripped the heavy tool tightly. 

He didn't swing it at the dead. He raised the heavy iron shovel high above his head, his muscles bunching one final time. 

With the last ounce of his immense, living strength, Elias brought the heavy iron edge of the shovel down directly onto the main high-pressure water intake pipe leading from the tender to the superheated boiler. 

The heavy iron pipe shattered instantly. 

A massive, solid wall of pressurized cold water surged violently directly into the white-hot, two-thousand-degree firebox. 

The physical reaction was catastrophic and instantaneous. The cold water expanded into steam at an exponential, terrifying rate inside the already over-pressurized boiler. 

Elias closed his eyes. The show was over. 

Ten hours later, the Ohio thunderstorm finally broke, giving way to a pale, washed-out morning sun. 

A local county sheriff, leading a posse of six deputies on horseback, rode slowly through the deep mud toward the twisted, smoking wreckage of "The Moonlight Express". They had heard the deafening roar of the derailment during the night, and a second, even louder explosion shortly before dawn. 

The sheriff pulled his horse to a stop near the front of the consist. 

"Mother of God," one of the deputies whispered, taking his Stetson off. 

The heavy steel boxcars and the wooden sleeper coaches were a ruined, chaotic mess, but the front of the train simply didn't exist anymore. 

The massive steam locomotive had suffered a catastrophic boiler explosion. The eighty-ton iron engine had been ripped apart like a cheap tin can. The thick steel plates of the boiler were peeled backward, twisted into grotesque, jagged shapes. 

Where the engineer's cab had been, there was nothing but a massive, blackened crater in the muddy embankment. 

The sheriff slowly dismounted his horse and walked toward the edge of the crater. 

He was looking for survivors. He was looking for bodies. 

He found neither. 

The explosion had been so immense, so violently absolute, that it had obliterated everything within a hundred-foot radius. There were no dead performers crawling through the mud. There were no infected clowns or ruined strongmen. The intense, explosive heat had vaporized the flesh and shattered the bone into unrecognizable ash. 

The sheriff stood at the edge of the crater for a long time, listening to the quiet dripping of the rain from the twisted steel. 

He didn't know about the sickness. He didn't know about the horde that had emerged from the sleeper cars, or the lion that had fought them, or the strongman who had chosen destruction over turning into a monster. 

All he knew was that the circus had come to town, and it was never leaving. 



Story Eight: The Black Lung

Chapter One: The Canary

The darkness at the bottom of Shaft Four was a physical weight, heavier and more oppressive than the thousands of tons of West Virginia bedrock suspended directly above their heads. 

It was 1934, and the Appalachian coal companies didn't care about the Dustbowl ravaging the Midwest. They cared about tonnage. And tonnage required men willing to sell their lungs for seventy-five cents a day. 

Caleb swung his heavy iron pickaxe, the sharp point biting deep into the black seam of bituminous coal. He pulled the handle back, but the exertion triggered a violent spasm deep in his chest. He dropped the heavy tool, bent over at the waist, and coughed. It was a wet, rattling sound that ended with him spitting a thick gob of black phlegm onto the dusty floor. 

"You alright, Mr. Caleb?" a young voice asked from the shadows. 

Tommy was barely sixteen, a former "breaker boy" who had just been promoted to the deep shafts. His face was already completely covered in a thick layer of fine black dust, making his wide, terrified eyes stand out starkly in the dim yellow light of their carbide headlamps. 

"I'm fine, kid," Caleb rasped, wiping his mouth with the back of his filthy glove. He had been digging in the dark for twenty years. The black lung was a slow executioner, but it was a familiar one. 

"The timbers are singing again," Tommy whispered, looking nervously up at the massive, rough-hewn oak beams supporting the roof of the tunnel. 

Caleb listened. Deep within the bedrock, a slow, terrifying groan echoed through the shaft. It was the sound of the mountain shifting, settling its unimaginable weight against the wooden supports. Miners called them "bumps." 

"Just the mountain stretching her legs," Caleb lied, picking his axe back up. In truth, the bumps had been getting more frequent over the last hour. The seam they were working was dangerously unstable. 

Heavy, aggressive footsteps splashed through the ankle-deep water at the bottom of the tunnel. 

Foreman O'Malley emerged from the gloom, his expensive leather boots completely ruined by the acidic mine water. He wasn't carrying a pickaxe; he was carrying a heavy wooden clipboard. 

"You're behind quota, Caleb!" O'Malley barked, his voice echoing sharply off the coal face. "The topside conveyor is running light. I need three more carts out of this drift before the whistle blows!"

"The roof is unstable, O'Malley," Caleb said, his voice hard. He didn't use the honorific 'Mister'. "We're hearing bumps. We need to stop digging and set more timber before we push further into the seam."

"We don't have time to set timber!" O'Malley snapped, stepping closer, his face turning red with anger. "The company is breathing down my neck. You dig the coal, or you don't get paid. It's that simple."

"It's going to come down on us," Caleb insisted, his grip tightening on the handle of his pickaxe. 

O'Malley sneered. He pointed a thick, stubby finger up toward the roof of the tunnel. Hanging from a rusty iron nail driven into a timber support was a small, ornate brass cage. Inside the cage, a tiny, bright yellow canary was hopping actively from perch to perch, occasionally letting out a trilling chirp. 

"The bird is singing, Caleb," O'Malley said, his tone dripping with condescension. "The air is sweet. The mountain is safe. Now stop whining and fill those damn carts!"

O'Malley turned his back and began stomping back down the tunnel toward the main shaft. 

Caleb glared at the foreman's back, then sighed heavily, his chest rattling. He turned back to the coal face. 

"He's right, Mr. Caleb," Tommy said softly, trying to sound brave. He looked up at the brass cage. "The bird is singing."

Caleb raised his pickaxe to strike the coal again. 

But as the heavy iron tool reached the apex of its swing, the trilling chirp in the brass cage abruptly stopped. 

Caleb froze. The silence in the tunnel was sudden and absolute, broken only by the distant dripping of water. 

He lowered his pickaxe and turned slowly toward the cage. 

The tiny yellow canary was no longer hopping between the perches. It was lying perfectly still on the bottom of the brass cage, its tiny claws curled inward. 

It hadn't fluttered. It hadn't panicked. It had simply dropped dead. 

Caleb's blood ran cold. 

The canary wasn't there to warn them about the roof collapsing. It was there to warn them about the invisible killers in the deep earth. Blackdamp—carbon dioxide that suffocated silently. Or worse, firedamp. Methane gas. Completely odorless, completely invisible, and highly explosive. 

"Tommy," Caleb whispered, his voice trembling with real terror. "Don't move. Don't speak."

If it was firedamp, even the open flame of their carbide headlamps could be enough to trigger it. 

"What is it?" Tommy asked, his voice shaking. 

"Gas," Caleb hissed. "We have to—"

Seventy feet further down the tunnel, another crew was working a harder section of the seam. A miner there, exhausted and rushing to meet O'Malley's quota, swung his iron pickaxe with everything he had. 

He missed the soft coal and struck a solid vein of iron pyrite embedded in the rock. 

The iron sparked brilliantly in the dark. 

For a fraction of a second, the spark illuminated a thick, shimmering cloud of invisible gas pooling near the roof of the tunnel. 

Then, the world ended in fire. 

The firedamp explosion didn't just burn; it violently expanded. A massive, concussive shockwave of rolling orange flame and pulverized coal dust tore down the tunnel at the speed of sound. 

Caleb didn't even have time to yell. 

The blast wave hit them like a solid brick wall. The deafening roar shattered Caleb's eardrums instantly. He was lifted entirely off his feet, thrown violently backward into the absolute darkness, as the massive oak timbers supporting the roof above them shattered into a million burning splinters.



Chapter Two: The Cave-In

Consciousness returned to Caleb not as a sudden awakening, but as a slow, agonizing crawl out of a suffocating black void. 

His lungs burned. He opened his mouth to gasp for air, but his throat immediately filled with thick, abrasive coal dust. He coughed violently, a wet, rattling spasm that sent sharp bolts of pain shooting through his bruised ribs. 

He pushed his heavy, leather-gloved hands against the ground, forcing himself up onto his knees. 

The darkness was absolute. It was a terrifying, sensory-depriving blackness that pressed against his eyes like a physical weight. The flame of his brass carbide headlamp had been blown out by the concussive shockwave of the firedamp explosion. 

"Tommy," Caleb rasped, his voice sounding thin and dry in the heavy silence. 

There was no answer. The only sound in the tunnel was the terrifying, rhythmic *crr-crack* of settling rock above his head. The explosion had shattered the oak timber supports, and the mountain was reasserting its dominance. 

Caleb fumbled in the deep pockets of his denim overalls, his thick fingers searching frantically for his brass flint striker. He found the small cylinder, held it up to the nozzle of his headlamp, and struck the flint wheel violently against his thumb. 

Sparks showered in the dark. The acetylene gas hissed, caught the spark, and bloomed into a bright, focused yellow flame. 

The narrow beam of light cut through the thick, swirling clouds of suspended coal dust. 

Shaft Four was a disaster. The roof had collapsed in three separate places within a fifty-foot radius, dropping massive, jagged slabs of black shale onto the iron tracks of the coal carts. 

"Tommy!" Caleb yelled louder, sweeping the beam of light across the rubble. 

"Mr. Caleb..." a weak voice groaned from the dust. 

Caleb scrambled over a pile of shattered shale, his boots slipping on the uneven rock. Ten feet away, pinned beneath a massive, splintered section of an oak support beam, was the young breaker boy. 

Tommy was alive, but the heavy timber was lying across his shins, trapping his legs against the iron rail of the cart track. 

"Hold on, kid," Caleb said, dropping to his knees beside the boy. He grabbed the thick oak beam with both hands and pulled, but it was easily four hundred pounds of solid wood. It didn't budge. 

"It hurts," Tommy whimpered, his face streaked with tears that cut clean lines through the thick coal dust on his cheeks. "I think my leg is broke."

"I'll get you out," Caleb said, his voice completely level. Panic in a deep shaft was a death sentence. 

Caleb searched the rubble, his headlamp sweeping the floor. He found his heavy iron pickaxe lying half-buried in the coal dust. He grabbed the tool, jammed the sharp, curved point deep into the dirt directly under the massive oak beam, and used a jagged slab of shale as a fulcrum. 

"When I lift," Caleb instructed, spitting another gob of black phlegm, "you pull yourself backward. Understand?"

Tommy nodded, his teeth gritted. 

Caleb threw his entire weight onto the wooden handle of the pickaxe. 

The heavy iron lever groaned, biting deep into the dirt. The massive oak beam shifted, lifting an inch, then two inches off the boy's legs. 

"Now!" Caleb roared, his black lung screaming as he held the immense weight. 

Tommy scrambled frantically backward, his elbows digging into the dirt, dragging his trapped legs out from under the timber. 

Caleb let the pickaxe handle go. The oak beam crashed heavily back onto the iron track. 

Caleb dropped to his knees, his chest heaving violently, coughing until he tasted blood. When he finally caught his breath, he looked at Tommy's legs. The right shin was bent at an unnatural angle. 

"It's broken," Caleb confirmed grimly. "Can you put any weight on it?"

"I don't think so," Tommy sobbed, clutching his leg. 

"We can't stay here," Caleb said, looking up at the groaning ceiling. "The firedamp burned off, but it left afterdamp behind. Carbon monoxide. If we don't get to the main ventilation shaft, we'll go to sleep and never wake up."

Caleb grabbed Tommy by the back of his canvas jacket and hauled the boy upright, draping Tommy's arm over his broad shoulders to support his weight. 

They turned to look down the tunnel, toward the section of the seam where the other crew had been working—the epicenter of the explosion. 

The tunnel there was completely blocked by a massive, floor-to-ceiling rockfall. The explosion had brought the entire roof down on the other crew. There was no way anyone could have survived the crushing weight of thousands of tons of rock, let alone the searing heat of the methane ignition. 

"They're gone," Tommy whispered, staring at the massive wall of rubble. 

Caleb nodded. He turned to lead Tommy back toward the main shaft. 

*Clack. Scrape.*

Caleb froze. The sound hadn't come from the ceiling. It had come from the wall of rubble blocking the tunnel. 

He swung the beam of his headlamp toward the rockfall. 

A large slab of shale near the top of the pile suddenly shifted, sliding downward with a heavy grinding sound. 

A hand thrust out from the gap in the rocks. 

"Someone's alive!" Tommy gasped, a surge of desperate hope flooding his voice. 

Caleb didn't answer. He watched closely. 

The hand clawed at the rocks, pulling a ruined body out of the rubble. 

It was one of the miners from the other crew. His canvas jacket was completely burned away, exposing horrific, charred flesh that was still actively smoking in the damp air. 

He tumbled down the pile of rubble, landing heavily on the iron cart track. 

"Hey!" Tommy yelled, ignoring the pain in his leg. "Hey, we're over here!"

Caleb clamped a heavy, gloved hand over the boy's mouth. "Quiet," Caleb hissed, his eyes locked on the burned miner. 

The man didn't groan in agony from the horrific burns. He didn't cough from the dust. He simply pushed himself slowly up onto his hands and knees. 

As the burned miner raised his head, the yellow beam of Caleb's headlamp illuminated the horrific truth.
