The man's neck had been completely crushed by the falling rock. His head hung at a terrifying, impossible ninety-degree angle, resting grotesquely against his right shoulder. 

And as the broken head turned toward them, the eyes that reflected the lamplight were a dull, milky white. 

The miner opened his mouth, and a wet, clicking sound echoed down the dark tunnel. 

The Dustbowl sickness had found its way into the deep earth.



Chapter Three: The Pickaxe

The burned miner moved with a terrifying, uncoordinated speed. His legs tangled, his boots dragging heavily against the iron track, but the primal hunger driving him forward compensated for the lack of motor control. 

With his neck snapped, his head bobbed sickeningly against his right shoulder with every step, the milky white eyes locked onto the yellow beam of Caleb's headlamp. 

Tommy stood frozen, leaning heavily on his good leg, his mind entirely unable to process the horrific reality marching toward him. "Mr. Caleb," Tommy stammered, his voice cracking. "What's wrong with him? Why isn't he screaming?"

Caleb didn't answer. He had heard the frantic, whispered stories in the company towns—tales of the dead refusing to stay buried in the Dustbowl states out West. He hadn't believed them. Until now. 

"Stay behind me, kid," Caleb rasped, stepping forward and dropping Tommy's arm. 

Caleb tightened his grip on the thick oak handle of his heavy iron pickaxe. The tool weighed twelve pounds, designed to shatter solid seams of bituminous coal. 

The burned miner lunged, his charred, smoking hands reaching for Caleb's throat. 

Caleb didn't retreat. He pivoted his hips, drawing on twenty years of muscle memory built in the dark, and swung the heavy pickaxe in a brutal, horizontal arc. 

*THWACK.*

The sharp, curved iron point of the pickaxe bypassed the miner's reaching hands and buried itself two inches deep into the side of his skull, just above the ear. 

The concussive force of the blow shattered the temporal bone instantly. The dead miner's eyes rolled backward, and the body dropped heavily onto the iron cart track, the horrific, driving animation severed at the source. 

Caleb stood over the body, breathing heavily, the yellow beam of his headlamp illuminating the dark blood pooling around the shattered skull. 

"Jesus," Tommy whispered, horrified. "You killed him, Mr. Caleb. You killed him."

"He was already dead, Tommy," Caleb said grimly. He planted his boot on the dead man's shoulder and pulled sharply on the oak handle to free his weapon. 

The pickaxe didn't move. The sharp iron point was wedged tightly in the shattered bone. 

Caleb pulled harder, grunting with exertion, but the tool was stuck fast. 

*Scrape. Thud. Clack.*

The sharp, sudden sounds echoed from the massive rockfall seventy feet down the tunnel. 

Caleb snapped his headlamp up, illuminating the wall of rubble. 

The struggle had not gone unnoticed. The violent movement and the smell of fresh blood had awakened the rest of the entombed crew. 

Two more figures were dragging themselves out of the twisted wreckage and shattered shale. 

The first miner was missing his left arm entirely, the limb torn off by the violent concussive force of the firedamp explosion. A ragged, pulsing stump of bone and muscle swung loosely at his shoulder. 

The second miner was even worse. A massive slab of falling rock had crushed his face completely flat, driving his nose and jaw backward into his skull. He had no eyes left, only dark, bloody sockets, but he turned his ruined face directly toward the sound of Caleb's heavy breathing. 

They began to drag their ruined bodies over the rubble, moving toward the two living men. 

Caleb gave the pickaxe one final, desperate yank. The oak handle groaned, but the iron point remained firmly lodged in the skull. 

He didn't have time. 

Caleb abandoned his primary tool. He dropped to his knees and frantically searched the debris covering the floor. He found a massive, jagged slab of heavy black shale, easily thirty pounds of solid rock. He picked it up with both hands. 

"Tommy, we have to run!" Caleb yelled, backing away from the approaching dead. "The main shaft elevator is half a mile back!"

"I can't!" Tommy cried, gripping his broken shin. "My leg, Mr. Caleb!"

Tommy tried to hop backward on his good leg, but his boot slipped on a patch of wet coal dust. He collapsed heavily onto the iron track, screaming as a sharp bolt of agony shot through his shattered tibia. 

Caleb looked at the boy, and then at the narrow, dark tunnel. It was a chaotic obstacle course of fallen timber, twisted cart tracks, and sharp rubble. There was absolutely no way he could carry the boy over his shoulder and fight off the infected at the same time. He needed both hands to swing the heavy shale. 

The two dead miners stumbled off the rubble pile and onto the clear track, closing the distance rapidly. 

Caleb made a brutal, pragmatic calculation. 

He dropped the heavy slab of shale. He grabbed Tommy by the thick canvas collar of his jacket and began dragging the screaming boy backward over the rough, debris-strewn floor. 

"I'm sorry, kid," Caleb grunted, his boots slipping in the mud as he hauled the dead weight. "But this is going to hurt."

The jagged rocks tore at Tommy's clothes and bruised his back, but Caleb didn't stop. He dragged the boy into the absolute darkness of the tunnel, the two infected miners following relentlessly behind them, their wet, dragging footsteps echoing off the coal walls.



Chapter Four: The Ventilation Door

The retreat down the ruined shaft was an agonizing, disjointed nightmare. 

Caleb hauled Tommy backward, his leather boots sliding on the wet, uneven coal floor. Every time he pulled, a hot knife of pain twisted in his bruised ribs, and the deep, wet rattle in his chest grew louder. He was coughing constantly now, no longer spitting just coal dust, but thick, bright red arterial blood. 

Tommy's screams bounced sharply off the narrow walls. The jagged pieces of shattered shale tore through the canvas back of his jacket, and his broken shin bounced sickeningly over the iron cart tracks. 

Fifty feet behind them, the yellow beam of Caleb's headlamp caught the relentless pursuit. 

The miner missing an arm was moving surprisingly fast, his remaining hand reaching out, his jaw snapping rhythmically. The miner with the crushed face was slower, stumbling blindly into the timber supports, but the dark, bloody ruin of his face always reoriented toward the sound of Tommy's screams. 

"Mr. Caleb, I can't!" Tommy sobbed, his hands clutching desperately at the dirt as he was dragged. "Leave me! Just leave me!"

"Shut up and keep your head up," Caleb wheezed, his lungs burning with a terrifying, suffocating heat. 

The yellow beam cut through the dust ahead, illuminating a solid wall of heavy oak planks. 

It was a primary ventilation door. In the deep mines, these massive wooden barricades were used to control and direct the flow of fresh air pumped down from the surface. The door was framed with thick iron hinges and secured by a massive, drop-bar iron latch. 

Caleb dug his boots into the mud and hauled Tommy over the wooden threshold, dragging the boy ten feet past the heavy frame. 

Caleb dropped the collar. He turned and threw his entire body weight against the heavy oak door. The rusty iron hinges shrieked in protest, but the massive wooden barricade swung shut. 

*SLAM.*

Caleb grabbed the heavy iron drop-bar and slammed it down into the thick iron brackets on the door frame just as a heavy, wet thud echoed from the other side of the wood. 

The dead had arrived. 

Another thud, followed by the frantic, scraping sound of broken fingernails tearing wildly at the thick oak planks. They were unthinking, unfeeling. They would simply tear at the wood until their fingers wore down to the bone, and then they would tear with the bone. 

Caleb slumped against the heavy door, sliding down the rough wood until he hit the wet floor. 

The coughing fit took him immediately. It was violent and prolonged. He bent double, his chest heaving, his mouth filling with hot copper. He hacked violently, spitting a massive, dark puddle of blood onto the coal dust between his boots. 

The black lung had been killing him slowly for two decades. The firedamp explosion and the brutal physical exertion of dragging the boy had accelerated the execution. He was drowning in his own blood. 

"Mr. Caleb?" Tommy whispered, pushing himself up on his elbows. The boy's face was pale beneath the soot. 

"I'm... alright," Caleb lied, his voice barely a wet whisper. 

He raised his headlamp, sweeping the beam across the small section of tunnel behind the ventilation door. This area was designated as a secondary refuge pocket. Carved into the coal face was a shallow shelf holding a heavy wooden supply crate. 

Caleb pushed himself up, leaning heavily against the wall, and stumbled to the crate. He threw open the lid. 

Inside, there was a heavy, four-foot iron pry bar, a spool of thick copper detonation wire, an ancient plunger-style blasting box, and a small, heavily padded wooden box. 

Caleb opened the padded box. Nestled inside the sawdust were three sticks of commercial mining dynamite. They were old, sweating slightly with nitroglycerin, but they were viable. They were kept in the refuge pockets for extreme emergencies—specifically, for blasting through cave-ins if a crew was trapped. 

Caleb looked at the dynamite, and then back at the heavy oak door. The wood was thick, but the scraping on the other side was relentless. It wouldn't hold them forever. 

He looked at Tommy, shivering on the floor, his leg broken and useless. 

Then, Caleb looked down at his own trembling, bloody hands. 

He knew exactly what was happening inside his chest. The alveoli in his lungs were rupturing. He was bleeding out internally. He had maybe twenty minutes before he suffocated. 

And Caleb knew the rules of the new world. It didn't matter how you died. If your brain was intact, you came back. 

If he died in this narrow tunnel, he would reanimate. And the first thing Zombie-Caleb would do was tear young Tommy apart. 

Caleb couldn't carry the boy to the main shaft. He couldn't fight off the dead on the other side of the door. And he couldn't survive his own failing lungs. 

But he could clear the path. 

Caleb reached into the crate and pulled out the heavy iron pry bar and the three sweating sticks of dynamite.



Chapter Five: The Fuse

The heavy scraping on the other side of the oak door grew more frantic. The dead miners didn't tire, and they didn't feel the splinters driving deep under their fingernails. 

Caleb sat on the coal-dusted floor, his back resting against the jagged wall of the tunnel. His breathing was a horrific, bubbling sound, wet and heavy with arterial blood. 

He looked at the three sticks of dynamite resting in his lap, and then at young Tommy, who was curled into a ball on the floor, clutching his broken leg. 

"Listen to me, Tommy," Caleb wheezed, the effort of speaking costing him dearly. "You have to listen close."

Tommy looked up, his face a mask of soot and terror. "Mr. Caleb, you're bleeding. A lot."

"I know," Caleb said, spitting another mouthful of red onto the black rock. "The black lung finally caught me. I'm not making it out of this hole."

"Don't say that!" Tommy cried. "We'll get to the main shaft! We'll ring for the cage!"

"I'm not making it to the cage, kid," Caleb said firmly, holding up one of the sweating sticks of dynamite. "And if I die here, you know what happens. I saw the look in your eyes when that burned man stood up with a broken neck. You know the stories."

Tommy trembled violently, staring at Caleb's bloody mouth. "The sickness."

"The sickness," Caleb confirmed. "It doesn't care how you die. It only cares that your brain is intact. If my heart stops, I'll wake up hungry. And you can't run."

Caleb reached over and grabbed the heavy wooden plunger of the ancient blasting box. He connected the thick copper wires from the box to the blasting caps embedded in the dynamite sticks. 

"I'm going to wire this door," Caleb rasped. "When those sticks blow, they'll vaporize the dead on the other side." He paused, looking Tommy dead in the eye. "And they'll vaporize me. My brain will be gone. I won't come back."

"No," Tommy sobbed, shaking his head frantically. "I can't!"

"You can, and you will!" Caleb roared, the sudden burst of anger triggering another violent, bubbling cough. When he recovered, he grabbed the spool of copper detonation wire and shoved it into Tommy's trembling hands. 

"Take the box," Caleb ordered, his voice dropping to a terrifying, deadly serious whisper. "Take the box and drag yourself down this tunnel. Go as far as the wire will let you. Around the first bend in the rock."

Tommy clutched the heavy wooden blasting box to his chest, the tears carving fresh tracks through the coal dust. 

"If you don't blow me up, Tommy," Caleb said, his eyes cold and hard, "I will die, I will stand back up, and I will eat you alive. Do you understand?"

The sheer horror of the promise finally broke through the boy's panic. Tommy nodded slowly. 

"Good," Caleb wheezed. "Now go. Fast."

Tommy turned away. Dragging his useless, broken leg behind him, the boy began to claw his way down the pitch-black tunnel, the heavy copper wire unspooling rapidly from his hands. 

Caleb watched the yellow beam of Tommy's headlamp fade into the darkness. 

When the boy was gone, Caleb turned back to the heavy oak door. He used a piece of wire to lash the three sticks of dynamite directly to the center of the wood, right where the scraping was the loudest. 

He didn't move away. 

Caleb sat directly in front of the door, leaning his head back against the heavy iron hinges. The dead miners were tearing at the wood inches from his ear. 

He didn't feel fear. He felt a profound, heavy exhaustion. Twenty years in the dark. It was time to rest. 

"I'm at the end of the wire, Mr. Caleb!" Tommy's voice echoed faintly from down the tunnel. 

Caleb closed his eyes. His lungs were full. He couldn't draw another breath even if he tried. 

"Do it, kid," Caleb whispered into the dark. 

He didn't know if Tommy heard him. 

A moment later, the absolute darkness of the tunnel was violently erased by a blinding, apocalyptic flash of pure white light.



Chapter Six: The Blind Crawl

The concussive shockwave from the commercial dynamite was exponentially more powerful than the firedamp explosion. The confined space of the narrow tunnel focused the blast wave, sending a solid wall of displaced air and pulverized rock tearing around the bend. 

Tommy was thrown violently backward, the heavy wooden blasting box ripped from his hands. He struck the coal face hard, his brass carbide headlamp smashing against the unyielding rock. 

The yellow flame was instantly extinguished, the glass lens shattered, the brass nozzle bent beyond repair. 

Then, there was only the ringing in his ears and the absolute, suffocating darkness. 

Tommy lay on the wet floor for a long time, the thick dust settling over his body like a burial shroud. He couldn't see his hand an inch in front of his face. The blackness was so profound it felt heavy, a physical pressure pushing against his open eyes. 

"Mr. Caleb?" Tommy whispered. 

He knew the veteran miner was dead. He had felt the massive vibration of the explosion. He had pushed the plunger. But the terror of the absolute dark made him crave the sound of another human voice, even the rough, bloody wheeze of a dying man. 

There was no answer. Only the sharp, high-pitched ringing in his ears, slowly giving way to the terrifying ambient sounds of the deep earth. 

Water dripped somewhere nearby. A wooden timber groaned under the immense weight of the mountain. 

Tommy pushed himself up onto his elbows, biting his lip to stifle a scream as the jagged ends of his broken tibia ground together. 

He was alone. He was crippled. And he was completely blind. 

He had to get to the main shaft elevator. If he stayed here, he would bleed to death, starve, or suffocate when the afterdamp finally settled into this section of the tunnel. 

He reached out his right hand, sweeping his fingers through the wet mud until he felt the cold, hard ridge of the iron cart track. That was his lifeline. The tracks led to the main shaft. 

Tommy began to drag himself forward, using his elbows and his good leg, keeping his right hand firmly clamped onto the iron rail. 

The crawl was pure agony. Every movement sent a fresh wave of blinding pain through his shattered shin. The jagged chunks of loose shale on the floor tore at the canvas of his jacket and scraped his forearms raw. 

But the physical pain was nothing compared to the psychological horror of the dark. 

The human mind, starved of visual input, begins to invent its own terrors. Every drip of water sounded like a snapping jaw. Every groan of the timber sounded like a heavy boot dragging across the stone. Tommy kept waiting to feel cold, dead fingers wrap around his ankle. He kept waiting to feel teeth sink into his calf. 

He didn't know if the dynamite had actually killed the two infected miners on the other side of the door. He only knew that Caleb had sacrificed himself to try. 

After what felt like hours of agonizing crawling, Tommy's hand traced the iron rail as it suddenly curved sharply to the left. 

He paused, sweeping his other hand out into the dark. He felt a second iron rail branching off to the right. 

He had reached a junction. The tunnel split in two different directions. 

Panic surged in Tommy's chest, threatening to overwhelm him. In the pitch black, he had no idea which set of tracks led to the main shaft elevator, and which set led deeper into the abandoned, unstable workings of the mine. If he chose wrong, he would crawl into a dead end and die in the dark. 

He lay in the mud, crying silently, the despair finally breaking him. 

Then, a memory surfaced through the terror. A rough, dismissive piece of advice Caleb had given him on his very first day in the deep shafts. 

*"You get lost in the dark, kid, you don't guess. You feel the floor. Always follow the water flow. The main shaft sumps are at the lowest point of the mine. Water always runs to the shaft."*

Tommy stopped crying. He took his hand off the iron rail and pressed both of his bare palms flat against the cold, wet mud of the tunnel floor. 

He felt a tiny, steady trickle of acidic mine water moving against his skin. He closed his eyes, concentrating all his remaining focus on the sensation in his fingertips. 

The water was flowing slightly to the right. 

Tommy grabbed the right-hand rail and began to drag himself forward again, his heart pounding a frantic rhythm against his bruised ribs. 

He pulled himself ten feet down the right-hand tunnel. 

Then, his outstretched hand touched something in the dark. 

It wasn't a jagged rock. It wasn't an iron rail. 

It was smooth, heavy leather. The thick toe of a miner's boot. 

Tommy froze, his breath catching in his throat. The boot wasn't his. He was wearing cheap, canvas work boots. This was expensive, heavy leather. Like Foreman O'Malley's boots. 

Before Tommy could pull his hand away, a heavy, cold hand shot out of the pitch-black void and clamped down violently around his wrist.



Chapter Seven: The Foreman

Tommy screamed, a raw, high-pitched sound of absolute terror that echoed sharply through the dark tunnel. He thrashed wildly, trying to kick out with his broken leg, desperate to break the cold, iron grip on his wrist. 

"Shut your damn mouth, kid!" a harsh, familiar voice hissed from the blackness. 

Tommy froze. The voice wasn't wet or clicking. It was gruff, demanding, and human. 

A hand moved in the dark, and a faint, yellow light bloomed abruptly a few feet away. 

It was Foreman O'Malley. The company man was sitting heavily against the coal wall, his expensive leather boots caked in acidic mud. He had covered the glass lens of his carbide headlamp with his gloved hand to hide the light, only moving it away now to glare at the screaming boy. 

O'Malley looked terrible. The concussive shockwave of the firedamp explosion had thrown him hard against the rock. His hard hat was gone, and a deep, bleeding gash ran down the side of his scalp. His face was pale beneath the soot, his eyes wide and panicked. 

"O'Malley," Tommy gasped, the relief washing over him so fast it made him dizzy. "You're alive."

"No thanks to Caleb," O'Malley spat, wincing as he wiped blood from his forehead. "Where is that old fool?"

"He's dead," Tommy said, his voice dropping to a whisper. "He blew himself up to stop... to stop the dead ones."

O'Malley stared at the boy in the dim yellow light. "The dead ones. You saw them too."

"They're real," Tommy confirmed, shivering violently. "The sickness from out West."

O'Malley nodded slowly, a profound, cowardly terror settling into his features. "I saw them. When the explosion hit, the rockfall crushed three men from the secondary drift. I tried to dig one out. He... he bit my glove." O'Malley held up his heavy leather work glove. Two fingers had been torn completely off by powerful teeth. 

"The main shaft elevator is just ahead, Mr. O'Malley," Tommy said, pointing down the tunnel. "We have to go. Caleb blew the tunnel behind us, but we can't stay here. The afterdamp..."

"We can't go to the main shaft," O'Malley interrupted, his voice trembling. 

"Why not?" Tommy asked, confused. 

O'Malley reached over and grabbed the collar of Tommy's canvas jacket, hauling the boy roughly forward until they were crouching side-by-side at the edge of the tunnel junction. 

O'Malley pointed his dim headlamp down the long, straight corridor leading to the massive iron cage of the main shaft elevator. 

"Look," O'Malley whispered. 

At the end of the tunnel, about fifty yards away, was the large, open cavern of the main staging area. The massive iron elevator cage, capable of hauling fifty men to the surface at once, was currently parked at the bottom of the shaft. The heavy iron gate was closed. Inside the cage, a single, terrified cage operator was frantically cranking the communication bell, begging the surface winchman to pull him up. 

But the operator wasn't alone in the staging area. 

Surrounding the heavy iron cage were at least thirty coal miners. 

"The explosion," O'Malley breathed, his voice laced with horror. "The shockwave traveled down the central ventilation shafts. It shattered the timber supports in Drifts Two, Three, and Five. It brought the roofs down on all of them."

Tommy watched the men surrounding the cage. They weren't yelling for the operator to open the gate. They weren't demanding to be taken to the surface. 

They were just standing there, swaying slightly in the dark. 

Many of them were horrific casualties of the roof collapses. Some were missing limbs. Some had their ribcages entirely crushed. Others had simply suffocated in the silent, invisible cloud of afterdamp that followed the blast. 

But their brains were intact. 

As Tommy and O'Malley watched in horrified silence, one of the dead miners, a massive Polish immigrant whose face had been sheared off by falling shale, stepped up to the heavy iron grating of the elevator cage. 

He didn't ask to be let in. He simply reached his ruined hands through the iron bars, trying to grab the terrified operator cowering in the center of the cage. 

"They're trapped down here with us," O'Malley whispered. "The whole damn shift."

Tommy realized the horrific truth. Caleb's sacrifice had saved him from the two miners behind them, but the firedamp explosion had doomed the entire mine. 

They were trapped in the deepest, darkest hole in West Virginia, caught between a collapsed tunnel filling with poisonous gas, and a staging area swarming with thirty zombified miners.



Chapter Eight: The Cage

The air in the tunnel junction was growing heavy, the invisible afterdamp settling low to the floor. Tommy felt a sudden wave of dizziness wash over him. 

"We can't stay here," Tommy whispered, his voice trembling. "The gas is coming."

Foreman O'Malley didn't answer. He was staring intensely at the staging area, his mind racing. He was a man who had built his entire career on exploiting the labor of others to ensure his own survival and prosperity. The current situation was horrific, but the brutal arithmetic remained the same. 

There were thirty dead men standing between him and the elevator cage. 

But dead men were stupid. They were driven entirely by instinct and stimuli. Sound and blood. 

O'Malley looked down at the teenage breaker boy lying in the mud beside him. 

"You're right, kid," O'Malley said, his voice terrifyingly calm. "We can't stay here."

Before Tommy could react, O'Malley dropped his heavy leather glove directly onto Tommy's shattered shin. 

He didn't just press down. O'Malley gripped the broken bone through the canvas trousers and violently twisted his wrist. 

The pain was absolute, blinding agony. 

Tommy shrieked, a raw, piercing scream that tore from his throat and echoed deafeningly down the long, straight tunnel leading to the staging area. The boy thrashed wildly in the mud, clutching his leg, screaming until his lungs burned. 

O'Malley stood up instantly, pressing himself flat against the coal wall in the shadows. 

The brutal tactic worked perfectly. 

At the main shaft, all thirty of the infected miners snapped their ruined heads toward the source of the screaming. Their unthinking hunger was immediately redirected. They abandoned the heavy iron grating of the elevator cage and began to stumble, crawl, and drag themselves toward the dark mouth of the tunnel where Tommy was writhing in the mud. 

"Help me!" Tommy sobbed, reaching a hand out in the dark, completely unaware of the foreman's betrayal. 

O'Malley didn't help him. 

As the vanguard of the horde entered the tunnel, their milky white eyes locked onto the screaming boy on the floor, O'Malley pushed off the wall. 

He sprinted silently past the distracted zombies, his expensive leather boots flying over the iron cart tracks. He was moving with the desperate, adrenaline-fueled speed of a terrified coward. 

He broke clear of the tunnel and burst into the massive, open staging area. 

The massive iron elevator cage was directly in front of him. 

"Open the gate!" O'Malley roared, grabbing the thick iron bars of the cage and shaking them violently. "Open the damn gate!"

Inside the cage, the terrified operator jumped, startled by the sudden noise. He recognized the foreman's expensive boots and relatively clean clothes. 

"Mr. O'Malley!" the operator cried, fumbling frantically with the heavy iron drop-latch that secured the sliding gate. His hands were shaking so badly he couldn't lift the heavy bar out of the bracket. 

"Hurry up, you idiot!" O'Malley screamed, looking back over his shoulder. 

His shouting had ruined his own distraction. 

The horde heard his voice. The thirty dead miners stopped moving toward Tommy's fading whimpers. They turned around in the tunnel, their dead eyes locking onto the foreman standing directly under the bright electric lights of the staging area. 

They surged backward out of the tunnel, a terrifying, silent wave of ruined flesh and black coal dust. 

"Open it!" O'Malley shrieked, his voice cracking with pure panic. 

The operator finally managed to heave the heavy iron drop-latch upward. He grabbed the iron handle and hauled the heavy sliding gate open, leaving a two-foot gap. 

"Get in!" the operator yelled. 

O'Malley lunged for the opening. 

But he was a second too late. 

The vanguard of the horde hit him like a collapsing rock face. Five dead miners crashed into O'Malley's back simultaneously, driving him violently forward. 

O'Malley screamed as he was slammed chest-first into the heavy iron grating of the cage. His hands shot through the bars, desperately reaching for the operator inside. 

"Pull me in!" O'Malley begged, blood spraying from his mouth as the iron bars crushed his ribs. 

The operator grabbed O'Malley's forearms and pulled with all his might. 

But the horde was too heavy. A dead miner, his face burned beyond recognition, sank his teeth deep into the back of O'Malley's neck. Another grabbed the foreman's leather boots and yanked backward. 

O'Malley was torn away from the iron grating, his fingers slipping from the operator's grasp. He fell backward into the surging mass of the dead, his screams quickly muffled by the wet sound of tearing flesh. 

The operator stumbled backward in the cage, horrified. 

He lunged forward to grab the heavy iron handle and slam the sliding gate shut. 

But the horde was already pressing into the gap. 

As the heavy iron gate slammed shut and the drop-latch fell into place, three dead miners managed to squeeze their ruined bodies through the opening, tumbling heavily onto the iron floorplates directly inside the confined space of the elevator cage. 

The operator backed away slowly, his eyes wide. He was trapped in a ten-by-ten iron box with three of the walking dead.



Chapter Nine: The Signal

The massive iron elevator cage was designed to haul fifty tired men to the surface after a ten-hour shift in the deep earth. It was a ten-by-ten foot box of riveted steel and heavy iron grating. 

Right now, it was a slaughterhouse. 

The three dead miners who had squeezed through the gap in the gate didn't hesitate. They lunged at the terrified cage operator, their hands clawing blindly, their jaws snapping wetly in the bright glare of the staging area's electric lights. 

The operator backed into the control console, his hands frantically searching the iron shelf behind him. His fingers wrapped around the heavy, cold steel of a massive crescent wrench used for tightening the cage's suspension bolts. 

He swung the heavy tool with both hands. 

The steel wrench struck the first dead miner directly in the temple. The concussive force shattered the bone, driving the sharp steel deep into the frontal lobe. The miner's eyes rolled backward, and he dropped heavily to the iron floorplates, his ruined brain instantly short-circuiting. 

The operator pulled the wrench free and swung again, a wild, desperate arc that caught the second zombie in the collarbone, knocking him backward against the iron grating. 

But the third infected miner, a massive man whose canvas jacket was slick with O'Malley's fresh blood, simply walked through the swinging tool. He absorbed a glancing blow to the shoulder and grabbed the operator by the throat, driving him violently back against the control console. 

The operator gasped, his airway instantly crushed. 

He didn't try to pry the dead hands away. He knew it was over. He had felt the terrible, inhuman strength of the dead man's grip. 

With his last ounce of conscious thought, the operator reached over the console and grabbed the thick, braided steel signal cable that ran vertically up the entire length of the thousand-foot shaft. 

He pulled the heavy cable down violently. Three sharp tugs. 

*Clang. Clang. Clang.*

On the surface, a thousand feet above the nightmare, the massive brass bell in the winch house rang out the emergency ascent signal. 

Instantly, the massive, steam-powered winch engaged with a deafening roar. 

Deep in the staging area, the two-inch-thick braided steel cables supporting the cage snapped taut. The heavy iron box groaned violently, tore itself free from the muddy floor, and began to rapidly ascend the dark, vertical shaft. 

The sudden, violent lurch threw the massive zombie off balance. The operator collapsed to the floor, gasping for air, but the two remaining dead miners immediately swarmed him, tearing into his canvas jacket with their teeth. 

Outside the rising cage, the horde reacted instantly. 

The thirty dead miners who had been tearing Foreman O'Malley apart turned their attention to the massive iron box rising into the ceiling. They didn't understand the machinery, but they understood that their prey was escaping. 

They swarmed the shaft opening, their ruined hands grabbing blindly at the thick, greased steel cables and the heavy iron grid on the underside of the rising cage. Several of them were lifted a few feet into the air before their slick fingers lost their grip, sending them tumbling back down onto the muddy floor. 

Tommy lay in the dark tunnel, his heart hammering against his ribs. He had stopped screaming the moment the horde turned its attention to the cage. 

He watched the massive iron box rise into the shaft, the operator's horrific screams echoing downward over the roar of the steam winch. 

Tommy knew this was his only chance. The horde was entirely focused on the ascending cage. 

He grabbed the iron rail and began to drag himself out of the tunnel and into the bright glare of the staging area, his broken leg trailing uselessly behind him in the acidic mud. 

He reached the edge of the staging area just as the bottom of the cage cleared the ceiling of the cavern. 

The moment the cage ascended past the staging level, a heavy, automated safety mechanism triggered. To prevent miners from accidentally falling into the thousand-foot vertical drop when the cage was at the surface, two massive, solid iron doors slammed shut horizontally over the mouth of the shaft. 

*CLANG.*

The heavy doors sealed the vertical shaft completely, creating a solid iron platform in the center of the staging area. 

Tommy dragged himself off the muddy floor and onto the cold, solid iron of the closed safety doors. He was out of the tunnel, but he was trapped in the open, directly under the bright electric lights. 

He looked around the staging area. 

The horde was slowly turning away from the shaft ceiling. They were covered in O'Malley's blood and the black coal dust. 

And now, all twenty-five remaining dead miners were staring directly at the sixteen-year-old boy lying on the iron doors in the center of the room.



Chapter Ten: The Cage Descends

Tommy lay flat on his back on the heavy iron safety doors, his chest heaving, his broken leg throbbing with a sickening, rhythmic pulse. The bright electric lights of the staging area beat down on him, illuminating the horror closing in from every side. 

The twenty-five infected miners didn't rush. They didn't need to. Their prey was crippled and trapped in the center of the room. 

They began to stumble out of the acidic mud and onto the solid iron platform. Their heavy boots clanked loudly against the metal. The massive Polish miner without a face was leading the pack, his bloody, ruined jaw snapping in anticipation. 

Tommy didn't scream. He didn't try to crawl backward. There was nowhere left to go. The walls of the cavern were solid rock, and the tunnel behind him was filled with poisonous gas and the pulverized remains of Caleb's sacrifice. 

He closed his eyes. He thought of his mother in the company town above. He hoped they never opened the shaft again. He hoped she never found out what happened in the dark. 

He felt a cold, stiff hand clamp onto the canvas collar of his jacket. 

Another hand, this one slick with blood, grabbed his broken shin. Tommy gasped in pain, but he kept his eyes tightly shut, waiting for the teeth. 

Suddenly, the heavy iron doors vibrated violently beneath his back. 

Tommy's eyes snapped open. 

A deafening, mechanical roar echoed down the massive vertical shaft from the surface a thousand feet above. The massive steam winch had re-engaged. The massive steel gears were grinding in reverse. 

The surface winchman, ignorant of the massacre below, was sending the massive elevator cage back down to the staging area. 

"No," Tommy whispered, the vibration rattling his teeth. 

The heavy iron doors didn't slide open. They were designed to swing downward on massive steel hinges, pushed open by the tremendous weight of the descending cage as it passed through the threshold. 

The vibration intensified into a terrifying rumble. 

The twenty-five dead miners didn't react to the noise or the vibration. They were entirely focused on the warm flesh beneath them. Three of them lunged simultaneously, their ruined jaws opening wide. 

But before their teeth could reach Tommy's throat, the massive iron underside of the descending elevator cage slammed into the top of the safety doors. 

The solid iron floor vanished instantly. 

The heavy safety doors swung violently downward on their hinges, dropping completely vertical. 

The sudden loss of gravity was terrifying. Tommy's stomach lurched as he fell backward into the dark abyss of the main shaft. The twenty-five infected miners tumbled down with him, a chaotic, thrashing mass of ruined limbs and snapping jaws. 

But Tommy didn't fall a thousand feet. He didn't even fall twenty feet. 

He fell ten feet, crashing heavily onto the thick, riveted iron roof of the descending elevator cage. 

The impact was brutal. The breath was driven violently from Tommy's lungs, and his head cracked painfully against a massive steel rivet. 

But he wasn't alone on the roof. 

The entire horde rained down around him. Twenty-five heavy bodies crashed onto the iron roof of the cage in a horrific shower of shattering bone and tearing flesh. The impact broke ribs, snapped arms, and crushed pelvises. 

But the Romero rule was absolute. The ten-foot fall wasn't enough to completely destroy their brains. 

Tommy gasped for air, his vision swimming. The cage was plunging rapidly down the pitch-black shaft, descending far past the staging area, heading directly for the deep, flooded sumps at the very bottom of the mine. 

The wind screamed past his ears in the absolute darkness. 

Tommy felt a heavy weight shift on top of him. 

The dead miners were still moving. Their legs were shattered, their spines broken, but they were dragging themselves across the iron roof of the plunging cage, their unthinking hunger entirely undiminished by the catastrophic fall. 

Cold fingers dug into Tommy's throat. A heavy body pinned his broken leg to the iron. 

Below him, through the thick iron grating of the cage roof, Tommy could hear the two zombies who had killed the operator thrashing wildly against the walls. 

Above him, the twenty-five ruined bodies of the horde were swarming over him in the rushing wind. 

There was no light. There was no rescue. There was only the terrifying rush of the descending cage and the wet, tearing sounds of the dead in the dark. 

Tommy screamed as the teeth finally found his neck, his horrific cry swallowed entirely by the roaring darkness of the deepest hole in West Virginia.



Story Nine: The Lighthouse

Chapter One: The Wreck of the Banshee

The wind off the coast of Maine didn't howl; it shrieked. It was a physical force, a wall of freezing salt spray and driving rain that hammered relentlessly against the thick granite blocks of the Blackwood Shoals Lighthouse. 

It was November 1935, and the worst Nor'easter of the decade had swallowed the Atlantic in absolute, churning darkness. 

At the very top of the hundred-foot tower, in the glass-enclosed lantern room, Arthur was terrified. The young assistant keeper was clinging tightly to the brass railing that circled the massive, two-ton Fresnel lens. The entire stone tower was vibrating violently with every concussive impact of the rogue waves crashing against the rocks below. 

"The tower's swaying, Silas!" Arthur yelled over the roar of the storm, his voice cracking with panic. 

Silas, the veteran principal keeper, sat heavily in a canvas chair by the iron stove, his heavy woolen sweater smelling strongly of damp wool and cheap rum. He didn't look at the trembling boy. He kept his steely gray eyes fixed on the driving rain lashing against the thick storm panes. 

"She's built to sway, boy," Silas grunted, taking a long pull from a silver flask. "If she didn't sway, the granite would snap like a dry twig. Keep the clockwork wound. The lens has to turn. It's the only thing keeping the sailors off the reef."

Arthur swallowed hard, wiping the condensation from the brass gears. The massive clockwork mechanism that rotated the heavy glass lens required constant winding. It was exhausting, tedious work, but Silas was right. In this storm, the rhythmic pulse of the Blackwood light was the only beacon of survival in a fifty-mile radius. 

Silas leaned forward suddenly, his eyes narrowing. 

"What is it?" Arthur asked, pausing his winding. 

"Distress flare," Silas said, standing up and pointing a calloused finger into the darkness. "Three miles out. South-southeast."

Arthur squinted through the rain-streaked glass. For a fraction of a second, he saw it. A faint, desperate streak of red light burning against the black sky. 

"They're too close," Arthur breathed, realizing the horrifying geometry of the flare's position. "They're inside the outer shoal."

"They're already dead," Silas said grimly, reaching for his flask again. "No ship can hold anchor in this swell. And the current is pulling them straight onto the Devil's Teeth."

The Devil's Teeth was the jagged, submerged granite reef that ringed the lighthouse island. 

"We have to do something!" Arthur yelled, abandoning the clockwork. 

"We are doing something," Silas snapped, gesturing to the massive rotating lens. "We're keeping the light on. That's our job. We can't launch the dory in this surf. It would be smashed to splinters before we cleared the slipway."

Ten minutes later, the inevitable tragedy occurred. 

It wasn't a visual spectacle; the storm was too thick. It was a sound. A horrific, grinding screech of tearing iron and shattering wood that somehow pierced through the deafening roar of the Nor'easter. 

A heavy cargo steamer had struck the Devil's Teeth directly below the lighthouse. 

A moment later, a massive, dull explosion rattled the storm panes of the lantern room. 

"Boiler blew," Silas said softly, the harshness fading from his voice, replaced by the grim resignation of a man who had seen the ocean claim too many lives. "The cold water hit the superheated iron. She broke her back on the rocks."

Arthur didn't listen to the rest of the veteran's grim assessment. The young assistant grabbed a heavy brass kerosene lantern from the wall bracket, snatched his yellow oilskin slicker, and bolted for the heavy iron hatch that led to the spiraling descent of the tower. 

"Arthur!" Silas yelled, but the boy was already gone. 

"Stupid, soft-hearted fool," Silas cursed, jamming the flask into his pocket and grabbing his own oilskins. 

Arthur ran down the hundred and twenty cast-iron steps of the spiral staircase, his boots clanging loudly against the metal. The air inside the tower grew colder and damper with every revolution. 

He reached the ground floor, a circular stone room that served as the primary storage area. The heavy, iron-reinforced oak door that led outside to the rocky ledge was shuddering violently under the assault of the wind. 

Arthur hauled the heavy iron drop-latch up and shoved his shoulder against the oak. 

The wind caught the door and tore it out of his hands, slamming it violently back against the exterior granite wall. The storm rushed into the base of the tower, instantly extinguishing Arthur's lantern and soaking him to the bone. 

He stepped out onto the jagged, slippery rock ledge. 

The scene below was chaotic. The massive, broken hull of the steamer *Banshee* was pinned against the reef just fifty yards away, completely at the mercy of the crashing waves. The surf was churning with shattered crates, splintered wood, and thick, black oil. 

And bodies. 

The freezing Atlantic was throwing the drowned crew of the *Banshee* directly onto the rocky ledge at the base of the lighthouse. 

Arthur spotted a figure lying face down in the shallow surf, tangled in a massive piece of heavy rigging. 

"Hey!" Arthur yelled, slipping on the wet granite as he scrambled down toward the water's edge. 

He reached the sailor. The man was wearing a heavy woolen peacoat. Arthur grabbed his shoulder and hauled him over onto his back. 

The sailor was clearly dead. His chest cavity was entirely crushed, completely caved in by a massive impact with the rocks. His face was pale and bloated with seawater. 

"I'm sorry," Arthur whispered, the freezing rain mixing with the tears on his face. He reached out to check the man's neck for a pulse, hoping against hope for a miracle. 

As Arthur's warm fingers brushed the cold, wet skin of the sailor's neck, the miracle occurred. 

But it wasn't a divine intervention. It was a biological nightmare born in the dust of the Midwest, carried across the continent by desperate men, and finally taking root in the freezing saltwater of the Atlantic. 

The sailor's chest didn't rise. He didn't draw a breath. 

But his eyes snapped violently open. 

They weren't the blue or brown of a living man. They were a dull, vacant, milky white. 

Before Arthur could pull his hand away, the dead sailor's ruined jaw snapped open, and his cold, stiff fingers closed like a vise around Arthur's wrist.



Chapter Two: The Iron Door

The freezing Atlantic surf crashed heavily over the rocky ledge, completely submerging Arthur and the dead sailor. The saltwater stung Arthur's eyes, blinding him as the terrible, crushing grip on his wrist tightened. 

The young assistant keeper thrashed wildly, his rubber boots slipping uselessly on the thick layer of green algae coating the granite. 

The drowned sailor didn't slip. He didn't gasp for air when the waves rolled over them. His ruined jaw simply snapped rhythmically, seeking warm flesh in the icy water. With a terrifying surge of inhuman strength, the dead man pulled Arthur down, pinning him against the jagged rocks. 

Arthur screamed, the sound instantly swallowed by the roaring wind. He brought his free boot up and kicked violently, driving his heavy heel directly into the sailor's already crushed chest cavity. 

The ribcage gave way completely with a sickening crunch. The impact dislodged the dead man's grip just enough for Arthur to tear his wrist free. 

Arthur scrambled backward on his hands and knees, scrambling up the steep incline of the ledge away from the surf. 

He looked back, expecting to see the man drowning. 

Instead, he saw a nightmare. 

The sea was vomiting the dead onto the rocks. Dozens of bodies in heavy woolen coats and canvas trousers were rolling in the surf, washing up onto the ledge. They were battered, broken, and waterlogged. Some were missing limbs. Others had their heads split open by the reef. 

But they were all moving. 

They began to drag themselves out of the foam, their milky white eyes fixing on the young man crawling up the granite. 

"Help!" Arthur shrieked, finally finding his feet and sprinting toward the base of the lighthouse. "Silas! Help!"

He reached the heavy oak and iron door just as a massive, calloused hand shot out and grabbed him by the collar of his oilskin slicker. 

Silas hauled the boy violently into the circular stone room at the base of the tower. 

The veteran keeper didn't look surprised. He stepped past the terrified boy, standing square in the open doorway. In his right hand, he held a heavy, wrought-iron fire poker from the lantern room stove. In his left, a bright kerosene lantern. 

The first dead sailor, a massive boatswain with a broken jaw, lunged out of the darkness and into the doorway. 

Silas didn't hesitate. He swung the heavy iron poker like a baseball bat. 

The thick iron struck the dead man directly across the temple. The sharp sound of shattering bone echoed loudly over the storm. The zombie's head whipped violently to the side, and the massive body dropped instantly to the stone floor, the brain utterly destroyed. 

The Romero rule was enforced with brutal, practiced efficiency. 

"Get back!" Silas roared, kicking the twitching corpse out onto the ledge. He grabbed the heavy iron handle of the door and slammed it shut with a deafening *CLANG*. 

He threw the massive, solid iron drop-latch down into its thick bracket, sealing them inside the base of the tower. 

Instantly, a heavy, rhythmic pounding began on the other side of the wood and iron. 

"Silas!" Arthur gasped, leaning against the cold, curved stone wall of the tower, shivering violently. "They survived! They survived the wreck! But they're crazy! They attacked me!"

Silas turned around, holding the lantern up. The yellow light cast long, terrifying shadows across his deeply lined face. 

"They didn't survive, Arthur," Silas said, his voice hard and utterly devoid of pity. "They're dead. The water killed them. The rocks killed them."

"But they were moving!" Arthur argued hysterically. "They grabbed me!"

"It's the sickness," Silas said, walking toward the heavy iron staircase. "I read the telegraphs from the mainland last month. I thought the Coast Guard boys were just drunk. Said the dead were walking out West. In the Dustbowl. Looks like it finally caught a ride east."

Arthur stared at the heavy door. The pounding was relentless, but the barricade was built to withstand a Category Five hurricane. It wasn't going to yield to the fists of drowned men. 

"We're safe," Arthur whispered, a desperate, shuddering breath escaping his lungs. "The door will hold. We just have to wait for morning."

"The door will hold," Silas agreed, his gray eyes slowly trailing down Arthur's yellow oilskin slicker to the boy's heavy rubber boots. 

Silas stopped at the bottom step of the iron staircase. His grip tightened on the fire poker. 

Arthur followed the veteran keeper's gaze. 

During the frantic struggle in the freezing surf, Arthur hadn't felt the bite. The icy water had numbed his legs completely. 

But looking down now, in the yellow light of the lantern, the truth was undeniable. 

A jagged, tearing bite mark was clearly visible, punched cleanly through the thick black rubber of his right boot, just above the ankle. A steady stream of dark red blood was mixing with the seawater pooling on the stone floor. 

Arthur looked up at Silas. 

They were perfectly safe from the dead men outside. But they were trapped in a hundred-foot vertical tube of solid granite. And the sickness was already inside.



Chapter Three: The Iron Spiral

The heavy, rhythmic pounding of the dead men against the exterior iron door was entirely eclipsed by the deafening roar of the blood rushing in Arthur's ears. 

He stared down at his heavy rubber boot. The neat, semicircular ring of punctures was impossible to misinterpret. The seawater pooling around his heel was turning a bright, arterial red. 

He looked up at the veteran keeper. 

Silas's face was completely unreadable. The harsh lines carved by decades of sun and salt spray were set like granite. He didn't offer a word of comfort. He didn't ask if it hurt. 

He simply raised the heavy iron fire poker, his knuckles white around the grip. 

"Silas... no," Arthur stammered, holding his hands up defensively. "It's just a scratch. The saltwater will clean it. It's just a scratch!"

"I read the telegraphs, Arthur," Silas said, his voice dropping to a low, deadly rumble that cut through the sound of the storm outside. "The sickness doesn't care if it's a scratch or a severed artery. Once the venom is in the blood, the heart stops. And then it starts again."

"I feel fine!" Arthur lied, scrambling backward until his spine hit the cold iron railing of the spiral staircase. 

"I'm sorry, boy," Silas said, stepping forward. "I truly am. But I'm not going to let you eat me in my sleep."

Silas swung the heavy iron poker in a brutal, downward arc, aiming directly for the top of the young assistant's skull. 

Arthur shrieked and threw himself to the left, diving onto the first step of the staircase. 

The heavy iron poker missed his head by an inch and struck the cast-iron newel post with a deafening *CLANG*. A bright shower of orange sparks erupted in the dim light of the kerosene lantern, briefly illuminating the stark terror in Arthur's eyes. 

"Silas, please!" Arthur begged, scrambling on his hands and knees up the first narrow curve of the stairs. 

Silas didn't answer. He recovered his swing and began to climb. 

The chase was a claustrophobic nightmare. The spiral staircase of the Blackwood Shoals Lighthouse was a tight, vertical coil of cold iron, entirely enclosed by the thick granite walls. There was barely enough room for two men to pass each other on a good day. 

Arthur's rubber boots clanged loudly against the metal treads as he ran blindly upward in the dark. 

Below him, Silas's heavy, measured footsteps followed relentlessly. 

The wind shrieked through the small, slatted ventilation windows set into the thick stone, creating a terrifying, chaotic noise that masked the sound of the pursuit. 

Arthur's lungs burned. The adrenaline was pumping hard, but a cold, heavy numbness was beginning to spread upward from his bleeding ankle. The venom of the dead was already working its way into his nervous system. 

He reached the first landing—a small, circular room used for storing the massive brass drums of kerosene oil required to keep the main lens burning. 

Arthur didn't stop. He knew Silas would check the room. He kept running, his hands burning as he pulled himself up the iron railing. 

He heard Silas reach the oil room landing. The heavy footsteps paused for a moment. 

"Arthur," Silas's voice echoed up the stone tube, calm and terrifying. "There's nowhere to go, boy. Just the sky and the sea."

Arthur didn't answer. He reached the second landing, fifty feet above the storm—the keeper's quarters. 

It was a small, wedge-shaped room containing two narrow bunks, a small cast-iron stove, and a writing desk. 

Arthur threw himself into the dark room and slammed the heavy oak door shut. His shaking fingers fumbled with the brass deadbolt, finally throwing the lock just as a heavy footstep landed on the iron tread outside. 

Arthur backed away from the door, his chest heaving, his right leg beginning to tremble violently. 

He expected the heavy thud of the iron fire poker smashing against the wood. He expected Silas to yell, to curse, to try to kick the door in. 

Instead, there was only silence from the landing. 

Then, Arthur heard the distinct metallic clink of Silas's silver flask opening. 

"I can wait, Arthur," Silas said softly through the thick oak planks. His voice wasn't angry; it was just tired. "I've got the rum. I've got the time. You've got the sickness."

Arthur slumped onto his narrow bunk, the sweat pouring down his face despite the freezing temperature of the tower. 

Silas was right. 

Arthur was trapped in a tiny room suspended fifty feet in the air. A veteran killer was sitting outside the only door, waiting patiently for him to die. And the fever was already setting in.



Chapter Four: The Fever

The small, wedge-shaped keeper's quarters felt like an icebox, the thick granite walls radiating the freezing temperature of the storm outside. But Arthur was burning alive. 

He stripped off the heavy yellow oilskin slicker and his wool sweater, tossing them onto the narrow cot. Sweat poured down his face, stinging his eyes and soaking his undershirt. 

He sat on the floor, dragging his right leg toward him. He pulled the heavy rubber boot off. 

The bite mark on his ankle was horrific. It hadn't bled much—the saltwater had contracted the vessels initially—but the flesh around the puncture wounds had already turned a bruised, necrotic black. Red streaks of infection were webbing their way up his calf, visibly pulsing under the pale skin. 

"Oh, God," Arthur whispered, his teeth chattering uncontrollably as a sudden, violent shiver wracked his body. "Oh, dear God."

He grabbed a relatively clean rag from the writing desk and wrapped it tightly around his ankle, pulling it as hard as he could to stop the venom from spreading. But it was a futile gesture. He could feel the poison in his blood, a heavy, cold sludge pushing its way toward his heart. 

The fever spiked. 

The small room began to warp and shift. The shadows cast by the small, slatted window seemed to lengthen and writhe across the stone floor. 

Arthur squeezed his eyes shut, but the hallucinations followed him into the dark. He saw the dead sailor in the surf, his chest crushed, his milky white eyes staring blindly into the storm. He saw his mother standing in the kitchen of their home in Portland, her back turned, ignoring his screams. 

He opened his eyes, gasping for air. The room was spinning. 

Outside the heavy oak door, he heard a sharp, metallic sound. It was the heavy iron fire poker tapping rhythmically against the stone steps. 

"Silas," Arthur called out, his voice weak and raspy. "Silas, please. It hurts."

The tapping stopped. 

"I know it does, Arthur," the veteran keeper's voice drifted through the thick wood, muffled but distinct. "The fever boils the brain. But it won't last long. A few hours, at most."

"I don't want to die," Arthur sobbed, crawling across the cold stone floor until his forehead rested against the bottom of the oak door. 

"Nobody does, boy," Silas said, taking another drink from his flask. "But the ocean doesn't care what you want. And neither does the sickness."

Arthur pressed his ear against the wood. He wanted Silas to open the door. He wanted the older man to come in, to tell him it was going to be alright, to offer some small comfort in the terrifying dark. 

But a primal, terrified part of Arthur's fading mind knew exactly what would happen if the door opened. Silas wouldn't bring comfort. He would bring the iron poker. He would smash Arthur's skull to pieces to prevent him from rising. 

Arthur's heart stuttered in his chest. It was a terrifying, hollow flutter, like a dying bird trapped behind his ribs. 

He gasped, clutching his chest. 

"Silas," Arthur wheezed, his vision darkening around the edges. 

"I'm here, Arthur," the old man said from the stairs. "I'm not going anywhere."

Arthur's breathing became shallow and ragged. The cold sludge in his veins had reached his heart. The muscle struggled against the venom, fighting desperately to pump the infected blood, but it was a losing battle. 

The storm outside shrieked against the tower, a deafening roar that seemed to swallow the small room. 

Arthur let his head fall back against the cold stone floor. He stared up at the wooden planks of the ceiling. 

His heart gave one final, violent spasm, a sharp pain that radiated down his left arm. 

Then, it stopped. 

The frantic, terrifying rhythm of his life ceased completely. His lungs emptied their final breath into the freezing air of the room. His eyes remained open, staring blankly at the ceiling. 

The fever broke. The pain vanished. 

Arthur, the young assistant keeper of the Blackwood Shoals Lighthouse, was dead in the locked room.



Chapter Five: The Dead Boy

Silas sat on the cold iron step outside the keeper's quarters, the silver flask empty in his left hand, the heavy iron fire poker resting across his knees. He didn't check his pocket watch. He had spent forty years maintaining the Blackwood Shoals light; his internal clock was set perfectly to the slow, agonizing passage of time in the dark. 

The frantic whimpering from the other side of the heavy oak door had stopped ten minutes ago. 

Silas knew the silence wasn't a reprieve. It was the transition. 

He didn't feel relief, and he didn't feel guilt. He felt a profound, heavy exhaustion. He had liked Arthur. The boy was soft, but he was a hard worker and he kept the brass polished. But liking a boy didn't change the brutal arithmetic of the sickness. 

Suddenly, a loud, heavy *THUD* echoed from inside the small room. 

It was the sound of a hundred and fifty pounds of dead weight falling from the narrow cot onto the stone floor. 

Then came the rustling. It wasn't the tentative, careful movement of a sick boy seeking comfort. It was the clumsy, uncoordinated dragging of a body that no longer understood the concept of gravity or pain. 

Silas stood up slowly, his joints popping in the cold air. He gripped the heavy iron poker with both hands. 

*BANG.*

The heavy oak door rattled violently on its iron hinges as the dead boy threw himself entirely against the wood. There was no knock. There was no plea for help. Just the wet, heavy sound of ruined flesh seeking purchase. 

"Arthur," Silas said quietly, his voice hard. "You're dead now. Rest."

The zombie didn't rest. The mindless, frantic scratching of fingernails against the thick oak planks began, accompanied by a low, wet clicking sound from Arthur's ruined throat. 

Silas raised the iron poker, preparing to smash the boy's skull the moment the door opened. 

But the door didn't open. 

Silas stared at the heavy brass handle. It was completely stationary. 

A cold spike of genuine panic pierced the veteran keeper's rum-soaked apathy. 

Arthur had thrown the deadbolt from the inside before the fever took him. The thick oak door was solidly locked. 

Silas lowered the poker. The implications were immediate and disastrous. 

The keeper's quarters were situated exactly halfway up the hundred-foot tower. The heavy iron door at the base of the lighthouse was currently holding back the horde of drowned sailors. But that door wasn't Silas's immediate concern. 

His concern was the massive Fresnel lens fifty feet above him. 

The clockwork mechanism that rotated the two-ton glass lens required winding every two hours. Arthur had abandoned his post to check the wreck. That was almost an hour ago. 

If the light stopped rotating, it would become a fixed beam. In a Nor'easter of this magnitude, a fixed beam was useless. Any ship navigating the outer shoals would think it was a coastal town light and steer directly into the Devil's Teeth. 

Silas had to get to the lantern room. But the only path up the narrow spiral staircase was directly past the door to the keeper's quarters. 

He couldn't just walk past. If he left the infected boy trapped in the room, Arthur would eventually batter the door down. And he would do it while Silas's back was turned, high in the lantern room. Silas couldn't maintain the light while constantly looking over his shoulder for a monster. 

He had to destroy Arthur's brain before he ascended to the light. 

But he couldn't break the door down. It was three inches of solid Maine oak, designed to withstand the internal pressure of a massive fire in the oil storage room below. 

Silas stepped back, his gray eyes scanning the heavy door frame in the dim light of his kerosene lantern. 

Directly above the heavy oak door was a narrow transom window, designed to allow heat from the lower stove to circulate upward into the quarters. It was a rectangle of thick glass, barely a foot high and two feet wide, mounted on a brass swivel hinge. 

It was far too small for a man to climb through. But it was large enough to fit an iron fire poker. 

Silas grabbed the cold iron railing of the spiral staircase and hoisted himself up. He balanced precariously on the narrow rail, leaning his considerable weight against the curved stone wall of the tower to steady himself. 

He was now eye-level with the transom window. 

He raised the lantern, shining the yellow light through the thick, salt-stained glass into the small room. 

Arthur was standing directly on the other side of the door. 

The boy's face was a horrific mask of necrosis and fever. The veins in his neck were bulging and black. But the worst part was his eyes. They were wide open, staring directly up at Silas through the transom window, completely devoid of humanity. The milky white irises caught the lantern light, glowing with an unholy, predatory hunger. 

Before Silas could raise the poker, the dead boy lunged upward. 

Arthur didn't try to open the transom latch. He simply drove his face directly into the thick glass. 

The glass shattered with a deafening crack, spraying sharp shards outward over Silas's chest.



Chapter Six: Through the Glass

The shower of shattered glass rained down over Silas, slicing hot, bright lines across his cheeks and the back of his hands. 

He didn't flinch. Decades of tending the massive, dangerous clockwork of the Fresnel lens had taught him to ignore sudden pain when balance was the only thing keeping him alive. 

Silas was standing precariously on the two-inch-wide cast-iron railing of the spiral staircase, leaning his heavy bulk backward against the curved granite wall. If he slipped, he wouldn't just fall onto the landing. He would fall backward into the hollow center of the spiral, plummeting fifty feet straight down to the hard stone floor of the oil storage room. 

Through the jagged hole of the broken transom window, Arthur's bloody hands lunged outward. 

The sharp remnants of the glass pane sliced deep into the dead boy's forearms, tearing through his canvas shirt and peeling the pale flesh back to the bone. The zombie didn't react. It felt no pain. It only knew the blind, consuming hunger that drove it toward the living warmth on the other side of the door. 

Cold, stiff fingers clamped violently onto the thick wool of Silas's heavy sweater. 

Silas cursed, feeling the incredible, unyielding strength of the dead pull him forward. He lost his footing on the iron rail, his right boot slipping off the edge. 

He pitched violently forward, catching himself against the thick oak frame of the door. His face was now inches from the broken transom window. 

Arthur's ruined, bleeding face lunged through the gap, his jaw snapping wetly, spraying Silas with necrotic blood and saliva. 

Silas tried to swing the heavy iron fire poker, but the space was too confined. The long, curved hook of the poker snagged on the iron railing above him, ruining the arc of the swing. 

"Damn it," Silas hissed, feeling Arthur's teeth scrape against the thick collar of his wool sweater. 

Silas let go of the kerosene lantern. 

The brass lamp tumbled backward over the railing, falling fifty feet down the center of the staircase. It struck the stone floor of the oil storage landing with a sharp crash, instantly shattering the glass chimney and splashing burning kerosene over the wooden floorboards. 

In the sudden darkness of the keeper's landing, lit only by the faint, flickering glow of the fire below, Silas gripped the heavy iron fire poker with both hands. 

He couldn't swing it. But he could thrust it. 

As Arthur lunged forward through the broken glass again, his jaw opening wide to tear at Silas's throat, the veteran keeper drove the heavy iron poker forward like a spear. 

The thick, pointed tip of the wrought-iron tool entered Arthur's open mouth with terrifying force. It punched through the soft palate, shattered the back of the throat, and drove straight upward into the base of the skull, destroying the brain stem completely. 

The effect was instantaneous. 

The unholy strength vanished from Arthur's fingers. The dead boy went entirely limp, his eyes rolling backward into his head. 

Silas pulled the heavy iron poker free with a sickening, wet crunch. 

Arthur's dead weight pulled him backward. The boy's body slid down the inside of the oak door, finally coming to a rest on the floor of the locked room. 

Silas gasped for air, his chest heaving as he carefully stepped down from the iron railing and back onto the solid iron tread of the landing. His hands were shaking, covered in the boy's dark, infected blood. 

He leaned against the granite wall, closing his eyes for a moment. He had done it. He had survived the transition. 

But the victory was incredibly short-lived. 

Silas opened his eyes and looked down the spiral staircase. 

The small kerosene fire on the landing below had already found a larger fuel source. The heavy oak door of the oil storage room was catching fire. Inside that room were ten fifty-gallon brass drums of highly flammable kerosene. 

"No," Silas whispered, the true magnitude of the disaster finally dawning on him. 

He couldn't go down. If he went down to fight the fire, the drums would eventually explode, killing him instantly. 

He could only go up. He had to reach the lantern room and keep the clockwork wound until the storm passed, hoping the thick granite walls would contain the blast of the oil drums. 

Suddenly, a massive, shuddering boom echoed through the entire tower, completely different from the sharp impact of the waves. 

The entire lighthouse vibrated so violently that Silas was thrown against the iron railing. 

He looked down toward the base of the tower. 

The heavy iron and oak door at the bottom of the lighthouse, the one currently holding back the horde of drowned sailors, was buckling. 

It wasn't the dead men pounding against the wood. They didn't have that kind of mass. 

It was the sea. The massive, churning Nor'easter had lifted the ruined, multi-ton iron hull of the wrecked *Banshee* off the Devil's Teeth reef and was repeatedly ramming the massive shipwreck directly into the base of the lighthouse like a colossal battering ram. 

Silas was trapped in a hundred-foot stone chimney. 

Below him, a massive fire was burning its way toward five hundred gallons of kerosene. 

Below that, a shipwreck was battering the iron door down, preparing to let the freezing ocean and a horde of the walking dead inside the tower. 

Silas turned his back on the fire below and began to run up the iron spiral toward the lantern room.



Chapter Seven: The Lantern Room

Silas didn't look back. His heavy boots hammered against the cast-iron treads of the spiral staircase as he forced his exhausted, aging body up the final fifty feet of the tower. 

Smoke, thick and acrid from the burning oil below, was already beginning to coil its way up the central shaft, chasing him in the dark. 

He reached the top landing and threw open the heavy iron hatch that led into the lantern room. 

The roar of the Nor'easter hit him like a physical blow. Surrounded by thick, curved storm panes on all sides, the lantern room was the most exposed point of the lighthouse. The wind shrieked against the glass, and the entire room vibrated violently with the storm's fury. 

But Silas ignored the tempest. His eyes locked onto the massive brass clockwork mechanism sitting beneath the two-ton Fresnel lens. 

The heavy brass gears were turning agonizingly slowly. The massive weight that drove the rotation had almost reached the bottom of its drop tube. If it hit the bottom, the light would stop rotating. 

Silas threw himself at the heavy brass crank handle. 

He gripped the cold metal with his bloody hands and began to turn. It was backbreaking work, requiring immense upper body strength to haul the massive lead weight back up the tube against gravity. 

His shoulders screamed in protest, and his breath came in ragged, wheezing gasps, but he kept turning. He was a keeper. He would keep the light burning until the sea swallowed the tower whole. 

*Click. Click. Click.*

The heavy pawl engaged with the ratchet gear, and the massive weight slowly began to rise. The rotation of the giant glass lens sped back up to its proper, life-saving rhythm, casting its brilliant, sweeping beam out into the churning darkness. 

Silas let go of the crank, collapsing against the brass railing, his chest heaving. He wiped the mixture of sweat and Arthur's dark blood from his eyes. 

He had done his duty. 

Suddenly, a catastrophic boom echoed up the stone shaft. It wasn't the sound of the shipwreck ramming the door. 

It was the unmistakable roar of an explosion. 

The kerosene fire on the first landing had finally superheated the brass fifty-gallon drums in the oil storage room. 

The explosion was devastating. A massive fireball erupted upward, vaporizing the remaining wooden steps of the spiral staircase and sending a shockwave of searing heat straight up the hollow center of the tower. 

Silas threw himself backward, grabbing the heavy iron handle of the floor hatch. He slammed the hatch shut with a deafening *CLANG* just as a geyser of thick, black, oily smoke and orange flame erupted through the opening. 

He spun the heavy iron locking wheel, sealing the lantern room from the inferno below. 

The heavy iron hatch was designed to act as a firebreak, but Silas knew it was only a temporary measure. The entire hundred-foot granite tower was now acting as a massive, perfectly drafted chimney for a five-hundred-gallon kerosene fire. 

The iron floorplates of the lantern room were already beginning to grow warm beneath his heavy boots. 

Silas stood up, leaning against the cold glass of the storm panes, gasping for relatively clean air. 

He looked down through the glass, trying to see the base of the tower through the driving rain. The wind was howling, whipping the salt spray into a frenzy. 

He couldn't see the ground. But he saw something else. 

A wet, sliding sound, distinctly different from the impact of the rain, caught his attention. It sounded like thick canvas dragging against wet stone. 

Silas pressed his face closer to the storm pane, squinting into the darkness just below the edge of the lantern room's exterior gallery deck. 

A pale, bloated hand, missing two fingers, slapped wetly onto the iron railing of the exterior deck. 

Silas recoiled in horror. 

The dead sailors from the wrecked *Banshee* hadn't just stayed at the base of the tower. When the shipwreck had finally battered the iron door down, they had swarmed into the base, only to find the spiral staircase destroyed by the inferno. 

Driven entirely by an unthinking, predatory instinct, they had turned their attention upward. 

The exterior of the Blackwood Shoals Lighthouse was constructed from massive, rough-hewn granite blocks. The deep mortar joints and jagged stone faces provided more than enough handholds. 

Dozens of the drowned dead were slowly, relentlessly scaling the outside of the hundred-foot stone tower in the middle of a freezing hurricane. 

They were drawn by the brilliant, sweeping beam of the light, and the warm, living flesh trapped inside the glass room at the top. 

Silas stepped back from the storm pane, his heart hammering against his ribs. 

He was perfectly trapped. Below him, an uncontrollable inferno was slowly superheating the iron floor. And outside, pressing their ruined faces against the glass, was a horde of the climbing dead.



Chapter Eight: The Weight

The lantern room of the Blackwood Shoals Lighthouse was an octagon of thick, reinforced storm panes, framed in heavy brass and mounted directly onto the granite capstone of the tower. It was built to withstand the fury of the Atlantic Ocean. 

It was not built to withstand a siege. 

Silas watched through the rain-streaked glass as the first dead sailor hauled himself over the iron railing of the exterior gallery deck. The man's woolen peacoat was heavy with water, his face a bruised, bloated ruin. He didn't pause to catch his breath. He immediately stepped toward the glass and began to pound his cold fists against the pane. 

*Thud. Thud. Thud.*

A second sailor climbed over the railing. Then a third. 

Within minutes, the narrow exterior deck was packed with the dead. They pressed their ruined faces against the glass, their milky white eyes tracking Silas's movements in the bright light of the massive, rotating Fresnel lens. 

Silas didn't move. He knew the glass was thick enough to stop a flying piece of timber in a hurricane. Their bare fists wouldn't break it. 

But as more and more dead men joined the horde on the narrow deck, a new, terrifying reality set in. 

They weren't just striking the glass. They were leaning against it. 

The sheer, combined weight of thirty waterlogged corpses pressing mindlessly against the storm panes was putting an incredible strain on the brass framework. 

Silas heard a high-pitched, agonizing squeal of metal bending under pressure. 

He looked down. The iron floorplates of the lantern room were no longer just warm. They were hot enough to burn through the thick rubber soles of his boots. The paint on the heavy iron hatch was blistering and peeling away, filling the small room with a toxic chemical stench. 

The fire below was raging, turning the tower into a blast furnace. 

Silas was out of options. He couldn't go down, and he couldn't stay in the lantern room. 

*CRACK.*

A massive, spiderweb fracture appeared in the center of the storm pane directly in front of him. 

The pressure was too much. The glass was failing. 

Silas spun around, his desperate eyes searching the cramped lantern room for a weapon. The heavy iron fire poker was fifty feet below, sticking out of Arthur's dead skull. 

His eyes fell on the massive brass clockwork mechanism. 

Silas lunged forward and grabbed the heavy brass crank handle he had just used to wind the weight. He didn't turn it. He braced his boot against the iron housing of the clockwork and pulled backward with all of his remaining strength. 

The heavy brass crank groaned in protest. 

Silas roared, his muscles burning as he ripped the crank violently sideways, snapping the thick brass retaining pin. The heavy handle came free in his hands, a solid, two-foot club of heavy nautical brass. 

The moment the crank snapped, the clockwork mechanism seized with a horrific grinding noise. The massive two-ton Fresnel lens groaned and ground to a sudden, permanent halt. 

The sweeping beam of light froze, pointing rigidly out into the storm. 

*SMASH.*

The fractured storm pane finally gave way under the massive weight of the dead. 

The thick glass shattered inward, showering the hot iron floorplates with deadly shards. The Nor'easter instantly roared into the lantern room, bringing the freezing rain and the deafening howl of the wind with it. 

And it brought the dead. 

The first sailor tumbled through the broken window frame, landing heavily on the hot iron floor. He didn't seem to notice the blistering heat burning his wet hands. He pushed himself up, his dead eyes locking onto Silas. 

Silas didn't wait for the man to stand. 

He swung the heavy brass crank like a woodsman's axe. The heavy brass struck the sailor's wet skull with a sickening crunch, caving the side of his head in and destroying the brain instantly. 

The zombie collapsed onto the hot floorplates, his wet clothes hissing and steaming violently in the heat. 

But the victory was meaningless. 

As Silas pulled the brass club free, two more dead sailors climbed through the shattered window, their ruined hands reaching for the veteran keeper in the freezing storm.



Chapter Nine: The Liquid Fire

The fight in the lantern room was brutal, chaotic, and completely devoid of hope. 

Silas swung the heavy brass crank in a tight, desperate arc. The solid metal connected with the collarbone of the second dead sailor, shattering the bone with a sharp *CRACK*. 

But the Romero rule dictated the terms of the fight. A broken collarbone didn't stop the brain. The zombie didn't even stumble. It simply reached out with its good arm and clamped its cold, wet fingers into the thick wool of Silas's sweater. 

The third dead sailor lunged forward, snapping its ruined jaw inches from Silas's face. 

Silas roared, using his massive bulk to drive both zombies backward. He slammed them hard against the heavy iron floor hatch in the center of the room.
