The hatch was glowing a dull, angry cherry red. The massive kerosene fire roaring up the hundred-foot chimney of the tower was superheating the iron plates. The moment the dead sailors' waterlogged woolen coats touched the iron, they hissed violently, sending up thick clouds of scalding steam. 

The zombies didn't care. They didn't feel the burning heat searing their flesh. They continued to tear at Silas's clothes, trying to pull him down onto the burning floor. 

Silas looked over their shoulders. Through the shattered storm pane, three more dead men were climbing over the sill. 

He couldn't win a war of attrition in a ten-foot room. He needed a weapon of mass destruction. 

His eyes darted from the glowing floor hatch to the massive, stationary Fresnel lens towering above them. 

The two-ton assembly of solid glass prisms and heavy brass framing didn't sit on ball bearings. The friction would have been too great for the clockwork mechanism to turn. To allow the massive weight to rotate smoothly, the entire lens assembly floated in a circular, brass trough filled with fifty gallons of liquid mercury. 

And right now, that circular brass trough was sitting directly above the superheated iron floorplates. 

The liquid mercury was boiling. 

Silas didn't hesitate. He planted his right boot against the chest of the closest zombie and kicked backward with all his might, tearing his wool sweater free from the dead hands. 

He spun around, raising the heavy brass crank high above his head. 

He didn't aim for the dead men pouring through the window. He aimed for the thick brass retaining collar that held the boiling mercury inside the circular trough. 

Silas brought the heavy crank down with a terrifying, primal roar. 

The brass collar shattered. 

Instantly, a massive wave of boiling, toxic liquid mercury erupted from the ruptured trough. 

The heavy liquid splashed outward in a perfect, glowing silver arc, washing over the iron floorplates and directly into the surging horde of dead sailors. 

The effect was horrific and instantaneous. 

The boiling mercury didn't just burn; it dissolved. The superheated liquid metal seared through the wet woolen coats, instantly boiling the necrotic blood and melting the cold flesh beneath. The three zombies closest to the trough collapsed onto the iron floor, their bodies writhing and dissolving in a horrific cloud of toxic, silver vapor. 

The sudden, catastrophic heat destroyed their brains before they even realized they were falling. 

Silas stumbled backward, shielding his face from the searing vapor. 

He had stopped the vanguard of the horde. But the victory came with a terrible price. 

A heavy splash of the boiling silver liquid caught Silas directly across his heavy leather boots. 

Silas screamed, a raw, agonizing sound that echoed loudly over the roar of the Nor'easter. The boiling mercury burned straight through the thick leather and the wool socks beneath, searing the flesh of his feet down to the bone. 

He fell heavily against the thick storm panes, unable to stand on the molten agony of his ruined feet. 

But the horror was far from over. 

Without the massive volume of liquid mercury to support its weight, the two-ton Fresnel lens was no longer floating. The massive brass and glass assembly crashed down onto the empty iron trough. 

The sudden impact, combined with the violent shuddering of the tower as the shipwreck continued to batter the base, was too much for the unsupported structure. 

With a deafening groan of tearing brass, the massive, two-ton glass lens began to tilt dangerously toward the shattered window.



Chapter Ten: The Fall

The groaning of the heavy brass framework was a sound Silas had dreaded his entire life. The massive, two-ton Fresnel lens, the pride of the Blackwood Shoals Lighthouse, was tearing itself apart. 

Without the buoyant support of the boiling mercury, the immense weight of the solid glass prisms and brass rings was resting entirely on the fragile drive gear. 

The gear snapped with a sound like a gunshot. 

The massive assembly tilted violently toward the shattered storm pane, the brass framing screaming in protest. 

Silas lay on the hot iron floorplates, his ruined feet screaming in agony. The boiling mercury had cooled rapidly, leaving a toxic, silver crust over the charred leather of his boots. He couldn't stand. He couldn't crawl. 

And the dead were still coming. 

The three zombies destroyed by the liquid fire had cleared the path for the rest of the climbing horde. They began to pour through the shattered window frame, ignoring the heat of the floor and the toxic silver vapor in the air. 

They swarmed over Silas, their cold, wet hands grabbing his wool sweater, their ruined jaws snapping at his face. 

Silas didn't try to fight them off. He knew it was over. He had lost the tower. He had lost the light. 

But he wasn't going to let them eat him alive in the dark. 

He still held the heavy brass crank in his right hand. He didn't swing it at the dead sailors tearing at his clothes. 

He swung it directly upward, aiming for the weakest point of the tilting brass framework supporting the two-ton lens. 

He struck the main vertical support strut with all of his remaining strength. 

*CRACK.*

The heavy brass strut buckled under the impact. 

That was all it took. The delicate balance of the massive assembly was completely destroyed. 

The two-ton Fresnel lens collapsed. 

It didn't just fall; it sheared sideways, the massive weight tearing the remaining brass supports out of the floorplates. 

The immense structure of solid glass and heavy brass crashed down directly onto the horde of dead sailors swarming over Silas. The impact instantly crushed half a dozen skulls, splattering necrotic blood across the hot iron floor. 

But the momentum of the falling lens didn't stop. 

It carried the massive weight directly through the shattered storm pane and into the iron framing of the lantern room wall. 

The entire side of the lantern room exploded outward in a shower of shattered glass, twisted iron, and solid brass. 

Silas was caught in the avalanche. The collapsing lens scooped him up, along with the remaining dead sailors, and threw them all out into the roaring Nor'easter. 

The sudden silence of the wind rushing past his ears was terrifying. 

Silas plummeted one hundred feet down through the freezing storm, surrounded by the falling debris of his life's work and a dozen thrashing, ruined corpses. 

Below him, the ocean boiled white as the massive waves crashed against the Devil's Teeth. 

The impact was like hitting solid concrete. 

The freezing saltwater immediately filled Silas's lungs as he was driven deep beneath the surface by the weight of the falling glass. 

The cold was absolute. It extinguished the burning agony in his feet and the terrible heat of the fire raging in the tower above. 

In the crushing darkness beneath the waves, Silas felt a cold, dead hand brush against his cheek. 

The ocean had claimed them all. The dead sailors were finally returning to the deep, and they were taking the keeper of the light with them.



Story Ten: The Meatpacker's Casino

Chapter One: The Trapdoor

Chicago in 1936 was a city built on blood and graft. Prohibition may have officially ended three years earlier, but the Syndicate hadn't abandoned its lucrative underground empires; it had simply rebranded them. 

Deep beneath the sprawling, stench-filled expanse of the O'Bannon Meatpacking Plant on the South Side, Boss Moroni ran the most exclusive illegal casino in the state. 

Frankie "The Chin" Scalise stood at the top of a narrow, concrete stairwell, adjusting the lapels of his sharp, pinstriped suit. He was Moroni's primary enforcer, a man paid very well to stand in front of a heavy, riveted steel trapdoor and ensure that only high rollers and corrupt aldermen made it down into the subterranean opulence. 

Above him, the relentless, mechanical screech of the slaughterhouse kill-floor echoed through the concrete. It was the night shift, a brutal, bloody affair of rendering cattle into profit. Frankie hated the smell—a thick, metallic stench of fresh blood, offal, and fear that clung to his expensive clothes no matter how much cheap cologne he used. 

Lately, there had been rumors filtering in from the rural routes. Whispers of a "rabid flu" spreading out from the devastated Dustbowl states. Moroni had dismissed it as hick panic. The Syndicate controlled Chicago; a little flu wasn't going to stop the roulette wheels. 

A sudden, frantic pounding on the steel trapdoor above Frankie's head interrupted his thoughts. 

It wasn't the rhythmic, pre-arranged knock of a VIP. It was a desperate, panicked hammering. 

Frankie frowned, resting his hand on the cold, checkered grip of the Colt M1911 tucked into his shoulder holster. He climbed the last three steps and peered through the small, reinforced viewing slit. 

A slaughterhouse worker, wearing a heavy rubber apron slick with fresh gore, was pressed against the steel, his eyes wide with absolute terror. 

"Let me in!" the man screamed, his voice muffled by the thick metal. "They've gone crazy! They're eating everyone!"

Frankie sighed. It wasn't the first time a slaughterhouse worker had snapped from the brutal conditions on the kill-floor. 

Frankie threw the heavy iron bolt and pushed the trapdoor open an inch. "Beat it, pal. This is a private—"

The worker didn't wait for him to finish. He threw his entire weight against the trapdoor, forcing it open. He tumbled down the stairs, crashing heavily into Frankie and sending them both tumbling down the concrete steps. 

They landed in a heap at the bottom of the stairwell, just outside the velvet ropes of the casino entrance. 

"What the hell is this?" a booming voice demanded. 

Boss Moroni, a massive man in a tailored tuxedo, was standing by the craps table, glaring at the commotion. Beside him stood his personal bodyguard, a hulking brute named Rocco. 

Frankie shoved the worker off him and stood up, brushing the dirt from his suit. "Just a crazy butcher from upstairs, Boss. I'll handle it."

Frankie reached down to grab the worker by the collar of his rubber apron. 

But the worker didn't try to run or apologize. He lay perfectly still on the concrete floor. His neck was bent at a completely unnatural angle. The fall down the concrete stairs had snapped his cervical spine. 

"He's dead, Boss," Frankie said, a cold knot forming in his stomach. The Syndicate didn't need a dead civilian in their casino. 

"Get him out of here, Rocco," Moroni barked, waving a fat, cigar-clutching hand. "Before the alderman from the 4th Ward loses his appetite."

Rocco grunted, stepping forward and bending down to grab the dead worker's ankles. 

As Rocco's massive hands wrapped around the rubber boots, the impossible happened. 

The worker's neck didn't straighten, but his eyes snapped open. They were a dull, milky white, entirely devoid of human intelligence. 

Before Rocco could react, the dead worker lunged upward with terrifying speed. His jaw snapped shut, sinking his teeth deep into the thick, meaty flesh of Rocco's neck, tearing out a massive chunk of the bodyguard's jugular vein.


Chapter Two: The Tommy Gun

Rocco screamed, a wet, gurgling sound that was instantly drowned out by the chaotic music of the casino floor. Blood sprayed across the velvet ropes in a bright, arterial arc. 

The massive bodyguard stumbled backward, clutching his ruined throat, his eyes wide with shock. The dead worker, his broken neck lolling grotesquely to the side, scrambled to his feet and lunged again, driven by a blind, unthinking hunger. 

Frankie didn't hesitate. He was a professional killer. When a man attacked the boss's crew, you put him down. 

He didn't draw the Colt. He reached under the maître d's podium and pulled out the weapon he kept there for major disputes: a Thompson submachine gun, fully loaded with a fifty-round drum magazine. 

Frankie braced the heavy wooden stock against his hip and pulled the trigger. 

The deafening roar of the Tommy gun ripped through the subterranean casino, shattering the ambient noise of the roulette wheels and the jazz band. 

Frankie didn't spray wildly. He aimed center mass, the standard doctrine for taking down a charging man. A three-second burst sent twenty heavy .45 caliber rounds tearing through the dead worker's chest. 

The impact of the heavy slugs tore the man's rubber apron to shreds and punched massive, fist-sized holes through his ribcage. A normal man would have been dead before he hit the floor. 

But the Romero rule cared nothing for ballistics. The heart was a pump, not the engine. 

The dead worker absorbed the massive kinetic energy, stumbling backward for a second, but he didn't fall. The milky white eyes didn't even blink. He simply reoriented himself and lunged toward Frankie, blood and necrotic tissue spilling freely from the massive holes in his chest. 

"What the hell are you on?" Frankie yelled, stepping back and firing another sustained burst. 

The heavy rounds tore through the man's stomach and shattered his pelvis. The dead worker's legs collapsed, but his upper body kept moving, dragging himself across the marble floor with his hands, his ruined jaw snapping furiously. 

"Shoot him in the head, Frankie!" Boss Moroni roared, retreating behind the craps table, his cigar forgotten. 

Frankie adjusted his aim, his hands shaking slightly. He fired a short, controlled burst. 

The first round missed, shattering a crystal chandelier above them. The second round struck the dead worker directly between the eyes. 

The back of the man's skull exploded in a shower of bone and gray matter. The brain was destroyed. The unholy engine was finally shut down. The dead worker collapsed onto the marble, completely motionless. 

The casino fell into a stunned, horrified silence. The jazz band had stopped playing. Dozens of wealthy patrons in tuxedos and evening gowns were staring at the carnage by the entrance. 

Frankie lowered the smoking barrel of the Tommy gun, his breathing heavy. 

"Jesus Christ," Frankie whispered, staring at the ruined body. "I put thirty rounds in his chest."

A wet, gasping sound drew his attention back to the velvet ropes. 

Rocco, the massive bodyguard, was lying in a rapidly expanding pool of his own blood. His hands were still clutching his torn throat, but the bleeding had stopped. His chest had stopped rising. 

"Rocco's dead, Boss," Frankie said grimly. 

"Leave him," Moroni barked, recovering his composure. "Frankie, lock that trapdoor. Nobody gets in or out until we figure out what the hell kind of dope that butcher was on."

Frankie turned toward the concrete stairs. 

But he didn't take a step. 

Behind him, Rocco's massive, dead hands slowly pulled away from his ruined throat. The bodyguard's chest didn't rise. But his eyes opened. 

They weren't brown anymore. They were milky white. 

Rocco, all two hundred and fifty pounds of mob muscle, slowly pushed himself off the marble floor, his dead eyes locking onto Boss Moroni.


Chapter Three: The Vault

The reanimation of Rocco shattered the stunned silence of the casino. 

A high-pitched scream from a woman in a sequined gown triggered a wave of absolute panic. Dozens of wealthy patrons, corrupt politicians, and mob associates scrambled over each other, knocking over roulette tables and scattering thousands of dollars in chips across the floor. 

Rocco lunged. He didn't move like a sluggish zombie from the picture shows. He moved with the terrifying, uninhibited strength of a dead muscleman. He crashed into the craps table, sending dice and chips flying, trying to reach Moroni. 

Frankie raised the Tommy gun again. He didn't bother with center mass this time. He aimed high and squeezed the trigger. 

The heavy .45 rounds tore through Rocco's cheek and jaw, failing to penetrate the braincase. The bodyguard didn't even flinch. He grabbed the heavy oak rim of the craps table and flipped it effortlessly, forcing Frankie to dive out of the way. 

"Frankie!" 

A sharp, demanding voice cut through the chaos. 

Frankie rolled to his feet and turned. Sadie, one of the cocktail waitresses, had grabbed his shoulder. She was twenty-two, sharp as a tack, and entirely focused on survival. She had already kicked off her high heels and torn a long slit up the side of her silk skirt to allow her to run. 

"We have to get out of here!" Sadie yelled over the roar of the panicked crowd and the gunfire. "The service elevator in the back! It goes straight to the loading dock!"

Frankie looked back at the entrance. The heavy steel trapdoor at the top of the stairs was still open. And through that opening, a terrifying sound was echoing down into the casino. 

It was a low, collective moan, accompanied by the wet, slapping sound of hundreds of heavy rubber boots descending the concrete steps. 

The entire night shift of the slaughterhouse had turned. 

"We can't go up," Frankie said, his eyes wide. "They're coming down."

"Then we take the elevator!" Sadie insisted, pulling him toward the back of the massive underground room. 

They sprinted past the panicked patrons, many of whom were hopelessly trying to pry open the reinforced steel doors of the main entrance, unaware that the real threat was coming down the stairs. 

Frankie and Sadie reached the rear of the casino, where the massive, polished steel door of Boss Moroni's private vault stood open. Inside, Moroni was frantically shoving stacks of hundred-dollar bills into leather duffel bags. 

"Boss!" Frankie yelled. "We gotta go! The whole slaughterhouse is coming down those stairs!"

Moroni looked up, his face red, his eyes manic. "I'm not leaving the money, Frankie! This is my entire operation!"

"The money won't do you any good if you're dead!" Sadie screamed. 

The first of the slaughterhouse zombies reached the casino floor. They were terrifying to behold—men in heavy rubber aprons, slick with the blood of cattle, their faces pale, their eyes milky white. Many of them were still clutching the tools of their trade: massive meat cleavers and sharp, curved rendering hooks. 

The massacre began instantly. The slow, methodical zombies waded into the panicked crowd of high rollers. The screaming was deafening as silk gowns were torn and throats were ripped open. 

"Boss, the elevator!" Frankie yelled, pointing to the heavy iron grate of the service elevator just past the vault. 

"Hold them off!" Moroni ordered, throwing another bag of cash toward the back of the vault. 

Moroni stepped out of the vault, grabbed the heavy steel wheel on the outside of the door, and began to spin it. 

He wasn't locking himself in. He was locking Frankie, Sadie, and the entire casino out. 

"What are you doing?!" Frankie yelled, lunging forward. 

But it was too late. The heavy steel door slammed shut with a final, resonant *CLANG*, and the locking bolts slid heavily into place. Moroni had sealed the vault, securing his money, and leaving his enforcer trapped in the casino with the dead.


Chapter Four: The Kill Floor

The polished steel of the vault door reflected the horrific chaos erupting across the casino floor. Frankie pounded his fist against the impenetrable metal, cursing Moroni's greed. 

"He locked us out," Frankie growled, turning back to the room. 

"Forget him," Sadie said, her voice trembling but her eyes completely focused. She pointed toward the heavy iron grate of the service elevator located in a small alcove just past the vault. "The elevator. We have to call it down."

Frankie nodded. He raised the Tommy gun and pulled Sadie behind him, moving slowly toward the alcove. 

The casino was a slaughterhouse. 

The infected workers from the meatpacking plant were brutally efficient. They didn't feel pain, and they didn't tire. They moved with a slow, relentless determination, tearing into the panicked patrons with their teeth and the heavy iron tools they still gripped in their dead hands. 

A wealthy alderman in a tuxedo tried to crawl under a blackjack table. A massive zombie wearing a blood-soaked rubber apron reached down, grabbed the man by his ankle, and dragged him back out into the open. The zombie raised a heavy, curved rendering hook and drove it effortlessly through the politician's chest, pinning him to the marble floor before ripping into his throat with its teeth. 

"Oh, God," Sadie whispered, covering her mouth to muffle a scream. 

"Keep moving," Frankie ordered, his eyes constantly scanning the room for threats. 

He fired short, controlled bursts from the Tommy gun, explicitly aiming for the head. He dropped three slaughterhouse zombies that stumbled too close, their skulls exploding in showers of dark blood and gray matter. 

They reached the alcove. Sadie slammed her palm against the heavy brass call button for the service elevator. 

Nothing happened. 

She hit it again, frantically. "It's not moving!"

Frankie looked through the heavy iron grate. The elevator shaft was dark, but he could see the thick steel cables hanging slack. 

"Moroni," Frankie realized, a cold fury settling over him. "The control box for the service elevator is inside the vault. He killed the power so we couldn't use it to escape and leave him behind."

They were completely trapped. The massive steel doors of the main entrance were locked from the outside. The trapdoor to the kill-floor was vomiting the dead into the room. The vault was sealed. And the elevator was dead. 

The subterranean casino, built to be an impenetrable fortress against police raids, was now an inescapable tomb. 

The screams in the main room were beginning to die down, replaced by the horrific, wet sounds of tearing flesh and crunching bone. 

The slaughterhouse horde was finishing its initial meal. 

And then, as if responding to a silent, collective signal, the dead workers stopped feeding. Dozens of milky white eyes turned away from the ruined corpses of the high rollers. 

They turned toward the alcove. They turned toward Frankie and Sadie. 

"Frankie," Sadie whispered, pressing her back against the cold iron grate of the useless elevator. 

"Get behind me," Frankie said, his voice terrifyingly calm. He checked the drum magazine on the Tommy gun. He had maybe twenty rounds left. 

The horde began to move. It was a terrifying, slow-motion wave of blood-soaked rubber and dead, hungry eyes. They shambled across the marble floor, slipping occasionally on the blood, but always recovering, always moving forward. 

A massive zombie, a former line foreman clutching a heavy, blood-stained meat cleaver, led the pack. 

"Here they come," Frankie said, raising the heavy wooden stock to his shoulder.


Chapter Five: The High Rollers

Frankie didn't wait for them to close the distance. He had twenty rounds, and there were at least forty dead men shambling toward the alcove. 

He aimed for the massive line foreman leading the pack. 

*Bang. Bang.*

Two heavy .45 rounds struck the zombie directly in the face. The heavy slugs shattered the cheekbone and tore through the right eye, exiting through the back of the skull. The massive man dropped like a stone, the Romero rule proving absolute. 

But for every zombie Frankie dropped, two more stepped over the ruined body to take its place. 

The horde was too tightly packed. Even with perfect aim, Frankie couldn't kill them fast enough. 

"Frankie!" Sadie screamed. 

Frankie spun around. While he was focused on the main horde approaching from the front, two infected casino patrons—a woman in a torn silk gown and a man in a ruined tuxedo—had flanked them, stumbling out from behind a row of overturned slot machines. 

The dead high rollers didn't have meat cleavers, but their snapping jaws were just as deadly. 

Frankie swung the Tommy gun, firing a short burst. The heavy rounds tore through the woman's chest, knocking her backward, but failing to stop her. 

He didn't have time to correct his aim. The male patron lunged, tackling Frankie to the marble floor. 

The heavy Tommy gun was knocked from Frankie's hands, clattering uselessly across the floor. 

Frankie grunted as the dead man's full weight crushed him against the marble. The zombie's jaw snapped furiously, cold teeth scraping against Frankie's jawline. Frankie shoved his forearm under the dead man's chin, desperately trying to keep the snapping teeth away from his throat. 

"Get off him!" Sadie yelled. 

She had grabbed a heavy, solid brass ashtray from a nearby cocktail table. She raised it high with both hands and brought it down with all her strength onto the back of the dead patron's head. 

The heavy brass struck with a sickening crunch. The zombie's skull cracked, but it wasn't enough to destroy the brain. The dead man simply ignored her, continuing to force his jaw down toward Frankie's neck. 

Sadie didn't hesitate. She swung the heavy brass ashtray again, this time aiming for the side of the head, directly over the ear. 

The impact was brutal. The brass crushed the temporal bone, driving sharp fragments deep into the brain. 

The zombie went instantly limp, its dead weight collapsing fully onto Frankie. 

Frankie shoved the heavy corpse off him and scrambled to his feet, gasping for air. 

"Thanks, kid," Frankie said, snatching the Tommy gun off the floor. 

He checked the drum. Empty. 

He tossed the useless submachine gun aside and drew the Colt M1911 from his shoulder holster. Seven rounds. 

He turned back to the main room. 

The distraction had cost them the precious distance they needed. The main horde of slaughterhouse workers had reached the edge of the alcove. They were surrounded. 

"We can't hold them here," Frankie said, grabbing Sadie's hand. "We have to move."

There was only one way out of the alcove: straight through the approaching horde. 

Frankie raised the Colt. He aimed directly for the face of the nearest slaughterhouse worker, a massive man wielding a heavy, rusted meat hook. 

"Stay close," Frankie ordered. 

He pulled the trigger, dropping the first zombie, and plunged directly into the sea of the walking dead.



Chapter Six: The Meat Hook

The plunge into the horde was pure, terrifying chaos. 

Frankie moved with the desperate, adrenaline-fueled precision of a cornered animal. He didn't try to shoot his way through the center; he skirted the edge of the mass, using the heavy, overturned baccarat tables as temporary barricades. 

*Bang.*

The Colt M1911 barked, sending a heavy .45 round through the eye of a dead waitress lunging from his left. 

"Stay behind me!" Frankie yelled, grabbing Sadie's wrist and pulling her forward. 

They were making progress. The slaughterhouse zombies were incredibly strong, but they were slow and entirely uncoordinated, constantly tripping over the ruined bodies of the high rollers scattered across the marble floor. 

Frankie dropped another dead worker with a shot to the temple. He had three rounds left. 

Suddenly, a massive shape lunged out from behind a toppled craps table directly into their path. 

It was the dead meatpacker Frankie had shot in the chest back at the entrance. The man's ribcage was a ruined crater of shattered bone and necrotic tissue from the Tommy gun blast, but his upper body was still functioning perfectly. 

The zombie dragged itself forward on its hands and lunged, wrapping its thick fingers around Frankie's ankle. 

Frankie stumbled, his balance completely thrown. He hit the marble floor hard, the Colt flying from his hand and skittering out of reach under a roulette wheel. 

"Frankie!" Sadie screamed, reaching down to pull him up. 

But the delay was fatal. 

The momentary pause allowed the main horde to close the gap. A towering slaughterhouse worker, his rubber apron slick with fresh gore, stepped over the crawling zombie and brought his right arm down in a vicious, sweeping arc. 

In his hand, he gripped a massive, rusted steel rendering hook used for hauling sides of beef. 

The heavy steel hook missed Frankie by an inch, striking the marble floor with a shower of sparks. 

But the backswing caught Sadie. 

The sharp, curved point of the heavy iron hook bit deep into the soft flesh of Sadie's right shoulder, tearing through her silk dress and catching the collarbone. 

Sadie screamed in absolute agony as the massive zombie violently yanked the hook backward, effortlessly lifting the hundred-and-ten-pound cocktail waitress off her feet. 

"Sadie!" Frankie roared, kicking the crawling zombie away and scrambling toward her. 

But he was too late. 

The massive worker pulled Sadie directly into the center of the surging horde. Half a dozen dead men instantly swarmed her, their ruined jaws opening wide. 

Frankie lunged forward, but a heavy hand clamped onto his shoulder, throwing him backward. He crashed into the heavy brass railing of the roulette pit. 

He looked up, gasping for air. 

He was completely unarmed. The Colt was gone. The Tommy gun was empty. And thirty dead men stood between him and the woman screaming in the center of the horde. 

He needed a weapon. 

His eyes fell on the massive, heavy brass stanchions used to hold the velvet ropes around the high-roller tables. They were four feet tall, solid brass, and weighed at least thirty pounds.


Chapter Seven: The Roulette Wheel

Frankie grabbed the heavy brass stanchion with both hands and ripped it from the marble floor, tearing the securing bolts out of the stone. 

He hoisted the massive, thirty-pound weapon onto his shoulder and roared, a sound of pure, unadulterated rage that cut through the cacophony of the massacre. 

He didn't skirt the edge of the horde this time. He charged directly into the center. 

He swung the heavy brass stanchion like a medieval mace. The incredible weight of the solid brass, combined with Frankie's desperate momentum, was devastating. 

The first swing caught a dead worker directly in the jaw, shattering the skull and snapping the neck with a sickening crunch. The zombie collapsed instantly. 

Frankie swung again, a brutal backhand that caved in the side of a dead politician's head. 

He waded through the sea of the walking dead, the heavy brass stanchion rising and falling with rhythmic, terrifying efficiency. Blood and gray matter sprayed across his pinstriped suit, but he didn't stop. He was a force of nature, a mob enforcer pushed far past the limits of human endurance. 

He carved a bloody path to the center of the room. 

The horde parted slightly as he brought the heavy brass down on the skull of the massive worker holding the rendering hook. 

Frankie dropped the stanchion and fell to his knees. 

Sadie was lying on the blood-soaked marble. 

She was still breathing, but the damage was catastrophic. The rendering hook had torn her shoulder open, and the horde had ripped her silk dress to shreds. Horrific, jagged bite marks covered her arms and neck. 

Frankie gathered her into his arms, his hands shaking violently. 

"I got you, kid," Frankie whispered, his voice cracking. "I got you. We're gonna get out of here."

Sadie looked up at him. Her eyes were clouded with pain, but they were still entirely human. She reached up with a trembling, blood-slicked hand and touched his cheek. 

She knew the rules of the sickness now. She had seen the dead rise. She knew what the bites meant. 

"Frankie," she gasped, blood bubbling at the corner of her mouth. "Don't... don't let me turn."

Frankie stared at her, his heart shattering. He was a killer, a man who had taken dozens of lives for Boss Moroni without a second thought. But this was different. 

"Please, Frankie," she begged, her eyes wide with terror as the venomous fever began to take hold. "Do it."

Frankie closed his eyes. The roar of the approaching horde seemed to fade into the background. 

He laid Sadie gently back onto the marble floor. 

He stood up and picked up the heavy brass stanchion. 

He didn't look down as he raised the massive weapon high above his head. He didn't look down as he brought it down with a sickening, definitive crunch. 

The Romero rule was enforced. Sadie would not rise. 

Frankie dropped the brass stanchion. He was completely alone. 

He looked up. The path he had carved through the horde had already closed behind him. He was entirely surrounded by thirty dead men, their milky white eyes locking onto the fresh blood dripping from his suit.


Chapter Eight: The Blowtorch

Frankie didn't charge the horde again. The brutal rage that had fueled his rescue attempt had vanished, replaced by a cold, pragmatic calculus of survival. 

He couldn't fight thirty zombies in open combat with a brass club. He needed a barricade. 

He spun around and sprinted toward the long, curved mahogany bar that dominated the east wall of the casino. 

He vaulted over the polished wood, crashing heavily into the shelves of expensive liquor behind it. Bottles of top-shelf gin and bourbon shattered, soaking his suit in high-proof alcohol. 

A moment later, the vanguard of the horde crashed against the front of the heavy mahogany bar. 

The thick wood groaned under the immense, unthinking pressure of thirty dead bodies, but it held. The zombies reached their ruined hands over the polished surface, their jaws snapping blindly at the air. 

Frankie scrambled backward, searching the narrow space behind the bar for anything he could use. 

His eyes fell on a heavy steel cylinder resting next to the icebox. It was a commercial blowtorch, normally used by the casino's ice sculptors to smooth out the elaborate swans and centerpieces for the high rollers. 

Next to it was a massive, shattered crate of imported Russian vodka, the 100-proof liquid pooling heavily on the floorboards. 

An idea, desperate and incredibly dangerous, formed in Frankie's mind. 

He grabbed the heavy blowtorch and cracked the brass valve. The hiss of pressurized gas filled the small space. He flicked the striker. A bright, blue jet of superheated flame roared to life. 

Frankie stepped up to the edge of the mahogany bar. 

The dead were packed tightly against the wood, a wall of blood-soaked rubber aprons and ruined tuxedos. 

Frankie didn't aim the blowtorch at them. He aimed it at the broken bottles of top-shelf liquor scattered across the bar top and the floor directly in front of the horde. 

He triggered the flame. 

The high-proof alcohol ignited instantly. A massive, roaring wall of orange fire erupted across the front of the mahogany bar, instantly engulfing the first row of zombies. 

The dead slaughterhouse workers didn't scream. They didn't retreat from the searing heat. The fire quickly caught the thick rubber of their aprons, sending thick, toxic black smoke billowing into the subterranean casino. 

The flames consumed their hair, blistered their pale skin, and boiled the necrotic blood in their veins. 

But the fire didn't destroy their brains. 

The Romero rule was absolute. Fire was a deterrent to the living, but it was merely an inconvenience to the dead. 

The burning zombies simply continued to press against the bar, their bodies engulfed in flames. The fire spread rapidly to the dead men behind them, turning the horde into a terrifying, advancing wall of fire. 

And then, the horrible truth of the tactic revealed itself. 

The burning zombies, ignoring the inferno consuming their bodies, began to climb over the heavy mahogany bar. 

They tumbled over the polished wood, a cascade of burning, thrashing corpses, landing heavily in the narrow space behind the bar. 

Frankie backed away, shielding his face from the intense heat. The entire bar was catching fire. The subterranean casino was rapidly turning into an oven. 

He had stopped them for a few seconds, but now he was trapped in a burning corner with a horde of flaming zombies closing in.


Chapter Nine: The Safe Cracker

The heat behind the bar was unbearable. The toxic black smoke from the burning rubber aprons was choking Frankie, blinding his eyes and searing his lungs. 

He couldn't stay here. The burning zombies were pulling themselves off the floor, their charred arms reaching for him through the flames. 

Frankie turned and ran. He kicked open the small wooden service door at the end of the bar, bursting back out into the main casino floor. 

The entire room was a vision of hell. The fire from the bar was spreading rapidly, catching the velvet drapes and the heavy felt of the gambling tables. 

Frankie stumbled through the thick smoke, his destination clear. 

The massive, polished steel door of Boss Moroni's private vault at the back of the room. 

Frankie knew he wasn't going to survive. He was out of weapons, the room was burning, and the flaming horde was shambling relentlessly toward him through the smoke. 

But Frankie "The Chin" Scalise wasn't going to die alone while the boss who betrayed him sat safely inside a steel box with a million dollars. 

Frankie reached the vault. The heavy steel locking bolts were firmly in place. Moroni was securely sealed inside. 

Frankie looked around desperately. Near the entrance to the vault alcove lay the ruined body of 'Fingers' Malone, the Syndicate's best safecracker. Malone had been a regular at the casino, and the horde had caught him early. 

Malone's heavy leather toolkit lay spilled across the marble floor. 

Frankie dropped to his knees and dug frantically through the scattered tools. Lock picks, stethoscopes, brass hammers... 

And there it was. 

Wrapped carefully in oilcloth at the bottom of the bag was a thick, yellow half-stick of commercial dynamite, already fitted with a blasting cap and a short length of slow-burn fuse. Malone always carried a little "persuasion" for the stubborn safes. 

Frankie grabbed the dynamite and scrambled back to the massive vault door. 

He didn't have time to drill. He shoved the stick of dynamite directly into the recessed gap between the heavy steel door frame and the primary locking bolt. 

He looked over his shoulder. 

The burning horde emerged from the thick black smoke. They were a terrifying sight—charred skeletons wrapped in melting rubber and burning flesh, their jaws still snapping mechanically, driven entirely by the unholy engine in their skulls. 

They were twenty feet away. 

Frankie pulled a gold Zippo lighter from his suit pocket. His hands were shaking so badly he dropped it twice. 

He picked it up, flipped the lid, and sparked the flint. 

The small flame illuminated his blood-spattered face. 

He held the flame to the short fuse. It caught instantly, sparking and hissing angrily as it burned rapidly toward the blasting cap. 

Frankie didn't run. There was nowhere left to run. He simply stood up, turned his back to the heavy steel door, and faced the burning horde. 

"Tell the boss I said hello," Frankie whispered to the dead.


Chapter Ten: The Jackpot

The heavy steel vault was completely silent. 

Boss Moroni sat heavily on a leather chair in the center of the ten-by-ten room, surrounded by absolute silence and obscene wealth. The thick steel walls completely muffled the screams, the gunfire, and the roar of the fire outside. 

He wiped the sweat from his forehead with a silk handkerchief. He had packed four heavy duffel bags with nearly two million dollars in cash and negotiable bonds. 

It was a tragedy, of course. Losing the casino, losing Frankie and Rocco. But Moroni was a businessman. He had survived the Castellammarese War; he would survive this "rabid flu." He just had to wait for the cops to clear the building above, then he would call a few aldermen, pay a few bribes, and quietly slip out the back with his fortune. 

He reached into his tuxedo jacket, pulled out a fresh Cuban cigar, and snipped the end with a gold cutter. 

He struck a match and raised it to the cigar. 

*BOOM.*

The explosion was deafening in the confined space of the vault. The concussive shockwave threw Moroni backward out of his chair, scattering millions of dollars across the steel floor. 

The massive steel locking bolts, designed to withstand hours of drilling, were violently blown completely out of their heavy brass brackets. 

The massive, impenetrable steel door of the vault slowly swung outward on its perfectly balanced hinges. 

Moroni scrambled backward, his ears ringing, his eyes wide with absolute shock. 

The scene outside the vault was a nightmare beyond his comprehension. 

The subterranean casino was an inferno. Thick, toxic black smoke filled the air, lit by the angry orange glow of the burning gambling tables. 

But the fire wasn't the worst part. 

Standing in the massive doorway of the vault were the charred, smoking remains of the slaughterhouse horde. 

They had just reached Frankie when the dynamite detonated. The blast had pulverized Frankie's body, showering the front line of the horde in fresh blood and bone. 

The explosion had knocked many of them down, but the Romero rule was absolute. Shrapnel to the chest didn't stop the brain. 

The burning dead slowly picked themselves up from the marble floor. 

They didn't look at Frankie's ruined remains. 

Through the thick smoke and the open vault door, their milky white eyes locked onto the fresh, unbitten, terrified flesh of Boss Moroni sitting on a pile of money. 

Moroni didn't have a gun. He had a cigar and two million dollars. 

He opened his mouth to scream, to offer them money, to beg. 

The burning horde surged forward, a wave of charred flesh and melting rubber pouring into the confined space of the massive steel vault. 

They descended on Boss Moroni in silence, their ruined jaws opening wide. 

The heavy steel door, pushed by the weight of the entering horde, slowly swung shut again, trapping the screams of the mob boss inside the impenetrable vault with his fortune and the dead.



Story Eleven: The Dirigible

Chapter One: The Maiden Voyage

The *Icarus* was a marvel of modern engineering, a magnificent silver leviathan floating silently ten thousand feet above the freezing, churning expanse of the North Atlantic. It was 1935, and the massive German zeppelin was halfway through its maiden voyage from New York to London. 

Inside the sleek, aerodynamic gondola suspended beneath the massive hydrogen envelope, the atmosphere was one of refined luxury. A string quartet played softly in the main dining room, while wealthy industrialists and socialites drank champagne and marveled at the panoramic view of the clouds. 

Clara Vance, a sharp-eyed investigative journalist for the *Chicago Tribune*, wasn't drinking. She was observing. She had talked her way onto the maiden voyage to write a piece on the future of aviation, but her instincts told her there was a darker story aboard. 

Her gaze settled on a wealthy New York heiress, Eleanor Vanderbilt, sitting at a corner table. Eleanor wasn't touching her caviar. She was sweating profusely, her normally pale skin flushed a dangerous, mottled red. Her hands trembled violently as she gripped the stem of her champagne flute. 

"She doesn't look well," Clara muttered to her notebook. 

Suddenly, Eleanor's eyes rolled backward. She gasped, a horrific, wet sound that cut through the polite conversation of the dining room. 

The heiress collapsed forward, her face smashing into her crystal plate, sending shattered glass and caviar scattering across the white linen tablecloth. 

Screams erupted from the surrounding tables. Waiters rushed forward. 

Clara didn't scream. She grabbed her notepad and pushed her way through the panicked crowd. She had spent the last two months in the Midwest covering the aftermath of the Dustbowl. She had seen the sickness. She knew the signs. 

Captain Von Kesser, a stern, impeccably uniformed German veteran, arrived from the bridge, pushing the crowd back. 

"Give her air!" the captain ordered, signaling for the ship's doctor. 

The doctor, a nervous young man, rolled Eleanor onto her back. Her breathing was incredibly shallow, her lips already turning a faint shade of blue. 

As the doctor loosened her silk collar, Clara saw it. 

High on Eleanor's pale neck, partially hidden by a pearl necklace, was a jagged, angry bite mark. The flesh around the puncture wounds was a bruised, necrotic black. 

"Captain," Clara said sharply, stepping forward. "That woman is infected. You need to quarantine her immediately."

Captain Von Kesser shot her a cold, dismissive glare. "Fraulein Vance, do not spread panic on my ship. The woman has simply fainted from the altitude."

"Look at her neck!" Clara insisted. "It's the sickness from the States!"

The captain ignored her, ordering the waiters to carry the unconscious heiress to the infirmary. He turned to Clara, his jaw tight. "You will return to your cabin, Fraulein. And you will stay there."


Chapter Two: The Doctor's Quarters

The infirmary of the *Icarus* was a small, pristine white room located at the aft end of the passenger gondola. 

The young doctor laid Eleanor on the examination table and checked her pulse. It was erratic, skipping beats, fading rapidly. He reached for his stethoscope, trying to listen to her heart. 

He didn't notice that her eyes were no longer closed. 

They were wide open, staring blankly at the sterile white ceiling. And they were a dull, milky white. 

"Fascinating," the doctor muttered, adjusting the earpieces of his stethoscope. "Complete systemic failure. I've never seen..."

He leaned down, pressing the cold metal disk against her chest. 

Eleanor's heart stopped. 

And then, the Romero rule engaged. 

The dead heiress lunged upward with terrifying, uninhibited strength. Her ruined jaw snapped shut around the doctor's neck, her teeth tearing effortlessly through his white collar and plunging into his carotid artery. 

The doctor didn't even have time to scream. A massive spray of bright red blood coated the pristine white walls of the infirmary. He collapsed onto the floor, clutching his ruined throat, dead within seconds. 

Eleanor slowly sat up on the examination table, her silk dress soaked in blood. She didn't look at the dead doctor on the floor. She turned her milky white eyes toward the heavy wooden door of the infirmary. 

Outside, in the narrow, carpeted corridor, Clara Vance was ignoring the captain's orders. She had picked the lock of her cabin with a hairpin and crept down to the infirmary, determined to get the story. 

She pressed her ear against the wooden door. 

Instead of the doctor's voice, she heard a wet, tearing sound, followed by a heavy thud. 

Clara's blood ran cold. She backed away from the door just as a heavy fist slammed against the wood from the inside. 

*BANG.*

The door rattled in its frame. 

Clara didn't wait. She turned and sprinted down the narrow corridor toward the main dining room, her heart hammering against her ribs. 

"Captain!" Clara screamed, bursting through the double doors into the luxurious, crowded dining room. "Captain Von Kesser!"

The music stopped. A hundred wealthy passengers turned to stare at the disheveled journalist. 

Captain Von Kesser stepped forward, his face flushed with anger. "Fraulein Vance, I gave you a direct order—"

He was cut off by the sound of splintering wood echoing down the corridor. 

The heavy doors to the dining room burst open. 

Eleanor Vanderbilt stood in the doorway, her elegant silk gown ruined, her face a mask of necrotic flesh and fresh blood. And standing directly behind her, his white coat stained crimson, was the ship's doctor. 

The maiden voyage of the *Icarus* had just become a flying tomb.


Chapter Three: The Dining Room Massacre

The silence in the dining room lasted for one heartbeat. Then, absolute chaos erupted. 

The horrified screams of a hundred wealthy passengers filled the confined space. Men in tuxedos shoved women aside, scrambling desperately toward the forward observation deck. Waiters dropped trays of crystal flutes, adding the sharp crash of breaking glass to the cacophony. 

"What is the meaning of this?!" Captain Von Kesser roared, stepping forward, unable to comprehend the nightmare unfolding on his perfect ship. 

Eleanor didn't answer. She lunged. 

The zombie heiress tackled an elderly banker in the front row, driving him onto a shattered dining table. She sank her teeth into his cheek, tearing a massive chunk of flesh away as the old man shrieked in agony. 

The dead doctor moved with a slow, relentless determination, grabbing a panicked waiter by the hair and driving a surgical scalpel repeatedly into the man's chest. 

"Gott im Himmel," Von Kesser whispered, the reality of the situation finally shattering his rigid discipline. 

He reached under his uniform jacket and drew a heavy Luger P08 pistol. 

"Back away!" the captain yelled, aiming the weapon at the dead doctor. 

The doctor ignored him, continuing to butcher the waiter. 

Von Kesser squeezed the trigger. 

The sharp crack of the 9mm round was deafening in the enclosed space. The bullet struck the doctor directly in the shoulder, spinning him around. But the dead man simply reoriented himself and lunged toward the captain. 

Von Kesser fired again, hitting the doctor in the chest. Again, the zombie didn't stop. 

Clara grabbed the captain's arm. "You have to shoot them in the head! The brain!"

Von Kesser shoved her away, his eyes wild. He raised the Luger, aiming for the doctor's milky white eyes. 

"Wait!" the Chief Engineer roared, bursting out of the forward access hatch. He grabbed the captain's gun hand, forcing the barrel down. "Captain, no!"

Von Kesser glared at the engineer. "Are you mad? They are killing the passengers!"

"Look at the walls, you fool!" the engineer screamed over the panic. 

Clara followed his gaze. 

The 9mm round that had passed cleanly through the doctor's shoulder had struck the elegant, mahogany-paneled wall of the dining room. The heavy slug had splintered the wood and torn straight through the thin duralumin outer skin of the gondola. 

Through the bullet hole, Clara could see the rushing, freezing clouds of the Atlantic storm outside. 

"The walls are thin!" the engineer yelled. "If you miss, or if the bullet over-penetrates, it goes straight through the roof!"

Von Kesser's face went pale. He finally understood. 

Directly above the thin ceiling of the passenger gondola were sixteen massive, fabric gas bags containing seven million cubic feet of highly flammable hydrogen gas. 

"If a bullet strikes the steel framework up there and sparks," the engineer gasped, "the entire ship will incinerate in seconds."

They were trapped in a metal tube ten thousand feet in the air with a growing horde of the walking dead, and they couldn't fire a single shot to defend themselves.


Chapter Four: The Catwalks

"Secure your weapons!" Captain Von Kesser roared to the two armed crewmen who had just arrived from the bridge. "No firing! We fight them hand-to-hand!"

The crewmen stared at him in disbelief, but the strict discipline of the zeppelin crew held. They holstered their sidearms and grabbed heavy brass fire extinguishers and wooden chairs. 

It was a desperate, brutal slaughter. 

Without the ability to safely use firearms, the crew and passengers were forced into close-quarters combat with the infected. The zombies were relentless, ignoring the heavy blows from chairs and bottles that shattered their ribs and arms. 

"Captain!" Clara yelled, using a heavy silver serving tray to smash a crawling zombie in the face. "We can't hold them here! They're turning the passengers!"

It was true. The elderly banker Eleanor had attacked was already rising from the shattered table, his milky white eyes fixing on the screaming crowd. The infection was spreading exponentially within the confined space. 

"Retreat to the axial catwalk!" Von Kesser ordered the crew. He grabbed Clara's arm. "Follow me!"

They fought their way through the panicked crowd, using heavy brass candlesticks to batter the dead aside. They reached the aft access hatch, a heavy duralumin door that led out of the luxury gondola and up into the massive, echoing interior hull of the zeppelin. 

Von Kesser hauled the heavy door open. "Go! Go!"

Clara scrambled through the hatch, finding herself standing on a narrow, two-foot-wide aluminum catwalk suspended in absolute darkness. 

The interior of the *Icarus* was a breathtaking, terrifying cavern. It was a massive cylinder of intricate duralumin framework, stretching eight hundred feet into the darkness. Towering above the fragile catwalks were the massive, billowing fabric bags holding the millions of cubic feet of hydrogen gas. 

The air smelled intensely of rubber, engine oil, and the faint, sweet scent of the highly flammable lifting gas. 

Von Kesser climbed through the hatch behind her and slammed it shut, throwing the heavy locking bolts just as a dozen infected passengers slammed into the other side. 

"We are safe here for the moment," Von Kesser gasped, leaning against the cold metal of the hatch. 

"No, we're not," Clara said, pointing her flashlight down the narrow catwalk. 

The aft access hatch wasn't the only way into the interior hull. A secondary ventilation shaft from the infirmary connected directly to the lower catwalk. 

Crawling out of the narrow shaft, their white uniforms stained with blood, were the dead doctor and Eleanor Vanderbilt. 

They stood up on the narrow aluminum walkway, their milky white eyes locking onto the bright beam of Clara's flashlight. 

"They're inside the hull," Clara whispered, her voice trembling. 

They were trapped on a two-foot-wide aluminum bridge suspended over a thousand-foot drop, surrounded by millions of cubic feet of explosive gas, with the walking dead advancing toward them in the dark.


Chapter Five: The Fire Axe

The narrow catwalk offered no room for evasion. It was a straight line through the massive, echoing belly of the zeppelin. A single misstep meant plunging through the thin fabric envelope at the bottom of the hull and falling ten thousand feet into the freezing Atlantic. 

"Stay behind me, Fraulein," Von Kesser ordered, his voice echoing eerily in the massive cavern. He reached out and grabbed a heavy, red-painted fire axe mounted on a structural support beam. 

The dead doctor shambled forward, his ruined arms outstretched. 

Von Kesser didn't hesitate. The captain swung the heavy fire axe in a wide, desperate arc. 

The sharp steel blade caught the doctor directly in the side of the neck, cleaving through the collarbone and burying itself deep in the chest cavity. 

But the Romero rule dictated that body trauma was useless. 

The doctor didn't fall. He simply reached out and grabbed the handle of the axe, pulling himself closer to the captain. 

"Gott verdammt!" Von Kesser cursed, trying to wrench the axe free. 

The doctor lunged, snapping his teeth at the captain's face. 

Von Kesser let go of the axe, stepped back, and delivered a brutal kick to the zombie's chest. 

The dead doctor stumbled backward, losing his footing on the slick aluminum grating. He teetered on the edge of the narrow walkway for a horrifying second, his arms windmilling uselessly. 

Then, he fell. 

The zombie plummeted silently into the darkness below. A moment later, there was a sharp, tearing sound as the falling body ripped straight through the thin outer fabric envelope at the bottom of the hull. 

The doctor was gone, plummeting ten thousand feet to the ocean below. 

But Eleanor was still coming. 

The dead heiress stepped over the spot where the doctor had fallen, her milky white eyes fixed on Clara. She was moving faster now, unhindered by the heavy silk gown that was now torn to shreds. 

Von Kesser had no weapon. The heavy Luger at his hip was useless in the explosive atmosphere of the hull. 

Eleanor lunged. 

Clara didn't wait for the captain to act. She dropped her flashlight and charged forward. 

She slammed her shoulder directly into Eleanor's chest, using the element of surprise and the narrow confines of the catwalk to her advantage. 

The impact drove the dead heiress backward. Eleanor stumbled, her heel catching on an exposed rivet. 

She fell backward, but her hand lashed out, grabbing the thick wool of Captain Von Kesser's uniform sleeve. 

"No!" Von Kesser roared as Eleanor's dead weight pulled him forward over the edge of the catwalk. 

Clara lunged, grabbing the captain's thick leather belt just as his boots slipped off the aluminum grating. 

Eleanor was dangling over the abyss, holding onto the captain's sleeve with terrifying, dead strength, her ruined jaw snapping furiously at his dangling boots. 

Clara strained against the weight of the two bodies, her boots slipping on the metal. "I can't... hold you!" 

Von Kesser looked down at the snapping jaw inches from his foot. Then he looked up at Clara. 

"Save the ship, Fraulein," the captain said calmly. 

He reached into his pocket with his free hand and pulled out his heavy silver pocket watch. He brought it down with brutal force, smashing the heavy metal against Eleanor's wrist. 

The dead fingers slipped. 

Eleanor Vanderbilt and Captain Von Kesser plummeted silently into the darkness, tearing through the bottom envelope and disappearing into the freezing night sky. 

Clara collapsed onto the cold aluminum grating, completely alone in the massive, dark belly of the silver leviathan.



Chapter Six: The Breach

Clara lay shivering on the narrow aluminum catwalk, staring into the black abyss where the captain had fallen. The roar of the freezing wind tearing through the ruptured outer fabric at the bottom of the hull was deafening. 

But it wasn't the only sound. 

A sharp, metallic clanging echoed from the aft access hatch fifty feet behind her. 

The horde in the passenger gondola had finally broken through the heavy duralumin doors. 

Clara scrambled to her feet, picking up the flashlight she had dropped. The beam illuminated a terrifying sight. Dozens of infected passengers, their evening wear torn and bloodied, were pouring through the hatch and onto the narrow catwalk. 

They weren't moving with the sluggish, stumbling gait she had expected. Driven by a blind, predatory instinct, they were scaling the aluminum framework, crawling along the structural beams and support wires like a swarm of grotesque, white-eyed spiders. 

Clara turned and ran. 

The axial catwalk was a straight, terrifying path through the center of the zeppelin, running between the massive, billowing hydrogen bags. The air was getting thicker, the sweet smell of the lifting gas becoming overpowering. 

As she ran, Clara heard a horrific tearing sound from above. 

She pointed her flashlight upward. 

A dozen zombies had climbed the structural framework to reach the massive gas bags. Unthinking, driven only by the violent frenzy of the sickness, they were attacking the fabric. They were using their teeth and torn fingernails to rip at the massive bladders. 

The thick, rubberized cotton was strong, but the sheer volume of the horde was too much. 

*RIIIIIP.*

A massive tear appeared in the side of Gas Cell Number Seven. 

The sound of the escaping hydrogen was a high-pitched, terrifying hiss. The massive volume of highly explosive gas was venting rapidly into the enclosed interior hull. 

The *Icarus* was no longer just a flying tomb; it was an active bomb. 

Clara knew that if a single spark ignited the ambient hydrogen mixing with the oxygen inside the hull, the entire ship would turn into a fireball in a fraction of a second. 

She had to reach the forward control gondola. She had to send a mayday to any ship on the ocean below. She was an investigative journalist; she had to get the story out before the ship was destroyed. 

She sprinted down the catwalk, the aluminum grating clanging loudly under her boots. 

The zombies on the framework above were dropping down onto the catwalk behind her, cutting off her escape route to the aft of the ship. 

Ahead of her, the forward access hatch to the control car loomed in the darkness. 

She reached the heavy duralumin door and threw the locking wheel. It spun freely. 

She yanked the door open and tumbled into the pristine, brightly lit control car of the *Icarus*. 

She slammed the door shut and threw the heavy deadbolt, locking the terrifying cavern of the interior hull behind her. 

She turned around, gasping for air. 

The control car was silent. The massive wooden steering wheels for the rudders and elevators were abandoned. The navigation charts were scattered across the floor. 

The crew was dead. 

And in the corner of the room, sitting slumped over the radio console, was the Chief Communications Officer. 

His uniform was torn, and a massive pool of blood was expanding across the deck beneath his chair. 

Clara drew a deep breath and approached him slowly. 

As she stepped closer, the radio operator slowly turned his head. His eyes were milky white, and his jaw was stained with his own blood.


Chapter Seven: The Radio Room

The radio operator didn't stand. He simply reached out with a bloody hand, his jaw snapping weakly. The infection was fresh, his body still weakened by massive blood loss. 

Clara didn't hesitate. She had seen the Romero rule enforced. She knew what had to be done. 

She grabbed a heavy brass fire extinguisher from its bracket on the wall. She stepped forward, raising the heavy cylinder high above her head. 

"I'm sorry," she whispered. 

She brought the extinguisher down with brutal force, crushing the radio operator's skull and destroying the brain instantly. The dead man slumped forward over the console, motionless. 

Clara dropped the extinguisher, her hands shaking violently. She pushed the dead body out of the chair and slid into the seat. 

She was a journalist, not a pilot, but she knew the basics of a Marconi radio set. She flipped the main power toggle and grabbed the heavy Bakelite microphone. 

"Mayday, Mayday, Mayday," Clara said, her voice trembling but clear. "This is the airship *Icarus*, on route from New York to London. We are experiencing a catastrophic biohazard event. The passengers and crew are dead and reanimated. Repeat, the dead are walking."

She paused, listening to the heavy static of the Atlantic storm on the receiver. 

"The captain is dead. The hull has been breached. The hydrogen cells are compromised. If anyone is listening... we are going down."

She waited. For ten agonizing seconds, there was only the hiss of static. 

Then, a faint, heavily accented voice broke through the noise. 

"Airship *Icarus*, this is the freighter *Bremen*, positioned two hundred miles east of your coordinates. We are receiving your transmission. Please repeat... the dead are walking?"

A surge of desperate hope filled Clara's chest. "Yes! It's the sickness from the States. It's on board. We need immediate assistance!"

"*Icarus*, we cannot assist. We are weathering a severe gale. Even if we could reach you, we have no means of boarding an airship at altitude."

Clara's heart sank. She was in the middle of the ocean, thousands of feet in the air. 

"I understand," Clara said softly. "But you have to broadcast this warning. The sickness... it doesn't kill them. It changes them. They don't stop unless you destroy the brain."

"Understood, *Icarus*. We will relay your warning."

A loud, metallic crash from the access hatch interrupted the transmission. 

The horde from the interior hull had reached the door. They were battering the heavy duralumin with terrifying, unthinking force. 

The heavy locking bolts began to groan under the immense pressure. 

"They're breaking in," Clara said into the microphone. "I have to try for the escape baskets. This is Clara Vance of the *Chicago Tribune*, signing off."

She dropped the microphone and turned away from the console. 

The *Icarus* was equipped with four small, emergency parachute baskets, located in a narrow bay just aft of the control car. 

It was a desperate, near-suicidal gamble in a hurricane, but it was her only chance. 

She ran to the starboard escape bay door. 

As she reached for the handle, the heavy duralumin access hatch to the interior hull finally gave way. The metal buckled and tore, and the horde of dead passengers poured into the control car.


Chapter Eight: The Escape Basket

The control car was instantly filled with the walking dead. 

Clara threw the heavy lever on the escape bay door. The small, circular hatch hissed open, revealing a tiny wicker basket suspended over the open air by heavy steel cables. Below the basket, the freezing black abyss of the Atlantic storm churned violently. 

She stepped into the small basket and reached for the quick-release lever that would deploy the massive silk parachute and drop her away from the doomed zeppelin. 

But she wasn't fast enough. 

A massive zombie, a former banker in a torn tuxedo, lunged forward. He didn't reach for Clara; he simply fell forward into the open bay, his dead weight crashing down onto the wicker rim of the basket. 

The basket swayed violently, nearly pitching Clara out into the storm. 

The banker's ruined hands clamped onto the edge of the basket, his milky white eyes fixing on her. He began to pull himself into the small space, his jaw snapping furiously. 

"No!" Clara screamed, kicking him in the face. 

Her heavy leather boot struck him squarely in the nose, shattering the cartilage, but the zombie didn't even blink. He continued to pull himself up. 

Behind him, three more zombies poured into the bay, their hands reaching out for the open hatch. 

Clara knew she couldn't fight them all in the tiny basket. If they got in, the combined weight would overload the release mechanism, or they would tear her apart before she hit the water. 

She needed a weapon, but the fire extinguisher was back in the control car. 

She looked down. The banker was pulling himself up over the rim, his face inches from her boots. 

Clara didn't kick him again. She reached down and grabbed the heavy brass quick-release lever. 

She didn't pull it to drop the basket. She ripped the heavy lever completely out of its housing, breaking the release mechanism permanently. 

The basket was now locked in place, dangling uselessly beneath the control car. 

But Clara now had a heavy, two-foot length of solid brass pipe in her hand. 

She raised the brass pipe and brought it down with all her strength onto the top of the banker's skull. 

The heavy metal crushed the cranium, destroying the brain instantly. The dead man went limp, his body slipping backward out of the bay and plummeting ten thousand feet into the ocean. 

Clara stood in the doorway of the basket, the heavy brass pipe gripped tightly in her hands. 

The three remaining zombies in the bay lunged forward. 

Clara swung the pipe in a wide, desperate arc. 

The heavy brass struck the first zombie in the side of the head, shattering the temple and dropping the woman to the floor. 

Clara didn't hesitate. She stepped forward, driving the end of the pipe directly into the face of the second zombie, pulverizing the frontal lobe. 

The third zombie, a young steward, tackled her around the waist, driving her backward into the wicker basket. 

Clara fell hard, the heavy brass pipe slipping from her grasp. 

The dead steward crawled on top of her, his ruined jaw opening wide to tear at her throat. 

Clara struggled desperately, shoving her forearms against the zombie's chest, trying to keep those snapping teeth away from her flesh. 

She was losing. The dead strength was overwhelming. 

She needed the pipe. She reached out blindly with her right hand, her fingers scraping against the wicker floor of the basket. 

Her fingers brushed against cold metal. 

But it wasn't the brass pipe.


Chapter Nine: The Spark

Clara's fingers had found the heavy steel locking pin of the parachute deployment mechanism. 

She didn't have time to think. She gripped the cold steel and yanked it hard. 

The massive silk parachute, packed tightly above the basket, deployed with a sharp, explosive hiss. 

The sudden deployment of the chute in the violent, freezing winds of the Nor'easter was catastrophic. 

The heavy canvas lines snapped taut with terrifying force, violently yanking the small wicker basket completely out of its moorings beneath the control car. 

The sudden jolt threw the dead steward backward, his snapping jaws missing Clara's throat by a fraction of an inch. 

Clara screamed as the basket was ripped away from the massive silver leviathan of the *Icarus*. 

She was suddenly suspended in the freezing, roaring chaos of the hurricane, swinging wildly beneath the massive silk canopy. 

She looked up. 

The *Icarus* was above her, a magnificent, doomed silver cigar silhouetted against the dark storm clouds. 

But she wasn't alone in the sky. 

As the parachute deployed, the heavy steel cables that had anchored the basket to the control car whipped violently through the air. 

One of the heavy steel cables, trailing a jagged, broken brass coupling, lashed upward, striking the thin duralumin outer skin of the zeppelin's massive hull. 

The impact was violent. The jagged brass tore a ten-foot gash in the fabric envelope, exposing the massive, hydrogen-filled gas bags inside. 

But that wasn't the fatal blow. 

As the heavy steel cable whipped across the aluminum framework of the hull, the metal scraped against metal with terrifying force. 

A shower of bright, brilliant sparks erupted from the friction. 

In a normal atmosphere, the sparks would have been harmless. 

But the interior of the *Icarus* was no longer a normal atmosphere. 

The massive tear in Gas Cell Number Seven, caused by the climbing horde, had filled the entire cavernous interior hull with millions of cubic feet of highly explosive, leaked hydrogen gas. 

The air was saturated. It was a perfectly mixed, incredibly volatile bomb, waiting for a single catalyst. 

The shower of sparks from the whipping cable provided that catalyst. 

Clara, swinging wildly in the freezing darkness beneath her parachute, watched in absolute horror as the spark found the gas.


Chapter Ten: The Fire in the Sky

There was no sound at first. Just a sudden, blinding flash of pure, white-hot light that illuminated the entire storm system for miles around. 

The millions of cubic feet of hydrogen gas inside the *Icarus* ignited simultaneously. 

The massive silver leviathan didn't just burn; it detonated. 

The incredible force of the explosion instantly vaporized the thin duralumin outer skin, sending a shockwave of superheated air tearing outward through the freezing storm. 

The massive framework of the zeppelin was torn apart in a fraction of a second. The forward control car, filled with the walking dead, was blown completely clear of the ship, plummeting toward the ocean in a twisted mass of burning metal and charred flesh. 

Clara shielded her eyes from the searing heat, the force of the blast violently rocking her small parachute basket. 

The *Icarus* was a towering, magnificent inferno in the night sky. The fire roared with a terrifying intensity, the massive, burning skeleton of the airship plunging rapidly toward the freezing Atlantic below. 

The walking dead inside the hull, the wealthy passengers and the doomed crew, were incinerated instantly, the Romero rule rendered entirely moot by the sheer, absolute destruction of the fire. 

Clara watched, suspended in the freezing air, as the burning wreckage struck the surface of the ocean. 

The impact sent a massive plume of steam and boiling water hundreds of feet into the air. The fire hissed and fought against the freezing waves for a few desperate minutes, but the ocean was vast and uncompromising. 

Slowly, the flames were extinguished. The massive, twisted skeleton of the *Icarus* slipped beneath the dark, churning surface, taking its nightmare cargo down into the crushing depths. 

Clara was alone in the sky. 

The freezing wind howled around her, driving the freezing rain into her face. She was miles from land, suspended over a hurricane-swept ocean in the middle of the night. 

Her hands were numb, and the bitter cold was already seeping deep into her bones. She knew she couldn't survive long in the freezing water once she landed. 

She looked down at the dark, churning ocean below, the grave of the *Icarus* and its horrific secret. 

She had gotten the story. She had sent the warning. 

The sickness from the Dustbowl was no longer confined to the land. It was spreading across the oceans, carried by the very machines that were supposed to conquer the world. 

Clara Vance closed her eyes as the small wicker basket slowly drifted down toward the freezing, violent embrace of the Atlantic. 

There were no happy endings in 1935. There was only the cold, and the dark, and the dead.



Story Twelve: The Final Stand

Chapter One: The Dust Storm

Camp Omega was not built for comfort; it was built for survival. 

Located in the dead center of the Oklahoma Panhandle in 1938, it was one of the last remaining military quarantine zones in the Dustbowl. The base was surrounded by thick concrete walls, reinforced with concertina wire and high-voltage electric fences. Inside, a skeleton crew of exhausted, terrified soldiers and a handful of scientists guarded the last vestiges of federal authority. 

And they guarded the people. Three hundred uninfected civilians huddled in the subterranean bunkers beneath the motor pool, praying for an evacuation train that hadn't run in months. 

Sergeant Miller stood on the north watchtower, wiping the gritty, red dirt from the lenses of his binoculars. He was a hardened veteran of the zombie war, his face a map of scars and sun-baked leather. 

He looked out over the desolate, ruined landscape. The horizon was completely obscured by a massive, churning wall of black earth. 

It was a "black blizzard," the most severe type of dust storm the region produced. And this one was monstrous, stretching miles into the sky and rolling toward the camp like a tidal wave of suffocating darkness. 

"Sergeant!" 

Miller looked down. Dr. Aris Thorne, the camp's lead civilian scientist, was standing at the base of the tower, holding his seven-year-old daughter, Sarah, tightly by the hand. 

"The atmospheric pressure is dropping rapidly," Thorne yelled over the rising wind. "The generators won't be able to handle the particulate intake. We need to shut them down or the engines will seize!"

Miller's stomach tightened. The massive diesel generators powered the floodlights and, more importantly, the high-voltage electric fences that kept the wandering hordes at bay. 

"If we cut the power, we lose the fence, Doc," Miller yelled back. 

"If the engines seize, we lose the fence forever!" Thorne argued. "And we lose the ventilation to the civilian bunkers! They'll suffocate down there!"

Miller knew Thorne was right. He keyed the heavy radio handset clipped to his webbing. 

"Command, this is Tower One. Kill the main generators. Switch to auxiliary battery power for the interior lights only."

A moment later, the deep, reassuring hum of the massive diesel engines died. The floodlights around the perimeter flickered and went dark. The lethal crackle of the electric fences fell silent. 

The black blizzard hit the camp a minute later. 

The wind screamed, driving the fine, red topsoil into every crack and crevice of the base. Visibility dropped to absolute zero. The air became a thick, abrasive soup that choked the lungs and scoured exposed skin. 

Miller pulled a heavy canvas mask over his face and gripped the heavy .50 caliber machine gun mounted on the tower railing. 

Through the roaring wind and the suffocating darkness, he heard a sound that chilled him to the bone. 

It wasn't the wind. It was a low, collective moan, accompanied by the shuffling of thousands of dragging feet. 

The horde, which had been aimlessly wandering the wasteland for weeks, had used the cover of the black blizzard to approach the walls unseen. 

And now, the electric fences were dead.


Chapter Two: The Breach

The attack didn't begin with a coordinated assault. The dead didn't have tactics. They only had hunger and numbers. 

The vanguard of the horde blundered blindly into the concrete walls of Camp Omega, drawn by the scent of the living huddled inside. 

"Contact!" Miller roared, though no one could hear him over the screaming wind. 

He blindly aimed the heavy .50 caliber machine gun into the black dust and squeezed the butterfly trigger. 

The massive weapon roared, spitting out heavy armor-piercing rounds that tore through the darkness. Even without visibility, the sheer density of the horde meant every bullet found a target. The heavy slugs shattered dead skulls, severed limbs, and tore through rotten torsos. 

But the Romero rule was unforgiving. Unless the brain was destroyed, the dead kept moving. And for every zombie Miller dropped, ten more pressed forward against the wall. 

Down in the courtyard, panic erupted. The remaining soldiers of the garrison grabbed their Springfield rifles and rushed to the reinforced main gates. 

The gates were built of heavy steel plate, designed to withstand a truck ramming them. But they were not designed for the slow, relentless pressure of ten thousand bodies. 

The horde didn't try to climb the walls. The dead simply piled against the steel gates, wave after wave of rotting flesh pressing forward. The front ranks were crushed to death against the steel by the immense weight of the thousands behind them, their bodies forming a ramp of shattered bone and ruined meat. 

The heavy steel hinges of the main gates began to groan under the unimaginable strain. 

"Hold the line!" a young lieutenant screamed, his voice cracking with terror as he fired his rifle into the mass pressing over the top of the gates. 

*CRACK.*

The right hinge of the massive steel gate snapped with the sound of a cannon shot. 

The gate buckled inward, tearing free from the concrete frame. 

The horde poured into the courtyard of Camp Omega like a flood of rotting water. 

Miller swung the .50 caliber machine gun around, trying to cover the breach. But the heavy weapon jammed, the fine, abrasive dust of the black blizzard completely clogging the complex firing mechanism. 

Miller cursed, drawing his heavy Colt M1911 sidearm. 

The courtyard was instantly overwhelmed. The soldiers, blinded by the dust and completely outnumbered, were dragged down into the bloody mud. The screams of the living mixed with the low, terrifying moans of the dead. 

Rifles jammed. Bayonets stuck in dead ribs. The soldiers were forced into brutal, hand-to-hand combat using entrenching tools and combat knives against an enemy that felt no pain and never tired. 

The defense of Camp Omega had collapsed in less than five minutes. 

Miller knew the surface was lost. He grabbed his radio. 

"Doc!" Miller yelled into the handset. "The gates are breached! Get the civilians into the deep bunker and lock the blast doors! Do not open them for anyone!"


Chapter Three: The Bunker

Deep beneath the motor pool, the subterranean command bunker of Camp Omega was a claustrophobic maze of concrete corridors and heavy steel doors. 

Dr. Aris Thorne heard Miller's frantic warning over the shortwave radio. 

"Sarah, stay close to me," Thorne said, his voice trembling as he grabbed his daughter's hand. 

The bunker was already in a state of absolute panic. The three hundred civilians, hearing the screams and gunfire echoing down the ventilation shafts from the surface, were terrified. 

Thorne pushed his way through the crying, frantic crowd toward the primary blast doors. 

He reached the heavy steel wheel that controlled the locking mechanism.
