"Wait!" a woman screamed, grabbing Thorne's arm. "My husband is still up there! He's on the perimeter guard!"

Thorne looked at the woman, his eyes filled with sorrow. "I'm sorry. The sergeant said the perimeter is lost. If we don't lock these doors now, they will all get in, and everyone down here will die."

He grabbed the heavy steel wheel and began to turn it. 

The massive, foot-thick steel doors slowly began to close, groaning on their heavy hinges. 

Just before the doors sealed shut, a figure stumbled out of the dusty gloom of the stairwell. 

It was Sergeant Miller. His uniform was torn to shreds, and his face was covered in a mixture of red dust and dark blood. He was dragging a wounded soldier by the webbing of his gear. 

"Hold the door!" Miller roared, hauling the bleeding soldier through the narrowing gap. 

Thorne stopped turning the wheel. Miller and the wounded man tumbled into the bunker. 

Behind them, the stairwell was filled with the horrific, shuffling sounds of the descending horde. 

Miller didn't pause to catch his breath. He grabbed the heavy steel wheel and helped Thorne spin it violently. 

The massive blast doors slammed shut with a definitive, ringing *CLANG*. Miller threw the heavy locking bolts, sealing the bunker from the nightmare above. 

The immediate silence was deafening, broken only by the ragged breathing of the survivors and the crying of the terrified children. 

Miller collapsed against the cold steel of the door, sliding down to the concrete floor. He was exhausted, battered, and bleeding from a dozen minor cuts. 

"Did anyone else make it?" Thorne asked quietly. 

Miller shook his head. "No. The entire garrison is gone. They swarmed the barracks. They're tearing the camp apart up there."

He looked at the wounded soldier he had dragged inside. The young man was pale, clutching his stomach where a zombie had torn a deep, jagged gash through his uniform. 

The soldier's eyes were wide with shock, staring blankly at the concrete ceiling. 

"We need to get him to the infirmary," Thorne said, stepping forward. 

Miller put a heavy hand on Thorne's chest, stopping him. The sergeant's eyes were cold and uncompromising. 

"He's not going to the infirmary, Doc," Miller said softly. "Look at the wound. It's a bite."

The reality of the Romero rule settled heavily over the bunker. The young soldier wasn't just wounded. He was infected. 

And in a sealed, subterranean bunker packed with three hundred panicked civilians, an infected man was a ticking time bomb.


Chapter Four: The Ventilation Shaft

The execution of the bitten soldier was a quiet, grim affair. Miller did it himself, using his Colt M1911 with a makeshift suppressor fashioned from an oil filter, to avoid a panic among the civilians in the main holding area. 

The body was dragged into an empty storage closet and locked away. 

For the next six hours, the bunker was a tense, claustrophobic purgatory. Above them, the black blizzard raged on, and the muffled sounds of the dead tearing apart the surface camp echoed down through the concrete. 

The auxiliary batteries were holding, providing dim, flickering light to the subterranean corridors, but the air was growing stale and uncomfortably warm. 

Thorne found Miller sitting in the communications room, staring blankly at a dead radio console. 

"The air quality is dropping, Sergeant," Thorne said, checking a portable atmospheric monitor. "Carbon dioxide levels are rising dangerously in the main civilian holding area."

Miller frowned. "The ventilation system should be running on the batteries."

"It is," Thorne replied. "The fans are turning. But they aren't drawing any fresh air from the surface."

Miller stood up, a cold realization hitting him. "The dust storm. The intake filters must be completely clogged with dirt."

"Or something else," Thorne added grimly. 

Miller grabbed a heavy flashlight and a trench shotgun. "Show me the primary intake shaft."

Thorne led him through a maze of narrow maintenance corridors to a heavy iron grate set into the concrete wall. Behind the grate was a narrow, vertical shaft that stretched forty feet straight up to the surface. 

Miller peered through the iron bars. The shaft was pitch black. The massive intake fans at the bottom were whining in protest, struggling to pull air through a complete blockage. 

"I have to go up there and clear it," Miller said, unslinging the shotgun. "If we don't get fresh air, three hundred people suffocate in the dark."

"It's suicide," Thorne warned. "The top of that shaft opens directly into the motor pool. The horde is up there."

"Then I'll try not to make any noise," Miller said grimly. 

He unbolted the heavy iron grate and squeezed his broad shoulders into the narrow, claustrophobic shaft. 

The climb was agonizing. The concrete walls were slick with condensation, and the air inside the shaft was thick with the suffocating red dust of the black blizzard. Miller had to wedge his back against one wall and his boots against the other, slowly shimmying his way upward in total darkness. 

He reached the top of the shaft after twenty grueling minutes. 

His flashlight beam illuminated the problem. It wasn't just dust clogging the heavy steel intake grating. 

The storm had blown the protective housing off the vent, and the blind, stumbling zombies above were falling into the open hole. 

Three dead soldiers were wedged tightly against the steel grating, their ruined bodies completely blocking the airflow. They were still moving, their jaws snapping weakly as they lay crushed against the metal bars. 

Miller had to clear them. 

He drew his combat knife. He couldn't use the shotgun in the confined space without deafening himself and risking a ricochet. 

He reached through the steel grating, grabbed the nearest zombie by the uniform collar, and drove the heavy combat knife directly into its eye socket, destroying the brain. 

He struggled to pull the dead weight out of the way. 

As he shifted the first body, the second zombie suddenly lunged downward, its ruined face pressing against the steel grating. 

The dead hands reached through the bars, grabbing Miller's uniform. 

Miller cursed, trying to stab upward with the knife, but the angle was awkward. 

The zombie snapped its jaw violently, its teeth catching the sleeve of Miller's uniform. 

The fabric tore. 

Miller felt a sharp, burning pain in his left forearm.


Chapter Five: The Bite

Miller roared in pain and rage, driving the heavy combat knife upward through the steel grating with all his remaining strength. The blade pierced the underside of the zombie's jaw and drove straight up into the brain pan. 

The dead soldier went limp. 

Miller shoved the heavy corpse aside, finally clearing enough space for the intake fans below to pull a rush of fresh, albeit dusty, air into the shaft. 

The blockage was cleared. The bunker would breathe. 

But Miller's victory was entirely hollow. 

He slid rapidly back down the narrow concrete shaft, landing heavily on the metal grating at the bottom. 

Dr. Thorne was waiting for him, his face pale in the beam of the flashlight. 

"Did you clear it?" Thorne asked, reaching out to help the sergeant stand. 

Miller didn't take the doctor's hand. He stumbled backward, leaning heavily against the concrete wall. He was breathing hard, sweat pouring down his dusty face. 

He looked down at his left arm. 

The thick fabric of his uniform sleeve was torn. Beneath it, a jagged, bloody bite mark marred his forearm. The skin around the puncture wounds was already beginning to discolor, turning a sickly, bruised purple. 

The venomous fever of the Romero sickness was fast and completely uncompromising. 

Thorne saw the bite. He gasped, taking a step backward, his eyes wide with horror. 

"Oh, God. Sergeant..."

Miller didn't panic. He was a soldier. He had spent the last two years fighting the dead; he knew exactly what the bite meant. His war was over. He was a casualty. 

He looked at Thorne, his eyes cold and pragmatic. 

"Lock the maintenance door, Doc," Miller said, his voice terrifyingly calm. "I'm not going back into the main bunker."

"Sergeant, we might have time. We might be able to—"

"There is no cure, Thorne," Miller snapped, the fever already making his head pound. "You know that better than anyone. I've got maybe an hour before the fever kills me, and then I get back up and kill you and everyone else down here."

Miller unslung the trench shotgun and handed it to Thorne. Then he drew his Colt M1911 and checked the magazine. Six rounds. 

"Take the shotgun," Miller ordered. "Go back to the communications room and lock the door from the outside. Leave me in this corridor."

"I can't just leave you here to turn," Thorne argued, a desperate edge entering his voice. 

"You don't have a choice," Miller said, his vision beginning to blur as the infection raced through his bloodstream. "I'm going to lock myself in the armory down the hall. I'll do it myself before the fever takes me. Just make sure the door is locked behind you."

Thorne stared at the hardened veteran, the weight of the impossible situation crushing him. 

He looked at Miller's bitten arm. Then he looked at the heavy combat knife still clutched in Miller's right hand. 

An idea, desperate, brutal, and entirely unscientific, formed in Thorne's mind. 

"No," Thorne said softly. "You're not dying in the armory, Sergeant."

Miller glared at him. "Doc, don't be a fool. I'm a dead man walking."

"Not if the infection hasn't reached your brain yet," Thorne said, his eyes locking onto the heavy combat knife. "The fever spreads through the blood. If we stop the blood..."

Miller looked at the knife in his hand, then at his bitten arm. He understood what the doctor was suggesting. It was a primitive, horrifying gamble, born of absolute desperation. 

"You've got to be kidding me," Miller whispered. 

"It's the only chance you have," Thorne said, stepping forward. "But we have to do it right now."



Chapter Six: The Amputation

There was no time for anesthesia. There was no time for sterile technique. There was only the brutal, immediate necessity of stopping the venomous blood from reaching Miller's heart. 

"Bite down on this," Thorne said, shoving a thick leather belt into Miller's mouth. 

Miller leaned back against the cold concrete wall, his face pale and sweating profusely from the rapidly advancing fever. He clamped his teeth onto the thick leather and nodded curtly. 

Thorne grabbed a length of heavy rubber tubing from an emergency medical kit on the wall and wrapped it tightly around Miller's bicep, creating a crude tourniquet to slow the blood flow. 

"Hold your arm out," Thorne ordered, his voice remarkably steady despite the horrific task ahead. 

Miller extended his left arm, the purple discoloration of the bite already spreading up his forearm. 

Thorne didn't use the combat knife. It wasn't sharp enough to cut through bone cleanly. He went to a nearby emergency fire cabinet and grabbed a heavy, red-painted crash axe. 

"This is going to be the worst pain of your life," Thorne said, gripping the heavy wooden handle of the axe with both hands. 

Miller didn't blink. He just grunted around the leather belt. 

Thorne raised the heavy axe high above his head. He didn't aim for the joint. He aimed for the center of the humerus, halfway between the elbow and the shoulder, well above the spreading purple veins. 

He brought the heavy steel blade down with terrifying force. 

The axe cleaved through the thick fabric of the uniform, the muscle, and the heavy bone with a sickening, definitive crunch. 

Miller's entire body convulsed. He screamed around the leather belt, a raw, primal sound of absolute agony that echoed down the concrete corridor. 

The arm fell to the floor, landing with a wet thud. 

Blood sprayed wildly from the severed stump, painting the concrete wall in a bright, arterial arc. 

Thorne dropped the axe and immediately grabbed a heavy trauma dressing from the medkit. He pressed it brutally hard against the spurting stump, ignoring Miller's thrashing. 

"Hold still!" Thorne yelled, leaning his entire body weight into the wound to staunch the massive bleeding. 

Miller's eyes rolled backward, and he slumped against the wall, slipping rapidly into unconsciousness from the shock and massive blood loss. 

Thorne wrapped the heavy dressing tightly with thick gauze, securing the crude tourniquet. 

He leaned back, panting heavily, his hands and white shirt covered in the sergeant's blood. 

He looked down at the severed arm on the floor. The purple discoloration was dark and angry around the bite mark, but the flesh above the elbow where the axe had struck was still a healthy pale color. 

They had beaten the infection. 

But as Thorne looked at Miller's pale, motionless face and the massive pool of blood spreading across the concrete floor, he realized he might have just traded the fever for exsanguination. 

"Sergeant," Thorne whispered, checking Miller's pulse. It was faint, thready, and dangerously slow. 

Before Thorne could attempt any further first aid, a horrific, metallic booming sound echoed through the bunker. 

It wasn't coming from the ventilation shaft. It was coming from the massive steel blast doors of the primary entrance. 

The black blizzard had finally subsided enough for the horde to find the subterranean entrance. And they were breaking in.


Chapter Seven: The Final Wave

The booming sound against the primary blast doors was relentless. It wasn't the sound of fists or heavy boots. It was the sound of something massive and metallic being repeatedly rammed against the foot-thick steel. 

The horde had found the motor pool. They were using a two-ton military transport truck as a battering ram, blindly pushing the heavy vehicle down the concrete ramp and into the subterranean bunker entrance. 

Thorne stood up, his heart hammering in his chest. 

"Sarah," he yelled, running down the maintenance corridor toward the communications room. 

He burst through the door. His daughter was huddled under a metal desk, crying softly, clutching a small stuffed bear. 

"Come here, sweetheart," Thorne said, pulling her into his arms. "We have to move to the deep shelter."

The deep shelter was a small, heavily reinforced communications closet located at the very back of the bunker complex. It had a single, heavy steel door and no ventilation shafts. It was designed to withstand a direct artillery strike, but it was a tomb if they were trapped inside for more than a day. 

As they ran back into the maintenance corridor, Thorne saw Sergeant Miller weakly pushing himself up from the concrete floor. 

The veteran soldier was incredibly pale, his uniform soaked in his own blood. He was leaning heavily against the wall, his right hand gripping his Colt M1911. 

"Sergeant, you need to lean on me," Thorne said, reaching for him. 

"No," Miller rasped, his voice barely a whisper. He looked toward the booming sound echoing from the primary entrance. "I'm slowing you down, Doc. I've lost too much blood. I won't make it to the closet."

"I'm not leaving you here!" Thorne argued. 

*CRASH.*

The sound of tearing metal echoed through the bunker. The heavy steel locking bolts on the primary blast doors had finally given way. 

The horrific, shuffling sound of thousands of dragging feet immediately filled the subterranean complex. The screams of the three hundred civilians in the main holding area erupted seconds later, a cacophony of absolute, inescapable terror. 

The horde was inside. 

"They're through," Miller said, his eyes hard. He looked at Thorne, then down at the terrified seven-year-old girl. "Get her to the deep shelter, Doc. Now."

"Sergeant..."

"That's an order, Thorne," Miller growled, raising the Colt with a shaking hand. He pointed down the corridor toward the heavy iron door of the armory. "I'm going to the armory. I'll buy you as much time as I can."

Thorne stared at the dying soldier for a long moment, the devastating reality of the situation crushing him. 

"Thank you, Sergeant," Thorne whispered. 

He picked Sarah up and sprinted down the corridor toward the deep shelter. 

Miller watched them go. He took a deep, ragged breath, gritting his teeth against the searing agony of his severed arm, and stumbled toward the armory door.


Chapter Eight: The Last Stand

The main civilian holding area was a slaughterhouse. 

Hundreds of infected soldiers and civilians poured through the breached blast doors, falling upon the terrified survivors in the dark. The screams were deafening, echoing through the concrete corridors like the soundtrack to hell. 

Miller didn't look at the carnage. He knew there was nothing he could do for them. His only mission now was to buy time for the doctor and the girl. 

He reached the heavy iron door of the armory and kicked it open. 

The small room was packed with heavy munitions: crates of grenades, satchel charges, and thousands of rounds of ammunition. 

Miller stumbled inside, his boots slipping on the blood pouring from his bandaged stump. He collapsed against a wooden crate of high explosives, his vision swimming. 

He heard the heavy, shuffling footsteps approaching down the maintenance corridor. The vanguard of the horde had finished their initial meal and was fanning out into the deeper sections of the bunker. 

They were coming for him. 

Miller weakly raised the Colt M1911, pointing it at the open doorway. 

The first zombie appeared. It was the young lieutenant who had been commanding the main gates, his face a ruined mess of necrotic flesh and dark blood. 

Miller squeezed the trigger. The heavy .45 round caught the dead officer squarely between the eyes, destroying the brain instantly. 

The zombie collapsed, blocking the doorway. 

But two more immediately climbed over the body. 

Miller fired twice more, dropping both of them. He had three rounds left. 

The corridor outside was packed with the dead. They were pressing against the heavy iron doorframe, a massive, unthinking wall of rotting flesh and snapping jaws. 

Miller knew he couldn't hold them. He was bleeding out rapidly, his strength fading with every heartbeat. 

He lowered the Colt. 

He looked down at the wooden crate he was leaning against. It was stenciled with bold yellow letters: **COMPOSITION B - HIGH EXPLOSIVE**. 

Miller smiled grimly. A soldier's death. 

He reached down with his right hand and unfastened the heavy brass latch on the crate. Inside were dozens of one-pound blocks of military-grade explosives, already wired with blasting caps for rapid deployment. 

He pulled a heavy, plunger-style detonator from the webbing of his gear and wired it quickly to the nearest block of Comp B. 

The zombies were climbing over the pile of bodies in the doorway, their milky white eyes locking onto him. They shambled into the armory, their dead hands reaching for his throat. 

Miller didn't fight them. He simply placed his thumb on the heavy brass plunger of the detonator. 

"See you in hell," Miller whispered. 

He pressed the plunger. 

The explosion was catastrophic. 

The entire armory detonated simultaneously. The massive blast vaporized Miller and the vanguard of the horde instantly. The concussive shockwave tore through the subterranean complex, collapsing the main maintenance corridor and burying hundreds of zombies under tons of shattered concrete and twisted rebar. 

The explosion sealed the deep shelter from the rest of the bunker, but it also destroyed the structural integrity of the entire complex.


Chapter Nine: The Radio Silence

The shockwave from the armory explosion hit the heavy steel door of the deep shelter like a physical blow. 

Dr. Thorne was thrown across the small communications closet, crashing heavily into a rack of radio equipment. The dim, flickering auxiliary lights died completely, plunging the small room into absolute darkness. 

"Daddy!" Sarah screamed, terrified by the sudden darkness and the deafening explosion. 

"I'm here, sweetie," Thorne gasped, picking himself up and fumbling for his flashlight. 

He flicked it on. The beam illuminated the small, ten-by-ten closet. The heavy steel door was buckled inward, the locking bolts bent at terrifying angles, but it was still in its frame. 

The explosion had sealed them in. 

The silence that followed was heavy and oppressive. The screams from the main holding area had stopped. The shuffling of the horde was gone. 

"Are they gone, Daddy?" Sarah whispered, clutching her stuffed bear. 

"I don't know," Thorne said softly. 

He turned to the main shortwave radio console dominating the back wall. He flipped the power switch. Miraculously, the heavy battery bank beneath the desk was still functioning. The dials glowed a faint, ghostly green. 

Thorne grabbed the microphone. He needed to know if anyone was left alive on the surface, if any other quarantine zones had survived the black blizzard. 

"This is Dr. Aris Thorne at Camp Omega," he said, his voice echoing in the small steel tomb. "Is anyone receiving? Over."

He listened to the heavy static. 

"Camp Omega broadcasting on all emergency frequencies. We have suffered a catastrophic breach. The base is lost. Is there anyone out there? Over."

Nothing but the cold, uncaring hiss of the atmosphere. 

He tried every frequency. He broadcasted for an hour, his voice growing increasingly hoarse and desperate. 

The black blizzard had either destroyed all communication towers in the region, or there was simply no one left alive to listen. 

Thorne slowly lowered the microphone. The horrifying realization settled over him. They were the last living souls in the Dustbowl. And they were trapped in a steel box deep underground. 

He looked at the atmospheric monitor on the desk. The carbon dioxide levels in the small, unventilated room were already beginning to rise dangerously. They had a few hours of breathable air left. 

"Daddy," Sarah said quietly, pointing toward the buckled steel door. "I hear something."

Thorne held his breath and listened. 

At first, it was just a faint scratching sound. But it grew louder, more persistent. It was the sound of heavy fingernails tearing at the twisted metal of the doorframe. 

Then came the moaning. A low, collective, unholy sound echoing through the collapsed corridor outside. 

The explosion hadn't killed them all. The massive horde had simply begun digging through the rubble of the collapsed tunnel, driven by the scent of the living flesh trapped inside the closet. 

They were coming.


Chapter Ten: The End

The heavy steel door groaned as a massive weight pressed against it from the outside. 

The locking bolts, already bent by the explosion, began to shriek in protest. 

Thorne stared at the door, the flashlight beam illuminating the terrifying strain on the metal. He knew the door wouldn't hold. The horde had the numbers, and they had eternity. 

He looked at his daughter. Sarah was sitting on the floor, her knees pulled up to her chest, her eyes wide with terror. She wasn't crying anymore. The absolute horror of the situation had pushed her into a state of silent shock. 

Thorne felt a crushing wave of despair. He was a scientist. He had spent the last two years trying to find a cure for the venomous fever. He had failed. He had failed his country, he had failed the people in the bunker, and now, he was failing his daughter. 

"Come here, Sarah," Thorne said softly, kneeling on the floor and opening his arms. 

Sarah crawled into his lap, burying her face in his chest. Thorne wrapped his arms tightly around her small, fragile body, resting his chin on the top of her head. 

"I love you, Daddy," she whispered, her voice muffled by his shirt. 

"I love you too, sweetie," Thorne replied, tears streaming down his face. "More than anything in the world."

*CRACK.*

The top locking bolt on the steel door snapped. 

The heavy metal door buckled inward several inches, revealing a jagged, black gap at the top of the frame. 

Instantly, half a dozen ruined, bloody hands thrust through the gap, their cold fingers grasping blindly in the dark. 

The low, collective moan of the horde swelled into a deafening roar of predatory hunger. 

Thorne didn't look at the door. He didn't look at the dead hands reaching for them. He closed his eyes and buried his face in his daughter's hair. 

He didn't have a weapon. He didn't have a plan. He only had the crushing, absolute certainty that the world belonged to the dead now. 

*CRASH.*

The bottom locking bolt gave way. The massive steel door collapsed inward, falling heavily to the concrete floor. 

The horde poured into the small communications closet. They were a terrifying mass of ruined flesh, torn uniforms, and milky white eyes, their jaws snapping furiously as they lunged toward the corner where Thorne held his daughter. 

Thorne held Sarah tighter, whispering a silent prayer as the first cold, dead hands grabbed his shoulders. 

The dead descended upon them in the dark, their hunger absolute and uncompromising. 

In the heart of the Dustbowl, deep beneath the ruined earth, the final flickering light of Camp Omega was extinguished, swallowed completely by the endless, ravenous darkness.



Story Thirteen: The Hoover Dam

Chapter One: The High Scaler

The heat radiating off the sheer rock face of the Black Canyon was a physical weight, pressing down on Jack Thomas with the suffocating intensity of an open oven. It was July 1935, and the Nevada sun was relentless, baking the massive concrete face of the partially completed Hoover Dam and the men dying to build it. 

Jack was a "high-scaler." He spent ten hours a day dangling like a spider on a half-inch hemp rope, five hundred feet above the churning, muddy waters of the Colorado River. His job was to strip the loose rock from the canyon walls with a heavy jackhammer, preparing the sheer cliffs for the massive concrete abutments. 

It was brutal, terrifying work. The air was thick with the suffocating dust of pulverized granite, and the constant, deafening roar of the gasoline compressors rattled his teeth. 

Jack paused, wiping a thick layer of grit and sweat from his eyes with the back of a calloused hand. He looked down past the toes of his heavy leather boots. 

Fifty feet below him, dangling on his own lifeline, was a young kid from Oklahoma named Billy. Billy was new to the high-scaling crew, driven to the dangerous work by the sheer desperation of the Dustbowl. 

"Hey, kid!" Jack yelled over the roar of the machinery. "Take a break! Drink some water before you pass out!"

Billy didn't answer. 

Jack frowned, squinting through the dust. The young man wasn't drilling. His heavy pneumatic jackhammer hung uselessly by his side, suspended by its safety line. 

Billy was slumped forward in his heavy leather harness, his chin resting on his chest. His body was twitching violently, short, sharp spasms that shook the heavy hemp rope suspending him over the abyss. 

"O'Malley!" Jack yelled, looking up toward the rim of the canyon. "Billy's having a seizure down here! Haul him up!"

Foreman O'Malley, a massive Irishman standing at the top of the cliff, leaned over the edge. He yelled something back, but his voice was completely swallowed by the deafening roar of the compressors and the massive concrete mixers. 

Jack looked back down. 

Billy had stopped twitching. The young man hung completely motionless in his harness, his arms dangling limply. 

"Billy?" Jack called out, a sudden sense of dread settling over him. 

The heat stroke had finally claimed the kid. It happened more often than the government engineers liked to admit. 

Jack braced his boots against the canyon wall and prepared to swing down to check on the body. 

But Billy wasn't dead. 

Slowly, the young man lifted his head. The floppy brim of his canvas hat fell backward, revealing his face. 

Billy's eyes were wide open, but the brown irises were gone, replaced by a milky, opaque white. His lips were pulled back in a grotesque, silent snarl, and a thick string of dark saliva hung from his chin. 

The sickness from the Dustbowl, the venomous fever that had ravaged the flatlands, had finally reached the isolated canyon.


Chapter Two: The Rope

Jack stared at the young high-scaler in absolute disbelief. He had heard the horrific rumors echoing through the squalid shantytowns of Boulder City—stories of the dead rising in the dust storms of Oklahoma and Texas. But seeing it here, suspended halfway up a sheer cliff face, shattered his understanding of reality. 

Billy didn't look up at Jack. He didn't seem to recognize his surroundings. Driven by the primal, unthinking hunger of the Romero sickness, he simply reacted to the closest source of living flesh. 

The dead boy reached out and grabbed the thick hemp rope suspending him. 

He didn't use the intricate ascender knots the high-scalers relied on. He simply clamped his ruined, calloused hands around the rough hemp and began to pull himself upward. 

The zombie's movements were slow, lacking the agile coordination of a living man, but the dead strength was terrifying. Billy ignored the burning friction that was undoubtedly tearing the skin from his palms. He simply hauled his dead weight upward, hand over hand, his milky white eyes fixed entirely on Jack. 

"Hey! Stop!" Jack yelled, instinctively kicking off the canyon wall and swinging his body backward on his own rope. 

Billy ignored him. He climbed another five feet, his jaw snapping furiously as he neared the heavy steel carabiner connecting his harness to the main line. 

Jack realized with horrifying clarity that Billy wasn't just climbing his own rope. Because they were working the same vertical section of the cliff, their lifelines were anchored to the same heavy steel piton driven into the rock face a hundred feet above them. 

If Billy reached the top of his rope, he would be right next to Jack. 

"Pull us up!" Jack screamed, waving frantically at the rim of the canyon. 

Foreman O'Malley finally saw the commotion. He leaned over the edge, his face turning red with anger. "Get back to work, Thomas! You're not getting paid to swing around like a goddamn monkey!"

O'Malley couldn't see Billy's milky white eyes from the top of the cliff. He just thought the two men were slacking off. 

"He's sick!" Jack roared, his voice cracking. "Pull him up!"

O'Malley turned his back, waving a dismissive hand, and walked away from the edge to check on a concrete pour. 

Jack was on his own. 

He looked down. Billy was only thirty feet below him now, climbing with relentless, terrifying determination. The dead boy's hands were leaving thick smears of dark blood on the hemp rope, the rough fibers wearing away the flesh down to the bone, but he didn't stop. 

Jack needed a weapon. His jackhammer was too heavy and unwieldy to swing. His only tool was a heavy steel scaling bar, a sharp, four-foot crowbar used for prying loose rocks from the cliff face. 

He unhooked the scaling bar from his tool belt and gripped it tightly. 

"Stay back, Billy," Jack warned, his hands shaking. "I swear to God, I'll drop you."


Chapter Three: The Dynamite

The dead didn't listen to warnings. 

Billy continued his slow, inexorable climb, the bloody hands gripping the rope with the unyielding strength of rigor mortis. He was twenty feet away. Ten feet. 

Jack swung his body toward the rock face, bracing his heavy boots against a narrow outcropping of granite. He held the heavy steel scaling bar like a spear, pointing the sharp, forged tip directly down at the climbing zombie. 

"I'm sorry, kid," Jack whispered. 

As Billy's ruined face came within striking distance, his jaw opening wide to snap at Jack's heavy leather boots, Jack thrust the scaling bar downward with all his strength. 

The sharp steel tip struck Billy squarely in the forehead. 

It was a perfect strike. The heavy bar should have punched through the skull and destroyed the brain, enforcing the Romero rule instantly. 

But Jack was suspended on a rope, swinging freely in the air. He didn't have the leverage to drive the bar through bone. 

The steel tip glanced off the curved frontal bone of Billy's skull, tearing a deep gash through the scalp but failing to penetrate the braincase. 

The impact knocked Billy backward, but he didn't let go of the rope. He simply swung out over the abyss, his dead eyes locking back onto Jack as he swung back in. 

The zombie's bloody hand lunged upward, grabbing the heavy steel shaft of the scaling bar. 

Jack cursed, trying to wrench the bar free. 

The dead strength was too much. Billy didn't let go. He used the scaling bar as a handle, pulling himself violently upward, closing the distance instantly. 

Billy's ruined face was suddenly inches from Jack's boots, his teeth tearing into the thick leather of Jack's left heel. 

Jack screamed, kicking wildly with his right boot. He struck Billy in the face, shattering the zombie's nose, but the jaws didn't release. 

Jack let go of the scaling bar. 

He reached down to the heavy canvas satchel slung across his chest. High-scalers carried explosives to clear stubborn boulders. 

Jack pulled out a thick, red half-stick of commercial dynamite, already fitted with a short, ten-second fuse. 

He didn't have a lighter. The wind in the canyon made matches useless. He wore a specialized friction striker on a leather lanyard around his neck. 

He grabbed the heavy brass striker and scraped it violently against the rough, chemical-coated tip of the fuse. 

Sparks flew. The fuse caught instantly, hissing and spitting a trail of thick white smoke. 

Jack didn't drop the dynamite. He knew it would just fall five hundred feet into the river before detonating. 

He leaned down, the hissing explosive in his right hand. 

Billy was still chewing mindlessly on the heel of Jack's heavy leather boot, his milky eyes staring blankly at the canyon wall. 

Jack shoved the hissing half-stick of dynamite directly into the massive, gaping pocket of Billy's canvas tool belt. 

Then, Jack grabbed his own lifeline and kicked off the canyon wall with all his strength, swinging his body in a wide, desperate arc away from the dead boy.


Chapter Four: The Cave-In

Jack swung fifty feet out over the roaring Colorado River, suspended in the hot desert air, holding his breath and waiting for the end. 

The explosion was deafening, a sharp, violent crack that echoed like thunder through the narrow confines of the Black Canyon. 

The half-stick of dynamite detonated inside Billy's tool belt with devastating force. The massive blast instantly vaporized the zombie's lower torso, completely severing his legs and destroying his spine. 

The concussive shockwave hit Jack like a physical blow, violently twisting his body in the harness and sending him spinning wildly on his rope. 

He looked back toward the cliff face, his ears ringing with absolute silence. 

The upper half of Billy's body, still attached to his safety harness, swung limply against the rock wall. The brain had been destroyed by the massive overpressure of the blast, enforcing the Romero rule with absolute prejudice. 

Jack gasped for air, his heart hammering against his ribs. He had survived. 

But the victory was tragically short-lived. 

The explosion hadn't just destroyed the zombie. The concussive blast of the commercial dynamite had struck the sheer granite face of the canyon wall directly beneath a massive, unstable overhang of rock that the high-scalers were supposed to clear the next day. 

Jack heard a deep, terrifying groan echoing from deep within the rock. 

"Oh, God," Jack whispered, looking up. 

A spiderweb of massive cracks shot up the sheer face of the cliff, fifty feet above his head. 

The explosion had destabilized the entire section of the canyon wall. 

With a deafening roar that drowned out the sound of the river and the machinery, a massive section of the cliff face—thousands of tons of solid granite—sheared away from the canyon wall. 

The rockslide was catastrophic. 

Massive boulders the size of automobiles plummeted down the sheer face of the cliff, destroying everything in their path. The heavy wooden scaffolding platforms bolted to the rock were pulverized instantly. 

Jack was still swinging on his rope, seventy feet away from the rock face. 

He watched in horror as the avalanche of stone plummeted past him, missing him by a terrifyingly narrow margin. 

The massive boulders crashed into the Colorado River below, sending towering geysers of white water hundreds of feet into the air. 

The rockslide lasted for less than thirty seconds, but it completely changed the face of the Black Canyon. 

As the thick cloud of pulverized granite dust slowly began to settle, Jack swung back toward the cliff face, coughing violently. 

The entire section of rock where Billy had died was gone. 

In its place, exposed by the massive collapse, was a dark, gaping hole in the canyon wall. 

It wasn't a small crevice. It was a massive, natural cavern, thirty feet high and fifty feet wide, leading deep into the heart of the surrounding desert mountains. 

Jack stared into the dark abyss of the newly exposed cave, the hair on the back of his neck standing up. 

A low, collective moan drifted out of the darkness, carrying over the roar of the river. 

The cave wasn't empty.


Chapter Five: The River

The deep, resonant moan echoing from the dark cavern sent a spike of pure terror through Jack's exhausted body. He knew that sound. He had just heard it from the kid dangling below him. 

He stared into the absolute darkness of the newly exposed cave, his eyes straining against the blinding glare of the desert sun. 

At first, he saw nothing. Then, a pale shape emerged from the shadows. 

It was a man, but he wasn't dressed like a construction worker. He wore the tattered remains of a heavy wool coat and thick leather prospector boots. His skin was gray and desiccated, baked by the harsh desert air. His jaw hung open at a broken angle, and his eyes were milky white. 

Behind him, another figure emerged. And another. 

Within seconds, the massive opening of the cavern was filled with the walking dead. 

They were an army of the lost. Decades of prospectors, hermits, and indigenous people who had sought refuge in the deep, cool cave systems of the Black Mountains when the venomous fever first swept across the desert, only to die and reanimate in the dark. 

The explosion had opened their tomb. 

Drawn by the sound of the blast and the sudden influx of bright sunlight, the horde shuffled blindly toward the edge of the newly sheared cliff face. 

They didn't hesitate at the precipice. Driven entirely by the unthinking hunger of the Romero sickness, the front ranks simply walked off the edge of the cliff. 

It was a horrific, slow-motion waterfall of rotting flesh. 

Dozens of dead bodies plummeted five hundred feet through the air, their limbs flailing uselessly. 

They struck the churning, muddy waters of the Colorado River with terrifying force. The impact shattered bones and ruptured organs, but the Romero rule was absolute. Unless the skull was crushed against a submerged rock, the brain remained intact. 

Jack watched in horror as the river swallowed the dead. 

The Colorado was a massive, powerful force, currently being diverted through huge concrete tunnels to bypass the construction site. The current caught the broken, thrashing bodies of the dead prospectors and swept them violently downstream. 

Directly toward the massive, bustling construction site at the base of the Hoover Dam. 

Down in the massive concrete basin, thousands of men were working on the massive diversion tunnels and the foundation of the dam. The roar of the machinery was so loud they couldn't hear the moaning of the horde or the splashes of the bodies hitting the water. 

Jack looked down. 

The first dead prospector washed up against a heavy wooden cofferdam holding back the river water. His legs were completely shattered from the fall, but his upper body was still functioning perfectly. 

The zombie dragged itself out of the muddy water and onto the wooden planks. 

A young engineer, holding a clipboard and yelling at a foreman, didn't see the dead man crawling behind him. 

The zombie lunged, sinking its teeth deep into the engineer's calf. 

The engineer screamed, dropping his clipboard. 

The massacre of the Hoover Dam had begun. 

Jack, suspended five hundred feet in the air, could only watch as the horde continued to pour out of the cave, falling like rain into the river below, bringing the sickness to the greatest engineering marvel of the century.



Chapter Six: The Cable Car

The massacre at the base of the dam escalated with terrifying speed. 

The roar of the compressors and the massive concrete mixers masked the screams until it was too late. The dead prospectors, driven by the current and their unyielding hunger, washed up by the dozen, attacking the unawares construction workers. 

Jack dangled on his rope, powerless to intervene. He watched men he knew, men he had shared drinks with in the shantytowns, being torn apart and dragged into the muddy water. 

He had to get to the rim. 

He looked up. Five hundred feet above him, Foreman O'Malley was finally realizing what was happening below. The massive Irishman was leaning over the edge, his face pale, pointing frantically at the carnage. 

Jack waved his arms, pointing to the massive, eight-ton steel "skip"—a bucket used to transport wet concrete and men across the canyon—suspended on a heavy cable system nearby. 

O'Malley saw him. He turned and yelled at the winch house operator situated on the rim. 

A moment later, the massive steel skip began to traverse the canyon, moving slowly toward Jack's position. 

Jack gripped his lifeline tightly. The heat stroke, the adrenaline, and the sheer terror of the last ten minutes were taking their toll. His muscles burned, and his vision was swimming. 

The heavy steel bucket swung closer, the massive pulleys groaning under the strain. 

O'Malley guided the skip expertly, stopping it just five feet away from Jack. 

Jack didn't hesitate. He unhooked the heavy steel carabiner from his harness, severing his connection to the lifeline. 

He kicked off the canyon wall and launched himself through the empty air, aiming for the massive steel rim of the skip. 

His hands slapped against the hot metal. His boots scrambled for purchase on the smooth, riveted steel. 

For a terrifying second, he hung suspended by his fingertips over the five-hundred-foot drop. 

Then, he hauled himself up and tumbled over the rim, landing heavily in the empty, concrete-stained bucket. 

He lay on his back, gasping for air, staring up at the blinding blue desert sky. 

He felt the massive skip lurch as the winch operator engaged the hoist. 

He was going up. He had survived the cliff. He just had to reach the rim, find O'Malley, and get to a truck. 


Chapter Seven: The Hoist

The massive steel bucket rose slowly, swaying gently in the hot desert wind. 

Jack pulled himself up and looked over the edge. 

The construction site below was completely lost. The massive concrete forms were stained with blood. Hundreds of infected workers were now joining the dead prospectors, forming a massive, chaotic horde that swarmed over the heavy machinery and the incomplete structures of the dam. 

They were trapped in the canyon. There was nowhere for them to go but up the access roads or into the river. 

Jack looked up toward the rim. He was two hundred feet away from safety. 

Suddenly, the massive skip stopped moving. 

It didn't slow down gracefully. It jerked to a violent halt, the heavy steel suspension cables snapping taut with a terrifying, metallic *TWANG*. 

Jack was thrown against the side of the bucket, bruising his ribs. 

"Hey!" Jack yelled, looking up at the winch house perched on the edge of the cliff. "What are you doing? Keep pulling!"

There was no answer. The massive steam winch was silent. 

Jack squinted against the sun, trying to see into the small, wooden operator's shack. 

A figure stumbled out of the shack. It was the winch operator. 

But he wasn't walking right. He stumbled to the edge of the cliff, his arms hanging limply at his sides. 

Even from two hundred feet away, Jack could see the dark, wet stain covering the man's throat. 

Behind the operator, another figure emerged from the shack. This one was wearing the torn clothes of a surveyor. He grabbed the operator by the shoulders and violently pulled him backward into the wooden shack. 

The horde hadn't just attacked from the river. The infection had reached the rim of the canyon. 

The winch house, the heart of the cable system, was overrun. 

"O'Malley!" Jack screamed, his voice cracking with terror. "O'Malley, answer me!"

Silence. 

Jack was trapped in a steel bucket, suspended three hundred feet in the air, with no one operating the winch. 


Chapter Eight: The Operator

Inside the wooden winch house, the scene was one of brutal, chaotic slaughter. 

The surveyor, infected days ago in Boulder City, had wandered into the shack looking for living flesh. He had torn the operator's throat out before the man could even reach for his heavy iron wrench. 

Now, the zombie was kneeling on the wooden floorboards, tearing into the operator's chest. 

In the struggle, the operator's heavy boot had kicked the massive steel locking pawl of the steam winch. 

The pawl was designed to prevent the massive drum from unwinding under the immense weight of the skip. It was a heavy, forged iron wedge that locked into the teeth of the main gear. 

The operator's boot had dislodged it slightly. Not enough to free it completely, but enough to balance it precariously on the edge of the gear tooth. 

The dead operator suddenly opened his milky white eyes. The Romero sickness took hold quickly when the blood loss was catastrophic. 

He lunged upward, his dead hands grappling with the surveyor. 

The two zombies thrashed violently across the small shack, entirely unaware of the massive machinery surrounding them. 

They crashed heavily into the main control console of the steam winch. 

The impact was enough. 

The heavy steel locking pawl, already precariously balanced, slipped free of the gear tooth with a sharp, metallic *CLACK*. 

Without the pawl to hold it, the massive weight of the eight-ton steel skip, suspended hundreds of feet out over the canyon, exerted its full force on the winch drum. 

The massive drum began to spin backward. 

It started slowly, but the weight of the skip accelerated it rapidly. The heavy steel cable began to unspool with a terrifying, high-pitched scream. 

Out in the canyon, Jack felt the sudden, sickening drop in his stomach. 

The massive steel skip lurched downward, dropping ten feet in a second. 

"No!" Jack screamed, gripping the rim of the bucket. 

The skip was in freefall, plummeting backward down the cable toward the canyon floor. 


Chapter Nine: The Brake

The wind roared past Jack's ears as the skip plummeted. The sheer rock face of the canyon wall blurred past him in a terrifying rush of speed. 

He was going to die. When the skip hit the canyon floor or reached the end of the cable, the impact would pulverize him instantly. 

He had seconds left. 

He looked up at the heavy steel cable block suspending the skip. The massive pulleys were spinning so fast they were smoking. 

Mounted on the side of the block was a heavy, rusted iron lever. It was the emergency friction brake, designed to be engaged manually in case of a winch failure. 

It was his only chance. 

Jack didn't hesitate. He grabbed the thick steel suspension cables and hauled himself out of the bucket. 

The wind threatened to tear him away as he climbed the vibrating cables, his boots slipping on the metal. The skip was dropping faster now, the ground rushing up to meet him. 

He reached the heavy steel block. 

He grabbed the rusted iron lever and pulled downward with all his strength. 

The lever didn't move. It was rusted solid from years of disuse and exposure to the desert elements. 

"Come on!" Jack roared, throwing his entire body weight onto the lever. 

Nothing. 

He was one hundred feet from the ground. The horde swarming the construction site below was growing rapidly larger. 

Jack needed leverage. He needed a hammer. 

He reached to his tool belt. He still had his heavy scaling hammer, a solid iron mallet used for driving pitons. 

He unhooked the hammer. He didn't have time to swing it properly. He gripped the heavy iron head and used the handle to batter the rusted brake lever. 

He struck it once. Twice. 

He was fifty feet from the ground. He could see the milky white eyes of the dead looking up at him. 

He struck the lever a third time, pouring every ounce of his remaining strength and terror into the blow. 

With a sharp, metallic screech, the rust broke. 

The heavy iron lever slammed downward. 

The massive friction brake engaged violently. 


Chapter Ten: The Concrete

The sudden, violent deceleration was catastrophic. 

The massive brake pads clamped onto the rapidly unspooling steel cable with explosive force. The eight-ton skip, traveling at near terminal velocity, stopped in less than twenty feet. 

The immense kinetic energy had to go somewhere. 

The sudden, brutal stop placed an unimaginable strain on the entire cable system. 

At the rim of the canyon, the massive steel cable snapped violently at the winch drum. The severed cable whipped through the wooden shack like a massive steel razor, instantly bisecting the two zombies inside. 

But out in the canyon, the consequences were far worse. 

The snapped cable instantly released the tension on the skip. 

Jack, thrown violently against the steel block by the sudden deceleration, watched in horror as the heavy cable went slack above him. 

The skip plummeted the final fifty feet. 

It didn't hit the solid rock of the canyon floor. It crashed directly into the center of a massive, wooden form filled with hundreds of tons of freshly poured, wet concrete meant for the dam's primary foundation. 

The impact was a dull, wet explosion. 

The heavy steel skip sank deep into the thick, gray sludge. 

Jack was thrown from the cable block, tumbling violently through the air. He crashed heavily into the wet concrete, the impact shattering both of his legs instantly. 

He screamed, a raw sound of absolute agony, as he sank to his waist in the thick, heavy sludge. 

The wet concrete was suffocating, pulling him down like quicksand. He thrashed weakly, his broken legs entirely useless. 

He was trapped. 

He looked around, gasping for air. 

The massive crash of the skip had drawn the attention of the entire horde. 

Hundreds of infected construction workers and dead prospectors were swarming over the wooden forms, their milky white eyes locking onto the broken, bleeding high-scaler trapped in the concrete. 

They didn't hesitate. Driven by the unthinking hunger, they waded into the wet concrete, their heavy boots sinking into the sludge. 

Jack tried to drag himself backward, but the concrete held him fast. 

The first dead worker reached him, a massive man still wearing a hard hat, his jaw opening wide. 

Jack Thomas, the man who had hung above the abyss, was finally pulled under. 

The dead descended upon him, their teeth tearing into his flesh as the wet concrete slowly set around them, cementing the horror of the Hoover Dam into the very foundation of the modern world.



Story Fourteen: The Alcatraz Cell

Chapter One: The Lockdown

The fog rolling off the San Francisco Bay was thick and freezing, clinging to the sheer rock of Alcatraz Island like a shroud. It was 1937, and the federal penitentiary was the most secure, inescapable fortress in the world. 

Arthur "The Architect" Vance, Inmate 85, sat on the edge of his thin mattress in Cell 14, B-Block. He was a ruthless bank robber, a man who had engineered the escape of half a dozen men from Leavenworth before finally being captured and buried alive on the Rock. 

He was meticulously sharpening the edge of a spoon against the concrete floor when the klaxons began to wail. 

It wasn't the standard, rhythmic pulse of a shift change or a yard recall. It was the frantic, continuous shriek of a general emergency. 

Arthur stood up, moving to the heavy steel bars of his five-by-nine cell. He gripped the cold metal, peering down the long, three-tiered concrete canyon of B-Block. 

"What's the play, Mitchell?" Arthur yelled to the corrections officer sprinting past his cell. 

Guard Mitchell, a strict, uncompromising man who hated Arthur with a passion, didn't even pause to sneer. His face was pale, his eyes wide with an uncharacteristic terror. 

"Shut your mouth and step back from the bars, 85!" Mitchell barked, fumbling with his heavy ring of brass keys. 

Mitchell didn't head for the riot gun rack. He sprinted directly toward the heavy, electronically controlled steel doors that separated the cellblocks from the administrative wing. 

Arthur watched in confusion as Mitchell threw his weight against the manual override lever, slamming the massive steel doors shut. The heavy locking bolts engaged with a deafening *CLANG*, sealing B-Block completely. 

"Hey!" another inmate yelled from across the tier. "You can't lock us in here! What's happening?"

Mitchell stood on the other side of the reinforced glass window set into the steel doors. He looked back at the hundreds of prisoners locked in their cages. 

He didn't answer. He turned and ran deeper into the administrative wing, abandoning his post. 

Arthur gripped the bars tighter. A low, terrifying sound drifted down from the cafeteria on the level above them. It was a chaotic symphony of shattering plates, frantic screams, and a wet, tearing sound that made Arthur's stomach turn. 

The sickness from the mainland had finally arrived on the supply boat, and the Rock was in lockdown. 


Chapter Two: The Riots

The screams from the upper levels lasted for three agonizing hours. 

Arthur paced his small concrete cell like a caged tiger. The panic in B-Block was absolute. Inmates were screaming, banging their tin cups against the bars, and demanding answers from guards who were no longer there. 

Arthur didn't scream. He listened. He was a man who survived by understanding the mechanics of a situation. And the mechanics of this situation were horrifying. 

The sounds weren't those of a standard prison riot. There was no rhythmic chanting, no demands, no gunfire. There was only the frantic, desperate screaming of men being butchered, followed by a sudden, terrifying silence, and then... a low, collective moaning. 

Suddenly, the heavy steel door at the far end of the tier—the access door to the infirmary—burst open. 

Two guards stumbled through, dragging a third man between them. The injured guard was bleeding profusely from a massive, ragged wound on his neck. 

"Open the gate!" one of the guards screamed, looking toward the sealed administrative doors. 

But there was no one in the control room to throw the electronic switch. They were trapped on the tier with the inmates. 

Arthur pressed his face against the bars, watching the frantic guards. 

The injured man suddenly went limp, his body sagging between his comrades. 

"He's gone," one guard gasped, lowering the body to the concrete floor. 

The second guard backed away, his face pale. "We have to get out of here. They're coming."

"Who's coming?" Arthur demanded, his voice cutting through the panic of the surrounding cells. 

The guard looked at him, his eyes completely hollow. "Everyone."

Before the guard could explain, the dead man on the floor began to twitch. 

It wasn't the spasm of a dying muscle. It was deliberate. The dead guard's milky white eyes snapped open. He lunged upward with terrifying speed, grabbing his partner by the heavy leather belt and driving his teeth into the man's stomach. 

The guard screamed, a horrific sound of absolute agony, as the zombie tore through his uniform and into his flesh. 

The second guard drew his service revolver and fired wildly, hitting the zombie in the chest and shoulder. The heavy .38 rounds didn't even slow the dead man down. 

The unbitten guard turned and ran, abandoning his partner to be eaten alive on the concrete floor just ten feet from Arthur's cell. 


Chapter Three: The Silence

By nightfall, the prison was completely silent. 

The emergency lights had flickered and died hours ago, plunging the massive concrete cellblocks into absolute, impenetrable darkness. 

Arthur sat perfectly still on his mattress. He had watched the guard outside his cell die, and he had watched him stand back up. He understood the rules now. It wasn't a riot. It was a plague. And the dead were hungry. 

The silence of the prison was worse than the screaming. The only sound was the relentless, rhythmic crashing of the waves against the rocky shore outside, and the occasional, wet shuffling of feet echoing down the concrete tiers. 

The guards had abandoned the cellhouse, sealing the heavy steel doors to save themselves. In doing so, they had trapped hundreds of living men in their cages, perfectly preserved meals for the dead that were now roaming the open areas of the prison. 

Arthur was safe from the teeth. The heavy steel bars of Cell 14 were designed to hold the most dangerous men in America. A walking corpse wasn't going to bend them. 

But the bars that kept the dead out also kept Arthur in. 

He had no food. He had a small toilet that hadn't flushed since the power went out, and a small sink that had run dry an hour later. 

He was perfectly safe from the zombie apocalypse outside his door. 

But he was going to die of thirst in three days. 

"Hey," a hoarse whisper echoed from the cell next door. It was Inmate 86, a mob hitman named Sal. "Architect. You awake?"

"Yeah, Sal," Arthur replied quietly, keeping his voice down so as not to attract the shuffling feet on the tier below. 

"You think they're coming back for us?" Sal asked, his voice trembling with a fear the hitman had never shown before. 

"No," Arthur said coldly. "The guards are dead, or they ran. We're on our own."

"So what's the plan, Architect?" Sal asked. "You're the escape artist. How do we get out of these cages?"

Arthur looked at the heavy steel lock on his cell door. It was a complex, electronically controlled mechanism, backed up by a massive manual deadbolt. Without the master key or the control room switch, it was impenetrable. 

"We don't," Arthur said. "We wait."


Chapter Four: The Thirst

Seventy-two hours later, the waiting had become an agony worse than any beating Arthur had ever taken in solitary confinement. 

The thirst was absolute. It felt like his throat was lined with sandpaper. His lips were cracked and bleeding, his tongue swollen and useless in his dry mouth. He had drunk the water from the toilet bowl on the second day, fighting down the revulsion to survive. Now, even that was gone. 

The prison was still dark. The shuffling sounds of the dead had become a constant, maddening background noise. Sometimes, a zombie would bump blindly against the bars of Arthur's cell, its dead hands reaching through the steel, its jaw snapping at the scent of the living flesh inside. 

Arthur would simply retreat to the back wall and watch, too weak to taunt them. 

He needed water. And to get water, he needed to get out of the cell. 

He dragged himself off the mattress and crawled toward the front of the cell. He leaned heavily against the bars, his breathing shallow and ragged. 

He looked down the tier. The dim moonlight filtering through the high, barred windows of the cellhouse illuminated a grim scene. 

The concrete floor was littered with the bodies of guards and inmates who had been caught outside their cells. Many of them were still moving, slowly dragging their ruined bodies back and forth in a mindless, unceasing patrol. 

Arthur's eyes fixed on the body of the guard who had been killed outside his cell on the first day. 

The guard had reanimated, but his legs had been broken in the struggle. He was dragging his upper body across the concrete, leaving a dark, dried smear of blood behind him. 

But it wasn't the zombie that caught Arthur's attention. 

It was the heavy ring of brass keys still clipped to the guard's leather belt. 

If Arthur could get those keys, he could unlock the manual deadbolt on his cell door. He could get out. He could find water in the administration wing, if he could make it past the horde. 

It was a suicidal gamble, but staying in the cell meant certain death by dehydration. 

He just needed to reach the keys. 

But the crawling guard was ten feet away, completely out of reach.


Chapter Five: The Mirror

Arthur needed a tool. 

He turned away from the bars and surveyed his small, concrete tomb. He had a thin mattress, a wool blanket, a metal cup, and a small, rectangular shaving mirror bolted to the wall above the dry sink. 

He stumbled toward the sink. He gripped the edge of the metal mirror frame and pulled with all his remaining strength. 

The rusted bolts groaned, but they didn't give way. 

Arthur cursed, his parched throat burning. He grabbed his metal cup and began to hammer violently against the mirror frame. 

The glass shattered instantly, sending sharp shards clattering into the sink. Arthur ignored the glass. He focused on the thin metal frame holding the mirror to the wall. 

He battered the metal until the top bolt finally sheared off. He wrenched the frame back and forth until the bottom bolt snapped. 

He fell backward onto the concrete floor, clutching the jagged, two-foot strip of metal. 

He had a reaching tool. But it was far too short to reach the crawling guard ten feet away. 

He needed to extend his reach. 

He crawled to his mattress and stripped off the heavy wool blanket. He spent the next hour painstakingly tearing the tough fabric into long, narrow strips, his bleeding fingers slipping on the wool. 

He tied the strips together, testing the knots with his teeth. He created a makeshift rope, about twelve feet long. 

He tied one end of the wool rope securely to the jagged metal mirror frame, creating a crude, heavy grappling hook. 

He dragged himself back to the bars of the cell. 

He was exhausted. The physical exertion had burned the last of his energy reserves. His vision was swimming, dark spots dancing in the periphery. 

He leaned against the cold steel bars and looked out onto the tier. 

The crawling guard was still there, slowly dragging himself in a tight circle near the railing. 

"Hey," Arthur rasped, his voice barely a whisper. 

He picked up his metal cup and banged it weakly against the bars. 

*Clang.*

The crawling guard stopped. The milky white eyes slowly turned toward Cell 14. 

The zombie began to drag itself toward the noise, its jaw snapping in anticipation. 

Arthur waited, his heart pounding weakly against his ribs. He needed the zombie closer, but not too close. 

When the crawling guard was six feet away, Arthur threaded the jagged metal hook through the bars and carefully lowered it to the concrete floor. 


Chapter Six: The Neighbor

Arthur gripped the wool rope tightly, his muscles trembling with weakness. He watched the crawling guard drag itself closer, the brass key ring jingling faintly on its leather belt. 

*Five feet.*

Arthur swung the metal hook slowly, trying to judge the distance in the dim moonlight. 

*Four feet.*

Suddenly, a violent crashing sound erupted from the cell next door. 

"Sal?" Arthur croaked, looking toward the concrete wall dividing their cages. 

There had been no sound from Sal for two days. Arthur had assumed the hitman was conserving his energy, or had simply given up. 

A pale, bloodied arm suddenly thrust through the bars of Sal's cell, reaching out into the open space of the tier. 

It was Sal. But he wasn't asking for water. 

The hitman had died of dehydration or a heart attack hours ago. And the Romero rule had claimed him. 

Sal's reanimated corpse pressed its ruined face against the heavy steel bars dividing the two cells, its jaw snapping furiously, its milky white eyes locking onto Arthur. 

"Damn it, Sal," Arthur whispered. 

The sudden noise from Sal's cell distracted the crawling guard on the tier. The zombie stopped dragging itself toward Arthur and turned its milky eyes toward the thrashing hitman next door. 

The crawling guard changed direction, dragging itself toward Sal's cell. 

"No!" Arthur hissed, his panic rising. If the guard reached Sal's cell, the keys would be completely out of Arthur's reach. 

Arthur swung his crude grappling hook wildly, trying to snag the guard's uniform before it moved away. 

The metal mirror frame clattered loudly against the concrete floor, missing the guard entirely. 

The noise drew the guard's attention back to Arthur. 

The zombie hesitated, its dead brain unable to process the conflicting stimuli of the two living targets in the cages. 

It was a momentary pause, but it was all Arthur needed. 

He carefully drew the grappling hook back and threw it again. 

The jagged metal frame skidded across the concrete and caught the heavy leather belt of the crawling guard, hooking firmly near the brass key ring. 

Arthur had him. 


Chapter Seven: The Guard's Keys

Arthur pulled the wool rope taut. He didn't yank it; he knew the makeshift knots wouldn't hold against the full dead weight of the zombie. He pulled with a slow, steady pressure, dragging the guard across the concrete toward his cell. 

The guard didn't resist the pull. It simply reoriented itself, snapping its jaws as it was dragged closer to the source of the fresh meat. 

*Three feet.*

The smell of the rotting corpse was overpowering, a thick, sweet stench of decay that made Arthur gag. 

*Two feet.*

Sal, the reanimated hitman in the next cell, was thrashing violently against the dividing bars, trying to reach the guard as it was dragged past his cage. 

*One foot.*

The crawling guard reached the bars of Cell 14. 

The zombie lunged forward, thrusting its ruined hands through the steel bars, grabbing Arthur's pant leg. The jaw snapped wildly, teeth scraping against the cold steel as it tried to reach Arthur's flesh. 

Arthur scrambled backward, kicking the dead hands away. 

The guard was pressed tightly against the bars. 

Arthur dropped the wool rope and crawled forward. He kept his body low, out of reach of the snapping jaw. 

He reached through the bars, his fingers brushing against the cold brass of the keys on the guard's belt. 

He grabbed the heavy ring. 

But the keys were secured to the belt with a thick leather strap and a heavy brass snap. 

Arthur fumbled with the snap, his bleeding fingers lacking the dexterity needed to open it. 

The guard lunged again, its dead fingers clamping tightly around Arthur's wrist. 

The grip was terrifying, a vice of rigor mortis and unyielding dead muscle. The zombie began to pull Arthur's arm through the bars, pulling it toward its snapping teeth. 

Arthur screamed, planting his boots against the steel bars and pulling backward with all his might. 

He couldn't break the grip. 

He looked at the keys. He couldn't unclip them. 

He needed to take the entire belt. 

Arthur ignored the snapping jaw inches from his wrist. He reached his free hand through the bars and grabbed the heavy brass buckle of the guard's leather belt. 


Chapter Eight: The Reach

Arthur fumbled with the heavy brass buckle, his fingers slick with his own blood and the rotting fluids of the zombie holding his wrist. 

The dead guard continued to pull, drawing Arthur's arm deeper through the bars. The snapping teeth were an inch from his forearm. 

Arthur finally released the tension on the buckle. The thick leather belt slid free. 

Arthur grabbed the belt near the key ring and yanked violently. 

The belt slid out from under the crawling guard's ruined torso. 

Arthur had the keys. 

But the guard still had his wrist. 

Arthur roared, throwing his entire body weight backward, using the leverage of the steel bars against the zombie's arm. 

With a sickening *SNAP*, the dead guard's elbow dislocated. The grip loosened just enough. 

Arthur tore his arm free, stumbling backward into the center of the cell, clutching the heavy leather belt and the brass key ring to his chest. 

He lay on the concrete floor, gasping for air, staring at the ceiling. 

He had done it. He had the keys. 

He allowed himself five minutes to rest, letting his heart rate slow, listening to the frustrated snapping of the dead guard outside his bars. 

He dragged himself to his feet. He was incredibly weak, but the prospect of escape—and water—fueled a final surge of adrenaline. 

He moved to the cell door. The heavy manual deadbolt was located on the outside of the steel bars, designed explicitly so that inmates couldn't reach it from the inside. 

Arthur was an escape artist. He knew how to defeat it. 

He took off his heavy canvas prison shirt. He tied one end of the shirt securely around the handle of the deadbolt mechanism on the outside. 

He then passed the other end of the shirt back through the bars into his cell. 

He now had a leash on the deadbolt. 

He took the heavy brass key ring and selected the long, intricate master key. 

He reached his arm through the bars, twisting his shoulder painfully to reach the keyhole on the outside of the lock casing. 

He inserted the key. It slid home with a satisfying click. 

He turned the key, disengaging the primary locking tumblers. 

Now came the hard part. He had to throw the heavy deadbolt using the leash, while keeping the tumblers disengaged with the key. 

He pulled the canvas shirt tightly with his left hand, gripping the key with his right. 


Chapter Nine: The Keys

"Come on," Arthur hissed through cracked lips, pulling the canvas shirt with all his remaining strength. 

The heavy steel deadbolt groaned. It hadn't been oiled in weeks, and the mechanism was stiff. 

Arthur gritted his teeth, his muscles burning as he strained against the heavy lock. 

The dead guard on the floor outside was thrashing wildly, its dislocated arm slapping uselessly against the concrete, its jaw snapping at Arthur's boots. 

With a sudden, sharp *CLACK*, the heavy deadbolt slid back. 

The cell door swung open an inch. 

Arthur gasped, releasing his grip on the canvas shirt. He had done it. He had beaten the inescapable fortress of Alcatraz. 

But in his exhaustion and relief, his sweaty, bleeding fingers slipped. 

The heavy brass key ring tumbled from his grasp. 

It hit the concrete floor outside the cell with a loud, ringing crash, sliding three feet away down the tier. 

Arthur stared at the keys in horror. 

He didn't need them to open his cell; the door was already unlocked. But he needed them to open the heavy steel doors at the end of the cellblock leading to the administrative wing. Without the keys, he was just out of his cage, but still trapped in the massive concrete tomb of B-Block. 

He pushed the heavy steel cell door open. It creaked loudly on its hinges, the sound echoing through the silent prison. 

He stepped out onto the tier. 

Instantly, the crawling guard lunged, its unbroken arm grabbing Arthur's ankle. 

The zombie's jaw snapped shut, sinking its teeth deep into the thick leather of Arthur's heavy prison boot. 

Arthur cursed, kicking wildly with his other foot. He struck the zombie in the face, but the jaws didn't release. 

"Get off me!" Arthur yelled, his panic finally breaking through his disciplined exterior. 

He stomped heavily on the dead guard's skull with his free boot, a brutal, desperate blow that cracked the cranium and finally destroyed the brain. 

The jaw went slack. The zombie was permanently dead. 

Arthur stumbled backward, gasping for air. 

He looked down the tier. The keys were lying on the concrete, three feet away. 

But he had made too much noise. 

The heavy, metallic creak of the cell door, Arthur's shouts, and the sound of the struggle had echoed throughout the massive, silent cavern of B-Block. 

And the dead had heard it. 


Chapter Ten: The Cellblock

A low, collective moan rose from the darkness below. 

It wasn't just a few zombies. It was the sound of a massive, unthinking horde shifting its attention. 

Arthur looked over the railing of the third-tier catwalk, staring down into the pitch-black abyss of the cellhouse floor. 

Dozens of pale shapes were moving in the darkness. Infected guards, trustees, and prisoners who had been caught outside their cells when the lockdown occurred. 

They were all looking up. 

Arthur didn't hesitate. He dove for the keys, snatching the heavy brass ring from the concrete. 

He turned and sprinted toward the end of the tier, his legs trembling with exhaustion and dehydration. 

He reached the heavy steel stairs leading down to the second tier. 

He took them two at a time, his boots clanging loudly against the metal grates. 

As he reached the landing, the first wave of the dead began to climb the stairs from the ground floor. 

They were a terrifying sight—men in torn, blood-stained uniforms and prison denim, their eyes milky white in the dim moonlight filtering through the windows. 

Arthur gripped the key ring tightly. He had to reach the heavy steel doors leading to the administrative wing at the end of the ground floor. It was his only way out. 

He charged down the final flight of stairs, using the heavy momentum of his descent to bowl over the first two zombies that reached the landing. 

He hit the ground floor running. 

The cellhouse floor was packed with the dead. They shambled toward him, a wall of rotting flesh and snapping jaws. 

Arthur was an escape artist, not a fighter. He dodged and weaved, slipping past reaching hands, using his intimate knowledge of the cellhouse layout to navigate the narrow spaces between the cages. 

He saw the heavy steel doors of the administrative wing at the far end of the block. 

He sprinted for them, his lungs burning, his vision narrowing to a tunnel. 

He crashed heavily into the steel doors, frantically searching the key ring for the large, brass master key. 

He found it. He shoved it into the heavy manual lock casing. 

He turned the key. The tumblers clicked. 

But the door didn't open. 

Arthur pulled on the heavy handle. It was locked solid. 

He looked through the reinforced glass window set into the steel doors. 

On the other side, in the administrative control room, was the heavy manual override lever that Guard Mitchell had thrown on the first day. 

The master key unlocked the manual deadbolt, but it didn't override the electronic locking system that Mitchell had engaged from the outside. 

Arthur was locked in. 

He slowly turned around, his back pressed against the cold steel doors. 

The horde had closed the distance. A hundred dead men, the former guards and inmates of Alcatraz, surrounded him in the dark. 

Arthur "The Architect" Vance, the man who could escape any cage, had finally found a prison he couldn't break. 

He dropped the brass keys onto the concrete floor. 

The dead descended upon him, their hunger absolute, proving that on the Rock, there were no happy endings.



Story Fifteen: The Hollywood Premiere

Chapter One: The Red Carpet

Hollywood Boulevard in 1936 was a blinding collision of klieg lights, screaming fans, and the desperate illusion of perfection. 

Outside Grauman's Chinese Theatre, the premiere of the new historical epic *Cleopatra's Fall* was in full swing. The air smelled of expensive perfume, hot asphalt, and the sharp, metallic tang of flash powder. 

Veronica Vane stepped out of her black Packard limousine, adjusting the plunging neckline of her silver silk dress. She was a fading starlet, thirty years old and terrified that this supporting role would be her last chance at relevance. She needed the flashbulbs tonight. She needed the attention. 

She paused on the red carpet, flashing a brilliant, practiced smile at the bank of photographers. 

"Veronica! Over here, darling!" 

The voice belonged to Jimmy, a persistent paparazzo from the *Los Angeles Times*. He was leaning aggressively over the heavy velvet ropes, his heavy Graflex camera raised. 

Veronica turned toward him, striking a pose. 

Suddenly, a figure burst through the thick crowd of screaming fans standing behind Jimmy. 

It was a man in a filthy, torn suit. He didn't look like a crazed fan; he looked like a derelict who had wandered in from the rail yards. His face was pale, his eyes wide and unblinking, and he was stumbling forward with a terrifying, mindless momentum. 

"Hey, buddy, back off!" Jimmy yelled, trying to shield his camera from the filthy man. 

The man didn't back off. He lunged. 

He tackled the photographer, driving him over the velvet rope and onto the red carpet, right at Veronica's feet. 

The crowd gasped. The flashbulbs erupted in a blinding strobe effect, capturing the horrific scene in stark black and white. 

The filthy man didn't throw a punch. He simply sank his teeth deep into the side of Jimmy's neck. 

The photographer screamed, a raw, gurgling sound that was instantly drowned out by the panicked shrieks of the crowd. 

Veronica froze in absolute horror as a bright arc of arterial blood sprayed across the hem of her silver silk dress. 

"Get him off!" someone yelled. 

Two LAPD officers rushed forward, drawing their heavy nightsticks. They battered the crazed man over the head, finally pulling him off the bleeding photographer. 

But as they dragged the attacker away, Veronica saw his eyes. 

They weren't the eyes of a madman. They were a dull, milky white. 

"Inside! Everyone inside!" yelled Director Hughes, a demanding, eccentric man in a tailored tuxedo, grabbing Veronica's arm and pulling her toward the ornate entrance of the theatre. 

"Jimmy..." Veronica gasped, looking back at the photographer lying in a pool of blood on the red carpet. 

"He's dead, Veronica," Hughes snapped, dragging her through the brass doors. "And I am not letting a riot ruin my premiere."


Chapter Two: The Theatre

The massive, ornate auditorium of Grauman's Chinese Theatre was insulated from the chaos outside. The heavy velvet curtains were drawn, and the air was cool and smelled of expensive cigars. 

Nearly a thousand of Hollywood's elite, completely unaware of the bloody scene on the red carpet, were settling into their plush seats as the house lights began to dim. 

Veronica sat rigidly in the VIP section next to Hughes, her hands shaking violently. She kept staring at the dark bloodstain on the hem of her silver dress. 

"Calm down, darling," Hughes whispered, patting her knee patronizingly. "It was just a crazed fan. The police handled it."

"He bit him," Veronica whispered, her voice trembling. "He tore his throat out."

"People do crazy things for a picture," Hughes replied dismissively, his eyes fixed on the massive silver screen. 

The heavy, sweeping orchestral score of the opening credits roared through the theatre's massive sound system, vibrating the floorboards. 

In the grand lobby outside the auditorium, the LAPD officers had laid the body of Jimmy the photographer on a plush velvet sofa while they waited for the coroner. 

They had left a single young usher to watch the body. 

The usher, a nervous kid in a red uniform, stared at the horrific wound on the photographer's neck. The bleeding had stopped, and the man's skin was entirely gray. 

Suddenly, Jimmy's chest heaved.
