The usher jumped back, gasping. "Hey! He's alive! Get a doctor!"

But Jimmy wasn't alive. The venomous fever that had traveled the rails from the Dustbowl to California had claimed him. 

The dead photographer sat up slowly. His eyes opened, revealing the same milky white, unseeing stare as his attacker. 

He slid off the velvet sofa. He didn't stumble. He moved with a slow, relentless purpose. 

The usher backed away, his hands raised defensively. "Hey, buddy, just take it easy. The ambulance is coming."

Jimmy lunged. 

He grabbed the young usher by the lapels of his red uniform, driving him backward into the heavy mahogany doors leading to the main auditorium. 

The zombie's jaw snapped shut, tearing into the usher's cheek. 

The usher screamed, but the sound was completely swallowed by the deafening roar of the movie soundtrack blasting from the other side of the doors. 


Chapter Three: The Silver Screen

Inside the massive auditorium, the historical epic was in full swing. On the giant silver screen, massive armies clashed in the desert, accompanied by the thunderous boom of cinematic artillery. 

The sound was so incredibly loud that the wealthy audience didn't hear the muffled screams and the heavy thuds echoing from the lobby outside. 

Veronica Vane couldn't focus on the movie. Her eyes kept darting nervously around the dark theatre. 

She looked down the plush, carpeted aisle toward the heavy mahogany doors at the back. 

The doors slowly pushed open. 

A figure stumbled into the aisle, silhouetted against the dim light from the lobby. 

It was the young usher. But his red uniform was torn and soaked in blood. He stumbled forward a few steps, then collapsed heavily onto the carpet. 

"Look," Veronica whispered, grabbing Hughes's arm and pointing toward the aisle. 

Hughes sighed in annoyance, tearing his eyes from the screen. "What is it now, Veronica? Is someone drunk?"

He squinted in the darkness. 

Behind the fallen usher, more figures were stepping into the auditorium. 

They weren't ushers or late arrivals. 

It was Jimmy the photographer, his neck a ruined mess of torn flesh. And behind him, shambling through the open doors, was a horrific procession of infected pedestrians, LAPD officers, and crazed fans from Hollywood Boulevard. 

The horde had broken through the police line and swarmed the lobby. Now, drawn by the noise and the heat of a thousand living bodies, they were pouring into the theatre. 

The dead moved slowly down the dark aisles, perfectly masked by the deafening roar of the movie. 

A wealthy studio executive sitting in the back row felt a cold hand touch his shoulder. He turned around, an angry reprimand on his lips. 

A dead woman in a torn evening gown sank her teeth into his nose. 

The executive shrieked, a sound of pure, piercing agony that finally cut through the cinematic soundtrack. 

He stood up, thrashing wildly, pulling the zombie over the seat and into the row with him. 

The panic was instantaneous. 


Chapter Four: The Panic

The darkness of the theatre was a profound tactical advantage for the dead. 

The audience, suddenly realizing they were under attack, erupted into absolute pandemonium. A thousand people tried to stand up simultaneously, scrambling over plush velvet seats and trampling each other in the dark. 

The screams were deafening, a horrific chorus of terror that dwarfed the orchestral score still blasting from the speakers. 

"Out! Get out!" Hughes screamed, shoving Veronica forward. 

They were in the front row of the VIP section, the furthest point from the main exits at the back of the auditorium. And those exits were completely blocked by the advancing horde. 

"We can't go that way!" Veronica cried, watching the horrific massacre unfolding in the aisles. 

Zombies were pulling screaming men and women down into the rows, tearing into them with unyielding, dead strength. The plush carpet was quickly becoming slick with blood. 

"The emergency exits!" Hughes yelled, pointing toward a pair of heavy doors near the front of the stage. 

They scrambled out of their row, fighting against the surging tide of panicked celebrities trying to reach the stage. 

Hughes reached the emergency exit first and pushed hard against the push-bar. 

It didn't budge. The heavy steel door was chained shut from the outside, an illegal but common practice by theatre managers to prevent people from sneaking in. 

"It's locked!" Hughes screamed, hammering his fists against the steel. 

Veronica looked back. The horde was advancing down the aisles, moving slowly but inexorably, leaving a trail of butchered corpses in their wake. 

And the dead were beginning to stand back up. The wealthy patrons who had been bitten moments ago were reanimating, swelling the ranks of the horde. 

"Upstairs!" Veronica yelled, grabbing Hughes by his tuxedo jacket. 

She pointed to a narrow, spiral iron staircase tucked into an alcove near the stage, leading up to the projection booth. 

"Go!" Hughes yelled, pushing her toward the stairs. 

They scrambled up the narrow iron steps, their feet echoing loudly over the screams below. 

They reached a small landing at the top and found themselves facing a heavy steel fire door. 


Chapter Five: The Projection Room

Hughes grabbed the heavy iron handle of the fire door and yanked it open. 

They burst into the projection room, a small, cramped space smelling intensely of hot metal, ozone, and the sharp, chemical tang of highly flammable celluloid film. 

The roar of the massive, carbon-arc projector dominated the room, casting a brilliant, flickering beam of light through a small square window overlooking the chaotic auditorium below. 

Hughes slammed the heavy steel fire door shut and threw the massive iron deadbolt. 

"We're safe," Hughes gasped, leaning against the door, his tailored tuxedo soaked in sweat. "The door is solid steel. They can't get through."

Veronica didn't answer. She was staring at the floor near the massive projector. 

The projectionist, an older man in suspenders, was lying on his back. His head was a ruined, bloody mess, having been smashed violently against the heavy iron base of the projector. 

"He's dead," Veronica whispered, backing away. 

"Heart attack?" Hughes guessed, moving closer. "The panic outside..."

But the massive dent in the man's skull wasn't from a heart attack. 

A sharp, metallic scraping sound drew their attention to a dark corner of the cramped room. 

A figure stepped out of the shadows. 

It was a young man, a film runner whose job was to carry the heavy reels of film up from the basement. His neck was torn open, and his eyes were milky white. 

The infection had already been in the projection room before they arrived. 

The dead runner had killed the projectionist and was waiting in the dark. 

"Look out!" Veronica screamed. 

The zombie lunged at Hughes, its jaw snapping furiously. 

Hughes stumbled backward, tripped over a heavy canister of film, and fell hard onto the floor. 

The zombie scrambled over the canister, reaching for the director's face. 

Veronica grabbed the heaviest thing she could find: a solid steel film rewinding crank. 

She swung it like a baseball bat, striking the zombie directly in the side of the head. 

The heavy steel cracked the skull, but didn't destroy the brain. The zombie simply turned its milky white eyes toward Veronica and lunged. 

Veronica backed up, hitting the hot metal casing of the massive projector. She swung the crank again, this time aiming directly for the face. 

The heavy steel shattered the zombie's nose and drove upward into the brain pan. 

The dead runner went limp, collapsing onto the floor at her feet. 

Veronica dropped the crank, her hands shaking violently. 

"Are you alright?" she gasped, looking at Hughes. 

Hughes nodded, pulling himself up from the floor. He was pale, but unbitten. 

They were safe in the locked room. 

But their horrific ordeal was just beginning. 

*THUMP.*

A heavy fist struck the steel fire door from the outside. 

Then another. And another. 

The horde from the auditorium had found the spiral stairs. They were pressing against the steel door, their numbers growing by the second. 


Chapter Six: The Nitrate

The pounding on the heavy steel fire door was a relentless, terrifying rhythm. Dozens of dead hands and heads were battering against the metal, driven by the scent of the living flesh inside. 

"It'll hold," Hughes said, trying to convince himself as much as Veronica. "It's a fire door. It's meant to contain a blaze."

Veronica wasn't looking at the door. She was looking at the massive, roaring machine dominating the center of the cramped room. 

The carbon-arc projector was a beast of early cinema technology. It generated a brilliant, blinding light by creating a continuous electrical arc between two carbon rods. It was incredibly bright, and incredibly hot. 

And running through the heart of this intensely hot machine was thousands of feet of nitrocellulose film. 

Nitrate film was the industry standard in 1936. It produced beautiful, crisp images on the screen. 

It was also highly unstable, incredibly explosive, and burned with a terrifying, unquenchable intensity, producing highly toxic nitrogen dioxide gas as it burned. 

"The projector," Veronica said, her voice trembling. "There's no one running it."

Hughes looked at the machine. The massive reels were spinning, feeding the explosive film through the complicated series of sprockets and gates, directly past the intensely hot carbon arc. 

Normally, the projectionist had to constantly monitor the film feed and the carbon rods, ensuring smooth operation. If the film paused or jammed in the gate for even a fraction of a second, the intense heat of the arc would ignite it instantly. 

"We have to turn it off," Veronica said, stepping toward the machine. 

"No!" Hughes snapped, grabbing her arm. "If we turn it off, the auditorium goes dark. It's the only thing keeping those monsters from seeing properly down there. The light confuses them."

"Hughes, that film is nitrate!" Veronica argued, pointing at the spinning reels. "If it jams, this whole room will explode!"

"I've made a hundred pictures, Veronica," Hughes said dismissively. "I know how a projector works. It's fine."

But Hughes was a director, not a technician. He didn't know that the dead film runner's body had knocked heavily against the lower take-up reel during the struggle, slightly bending the delicate aluminum spindle. 

The take-up reel was wobbling, spinning unevenly, pulling the highly explosive film through the gate with erratic, dangerous tension. 

*Clack-clack-clack.*

The sound was subtle at first, barely audible over the roar of the carbon arc and the pounding on the steel door. 

But it was the sound every projectionist dreaded. 

The film was jumping the sprockets. 


Chapter Seven: The Jam

The wobbling take-up reel finally snagged. 

With a sharp, tearing sound, the delicate nitrate film jammed in the projector gate, directly in front of the blindingly hot carbon arc. 

The film stopped moving. 

"What happened?" Hughes demanded, staring at the sudden, glaring white light shining through the small window into the auditorium. The movie had stopped. 

"It jammed!" Veronica screamed, panic surging through her veins. 

Within a fraction of a second, the intense, concentrated heat of the carbon arc began to scorch the stationary frame of nitrocellulose film. 

A thick, dark blister appeared in the center of the frame, accompanied by a sharp, chemical hiss. 

"Turn it off! Turn off the lamp!" Veronica yelled, searching frantically for a master power switch on the complex control panel of the massive machine. 

Hughes didn't look for a switch. He reached directly for the film gate, his arrogant confidence overriding his common sense. 

"I'll just pull it through," Hughes said, grabbing the edges of the jammed film. 

He yanked hard. 

The highly unstable nitrate film tore, but it didn't clear the gate. 

Instead, the friction and the sudden influx of oxygen as the film snapped were the final catalysts. 

The scorching blister in the center of the film frame erupted. 

The nitrate ignited. 

It didn't catch fire like paper or wood. It exploded in a blinding, hissing jet of pure, white-hot flame. 

"Agh!" Hughes screamed, stumbling backward. 

The intense jet of flame had seared across the back of his hand, instantly blistering the skin. 

But the burn on the director's hand was the least of their problems. 

The fire spread instantly, racing up the torn strip of film toward the massive upper magazine containing two thousand feet of highly explosive nitrocellulose. 


Chapter Eight: The Fire Door

The small projection booth was instantly filled with blinding light and a terrifying, chemical roar as the fire consumed the film in the gate. 

Thick, yellow-brown smoke began to billow from the machine—toxic nitrogen dioxide gas, a deadly byproduct of burning nitrate. 

"The fire extinguishers!" Hughes yelled, coughing violently as the acrid smoke hit his lungs. 

He scrambled toward the wall, grabbing a heavy brass cylinder. He aimed the nozzle at the burning projector and squeezed the handle. 

A thick stream of white foam hit the flames. 

But nitrate fires don't need external oxygen to burn. The chemical composition of the film provides its own oxygen supply. Water and foam were completely useless against it. 

The flames simply hissed and continued to burn with terrifying, uncontrollable ferocity. 

The fire reached the upper magazine. 

With a deafening *WHOOSH*, the two thousand feet of tightly wound nitrate film ignited simultaneously. 

The massive metal canister exploded outward, sending a geyser of white-hot flame and burning celluloid shooting across the cramped projection room. 

The intense heat was instantaneous and unbearable. It felt like standing directly inside a blast furnace. 

"We have to get out!" Veronica screamed, covering her face and diving toward the heavy steel fire door. 

She grabbed the heavy iron deadbolt and tried to throw it. 

It wouldn't budge. 

"Help me!" she screamed over the roar of the inferno. 

Hughes abandoned the useless extinguisher and threw his weight against the iron bolt. 

It wouldn't move. 

The immense, unyielding pressure of the zombie horde pushing against the door from the outside was too great. The heavy steel door was wedged tight against its frame. 

They were trapped. 

They were locked inside a ten-by-ten steel box with thousands of feet of burning explosives. 


Chapter Nine: The Ignition

The heat inside the projection room became lethal within seconds. 

The paint on the walls began to blister and peel. The heavy steel casing of the projector glowed a dull cherry red. 

The fire spread from the upper magazine, igniting the spare film canisters stacked on the metal shelves. 

Each canister erupted like a flare, filling the small room with blinding light and a hurricane of toxic, yellow smoke. 

Veronica collapsed onto the floor, gasping for air, but the toxic smoke was heavier than air, settling near the floorboards in a suffocating cloud. 

Her eyes burned, her lungs felt like they were filled with crushed glass, and the intense heat was searing her skin through the silver silk dress. 

She looked up through the stinging tears. 

Hughes was standing near the door, tearing frantically at his tuxedo collar, coughing up blood as the toxic nitrogen dioxide ravaged his respiratory system. 

"Open the door!" Hughes screamed, his voice a ragged croak, pounding his blistering fists against the hot steel. 

The pounding from the outside answered him. 

The dead didn't care about the heat. They didn't care about the toxic smoke. They only knew that there was living flesh on the other side of the door. They continued to batter the steel, their sheer numbers holding the door firmly shut, sealing the projection booth like a pressure cooker. 

The massive lower take-up reel, containing the film that had already run through the projector, finally caught fire. 

The explosion of flame was blinding. 

The small projection booth was entirely consumed by the inferno. 


Chapter Ten: The Inferno

There was no rescue. There was no escape. 

The intense, localized heat of the burning nitrate reached over three thousand degrees Fahrenheit. 

The heavy steel fire door, designed to hold back a building fire, began to warp and buckle under the unimaginable temperature. 

But it didn't open. The horde outside continued to push, their own flesh beginning to roast against the superheated metal. 

Inside the booth, the air literally burned. 

Veronica Vane, the fading starlet desperate for the spotlight, found it in the most horrific way imaginable. 

She curled into a fetal position on the floor, her silver silk dress instantly melting to her skin. She couldn't scream; the toxic smoke had paralyzed her vocal cords and destroyed her lungs. 

She simply burned. 

Hughes fell next to her, his clothes entirely engulfed in flames. He thrashed blindly for a few agonizing seconds before the intense heat boiled the fluid in his brain, ending his misery. 

The Hollywood premiere of *Cleopatra's Fall* ended in absolute, uncompromising tragedy. 

The horde in the auditorium below would eventually be consumed as the massive nitrate fire burned through the floorboards and ignited the entire theatre. 

But in the small, locked projection booth, the flames burned bright and furious, a blinding, toxic inferno that consumed the director and the starlet, leaving nothing behind but ash and the melted remains of the silver screen. No happy endings.



Story Sixteen: The Submarine

Chapter One: The Dive

The ocean off the coast of California was a deep, unyielding blue, stretching endlessly toward the horizon. It was August 1939, and the USS *Shark*, an S-class diesel-electric submarine, was conducting routine dive exercises. 

Captain Reynolds, a stoic veteran of the Great War, stood in the cramped, humid conn, his eyes fixed on the depth gauge. 

"Take her down to periscope depth," Reynolds ordered. 

The klaxon blared, two sharp, terrifying blasts that echoed through the narrow steel tube. "Dive! Dive!" 

The deck tilted sharply downward as the massive ballast tanks flooded with seawater. The *Shark* slipped beneath the waves, leaving the bright California sun behind. 

Down in the forward torpedo room, the air was thick with the smell of diesel oil, sweat, and unwashed bodies. 

Torpedoman Jones, a young, nervous sailor from Kansas, clung to a heavy brass handwheel to steady himself as the deck pitched. He hated diving. He hated the claustrophobia, the constant creaking of the pressure hull, and the absolute darkness that surrounded them just a few hundred feet down. 

He looked over at his bunkmate, Seaman Miller. 

Miller hadn't looked right since they left San Diego. He was pale, sweating profusely, and shivering despite the stifling heat of the torpedo room. He had claimed he caught a bug on shore leave. 

But Jones had seen the dark, bruised mark on Miller's forearm when he changed his shirt. It looked like a bite. 

"You okay, Miller?" Jones asked over the hum of the electric motors. 

Miller didn't answer. He was lying in his narrow bunk, his face turned to the steel bulkhead. 

"Miller?" Jones stepped closer, reaching out to shake his bunkmate's shoulder. 

The moment Jones touched him, Miller rolled over. 

His eyes were wide open, but they weren't blue anymore. They were a dull, milky white. A thick stream of dark blood trickled from the corner of his mouth. 

The sickness from the mainland had slipped past the quarantine and hitched a ride to the bottom of the ocean. 


Chapter Two: The Torpedo Room

Miller didn't speak. He didn't blink. He simply lunged. 

The zombie sailor launched himself from the narrow bunk with terrifying speed, tackling Jones to the steel deck plates. 

The heavy thud of their bodies echoed through the cramped torpedo room. 

"Get off me!" Jones screamed, throwing his forearms up to protect his throat. 

Miller's jaw snapped furiously, his teeth scraping against the thick canvas of Jones's uniform shirt. The dead man's strength was overwhelming. He pinned Jones to the deck, his cold fingers clawing at the young sailor's face. 

The two other torpedomen in the room, paralyzed by shock for a fraction of a second, finally reacted. 

"Hey! What the hell are you doing?" one of them yelled, grabbing Miller by the back of his uniform collar and hauling him off Jones. 

Miller didn't resist the pull. He simply spun around and sank his teeth deep into the man's neck. 

The torpedoman screamed, a horrific, gurgling sound as Miller tore out his jugular vein. 

Blood sprayed across the polished brass and steel of the torpedo tubes. 

The third sailor, a massive, burly petty officer, grabbed a heavy brass wrench from the tool rack and swung it wildly at Miller's head. 

The heavy wrench struck the zombie in the temple, shattering the bone. 

Miller stumbled backward, releasing the dying torpedoman, but he didn't fall. The Romero rule was uncompromising. The blow hadn't penetrated deep enough to destroy the brain. 

Miller turned his milky white eyes toward the petty officer and lunged again. 

Jones, scrambling backward on the bloody deck plates, watched in absolute horror as the dead sailor tore into the massive petty officer, overwhelming him with sheer, unthinking ferocity. 

"Captain!" Jones screamed into the sound-powered telephone mounted on the bulkhead. "Captain, we need help in the forward torpedo room! Miller's gone crazy! He's killing everybody!"


Chapter Three: The Bulkhead

In the control room, Captain Reynolds heard the frantic, blood-curdling screams echoing through the receiver. 

He didn't hesitate. He was a submarine commander. He lived and died by protocol and the brutal calculus of survival in a steel tube. 

"Seal the forward bulkhead!" Reynolds roared. 

The Executive Officer stared at him, his face pale. "Captain, Jones is still in there!"

"I said seal the bulkhead, mister!" Reynolds bellowed. "If whatever is happening in there spreads to the rest of the boat, we all die. Secure the compartment!"

The heavy steel watertight door separating the forward torpedo room from the rest of the submarine slammed shut. The massive locking wheel spun, throwing the heavy steel dogs into place with a definitive, ringing *CLANG*. 

In the forward torpedo room, Jones heard the heavy door seal shut. 

He dropped the sound-powered telephone, his heart hammering against his ribs. 

He was locked in. 

He backed away from the heavy steel door, retreating until his back hit the cold metal of the forward torpedo tubes. 

Miller had finished butchering the petty officer. 

The zombie stood up slowly, its uniform soaked in the blood of its crewmates. The milky white eyes slowly scanned the cramped, bloody room. 

They locked onto Jones. 

And then, the lights went out. 

The struggle had ruptured a main electrical conduit running along the bulkhead. The sudden shower of sparks triggered a breaker, plunging the forward torpedo room into absolute, impenetrable darkness. 

The hum of the electric motors faded, replaced by the terrifying silence of the deep ocean. 

And in that darkness, Jones heard the wet, shuffling footsteps of the dead man moving toward him. 


Chapter Four: The Dark

The absolute darkness of a submarine is a physical weight, heavier and more complete than a starless night on the surface. There was no ambient light, no moonlight, no hope of visual reference. 

Jones was blind, trapped in a forty-foot steel tube with a hungry corpse. 

He held his breath, straining his ears over the creaking of the pressure hull. 

*Shuffle... scrape.*

The zombie's heavy boots dragged against the steel deck plates, moving slowly, cautiously. The dead didn't need to see. They hunted by sound and scent. 

Jones slowly, agonizingly, slid his hand along the tool rack mounted on the torpedo tube beside him. 

His fingers brushed against the heavy brass wrench the petty officer had dropped. 

He gripped the cold metal tightly, his knuckles turning white. 

*Shuffle... scrape.*

The sound was closer. Ten feet away. 

Jones didn't move. He knew that any sound he made, the scuff of a boot, the rustle of canvas, would pinpoint his location for the dead man. 

He waited in the terrifying dark, his heart pounding so hard he was sure the zombie could hear it. 

Suddenly, a cold, wet hand brushed against his face. 

Jones screamed, swinging the heavy brass wrench blindly in the dark. 

The heavy metal struck something solid. 

Miller stumbled backward, letting out a low, guttural moan. 

Jones swung again, wildly, driven by pure panic. He hit the heavy steel casing of the torpedo tube, the impact sending a jarring shockwave up his arm. 

He stumbled forward, losing his balance in the dark. 

Miller lunged, tackling him to the deck. 


Chapter Five: The Wrench

They rolled across the bloody deck plates, a desperate, blind struggle in the pitch-black torpedo room. 

Miller's snapping jaws missed Jones's face by an inch, teeth clacking loudly near his ear. The dead man's strength was terrifying, his grip unyielding. 

Jones shoved his left forearm under Miller's chin, desperately trying to keep the teeth away from his throat. 

His right hand was pinned under the zombie's body, still gripping the heavy brass wrench. 

"Get off!" Jones gasped, his lungs burning. 

He writhed and twisted, finally freeing his right arm. 

He didn't try to swing the wrench. There was no room. 

He simply brought the heavy brass head of the tool up and drove it violently downward, using the short, brutal leverage of his triceps. 

The heavy brass struck the back of Miller's skull. 

The impact was a sickening crunch that vibrated up Jones's arm. 

Miller's body shuddered, but the jaws continued to snap. 

Jones struck again, harder this time. 

The brass crushed the occipital bone, driving deep into the brain stem. 

The dead man finally went limp, collapsing heavily onto Jones. 

Jones shoved the heavy corpse off him, scrambling backward across the deck until he hit the cold steel of the forward bulkhead. 

He sat in the absolute darkness, his chest heaving, his hands shaking violently. 

He had survived. He had killed the monster. 

He waited for a long time, listening to the creaking of the pressure hull and his own ragged breathing. 

But then, he heard another sound. 

It wasn't a moan, and it wasn't a shuffling footstep. 

It was a sharp, hissing spray of water. 


Chapter Six: The Leak

Jones fumbled in the dark, crawling across the deck plates toward the sound. 

He found a heavy emergency battle lantern mounted on the bulkhead near the door. He clicked the heavy switch. 

The bright, white beam cut through the darkness, illuminating the horrific carnage of the torpedo room. 

But Jones wasn't looking at the bodies. He followed the hissing sound toward the massive nest of pipes and valves running along the port side of the hull. 

During the frantic struggle in the dark, one of them must have smashed against a high-pressure seawater line used to flood the torpedo tubes. 

The heavy brass valve wheel was completely sheared off, and a thick, high-pressure jet of freezing ocean water was spraying into the compartment. 

"Captain!" Jones screamed, scrambling to the sound-powered telephone. "Captain, we have a leak! The forward compartment is flooding!"

In the control room, Reynolds listened to the frantic report. 

"Can you secure the valve, Jones?" the captain asked, his voice tight. 

"No, sir! The wheel is gone! The pipe is sheared!"

Reynolds looked at the depth gauge. They were at one hundred and fifty feet. The pressure outside the hull was immense. 

"We have to blow ballast and surface," the Executive Officer said urgently. 

"We can't," Reynolds said quietly. "If we surface, the entire crew is exposed to whatever is in that room when we open the hatch. We stay down until we can secure the situation."

"Captain, he's going to drown in there!" 

"It's one man, or fifty," Reynolds stated, his face an unreadable mask. "The bulkhead stays sealed."

He picked up the handset. "Jones, listen to me. You have to find a way to plug that leak. Use mattresses, uniforms, whatever you can find. We cannot open that door."

Jones stared at the heavy steel bulkhead, the reality of his situation settling over him like a suffocating blanket. 

The water was already ankle-deep and rising fast. The freezing temperature of the Pacific Ocean was seeping into his bones. 

He was locked in a flooding steel tube at the bottom of the ocean, alone with the dead. 


Chapter Seven: The Pressure

Jones didn't try to plug the leak. It was useless. The high-pressure jet of water would simply blast through any makeshift plug he could construct. 

He waded through the freezing, knee-deep water, the heavy battle lantern illuminating the floating corpses of his crewmates. 

He returned to the heavy steel bulkhead door and pounded his fists against the cold metal. 

"Captain! Please! You have to open the door!" Jones screamed, his voice raw with terror. "I killed him! Miller's dead! It's just me!"

There was no answer from the control room. 

The water continued to rise, reaching his waist. 

The sheer weight of the thousands of gallons of seawater flooding into the forward compartment was beginning to significantly alter the trim of the submarine. 

The deck pitched sharply downward, angling the nose of the *Shark* toward the ocean floor. 

"Captain!" Jones yelled into the sound-powered telephone, the water now swirling around his chest. "We're going down by the bow! You have to surface!"

In the control room, the situation was rapidly deteriorating. 

"Blow forward ballast!" Reynolds roared, gripping the periscope stanchion as the deck tilted alarmingly beneath him. 

The chief of the boat threw the heavy levers, sending high-pressure air into the forward tanks to push the water out and regain buoyancy. 

But the sheer volume of water inside the pressure hull of the forward torpedo room was too much. The massive dead weight was dragging the nose of the submarine down. 

"We're passing two hundred feet, Captain!" the diving officer yelled, his voice strained. "The bow planes are full up, but she's not responding! The forward compartment is too heavy!"

"Full astern! Try to back her up!" Reynolds ordered. 

The massive diesel-electric motors strained, the propellers churning the water, but the heavy steel leviathan continued to sink, pulled relentlessly downward by the flooded forward compartment. 

In the torpedo room, the water reached Jones's neck. 

He was treading water in the freezing dark, his head pressed against the steel ceiling of the compartment. 

He took one final, deep breath before the rising water swallowed the last pocket of air. 


Chapter Eight: The Ascent

The *Shark* was caught in an inescapable, deadly plunge. 

"Two hundred and fifty feet!" the diving officer called out. "We're approaching test depth, Captain!"

The S-class submarines were built tough, but they were old. Their maximum safe operational depth—the test depth—was two hundred feet. Every foot below that increased the unimaginable pressure on the steel pressure hull. 

The hull began to groan, a deep, terrifying metallic shriek that vibrated through the entire boat. 

The heavy steel bulkheads bowed inward under the strain. Rivets began to pop, shooting across the control room like bullets. 

"We have to open the forward bulkhead!" the Executive Officer screamed over the noise of the straining hull. "If we let the water flood the rest of the boat evenly, we might be able to level her off and blow the main ballast!"

Reynolds hesitated. If they opened the door, they let the water in, but they also risked letting the infection spread. 

But if they did nothing, they were all going to die in the crushing depths. 

"Open it!" Reynolds finally roared. "Open the forward bulkhead!"

Two burly sailors rushed to the heavy steel door separating the control room from the forward compartment. 

They grabbed the massive locking wheel and strained against it. 

But the door wouldn't budge. 

"It's jammed!" one of the sailors yelled. 

The immense pressure differential between the flooded forward compartment and the dry control room was forcing the heavy steel door tight against its frame. It was locked shut by thousands of tons of water pressure. 

They were trapped. 

"Three hundred feet!" the diving officer yelled, his voice cracking with absolute terror. 

The groaning of the hull grew deafening. Tiny streams of high-pressure water began to spray into the control room from failing seals and popping rivets. 

The *Shark* was sinking into the abyss, dragged down by the flooded tomb in her nose. 


Chapter Nine: The Crush Depth

Inside the completely flooded forward torpedo room, the silence was absolute. 

Jones was dead, drowned in the freezing dark. 

The bodies of Miller and the other torpedomen floated lifelessly in the pressurized water, bumping gently against the steel ceiling. 

But the Romero sickness was an unholy, resilient plague. It didn't need oxygen to survive. It only needed an intact brain. 

As the submarine sank deeper, the immense pressure of the ocean began to affect the bodies. 

The dead Torpedoman Miller, his brain still functioning on its primal, infected level, slowly opened his milky white eyes in the pitch-black water. 

He didn't drown. He didn't suffocate. 

He simply existed, floating in the freezing dark, driven by a hunger he could never satisfy. 

The other dead sailors in the compartment, infected by Miller during the initial struggle, also began to twitch and reanimate, their bodies buoyant in the pressurized tomb. 

They were trapped at the bottom of the ocean, immortal and hungry. 

In the control room, the panic had subsided into a cold, terrifying acceptance of their fate. 

The depth gauge passed three hundred and fifty feet. 

The hull was screaming. The heavy steel ribs were visibly bending inward, the immense pressure of the ocean threatening to crush them like a tin can. 

Captain Reynolds stood silently by the periscope stanchion, his eyes closed. He had made the hard call. He had sealed the bulkhead to save his crew, and in doing so, he had doomed them all. 

He listened to the terrifying sounds of the dying submarine. 

He didn't hear the dead men moving in the flooded compartment forward of the bulkhead. 

He didn't know that even if they miraculously survived the crushing depths, the nightmare was already locked inside with them. 

"Four hundred feet," the diving officer whispered. 

It was the final depth call the *Shark* would ever make.


Chapter Ten: The Implosion

The end came with terrifying, merciful speed. 

At four hundred and fifty feet, the immense pressure of the Pacific Ocean finally overwhelmed the structural integrity of the aging S-class submarine. 

The heavy steel pressure hull failed. 

It didn't tear or leak. It imploded. 

The catastrophic structural collapse occurred in a fraction of a millisecond. 

The massive force of the ocean crushed the thick steel hull inward, instantly vaporizing the air inside the submarine due to the massive, instantaneous increase in pressure and temperature. 

The control room, the engine room, the aft torpedo room—the entire length of the *Shark* was pulverized, the steel twisted and torn into a mangled, unrecognizable wreck. 

Captain Reynolds and the fifty men of his crew were killed instantly, their bodies crushed beyond recognition before their brains could even process the event. 

The implosion was total and absolute. 

Even the flooded forward torpedo room was destroyed, the massive pressure wave crushing the floating bodies of the dead sailors, finally destroying their brains and enforcing the Romero rule with the sheer, uncompromising force of the ocean. 

The mangled wreckage of the USS *Shark* plummeted the final few hundred feet to the dark, silent floor of the Pacific. 

The ocean settled, the churning bubbles of the implosion quickly dissipating. 

The depths reclaimed their silence. 

The venomous fever had breached the unbreachable fortress of Alcatraz, consumed the glitz of Hollywood, and sunk to the bottom of the ocean. The sickness was everywhere, and it was absolute. 

In the crushing, freezing darkness of the abyss, there were no survivors, no witnesses, and absolutely no happy endings.



Story Seventeen: The Orphan Train

Chapter One: The Train

The steam engine tore across the Nebraska plains, a massive iron beast dragging six heavy passenger cars through the fading light of a November evening in 1934. 

This was the "Orphan Train." It was filled with two hundred children from the crowded, disease-ridden slums of New York City, bound for rural farming communities in the Midwest where they would be offered up for adoption—or indentured servitude. 

In Car 3, Sister Mary walked down the narrow aisle, steadying herself against the rhythmic rocking of the train. She was a young, compassionate nun, her black habit smelling faintly of lye soap and stale coal smoke. 

"Stay in your seats, children," she said softly, tucking a heavy wool blanket around a sleeping six-year-old girl. 

At the back of the car, sitting rigidly in his wooden seat, was Thomas. He was twelve years old, street-smart, and fiercely protective of the younger orphans. He didn't sleep. He watched everything. 

He watched the heavy iron door at the front of the car swing open. 

The conductor stumbled through. He was a portly man in a blue uniform, but his face was ashen gray. He was coughing violently into a blood-stained handkerchief, his eyes bloodshot and feverish. 

Sister Mary rushed forward. "Mr. Higgins, are you alright?" 

The conductor waved her away, leaning heavily against the brass luggage rack. "Just... a terrible flu, Sister. Catching my breath. Need to... get to the engine... tell the engineer to wire ahead for a doctor at the next station."

He stumbled down the aisle, coughing up a terrifying spray of dark fluid. 

Thomas watched him go. He had seen the "flu" in the slums before they left New York. He had seen the men coughing blood in the gutters, and he had seen what happened to them after they died. 

"Sister," Thomas said quietly as she passed his seat. "That wasn't the flu."

"Don't worry, Thomas," she smiled kindly, completely missing his warning. "Mr. Higgins will be fine."

The conductor pushed through the rear door of the car, heading toward the coal tender and the roaring engine at the front of the train. 

He wasn't going to be fine. 


Chapter Two: The Conductor

The narrow catwalk alongside the coal tender was slick with freezing rain. Conductor Higgins staggered forward, the deafening roar of the steam engine drowning out his hacking coughs. 

He reached the heavy iron door of the engine cab. 

He didn't open it. He collapsed against it, sliding down the hot metal to the vibrating steel floor plates. 

The venomous fever had completely ravaged his lungs. With a final, wet gasp, Higgins died, a hundred miles from the nearest hospital. 

The engine cab was a hellish inferno of heat and noise. The fireman was shoveling massive scoops of black coal into the roaring firebox, while the engineer stared out the narrow, soot-stained window at the dark tracks ahead. 

"Higgins?" the engineer called out, turning away from the window as the heavy iron door swung open. 

The conductor lay motionless on the floor plates. 

"Hey, help me get him up," the engineer yelled to the fireman, leaning down to grab Higgins's shoulders. 

As the engineer's hands touched the blue uniform, Higgins's eyes snapped open. The irises were gone, replaced by a dull, milky white. 

The Romero sickness had taken him. 

The zombie conductor lunged upward with terrifying speed, wrapping his dead hands around the engineer's throat. 

The engineer screamed, but the sound was lost in the roar of the firebox. 

Higgins drove his teeth deep into the engineer's neck, tearing through muscle and artery. 

The fireman, dropping his heavy iron shovel in absolute horror, scrambled backward until he hit the hot steel of the boiler. 

He watched as the dead conductor butchered the engineer on the floor plates. 

Panic overtook the fireman. He didn't try to fight. He scrambled over the pile of coal in the tender, desperate to escape the small, bloody cab. 

He reached the narrow catwalk leading back to the passenger cars. But his boots slipped on the freezing rain. 

He fell heavily onto the iron grate, twisting his ankle with a sharp snap. 

Behind him, Higgins slowly rose from the engineer's body, his mouth dripping with dark blood. 

The train, a massive, unstoppable juggernaut of steam and iron, was now completely unmanned, hurtling through the dark plains at seventy miles an hour. 


Chapter Three: The Sleeper Car

In Car 3, Sister Mary was pouring a cup of water from the brass dispenser when she heard the scream. 

It was faint, muffled by the roar of the tracks and the heavy wooden doors, but it was unmistakably human. 

She set the cup down, her heart fluttering nervously. "Stay here, children," she instructed the wide-eyed orphans. 

She moved to the front door of the car and pushed it open, stepping into the narrow, noisy vestibule connecting Car 3 to the coal tender. 

The heavy iron door leading to the catwalk was swinging wildly in the wind. 

Sister Mary stepped out onto the freezing, rain-slicked platform. 

"Mr. Higgins?" she called out into the dark. 

A figure was dragging itself across the iron grating toward her. It was the fireman. His face was a mask of absolute terror, his ankle broken and useless. 

"Help me, Sister!" he screamed, reaching out a bloody hand. "The conductor... he's gone mad!"

Sister Mary rushed forward, dropping to her knees on the cold iron. She grabbed the fireman's arm, trying to pull him toward the safety of the passenger car. 

But a massive shape loomed in the darkness behind the fireman. 

It was Higgins. The dead conductor shambled forward, completely ignoring the freezing rain and the terrifying speed of the train. 

He lunged, grabbing the fireman by the collar of his heavy canvas jacket and violently hauling him backward. 

Sister Mary screamed, losing her grip on the man's arm. 

She watched in paralyzed horror as Higgins sank his teeth into the fireman's shoulder, tearing a massive chunk of flesh away. 

The fireman shrieked, thrashing wildly on the iron grate, but the zombie's strength was absolute. 

Sister Mary stumbled backward, her habit catching on a protruding bolt. She fell heavily against the heavy iron door of the passenger car. 

Higgins, his face painted in the fireman's blood, slowly turned his milky white eyes toward the young nun. 

He released the dying fireman and began to shamble toward her. 

Sister Mary scrambled to her feet, frantically pulling on the handle of the heavy wooden door. 

But in her panic, she had pushed the locking latch on the inside before stepping out. 

She was locked out on the freezing platform, trapped with the dead. 


Chapter Four: The Coupling

Thomas watched through the small, dirty window of the passenger car. He saw Sister Mary pounding frantically on the glass, her face twisted in terror. 

He saw the dead conductor behind her. 

"Get away from the door!" Thomas yelled to the younger orphans, shoving them toward the back of the car. 

He grabbed the heavy brass fire axe mounted on the wall behind glass. He smashed the glass with his elbow and pulled the heavy weapon free. 

He ran to the front door and threw the locking latch. 

Sister Mary practically fell into the car, sobbing hysterically. 

Thomas slammed the heavy wooden door shut just as Higgins slammed into it from the outside. 

The dead conductor battered against the reinforced glass window, his jaw snapping furiously. The glass cracked, a spiderweb of terrifying lines spreading across the pane. 

"We have to stop the train!" Sister Mary cried, collapsing into a seat. 

Thomas looked past the zombie to the open door of the engine cab in the distance. He couldn't see the engineer, but he could see that the throttle was locked wide open. 

"Nobody's driving," Thomas said grimly. 

"We'll crash!" an older boy yelled, panic spreading through the crowded car. 

Thomas knew the boy was right. The train was flying through the dark, approaching sharp curves at a terrifying speed. They would derail within minutes. 

He looked down at the heavy iron coupling mechanism connecting the passenger car to the coal tender. 

It was a massive steel hook and pin system, located beneath the vestibule platform, right where the zombie was standing. 

If he could uncouple the cars, the engine would speed away, and the heavy passenger cars would gradually roll to a stop on the flat plains. 

"I have to unhook the cars," Thomas said, gripping the fire axe. 

"You can't!" Sister Mary cried, grabbing his arm. "He'll kill you!"

"If I don't, we all die," Thomas replied coldly. 

He knew he couldn't fight the zombie on the narrow platform. He had to go around him. 

He looked up at the ceiling of the passenger car. There was a small, emergency escape hatch leading to the roof. 


Chapter Five: The Roof

Thomas climbed onto the back of a wooden bench and pushed the heavy iron hatch upward. The freezing wind immediately howled into the car, whipping his hair into his eyes. 

"Thomas, please!" Sister Mary begged. 

Thomas ignored her. He hauled himself up through the hatch and onto the rounded, wooden roof of the passenger car. 

The wind was a physical force, threatening to tear him right off the top of the train. The freezing rain stung his face like icy needles. 

He dropped to his stomach, clinging to the narrow wooden walkway running down the center of the roof. 

The train was moving at seventy miles an hour. The noise was deafening, a constant roar of steel on steel and rushing wind. 

He began to crawl forward, inch by terrifying inch, toward the front of the car. 

Below him, he could hear the heavy thuds of the zombie conductor battering against the wooden door, trying to get to the children inside. 

He reached the edge of the roof, looking down at the narrow gap between the passenger car and the coal tender. 

The massive iron coupling mechanism was directly below him, a heavy steel pin locking the two massive vehicles together. 

To pull the pin, he would have to hang upside down over the gap, completely exposed to the zombie standing on the platform just inches away. 

Thomas took a deep breath, the freezing rain mixing with his sweat. 

He gripped the edge of the wooden roof with his left hand, holding the heavy brass fire axe in his right. 

He slowly swung his legs over the edge, lowering himself headfirst into the terrifying, roaring gap between the speeding cars. 


Chapter Six: The Fall

Hanging upside down, Thomas's face was mere feet from the heavy steel wheels grinding against the iron tracks below. The noise and vibration were overwhelming. 

He swung the heavy fire axe, using the curved pick end to hook the heavy iron ring of the coupling pin. 

He pulled with all his strength. 

The pin didn't budge. The immense tension between the pulling engine and the heavy passenger cars locked the steel pin tightly in place. 

"Come on!" Thomas screamed into the wind, straining his young muscles until they burned. 

Suddenly, a cold, dead hand clamped around his left ankle. 

Thomas gasped, looking up. 

The dead fireman, who had been bitten on the platform, had reanimated. The zombie had dragged its broken body up the side of the coal tender and was now leaning over the edge, its milky eyes fixed on Thomas. 

The zombie pulled violently on Thomas's leg, trying to drag him up onto the tender. 

Thomas's grip on the roof slipped. 

He was hanging by one hand, swinging wildly over the roaring steel wheels below. 

The zombie's jaw snapped furiously, tearing at the cuff of Thomas's trousers. 

"Let go!" Thomas roared. 

He swung the heavy brass fire axe upward, driving the sharp blade directly into the zombie's shoulder. 

The heavy blade bit deep into dead muscle and bone, but the zombie didn't feel pain. It simply pulled harder on Thomas's leg. 

Thomas was losing his grip. In seconds, he would fall beneath the wheels and be instantly pulverized. 

He knew he couldn't kill the zombie from this angle. He needed leverage. 

He used his remaining strength to swing his body violently toward the coal tender. 

He slammed his heavy boot directly into the zombie's ruined face, shattering its nose and driving it backward. 

The dead fireman's grip loosened just enough. 

Thomas yanked his leg free. 


Chapter Seven: The Uncoupling

Thomas swung back toward the passenger car, his fingers screaming in agony as he clung to the edge of the roof. 

He hung there for a terrified second, gasping for air in the freezing wind. 

The dead fireman was recovering, dragging its broken body back to the edge of the tender, reaching out with bloody hands. 

Thomas knew he had one last chance. 

He hooked the pick of the fire axe back into the iron ring of the coupling pin. 

He didn't try to pull it with muscle alone. 

He waited for the train to hit a slight curve in the track. 

As the massive locomotive banked into the turn, the tension on the coupling mechanism shifted for a fraction of a second. 

Thomas threw his entire body weight downward, using gravity and the heavy axe to yank the pin upward. 

With a loud, metallic *CLANG*, the heavy steel pin slid free. 

The uncoupling was violently sudden. 

Freed from the massive weight of the six passenger cars, the steam engine surged forward, instantly accelerating away into the darkness. 

The gap between the tender and the passenger car widened rapidly. 

The dead fireman, reaching across the widening gap, lost its balance and plummeted downward, instantly crushed beneath the steel wheels of the passenger car. 

Thomas hung upside down, watching the roaring engine disappear down the track, taking the dead conductor with it. 

He had done it. 

He hauled himself back up onto the roof, collapsing onto the wet wooden walkway, his heart hammering against his ribs. 

The heavy passenger cars, rolling freely on the steel tracks, began to gradually slow down. 

Thomas smiled, the freezing rain washing the sweat from his face. They were safe. 

But Thomas didn't know the route. He didn't know what lay ahead in the dark. 


Chapter Eight: The Derailment

Inside Car 3, Sister Mary and the orphans felt the sudden lurch as the train uncoupled. 

The deafening roar of the steam engine faded, replaced by the smoother, quieter sound of the heavy steel wheels rolling freely on the tracks. 

"He did it!" an older boy cheered. 

Sister Mary closed her eyes, offering a silent, desperate prayer of thanks. 

Up on the roof, Thomas sat up, shivering violently in the freezing rain. He needed to get back inside before he froze to death. 

He began to crawl back toward the open hatch. 

But the smooth rolling of the cars suddenly changed. 

The tracks ahead weren't flat. They were entering a steep, descending grade through a winding section of the Nebraska hills. 

Without the engine to provide braking power, the six heavy passenger cars—thousands of tons of unpowered steel and wood—began to accelerate down the steep incline. 

Thomas felt the wind picking up again. The click-clack of the wheels grew faster, more frantic. 

"Oh no," Thomas whispered, clinging to the wooden walkway. 

Inside the cars, the cheering stopped. The orphans were thrown against their seats as the massive cars rocketed down the steep grade, entirely out of control. 

"Hold on!" Sister Mary screamed, grabbing the two youngest children and pulling them under a heavy wooden bench. 

The unpowered cars hit the first sharp curve at eighty miles an hour. 

The physics were inescapable. 

The heavy steel wheels screamed against the iron rails, shooting massive showers of orange sparks into the dark night. 

The lead car—Car 1—lifted off the outside rail. 

With a deafening, terrifying roar of twisting metal and shattering wood, the Orphan Train derailed. 


Chapter Nine: The Wreckage

The derailment was catastrophic. 

The massive passenger cars tore free from the tracks, plowing into the rocky embankment and violently tumbling down a steep ravine. 

The heavy wooden cars shattered like kindling, throwing screaming children and seats into the dark, freezing night. 

Car 3 rolled three times before coming to a violent halt against a massive oak tree at the bottom of the ravine. 

Inside the crushed car, the silence was agonizing, broken only by the whimpers of the injured and the sound of falling rain. 

Sister Mary slowly opened her eyes. She was pinned beneath a heavy wooden bench, her left leg twisted at a sickening angle. 

She coughed, tasting blood and dust. 

"Children?" she called out weakly into the dark, ruined car. 

A few soft cries answered her, but many were silent. 

Up on the embankment, Thomas had been thrown clear of the roof when the first car derailed. He landed heavily in the thick brush, the impact knocking the wind out of him. 

He lay in the freezing mud, his vision swimming, his body aching from a dozen cuts and bruises. 

He slowly pulled himself up, looking down into the ravine. 

The wreckage of the Orphan Train was a horrifying scene of twisted metal and shattered wood, illuminated by small, scattered fires. 

"Sister Mary!" Thomas yelled, stumbling down the steep, muddy slope toward the crushed remains of Car 3. 

He reached the wreckage and began tearing at the heavy wooden debris, ignoring the splinters driving into his bleeding hands. 

He found Sister Mary pinned beneath the bench. 

"Thomas," she gasped, her face pale in the dim light of a nearby fire. "The children... you have to help the children."

Thomas gripped the heavy wooden bench and tried to lift it. It wouldn't budge. 

"I can't move it," Thomas cried, his voice breaking. 

Suddenly, a low, collective moan echoed from the darkness at the top of the ravine. 

Thomas froze. 


Chapter Ten: The Harvest

The derailment had occurred just two miles outside a small, rural farming town. 

The town was silent, not because the people were asleep, but because the venomous fever had swept through it a week ago. 

The massive crash of the Orphan Train had drawn them out. 

Hundreds of infected farmers, their wives, and their children slowly appeared at the top of the ravine, silhouetted against the dark sky. 

They looked down at the wreckage, their milky white eyes locking onto the scattered, injured orphans crying in the dark. 

Driven by the unyielding hunger of the Romero sickness, the horde began to stumble down the steep, muddy slope. 

"Thomas," Sister Mary whispered, her eyes widening in absolute terror as she saw the dead descending into the ravine. "Run. You have to run."

Thomas looked at the horde, then down at the trapped nun. He still held the heavy brass fire axe in his trembling hand. 

"I'm not leaving you," the twelve-year-old boy said, standing over her, raising the heavy axe. 

The first zombie, a massive man in blood-stained overalls, reached the bottom of the ravine. 

Thomas swung the axe, burying the sharp pick deep into the man's skull. The zombie collapsed. 

But there were hundreds more behind him. 

They swarmed over the wreckage like locusts. 

Sister Mary could only watch, trapped beneath the heavy wood, as the dead descended upon the surviving orphans. The screams of the children were a horrifying symphony of absolute despair, quickly silenced by the ravenous hunger of the horde. 

Thomas fought with the ferocity of a cornered animal, dropping three more zombies before the sheer weight of the horde dragged him down into the mud. 

His screams were the last thing Sister Mary heard before the dead hands finally reached through the shattered window of the crushed car to find her. 

The Orphan Train had delivered its cargo, but in the Dustbowl, the harvest belonged entirely to the dead.



Story Eighteen: The Hooverville

Chapter One: The Eviction

Central Park in 1933 was no longer a jewel of New York City; it was a sprawling, desperate monument to the Great Depression. Thousands of homeless men and families had constructed a massive "Hooverville" shantytown on the Great Lawn, a labyrinth of cardboard, scrap tin, and discarded lumber. 

"Mayor" Sullivan, a former bank manager who had lost everything in the crash, sat in his relatively sturdy wooden shack near the center of the camp. He was the self-appointed leader, trying to maintain a semblance of order among the desperate masses. 

"Sullivan! Get out here!" 

Sullivan stepped out of his shack, wrapping his threadbare coat tightly against the bitter October wind. 

Officer Kelly, a corrupt beat cop who collected weekly bribes from Sullivan to keep the city from bulldozing the camp, stood angrily by the camp's main entrance. 

"You're short this week, Mayor," Kelly sneered, tapping his heavy wooden nightstick against his palm. "The captain said if you're short, we start clearing tents. Starting with the sick ward by the wall."

"We don't have it, Kelly," Sullivan pleaded, stepping closer. "The soup kitchens are empty. The men are starving. Give us a few more days."

"No time," Kelly said coldly, turning toward the dense cluster of dilapidated tents near the high stone wall that bordered the park. 

He marched into the narrow, muddy alleyway between the tents, drawing his nightstick. 

"Alright, you bums, clear out!" Kelly yelled, kicking the side of a canvas tent. "Eviction time!"

A frail, filthy man stumbled out of the tent, coughing violently. He wore rags, and his skin was a terrifying, pallid gray. 

"Get moving," Kelly snarled, shoving the man with his stick. 

The man didn't move away. He stumbled toward the officer. 

"I said move!" Kelly raised his stick to strike. 

But the man lunged with surprising, frantic speed. He grabbed the officer's arm and sank his teeth deep through the thick wool of the uniform sleeve, tearing into the muscle beneath. 

Kelly screamed in shock and pain, swinging his nightstick wildly. He struck the man in the temple, dropping him to the mud. 

"You crazy bastard!" Kelly yelled, clutching his bleeding arm. 

He didn't know about the venomous fever. He didn't know that the sick man wasn't just starving; he was infected with the Romero sickness that had finally reached the city. 


Chapter Two: The Sprawl

Officer Kelly stumbled away from the tent, clutching his bleeding arm, his face pale with shock. 

"Kelly! Are you alright?" Sullivan called out, running toward the officer. 

"Stay away from me, you filthy animals!" Kelly yelled, drawing his service revolver and pointing it wildly at Sullivan and the gathering crowd of homeless men. 

He backed away, stumbling blindly into the dense, maze-like interior of the massive Hooverville. 

Sullivan watched him disappear into the labyrinth of cardboard and tin. He looked down at the man Kelly had struck. The man was dead, his skull cracked by the heavy nightstick. 

"Get some men to move the body," Sullivan ordered grimly. 

But ten minutes later, before anyone could touch the corpse, the dead man slowly opened his milky white eyes. 

Deep inside the sprawling camp, Officer Kelly collapsed against a wall of stacked wooden crates. The venomous fever raced through his bloodstream with terrifying speed, fueled by his panic and his racing heart. 

He died quietly in the shadows, far from the main paths of the camp. 

And then, he reanimated. 

The zombie cop, driven entirely by the unyielding hunger, stumbled out of the shadows and into the dense cluster of sleeping families. 

The Hooverville was the perfect hunting ground for the dead. The tents were packed tightly together, the walls made of nothing but canvas and cardboard. There were no heavy doors, no locks, no escape routes. 

The massacre began silently. The dead cop tore into a sleeping family, his snapping jaws tearing throats before they could even scream. 

As the victims bled out, they too succumbed to the Romero rule, rising minutes later with milky eyes and an insatiable hunger. 

The infection spread through the dense camp like an invisible, bloody wave. 


Chapter Three: The Mayor's Tent

Sullivan woke to the sound of screaming. 

It wasn't the usual argument over a crust of bread or a stolen blanket. It was the frantic, high-pitched shrieking of absolute terror, coming from everywhere at once. 

He grabbed a heavy iron pipe he kept near his cot and burst out of his shack. 

The camp was in absolute chaos. 

Men, women, and children were running blindly through the muddy, narrow alleyways, trampling each other in their panic. 

"What's happening?" Sullivan yelled, grabbing a running man by the shoulder. 

"They're eating people!" the man screamed, his eyes wide with horror. "The sick... they're getting up and eating people!"

Sullivan looked down the main thoroughfare of the camp. 

The horde was advancing. It was a terrifying mass of infected homeless people and dead police officers, their clothes soaked in blood, their jaws snapping furiously as they dragged the living out of their tents and butchered them in the mud. 

Two of Sullivan's "guards," burly men armed with baseball bats, tried to hold the line near the mayor's shack. 

They swung their bats wildly, crushing skulls and shattering ribs. But the Romero rule was absolute. Unless the brain was destroyed, the dead kept coming. 

The guards were quickly overwhelmed by the sheer numbers. They were dragged down into the mud, their screams echoing through the camp as the dead tore them apart. 

Sullivan didn't try to fight. He turned and ran into the dense labyrinth of the Hooverville, desperately searching for a way out. 


Chapter Four: The Maze

The sprawling Hooverville was a death trap. 

The narrow alleyways, constructed randomly over the years, twisted and turned, often ending in blind alleys or collapsing under the panicked stampede of the living. 

Sullivan ran blindly, the heavy iron pipe clutched tightly in his hand. 

He turned a corner and skidded to a halt in the mud. 

The alleyway was blocked by a wall of the dead. Dozens of zombies, their milky eyes shining in the dim light of the campfires, were tearing apart a group of trapped families. 

Sullivan backed away slowly, terrified of drawing their attention. 

He turned and ran in the opposite direction, diving through a collapsed cardboard shack to bypass the main path. 

He scrambled through the ruins, coughing on the dust and the smell of rot. 

He emerged into a small clearing, only to find himself face-to-face with Officer Kelly. 

The corrupt cop was a horrifying sight. His uniform was torn and soaked in blood, his face a ruined mess of necrotic flesh. 

Kelly lunged, his jaws snapping furiously. 

Sullivan swung the heavy iron pipe with all his strength, striking the zombie cop squarely in the jaw. 

The heavy iron shattered the bone, tearing the lower jaw completely off the skull. 

But Kelly didn't stop. He lunged forward, grabbing Sullivan's coat, his bloody, jawless mouth trying to gnaw on the mayor's neck. 

Sullivan roared, shoving the zombie backward and swinging the pipe again. This time, he aimed higher, crushing the side of the skull and destroying the brain. 

Kelly collapsed into the mud. 

Sullivan stood over the body, gasping for air. The camp was lost. He had to reach the high stone wall bordering the park. It was the only way out. 


Chapter Five: The Fire

The panic in the dense camp reached a critical, devastating breaking point. 

In a cluster of tents near the center of the Hooverville, a terrified family, trying to defend themselves in the dark, knocked over a kerosene lantern. 

The cheap, highly flammable fuel spilled across the dry cardboard and scrap wood of their shack. 

It ignited instantly. 

The fire spread with terrifying speed. The entire camp was essentially a massive tinderbox, built of highly combustible materials packed tightly together. 

Flames shot into the night sky, illuminating the horrific massacre taking place below. 

The heat was intense, and the thick, black smoke quickly filled the narrow alleyways, choking the living and blinding them. 

The dead didn't care about the fire. They didn't feel the heat, and they didn't need to breathe the smoke. They continued their relentless pursuit, their milky eyes locking onto the frantic shapes of the living fleeing through the flames. 

Sullivan coughed violently, wrapping his coat over his mouth and nose as he navigated the burning maze. 

The roar of the fire was deafening, competing with the screams of the dying. 

He saw burning zombies stumbling through the camp, their clothes and flesh entirely engulfed in flames, still trying to grab the living before the heat finally destroyed their brains. 

"To the wall!" Sullivan yelled to a group of terrified survivors running past him. "We have to get to the wall!"


Chapter Six: The Wall

Sullivan broke through the edge of the burning shantytown and emerged onto a small strip of grass bordering the massive, eight-foot-tall stone wall that enclosed the park. 

A crowd of several hundred uninfected survivors was already there, pressing frantically against the cold stone. 

"Climb!" Sullivan yelled, pushing his way through the panicked mass. 

But the wall was too high, and the stones were smooth and slick with the damp autumn air. A few young, agile men managed to scramble up and over, disappearing into the city streets beyond. 

But the vast majority of the crowd—the elderly, the children, the weak—couldn't scale it. 

They were trapped. 

Behind them, the massive Hooverville was an inferno. The flames towered fifty feet into the air, creating a solid wall of fire that blocked any retreat back into the park. 

And emerging from the thick, black smoke and the roaring flames, were the dead. 

Thousands of them. The infected population of the entire camp, driven by the noise and the scent of the massive crowd pinned against the wall. 

The horde advanced slowly, an unstoppable, terrifying wave of rotting flesh and snapping jaws, illuminated by the bright orange glow of the burning camp. 


Chapter Seven: The Stampede

As the massive horde closed in, the panic against the wall turned into absolute, mindless terror. 

The crowd surged forward, desperately trying to climb over each other to reach the top of the stone barrier. 

"Stop!" Sullivan screamed, trying to hold his ground as the crush of bodies pressed against him. "You're crushing them!"

But his voice was lost in the deafening roar of the fire and the screams of the crowd. 

The stampede was horrific. People were trampled underfoot, crushed against the cold stone by the immense pressure of the terrified mass behind them. Bones snapped, and lungs were compressed until they burst. 

Sullivan was lifted off his feet by the sheer force of the crowd, his ribs groaning under the unimaginable pressure. 

He fought desperately to stay upright. If he fell, he would be trampled to death in seconds. 

He swung his heavy iron pipe blindly, hitting living men and women just to create an inch of breathing room. 

The front ranks of the horde reached the edge of the screaming crowd. 

They didn't have to chase anyone. They simply waded into the panicked mass, tearing into the trapped people with unyielding ferocity. 

The screams reached a crescendo of absolute, primal agony. 

The dead were eating their way through the crowd, their numbers swelling rapidly as the newly bitten victims bled out and reanimated within minutes, joining the slaughter. 


Chapter Eight: The Smoke

The wind shifted, blowing the thick, black smoke from the burning Hooverville directly over the crowd trapped against the wall. 

The smoke was suffocating, filled with the toxic fumes of burning tar paper and cheap kerosene. 

Sullivan coughed violently, his eyes burning and watering so badly he was completely blind. 

He was suffocating, trapped in the crush of bodies, the heat of the fire searing his back, and the terrifying sounds of the dead tearing into flesh just a few yards away. 

"Help us!" a woman screamed next to him, holding a small child above her head, desperately trying to keep the infant out of the crushing pressure of the crowd. 

Sullivan reached out blindly, grabbing the woman's coat, trying to pull her toward him to create a pocket of space. 

But the crowd surged violently away from the advancing horde, pushing Sullivan and the woman hard against the unyielding stone wall. 

The pressure was immense. Sullivan felt his ribs crack. 

The woman screamed as she was crushed against the stone, the small child slipping from her grasp and disappearing into the chaotic mass of trampling feet below. 

Sullivan couldn't help her. He could barely breathe. 

The thick, black smoke descended completely, plunging the horrific scene into absolute darkness, illuminated only by the frantic, strobe-like flashes of the raging fire. 


Chapter Nine: The Trap

The trap was perfect and inescapable. 

The massive stone wall blocked their escape. The towering inferno of the burning camp cut off their retreat. And the horde, an unyielding, ravenous wall of the dead, was slowly pressing them tighter and tighter against the stone. 

Sullivan, his vision blurred by tears and toxic smoke, his ribs broken, finally realized that the Hooverville was gone, and so was he. 

He didn't swing the iron pipe anymore. There was no point. 

The crowd around him was rapidly dwindling, pulled down into the bloody mud by the dead or trampled by the living. 

The moans of the zombies were now louder than the screams of the survivors. 

A burning zombie, its clothes entirely engulfed in flames, stumbled out of the smoke and grabbed the man standing next to Sullivan. 

The intense heat of the burning zombie ignited the man's coat instantly. The man screamed, thrashing wildly, spreading the fire to the people packed tightly around him. 

The panic reached its absolute zenith. 

The crowd was no longer just fleeing the dead; they were fleeing the fire spreading among themselves. 

Sullivan was shoved violently sideways, losing his footing in the slick mud and blood. 

He fell heavily to the ground, instantly buried beneath a pile of screaming, thrashing bodies. 


Chapter Ten: The Pyre

Sullivan was trapped beneath the weight of the dying crowd, gasping for air in the toxic smoke. 

He couldn't move. He could only listen to the horrific sounds of the end. 

The dead swarmed over the pile of bodies, their jaws snapping as they tore into the trapped survivors. 

Sullivan felt heavy boots stepping on his back, the crushing weight of the zombies moving over him to reach fresh meat. 

Suddenly, a searing, unimaginable pain ripped through his leg. 

He screamed, a raw sound that tore his throat. 

A zombie, crawling through the mud beneath the pile of bodies, had found him. The dead teeth sank deep into his calf, tearing the muscle away from the bone. 

Sullivan thrashed violently, but the weight of the bodies above him held him perfectly still for the feast. 

The pain was absolute, overriding the terror and the suffocating smoke. 

But the end didn't come from the bite. 

The fire, spreading rapidly through the tightly packed crowd, finally reached the pile of bodies where Sullivan was trapped. 

The flames ignited the cheap fabric of their clothes, fueled by the spilled kerosene and the dry autumn leaves mixed in the mud. 

Sullivan felt the intense heat before he saw the flames. 

The fire engulfed the pile of bodies, a horrific pyre built against the cold stone wall of Central Park. 

Sullivan burned alive, trapped beneath the weight of his dying city, his screams mixing with the moans of the dead as the great Hooverville was reduced to ash and bone. No happy endings.



Story Nineteen: The Radio Tower

Chapter One: The Broadcast

The Nebraska plains in 1938 were a desolate, wind-scoured wasteland. The Dustbowl had stripped the topsoil, and the venomous fever had stripped the population. 

Standing alone in the center of the endless, flat expanse was a small concrete blockhouse and a massive, three-hundred-foot AM radio transmission tower. 

Inside the blockhouse, DJ "Wolfman" Jack leaned into a heavy RCA microphone. He was a solitary figure, keeping the airwaves alive long after the major networks had gone dark. 

"This is KNEB, the last voice of the plains," Jack rasped, his voice smooth and tired. "If there's anyone out there listening, stay inside. The fever is moving. The dead are walking."

He cued up a scratchy blues record on the turntable and took a long drag from a cigarette. 

He spun around in his chair and looked out the reinforced glass window of the broadcast booth. 

He almost dropped his cigarette. 

The horizon was moving. 

It wasn't a dust storm. It was a massive, undulating wave of dark shapes slowly creeping across the flat plains. 

It was a migrating horde. Thousands, perhaps tens of thousands, of the infected from Omaha and Lincoln, driven entirely by the unthinking hunger, wandering blindly across the state. 

And they were heading directly toward the lonely radio station. 

"Engineer!" Jack yelled, slamming his hand against the intercom button. 

The Engineer, a mute, massive man covered in grease who lived in the basement and maintained the massive diesel generators, appeared in the doorway a moment later. He held a heavy pipe wrench and looked a question at Jack. 

"Look," Jack said, pointing a trembling finger at the window. 

The Engineer stepped forward and stared at the horizon. 

The massive horde was drawn to the low, electromagnetic hum of the massive transmission tower and the noise of the diesel generators. 

They were coming for them. 

"We have to barricade the doors," Jack said, his voice dropping to a terrified whisper. 


Chapter Two: The Barricade

The Engineer didn't panic. He simply nodded and moved toward the heavy steel security doors at the front of the blockhouse. 

Jack followed, grabbing heavy wooden desks, filing cabinets, and whatever else he could find to stack against the entrance. 

They worked in frantic silence for an hour, turning the small concrete building into a fortress. The Engineer used his heavy wrench to weld the steel deadbolts in place with a portable acetylene torch. 

They were secure. But they were trapped. 

Jack went back to the broadcast booth and looked out the window. 

The vanguard of the horde had reached the chain-link fence surrounding the tower base. 

They didn't try to climb the fence. The sheer pressure of hundreds of bodies simply pushed it over, flattening the steel mesh into the dirt. 

The dead flooded into the compound, their milky white eyes locking onto the concrete blockhouse. 

They surrounded the building, their jaws snapping as they began to batter the heavy steel doors and the reinforced glass windows. 

The sound was a low, terrifying drumbeat of dead flesh against concrete and steel. 

"They can't get in," Jack whispered, trying to convince himself. "The glass is bulletproof. The doors are welded."

The Engineer stood beside him, watching the horde. He didn't look convinced. 

Suddenly, the lights flickered. The turntable slowed down, the blues record warping into a demonic, low-pitched groan. 

Then, the power cut out completely. 

The station was plunged into silence, broken only by the horrific pounding of the dead outside. 

The Engineer tapped Jack on the shoulder, pointing a greasy finger toward the basement door. 

The generator had failed. 


Chapter Three: The Generator

The Engineer grabbed a heavy flashlight and pushed open the door leading to the basement stairs. 

Jack grabbed a heavy steel microphone stand, his knuckles white. "I'm coming with you."

The basement was a labyrinth of thick concrete pillars, massive fuel tanks, and the massive, silent diesel generator. 

As they reached the bottom of the stairs, the Engineer shined his flashlight across the floor. 

The basement was flooded. 

A foot of dark, stagnant water covered the concrete floor, smelling strongly of diesel fuel and raw sewage. 

The Engineer frowned. He waded into the water, his heavy rubber boots splashing loudly. 

Jack followed, shivering in the damp cold. 

The Engineer reached the generator. He ran his light over the massive machine, quickly diagnosing the problem. The intake valve for the cooling system had clogged, causing the engine to overheat and automatically shut down. 

He pointed to a heavy iron wheel located near the floor, submerged in the dark water. 

He pantomimed turning the wheel, then pointed at Jack to hold the flashlight. 

Jack nodded, aiming the beam at the submerged valve. 

The Engineer knelt in the freezing water, reaching down to grab the heavy iron wheel. 

He pulled with all his strength, straining against the rusted metal. 

He didn't see the dark shape moving silently through the water behind the generator. 

It was a drowned zombie, an infected vagrant who had washed into the basement through the massive, four-foot concrete drainage pipe connected to a nearby creek. The Romero sickness didn't require oxygen, so the dead man simply waited in the flooded dark until he heard the splash of the Engineer's boots. 

The zombie surged out of the dark water. 

It didn't moan. It simply lunged, its jaws snapping shut. 


Chapter Four: The Basement

The drowned zombie sank its teeth deep into the back of the Engineer's neck. 

The massive, mute man let out a silent, ragged gasp, his body arching violently as the dead teeth tore into his flesh. 

"Hey!" Jack screamed, dropping the flashlight into the water. 

The basement plunged into absolute darkness. 

Jack heard the violent splashing, the heavy thuds of the Engineer fighting for his life, and the horrific, wet tearing sounds of the zombie feeding. 

Jack swung the heavy steel microphone stand blindly in the dark, hitting the metal casing of the generator with a loud, ringing crash. 

"Engineer!" Jack yelled, panic completely overwhelming him. 

A heavy body slammed into Jack's legs, knocking him backward into the freezing water. 

He thrashed wildly, expecting dead hands to grab his throat. 

But it was the Engineer. 

The massive man pushed Jack away, struggling to his feet in the dark. He shoved Jack violently toward the stairs, a silent, desperate command to run. 

Jack didn't need to be told twice. He scrambled up the concrete stairs on his hands and knees, terror fueling his ascent. 

He reached the top and slammed the heavy basement door shut, throwing the deadbolt. 

He collapsed against the door, gasping for air, listening. 

From the flooded basement below, he heard a few heavy splashes, and then, silence. 

The Engineer was dead. 

And in a few minutes, he would be something else. 

Jack was completely alone in a dark concrete box, surrounded by a massive horde of the dead, with an infected zombie trapped in the basement below him. 


Chapter Five: The Antenna

Jack sat on the floor of the broadcast booth, his knees pulled to his chest. 

The pounding on the walls outside had grown louder, more insistent. The horde was growing, drawn by the presence of the thousands already there. 

Then, Jack heard a sound that chilled him to the bone. 

*Clack... clack... clack.*
