Clara looked at the four heavy canvas body bags lying on the ceramic autopsy tables in the center of the room. They were the patients who had died that morning, waiting for the undertaker to arrive from Denver. 

One of the body bags slowly began to move. 

Clara froze, her breath catching in her throat. 

The Romero sickness didn't just infect the living through bites. It infected everyone who died, regardless of the cause. If the brain was intact, the dead would rise. 

The patients had died of TB. Their brains were perfectly fine. 

The heavy canvas bag unzipped from the inside. 

A pale, emaciated hand reached out, grabbing the edge of the ceramic table. 

Clara pushed herself away from the door, scrambling backward across the white tiled floor until her back hit the stainless steel drawers. 

The zombie slowly sat up, its milky eyes locking onto the terrified nurse. It was an older man, his chest hollowed out by the disease, but his jaw snapped with the same unyielding hunger as the rest of the horde. 

Then, the second body bag began to move. And the third. And the fourth. 

Clara was locked inside the morgue with four fresh zombies. 


Chapter Seven: The Autopsy Table

The four TB zombies slowly slid off the ceramic tables, their bare feet slapping wetly against the cold tile floor. 

They didn't move as fast as the zombies upstairs. Their bodies had been heavily ravaged by the disease before death. But they were relentless, and they were between Clara and the locked door. 

"Stay back," Clara whispered, her voice trembling. 

She looked frantically around the room for a weapon. 

The stainless steel surgical trays near the tables were filled with scalpels, forceps, and heavy surgical shears. 

She grabbed a large, heavy pair of shears, gripping them like a dagger. 

The first zombie, the older man, lunged at her. 

Clara swung the heavy shears, driving the sharp steel blades into the side of the zombie's neck. 

The blades sank deep into dead flesh, but they missed the brain entirely. 

The zombie didn't even flinch. It grabbed Clara's arm, its milky eyes wide, its jaw snapping inches from her face. 

Clara screamed, using her other hand to shove the zombie backward against the ceramic autopsy table. 

The zombie lost its balance and fell heavily, its head striking the hard ceramic edge with a loud *crack*. 

The blow shattered the back of the skull, destroying the brain. The zombie went limp. 

One down. Three to go. 

But the noise of the struggle had a terrifying consequence. 

A massive, heavy pounding began on the steel door behind Clara. 

The horde upstairs had heard the commotion. They had found the morgue. 


Chapter Eight: The Formaldehyde

The heavy steel door shuddered under the immense pressure of dozens of dead bodies pressing against it from the outside. 

It wouldn't hold forever. 

Clara backed away from the door, trapped between the pounding horde and the three remaining zombies inside the room. 

A female zombie, a young woman who had died only hours ago, lunged toward her. 

Clara grabbed a heavy glass jar from a nearby counter. It was filled with formaldehyde, used for preserving tissue samples. 

She didn't try to hit the zombie with the jar. She unscrewed the heavy metal lid and threw the contents directly into the zombie's face. 

The harsh, toxic chemical splashed across the zombie's milky eyes. 

The zombie didn't feel pain, but the chemical instantly burned and clouded the delicate tissue of the eyes, completely blinding the creature. 

The blinded zombie stumbled backward, grasping blindly at the air, its jaw snapping furiously but missing Clara entirely. 

Clara stepped around the blinded creature, heading for a heavy metal cabinet on the far wall. She remembered what was kept there. 


Chapter Nine: The Bone Saw

Clara ripped open the heavy metal cabinet. 

Inside was the surgical bone saw, a heavy, hand-cranked medical instrument designed to cut through skulls during autopsies. It had a sharp, circular blade attached to a heavy steel handle. 

She grabbed the saw, gripping the handle tightly. It was heavy and unwieldy, but it was exactly what she needed. 

The third zombie, a large man, charged her. 

Clara raised the bone saw, bringing it down with all her strength directly onto the top of the zombie's skull. 

The sharp circular blade bit deep into the bone, shattering the top of the skull and driving into the dead brain beneath. 

The zombie collapsed instantly, its legs folding underneath it. 

But Clara had swung too hard. 

The circular blade was wedged tightly in the shattered skull. 

She pulled frantically on the handle, trying to yank the saw free, but it was stuck fast in the dense bone. 

"No, no, no," Clara sobbed, struggling with the heavy tool. 

The fourth zombie, the blind one, had followed the sound of the struggle. It lunged blindly, its dead hands grabbing Clara's shoulder. 

Clara shrieked, letting go of the stuck bone saw. 

She shoved the blind zombie backward, but she had nowhere left to run. 

And then, the heavy steel door of the morgue finally gave way. 


Chapter Ten: The Epidemic

The massive steel deadbolt didn't break. The heavy iron hinges, screwed into the concrete wall, simply tore out under the immense pressure of the hundreds of bodies pressing against the door. 

The heavy steel door crashed to the tiled floor with a deafening *clang*. 

The horde spilled into the morgue like a horrific, bloody tide. 

Hundreds of infected patients, orderlies, and doctors flooded the small room, their milky eyes locking onto the terrified nurse. 

Clara backed up until she hit the cold stainless steel of the body drawers. There was nowhere left to go. 

She didn't have a weapon. She didn't have a plan. 

The blind zombie, knocked backward by the incoming horde, was trampled instantly by the sheer volume of bodies entering the room. 

The horde didn't hesitate. Driven entirely by the unyielding hunger of the Romero sickness, they swarmed over Clara. 

The heavy ceramic autopsy tables were overturned. The sterile white tiles were painted in blood. 

Nurse Clara, fiercely dedicated to saving lives, was torn apart in the freezing dark of the morgue, surrounded by the patients she had tried so desperately to cure. The sanatorium fell silent, a massive stone tomb high in the Rocky Mountains. No happy endings.



Story Twenty-Five: The Speakeasy

Chapter One: The Password

It was 1933, the final, desperate days of Prohibition. Chicago was a city run by gangsters and fueled by illegal, dangerous alcohol. 

Beneath a seemingly abandoned warehouse on the South Side, the "Blind Tiger" speakeasy was packed. The air was thick with cigarette smoke, jazz music, and the harsh, chemical smell of bathtub gin. 

Jimmy "The Weasel," a low-level bootlegger for the local syndicate, leaned against the heavy oak bar. He wore a sharp, pinstriped suit that didn't hide his nervous sweat. 

Next to him was Roxy, a young flapper with bobbed hair and a sequined dress. She was laughing, holding a glass of cloudy gin. 

The heavy iron door at the front of the speakeasy, equipped with a small sliding peephole, swung open. 

The bouncer, a massive man named "Tiny," pushed a stumbling man into the crowded room. 

The man was a mess. His coat was torn, and he was covered in dark, sticky blood. He looked pale, his eyes wide and feverish. 

"Said he got mugged in the alley," Tiny grunted, locking the heavy iron door behind him. "Give him a drink, barkeep. Put it on my tab."

The bartender poured a shot of gin and slid it down the bar. 

The mugged man didn't reach for the glass. He stumbled forward, leaning heavily against the brass rail. 

He coughed violently, a wet, rattling sound that turned heads down the bar. 

"Hey pal, you don't look so good," Jimmy said, taking a step back. 

The man didn't answer. He collapsed entirely, hitting the hardwood floor with a heavy thud. 

He was dead. The "mugging" in the alley had been an encounter with an infected vagrant. 

The venomous fever had entered the Blind Tiger. 


Chapter Two: The Gin

"Someone get a doctor!" Roxy screamed, dropping her glass. It shattered, the sharp smell of gin filling the air. 

A few patrons gathered around the fallen man, murmuring in shock. 

The bartender reached over the bar to check the man's pulse. 

"He's gone," the bartender said grimly. 

But the Romero rule was faster than any doctor. 

Before the bartender could pull his hand away, the dead man's eyes snapped open. The irises were gone, replaced by a dull, milky white. 

The zombie lunged upward, its jaw snapping shut on the bartender's wrist. 

The bartender screamed, a horrific sound of agony that completely silenced the jazz band. He thrashed wildly, trying to pull his arm free, but the dead jaws were locked tight. 

The zombie pulled the bartender violently forward, dragging him over the heavy oak bar. 

The bartender crashed into the shelves of illegal liquor, shattering dozens of glass bottles. High-proof alcohol cascaded over the bar and onto the floor. 

The panic was instantaneous. 

The crowded speakeasy, filled with drunk, easily frightened people, erupted into absolute chaos. 


Chapter Three: The Secret Door

"Get out!" Jimmy yelled, grabbing Roxy's hand and pulling her away from the bar. 

The patrons surged toward the front exit. 

But Tiny, the massive bouncer, wasn't thinking about the safety of the customers. He was a syndicate man, and his primary job was protecting the boss's massive safe located in the back room. 

He saw the chaos, the blood, and the panic. He assumed it was a raid, or a rival gang attacking. 

"Nobody moves!" Tiny roared, drawing a heavy .45 caliber revolver from his shoulder holster. 

He slammed the heavy iron deadbolt on the front door, locking it securely from the inside. 

He effectively sealed the tomb. 

The panicked crowd pressed against the locked iron door, screaming and pounding against the unyielding metal. 

Back at the bar, the bartender, his throat torn open by the first zombie, bled out quickly among the shattered glass. 

Minutes later, he reanimated. 

There were now two zombies in the locked, crowded room. 

And the patrons, packed tightly together in their panic, were easy prey. The zombies waded into the crowd, tearing flesh and spreading the venomous fever. 

Every person bitten died quickly from blood loss and rose minutes later to join the slaughter. 

The numbers of the dead swelled rapidly. 


Chapter Four: The Bar Fight

The speakeasy was a war zone. 

The mobsters and patrons who weren't trapped at the door fought back with whatever they could find. They smashed chairs over the heads of the dead. They used broken gin bottles as jagged knives. 

Tiny, realizing his mistake too late, fired his heavy revolver into the horde. 

The bullets tore through the dead bodies, but unless they hit the brain, they were useless. 

A zombie wearing a sequined dress, her neck broken and hanging at a terrible angle, lunged at Tiny. 

The bouncer fired point-blank into her chest, but she didn't stop. She grabbed his arm, burying her teeth into his bicep. 

Tiny screamed, dropping the gun. He was swarmed by three more zombies and dragged down into the bloody sawdust. 

Jimmy "The Weasel" didn't try to fight. He knew the layout of the Blind Tiger better than anyone. 

"This way!" he yelled to Roxy, pulling her toward the back of the room. 

He dragged her behind the bar, slipping on the shattered glass and the pooling, high-proof alcohol. 

He headed for the heavy wooden door leading to the boss's private office. 

"It's locked!" Roxy screamed, pounding on the wood. 

"I have the key," Jimmy said, his hands shaking violently as he fumbled in his pocket. 

He shoved the key into the lock and turned. 

They burst into the small office and slammed the door behind them, throwing the deadbolt. 


Chapter Five: The Cellar

The office was quiet, but the heavy wooden door shuddered as the horde began to pound against it from the outside. 

"They're going to break through!" Roxy sobbed, covering her ears. 

"No, they're not," Jimmy said, quickly moving a heavy filing cabinet in front of the door. 

He turned to the center of the office floor. He pulled back a large, Persian rug, revealing a heavy wooden trapdoor with an iron ring. 

It was the hidden liquor cellar, used to store the massive vats of uncut, high-proof alcohol before it was watered down and served at the bar. 

Jimmy grabbed the iron ring and pulled. The heavy trapdoor swung open, revealing a dark, steep wooden staircase leading down into the earth. 

"Get down there," Jimmy ordered. 

Roxy didn't hesitate. She scrambled down the dark stairs into the pitch-black cellar. 

Jimmy followed her, grabbing a small flashlight from the boss's desk before descending. 

He pulled the heavy wooden trapdoor shut above them and slid the iron bolt across, locking it securely from the inside. 

They were safe. The cellar was essentially a concrete bunker beneath the warehouse. 

Jimmy clicked on the flashlight. 

The beam illuminated a massive room filled with enormous, wooden vats of illegal alcohol. The air down here was thick and heavy, smelling strongly of raw ethanol and damp earth. 

"We're trapped," Roxy whispered, shivering in the cold. 

"We wait," Jimmy said, his voice trembling. "We wait until they leave, or until the boss sends a crew to clean up."

But they wouldn't have to wait long. 


Chapter Six: The Trapdoor

The heavy thumping from the speakeasy above grew louder. 

The horde had broken through the office door. 

Dozens of dead bodies crowded into the small office, their milky eyes scanning the room. They smelled the fresh meat below. 

They began to claw and pound frantically at the wooden floorboards and the heavy trapdoor. 

In the cellar, Jimmy and Roxy looked up in terror. 

The trapdoor was thick, but it was just wood. The immense weight and relentless pounding of the horde above were beginning to stress the heavy iron hinges. 

*Crack.*

The sound of splintering wood echoed through the dark cellar. 

"It's breaking!" Roxy screamed, grabbing Jimmy's arm. 

Jimmy drew a small, .38 caliber revolver from his jacket pocket. He pointed it up at the trapdoor, his hand shaking uncontrollably. 

He had six bullets. There were probably fifty zombies in the office above. 

"Stay back," Jimmy warned Roxy, stepping away from the stairs. 

The pounding intensified. A jagged hole appeared in the center of the trapdoor, and a pale, dead hand reached through, grasping blindly at the dark air. 

Jimmy raised the revolver, aiming at the hand. 

But before he could fire, he noticed something else. 


Chapter Seven: The Leak

During the chaotic gunfight in the speakeasy above, Tiny had fired his heavy .45 revolver wildly. 

Several stray bullets had pierced the floorboards directly above the massive vats of uncut alcohol in the hidden cellar. 

Jimmy shined his flashlight toward the corner of the room. 

A steady stream of clear liquid was pouring down from the ceiling, splashing heavily onto the concrete floor. 

It was the raw, high-proof ethanol from a punctured vat upstairs. 

The illegal alcohol was highly volatile, essentially pure grain alcohol before it was watered down. 

The stream hit the floor and rapidly spread across the concrete, pooling around the base of the wooden stairs. 

"What is that?" Roxy asked, pointing at the expanding puddle. 

"The hooch," Jimmy whispered, realizing the danger. "They shot the main vat."

The room was filling with the raw liquid, but worse, it was filling with the invisible fumes. 


Chapter Eight: The Fumes

The confined space of the concrete cellar rapidly filled with toxic, highly flammable ethanol fumes. 

The fumes were heavy, sinking to the floor and displacing the oxygen. 

Jimmy coughed violently, his eyes burning and watering. 

"I can't breathe," Roxy gasped, clutching her chest. 

She swayed unsteadily, the potent fumes overwhelming her small frame. 

She collapsed onto the damp concrete floor, unconscious. 

"Roxy!" Jimmy yelled, dropping the flashlight to grab her shoulders. 

He shook her, but she didn't respond. 

He was breathing heavily, his own mind becoming hazy and sluggish from the lack of oxygen and the potent alcohol vapors. 

He looked up at the trapdoor. 

The hole was larger now. Two dead hands were tearing at the splintered wood, trying to widen the gap. 

The iron bolt holding the door shut was bending under the immense pressure. 

Jimmy had to get out. But the only exit was blocked by a horde of the dead, and the room he was in was rapidly becoming a toxic gas chamber. 


Chapter Nine: The Breakthrough

The iron bolt finally snapped with a sharp *PING*. 

The heavy wooden trapdoor burst open, hanging uselessly on broken hinges. 

The horde didn't climb down the stairs. They simply fell through the opening, a horrific avalanche of dead bodies plummeting into the dark cellar. 

The first zombie hit the wooden stairs with a heavy crash, breaking its legs, but it continued to drag itself toward Jimmy, its jaw snapping furiously. 

Jimmy grabbed his dropped flashlight and pointed it at the stairs. 

There were dozens of them falling through the opening, creating a massive, writhing pile of the dead at the base of the steps. 

They were splashing directly into the massive pool of raw ethanol. 

Jimmy raised his .38 revolver. He was coughing violently, his vision swimming, his lungs burning from the fumes. 

He didn't have time to aim carefully. He simply pointed the gun at the approaching zombie and pulled the trigger. 

*BANG.*

The gunshot was deafening in the confined cellar. 

The bullet struck the zombie in the shoulder, completely ineffective against the Romero sickness. 

But the bullet wasn't the problem. 


Chapter Ten: The Flash

The small .38 caliber revolver produced a bright, sharp muzzle flash when fired. 

In a normal room, it was harmless. 

But the concrete cellar was no longer a normal room. It was essentially a bomb, completely saturated with highly volatile, invisible ethanol fumes. 

The hot spark from the muzzle flash ignited the fumes instantly. 

The chemical reaction was catastrophic. 

The air itself caught fire. 

A massive, brilliant blue flash illuminated the entire cellar for a fraction of a second, followed immediately by a deafening, concussive roar. 

The flash fire ignited the massive pool of raw ethanol on the floor. 

The confined space of the cellar turned into a massive blast furnace. The intense heat instantly vaporized the remaining alcohol in the punctured vats upstairs, triggering a massive secondary explosion that ripped through the entire warehouse. 

The "Blind Tiger" speakeasy was completely obliterated in a massive, fiery eruption that shattered windows for blocks. 

Jimmy "The Weasel" and Roxy were incinerated instantly in the initial flash, their bodies turned to ash before they could even register the heat. 

The entire horde of the dead, trapped in the cellar and the speakeasy above, was consumed by the massive inferno, the intense heat destroying their dead brains entirely. 

The illegal liquor had claimed its final victims, leaving nothing but a smoldering, fiery crater in the heart of Chicago. No happy endings.



Story Twenty-Six: The Logging Camp

Chapter One: The Timber

The Pacific Northwest in 1935 was an endless expanse of towering, ancient redwoods. The air was crisp, scented with pine needles and damp earth. 

"Big" Jim, a massive lumberjack with shoulders like boulders and a thick, sweat-stained flannel shirt, swung his heavy double-bitted axe. 

The sharp steel bit deep into the massive trunk of a two-hundred-foot redwood. 

"Timber!" yelled Pete, a younger, leaner axeman working the other side of the massive tree. 

The giant redwood groaned, a deep, wooden sound that vibrated through the forest floor. It began its slow, majestic arc toward the earth. 

"Clear out!" Jim roared, stepping back from the stump. 

As the massive tree fell, its branches tore through the canopy, sending a shower of pinecones and dead wood raining down upon the loggers. 

One of those branches, thicker than a man's torso, snapped violently off a neighboring tree. 

It plummeted toward the ground, striking a logger named Smitty squarely on the head. 

Smitty collapsed instantly, his skull crushed by the massive impact. 

"Smitty!" Pete yelled, dropping his axe and running toward the fallen man. 

Jim followed, his massive face grim. "He's gone, Pete. The widow-maker got him."

Pete knelt beside the body, wiping a mix of sawdust and blood from Smitty's face. "We can't just leave him here. We have to take him back to the camp."

Jim nodded, hefting his heavy axe. "Alright. Grab his legs."


Chapter Two: The Camp

The logging camp was a small, muddy settlement built directly into the side of the mountain. It consisted of a dozen rough-hewn log cabins, a massive mess hall, and a small medical tent. 

Jim and Pete carried Smitty's body down the steep, muddy trail, laying him gently on a wooden cot inside the medical tent. 

The camp doctor, an older man with a trembling hand and a strong smell of whiskey on his breath, examined the crushed skull. 

"Instantaneous," the doctor slurred, pulling a rough canvas blanket over Smitty's face. "Nothing to be done. I'll write up the certificate. You boys go get cleaned up."

Jim and Pete left the tent, heading for the mess hall to wash the sawdust and blood from their hands. 

They didn't know that Smitty hadn't just died from the falling branch. 

Three days prior, during a trip to the nearest town for supplies, Smitty had been bitten by a rabid vagrant in an alley. He had hidden the bite, hoping it would heal. 

But the venomous fever was already in his bloodstream when the branch struck him. 

Inside the quiet medical tent, the canvas blanket slowly began to rise. 


Chapter Three: The Awakening

The doctor sat at his small wooden desk, uncorking a bottle of cheap whiskey to steady his nerves before writing the death certificate. 

He took a long swig, closing his eyes. 

When he opened them, Smitty was standing in front of him. 

The logger's head was terrifyingly misshapen, the skull crushed inward on the left side. But his eyes were open. They were a dull, milky white, glowing faintly in the dim light of the tent. 

The doctor dropped the whiskey bottle. It shattered on the wooden floorboards, filling the tent with the sharp smell of alcohol. 

"Smitty?" the doctor whispered, his alcohol-addled brain unable to comprehend the nightmare standing before him. 

The zombie logger didn't speak. It lunged forward, grabbing the doctor's white coat with dead, calloused hands. 

The zombie's jaw, miraculously intact despite the crushing blow to the skull, snapped shut. 

It sank its teeth deep into the doctor's neck, tearing through the jugular vein. 

The doctor screamed, a gurgling, horrific sound that carried clearly across the quiet logging camp. 

He fell backward over his desk, pulling the zombie down on top of him. 


Chapter Four: The Chainsaw

Jim and Pete were washing their hands at the trough outside the mess hall when they heard the scream. 

"That was the doc!" Pete yelled, grabbing his heavy axe from the ground. 

Jim grabbed his own double-bitted axe and sprinted toward the medical tent. 

They burst through the canvas flaps just as the doctor bled out on the wooden floorboards. 

Smitty, covered in the doctor's blood, slowly stood up, turning his milky eyes toward the two lumberjacks. 

"What the hell is that?" Pete gasped, raising his axe. 

"It ain't Smitty," Jim roared. 

Jim swung his heavy axe, bringing the sharp steel down in a devastating arc. 

The blade sank deep into Smitty's shoulder, completely severing the arm. 

But the zombie didn't even flinch. It continued to shamble forward, reaching for Jim with its remaining hand. 

"The head!" Jim yelled, realizing the terrifying truth of the Romero sickness. "Go for the head!"

He ripped the axe free and swung again, this time burying the blade directly into Smitty's ruined face. 

The zombie collapsed, finally dead. 

But the nightmare was just beginning. 

The doctor, his throat torn open, slowly pushed himself up from the bloody floorboards. His eyes were already turning milky white. 

And outside the tent, a massive chorus of screams erupted from the mess hall. 

The infection had spread before Smitty died. The camp was overrun. 

Jim looked around the tent for a better weapon. A standard axe was too slow for a horde. 

His eyes fell upon a massive, early prototype of a two-man chainsaw resting in the corner. It was a terrifying machine, weighing over a hundred pounds, powered by a loud, two-stroke gasoline engine. 

"Grab the handle, Pete!" Jim yelled, hefting the heavy engine block. 


Chapter Five: The Slaughter

Pete grabbed the heavy iron handle at the end of the four-foot guide bar. 

Jim yanked the starter cord. 

The massive engine roared to life, a deafening, mechanical shriek that vibrated through the floorboards. The heavy steel chain, lined with razor-sharp teeth designed to cut through ancient redwoods, spun rapidly along the bar. 

They burst out of the medical tent, carrying the roaring chainsaw between them. 

The camp was a slaughterhouse. Dozens of infected loggers were tearing into their former friends. 

The dead turned toward the deafening roar of the chainsaw. 

"Hold it steady!" Jim roared, throttling the engine. 

They waded into the horde. 

The chainsaw was a devastating weapon. The razor-sharp chain tore through dead flesh and bone with terrifying ease. 

They swept the massive blade back and forth, severing limbs and decapitating the infected loggers in a spray of dark blood and flying tissue. 

For a few minutes, they were an unstoppable machine of destruction. 

But the chainsaw had a fatal flaw. 

The deafening roar of the two-stroke engine echoed for miles through the quiet forest. 

It drew the attention of every infected logger in the entire valley. 

The horde rapidly swelled from dozens to hundreds. They poured out of the surrounding woods, an unyielding wave of the dead converging on the loud, bloody machine in the center of the camp. 


Chapter Six: The Flume

"There's too many!" Pete screamed over the roar of the engine, his arms burning from the immense weight of the chainsaw. 

"Back to the flume!" Jim yelled, realizing they were surrounded. 

The log flume was a massive, V-shaped wooden trough that ran from the top of the mountain down to the lumber mill in the valley below. A continuous stream of water flowed down the flume, used to transport the heavy redwood logs quickly and efficiently. 

Jim and Pete fought a fighting retreat toward the edge of the camp, carving a bloody path through the horde. 

They reached the top of the massive wooden structure. The water rushed past them, cold and fast. 

"Drop it!" Jim yelled, cutting the engine. 

They dropped the heavy, blood-soaked chainsaw into the mud. 

They couldn't run down the steep, muddy mountain trail; the horde would easily catch them. The only way down was the flume. 

"Grab a log!" Jim ordered. 

A massive, freshly cut redwood log was waiting in the loading chute. 

They pushed the heavy log into the rushing water of the flume. 

"Jump!" 

Jim and Pete leaped onto the wet, slippery bark of the log just as the vanguard of the horde reached the top of the structure. 


Chapter Seven: The Jam

The log shot down the massive wooden slide, propelled by the fast-moving water. 

The wind roared in their ears as they accelerated down the steep mountain, clinging desperately to the rough bark. 

"We made it!" Pete yelled, laughing hysterically. 

Jim looked back up the mountain. 

The horde hadn't stopped at the edge of the flume. Driven entirely by the unyielding hunger, they simply followed the living men into the rushing water. 

Dozens of zombies plummeted down the steep wooden trough behind them, their bodies tumbling and thrashing in the cold water. 

But a zombie is not a smooth, aerodynamic log. 

A cluster of five zombies, tumbling awkwardly down the flume, became wedged tightly together at a sharp curve in the wooden structure. 

The bodies behind them piled into the bottleneck, creating a horrific, writhing dam of dead flesh. 

The water, unable to flow past the massive blockage, began to rapidly back up. 

Jim and Pete, riding their log ahead of the jam, suddenly slowed down. 

The water level in their section of the flume was dropping rapidly as the dam above them held the flow back. 

Their heavy log scraped against the dry wooden bottom of the flume, grinding to a halt. 

"What happened?" Pete yelled, standing up on the log. 

Jim looked back up the flume. "They jammed it!"


Chapter Eight: The Dam

The situation above them was catastrophic. 

Hundreds of zombies were now piled into the bottleneck, creating a massive, putrid dam holding back thousands of gallons of fast-moving water. 

The pressure building behind the dam was immense. 

The massive wooden structure of the flume, designed to carry logs, was not designed to withstand the crushing, localized pressure of a massive water backlog. 

The heavy wooden beams began to groan and splinter loudly. 

"We have to climb out!" Jim yelled, realizing the flume was going to fail. 

He jumped off the log, landing in the shallow water remaining in the trough. The wooden walls of the V-shaped flume were steep and incredibly slick with algae and water. 

He grabbed the top edge of the wall, struggling to pull his massive frame over. 

"Help me up!" Pete cried, slipping on the wet wood. 

Jim reached down, grabbing Pete's hand. 

Above them, the horrific, groaning sound of the failing wooden structure reached a terrifying crescendo. 

The dam of dead bodies couldn't hold the pressure anymore. 

With a deafening *CRACK*, the massive wooden side wall of the flume shattered outward. 


Chapter Nine: The Break

The failure of the flume was explosive. 

Thousands of gallons of backed-up water, carrying hundreds of tangled, snapping zombies, surged forward with the force of a tidal wave. 

The massive wall of water hit Jim and Pete before they could clear the edge of the flume. 

They were ripped from the wooden wall, tumbling wildly into the churning, freezing chaos. 

The world turned into a terrifying, suffocating nightmare of rushing water, splintering wood, and dead, grasping hands. 

Jim fought the current, trying to keep his head above water. But the sheer volume of zombies churning in the flood made it impossible. 

Dead hands grabbed his clothes, his hair, his arms. Milky white eyes stared at him from beneath the surface. 

He couldn't fight them off. The water robbed him of his strength, and the horde was overwhelming. 

The massive flood, unable to be contained by the shattered flume, tore a destructive path straight down the steep mountain, carrying the loggers and the dead toward the valley floor. 

"Pete!" Jim roared, breaking the surface for a brief second before a dead hand yanked him back under. 

He didn't see Pete again. 


Chapter Ten: The Mill

The violent, churning flood of water and bodies plummeted down the final, steep incline of the mountain. 

At the bottom of the valley sat the massive lumber mill. 

The mill was powered by the rushing water of the river, turning massive, fifteen-foot circular saw blades that cut the giant redwoods into usable lumber. 

The flood, carrying Jim and the massive horde of zombies, was funneled directly into the main intake channel of the mill. 

The noise was deafening, a horrific screech of massive steel blades spinning at high speed. 

Jim was thrown violently forward by the rushing water. He broke the surface, gasping for air, only to see the massive, spinning steel teeth of the primary saw blade looming directly ahead of him. 

He screamed, thrashing wildly against the current, trying to swim away. 

But the dead were holding him tightly, their unyielding grip dragging him relentlessly forward into the spinning steel. 

The Romero sickness made the zombies fearless. They didn't recognize the massive saw blades as a threat. 

The massive, horrific wave of dead flesh, tangled with the living lumberjack, hit the spinning blades simultaneously. 

The massive steel teeth tore through dead bone and living muscle with indiscriminate, terrifying power. 

The water in the intake channel turned instantly, brightly red. 

The logging camp was silenced, the mountain reclaimed by the deep, dark forest, leaving nothing behind but the relentless, bloody grinding of the mill. No happy endings.



Story Twenty-Seven: The Lighthouse

Chapter One: The Storm

The jagged coast of Maine in 1939 was battered by a massive, late-autumn nor'easter. The wind howled like a banshee, driving freezing rain against the heavy stone blocks of the isolated lighthouse standing on a small, rocky island. 

Keeper Thomas, an old, solitary man with a thick beard and a pipe perpetually clenched in his teeth, stood in the small kitchen at the base of the tower. 

The heavy oak door rattled violently under the force of the storm. 

*BANG. BANG. BANG.*

Thomas frowned, setting his pipe on the wooden table. Someone was pounding on the door. 

He unbolted the heavy iron latch and pulled the door open, fighting the wind that threatened to tear it from its hinges. 

A man stumbled in, completely soaked, collapsing onto the stone floor. 

It was the Inspector, a government official sent from the mainland to check the lighthouse's reserves before the winter set in. 

"Inspector?" Thomas asked, slamming the door shut against the storm. "What are you doing here in this weather? Where's your boat?"

The Inspector didn't answer. He lay on the floor, shivering violently, his breath coming in ragged, shallow gasps. His skin was a terrifying, pale gray. 

Thomas knelt beside the man, turning him over. 

"Good Lord," Thomas whispered. 

The Inspector was burning with a horrific fever. He grabbed Thomas's arm with surprisingly strong, trembling fingers. 

"The mainland..." the Inspector gasped, his voice barely a whisper. "It's gone... the sickness..."

His eyes rolled back, and he died on the cold stone floor. 

Thomas backed away, crossing himself. He had heard the radio broadcasts about the venomous fever spreading across the country, but he had believed the isolation of the island would protect him. 


Chapter Two: The Spiral Stairs

Thomas stood over the dead man, his mind racing. He had to wrap the body in canvas and store it in the cold shed outside until the storm passed. 

He turned toward the small closet to fetch a heavy tarp. 

Behind him, the Romero rule took hold. 

The Inspector's eyes snapped open. The irises were entirely gone, replaced by a dull, milky white. 

The zombie pushed itself up from the floor, its joints popping audibly in the quiet kitchen. 

Thomas turned back, holding the canvas tarp. 

"Inspector?" Thomas asked, confused. 

The dead man lunged, its jaw snapping furiously. 

Thomas reacted instinctively, raising the heavy canvas tarp to block the attack. The zombie's teeth tore into the thick fabric, missing Thomas's arm by inches. 

"Get away!" Thomas yelled, shoving the zombie backward. 

The zombie stumbled, falling against the wooden kitchen table, knocking it over with a loud crash. 

Thomas grabbed a heavy iron wrench from a small toolbox near the door. He wasn't a fighter, but he knew how to swing a tool. 

The zombie recovered instantly, its milky eyes locking onto the old keeper. It charged again, its dead hands reaching for Thomas's throat. 

Thomas swung the heavy wrench, striking the zombie hard on the shoulder. 

The blow would have shattered a living man's collarbone and dropped him to his knees. The zombie didn't even flinch. It continued its relentless advance. 

Thomas realized he couldn't fight it in the cramped kitchen. He needed distance. He needed high ground. 

He turned and bolted toward the narrow, winding spiral staircase that led to the top of the lighthouse. 


Chapter Three: The Retreat

The spiral staircase was a claustrophobic, cast-iron corkscrew winding up the center of the stone tower. It was narrow, steep, and completely unforgiving. 

Thomas scrambled up the steps, his old heart hammering against his ribs. The cold, damp stone walls offered no handholds, leaving only the thin iron railing separating him from the terrifying drop down the center of the tower. 

He reached the first landing, thirty feet up, and paused to catch his breath. 

He looked down through the spiraling iron steps. 

The zombie Inspector was climbing the stairs behind him. It didn't climb with the careful, measured steps of a living man. It scrambled upward with a terrifying, unyielding momentum, its dead hands pulling it up the iron rungs, its jaws snapping constantly. 

"Stay down!" Thomas roared, his voice echoing loudly in the stone cylinder. 

The zombie ignored him, continuing its rapid ascent. 

Thomas gripped the heavy iron wrench tightly. He couldn't outrun it forever. He had to stop it on the stairs. 

He waited on the landing, his back pressed against the cold stone wall, watching the horrific, milky-eyed face emerge from the darkness below. 


Chapter Four: The Climb

The zombie reached the landing, lunging at Thomas the second it cleared the final step. 

Thomas swung the heavy wrench, aiming for the head. 

The iron struck the zombie's temple with a loud *crack*, but it was a glancing blow. It fractured the skull but didn't destroy the brain. 

The zombie stumbled backward, losing its balance on the narrow landing. 

It pitched over the edge, tumbling down the spiral staircase, its body bouncing violently against the iron steps and the stone wall. 

It fell thirty feet, crashing heavily onto the stone floor of the kitchen below. 

Thomas leaned over the railing, gasping for air. 

He watched the body lying perfectly still at the bottom of the tower. 

"Thank God," Thomas whispered. 

But his relief was short-lived. 

The Romero sickness made the dead incredibly resilient. The fall had broken the zombie's legs and shattered its spine, but the brain was still intact. 

The zombie slowly rolled over, its milky eyes turning upward toward the landing. 

It couldn't walk anymore. But it could still crawl. 

The dead Inspector began to drag itself back up the spiral staircase, its bloody hands pulling its broken body upward, step by agonizing step. 

Thomas felt a cold spike of absolute dread. The thing was relentless. 

He turned and continued his climb, heading for the very top of the lighthouse. 


Chapter Five: The Lantern Room

Thomas reached the top of the tower, his lungs burning with exertion. 

He pushed open the heavy iron hatch that led into the lantern room. 

The lantern room was a massive, glass-enclosed dome sitting at the apex of the lighthouse. Inside sat the massive, intricate Fresnel lens, a complex array of curved glass prisms that focused the light of the massive, central halogen bulb into a powerful beam visible for miles. 

The noise in the lantern room was deafening. The storm outside was raging with terrifying ferocity, the wind screaming against the thick glass panes. 

Thomas slammed the heavy iron hatch shut and threw the massive steel deadbolt, locking it securely. 

He collapsed against the hatch, exhausted and terrified. 

He was safe. The dead man couldn't break through solid iron. 

He stood up, wiping the sweat from his forehead. He looked at the massive, spinning lens. The heavy clockwork mechanism that rotated the lens was humming loudly, keeping the massive beam of light sweeping across the dark, stormy sea. 

*THUMP.*

The sound came from beneath his boots. 

The zombie had reached the top of the stairs. It began to pound its bloody, broken hands against the heavy iron hatch. 

*THUMP. THUMP.*

Thomas backed away from the hatch, moving toward the massive glass lens. 


Chapter Six: The Bulb

The pounding on the hatch was relentless, a terrifying, rhythmic drumbeat against the iron. 

Thomas ignored it, focusing his attention on the raging storm outside. 

He looked through the thick glass panes, out over the dark, turbulent ocean. The massive waves were crashing violently against the rocky island, sending spray hundreds of feet into the air. 

He watched the powerful beam of the lighthouse sweep across the dark water. 

Then, he noticed something wrong. 

The beam was erratic. The massive halogen bulb, suspended in the center of the spinning lens, was flickering slightly, its intense heat causing the heavy brass mounting bracket to expand and warp. 

The focus of the beam was shifting, pointing too high into the storm clouds instead of sweeping the dangerous rocky shoals surrounding the island. 

"Damn it," Thomas muttered, realizing the danger. 

If the beam wasn't focused properly, a ship navigating the treacherous coastline in the storm would be completely blinded to the deadly rocks. 

He had to fix it. He had to manually adjust the heavy brass bracket to refocus the bulb. 

He moved toward the massive, spinning lens, reaching for the heavy iron brake lever that would stop the clockwork mechanism. 


Chapter Seven: The Wreck

Before Thomas could pull the brake lever, a massive shape appeared in the darkness over the ocean. 

It was a large commercial freighter, its heavy steel hull battling the massive waves. It was far too close to the island, driving blindly through the storm. 

Thomas watched in absolute horror as the freighter rode the crest of a massive wave, completely missing the erratic, poorly focused beam of the lighthouse. 

The ship was heading directly for the Devil's Teeth, a jagged reef of submerged rocks located just a few hundred yards offshore. 

"Turn!" Thomas yelled, pounding his fists against the thick glass panes, knowing they couldn't hear him. 

The freighter didn't turn. 

It struck the Devil's Teeth with a horrific, metallic scream that carried clearly over the roar of the storm. 

The massive steel hull tore open like a tin can. The ship listed violently to the side, taking on water rapidly. 

Thomas watched the disaster unfold, his heart sinking. 

He saw the crew frantically launching the small wooden lifeboats into the raging sea. 

They were abandoning ship. They would try to reach the island. 

He had to get the beam focused properly. He had to illuminate the safe channel through the rocks, or the lifeboats would be smashed to splinters. 

But the manual adjustment controls for the massive brass bracket were located inside a small service panel situated directly above the heavy iron hatch. 


Chapter Eight: The Choice

Thomas looked down at the iron hatch in the floor. 

The pounding hadn't stopped. The zombie Inspector was still down there, its dead hands battering the metal, driven by the unyielding hunger. 

To reach the service panel, Thomas had to stand directly on the hatch. 

He didn't know how badly the heavy iron hinges had been damaged by the relentless pounding. If he stood on the hatch, the additional weight might cause the damaged hinges to fail entirely. 

He looked back out the window. 

The lifeboats were in the water, pitching wildly in the massive waves. They were heading blindly toward the island, completely lost in the dark storm. 

They needed the light. 

Thomas had to choose. Stay safe in the lantern room and let the sailors drown on the rocks, or risk opening the hatch to quickly adjust the bracket. 

He couldn't let them die. He was a lighthouse keeper. 

He grabbed his heavy iron wrench, gripping it tightly. 

"Alright, you dead bastard," Thomas whispered. "Let's finish this."

He moved to the hatch, reached down, and slid the heavy steel deadbolt back. 


Chapter Nine: The Breach

The second the deadbolt cleared the housing, the hatch burst violently upward. 

The zombie Inspector, despite its broken body, surged into the lantern room, its bloody hands grabbing Thomas's ankle. 

Thomas screamed, swinging the heavy iron wrench downward. 

He struck the zombie's arm, shattering the bone, but the dead grip didn't loosen. 

The zombie pulled violently, dragging Thomas down onto the grated floor of the lantern room. 

The zombie's broken body dragged itself over the hatch, its milky eyes locking onto Thomas's face, its jaw snapping furiously. 

Thomas fought desperately, kicking his heavy boots against the zombie's chest, trying to push it back down the stairs. 

But the confined space of the lantern room offered no leverage. 

The zombie lunged forward, its dead teeth tearing into the heavy canvas of Thomas's coat. 

Thomas roared, swinging the wrench wildly, striking the zombie's shoulder, its ribs, its neck. But he couldn't get a clean shot at the head. 

In the chaotic struggle, Thomas rolled violently to the side, trying to escape the snapping jaws. 

He misjudged his position. 

His heavy boots kicked wildly against the massive, intricate glass structure of the spinning Fresnel lens. 

The impact shattered a thick, curved prism, sending sharp shards of glass raining down upon them. 

But worse, his flailing arm struck the massive, burning hot halogen bulb in the center of the lens. 


Chapter Ten: The Glass

The massive halogen bulb, burning at several thousand degrees to produce the intense beam, was incredibly fragile. 

The sudden, violent impact of Thomas's heavy wool coat against the superheated glass was catastrophic. 

The bulb shattered instantly. 

The explosion of hot, sharp glass was blinding. Thousands of superheated shards rained down upon the small, confined lantern room. 

The massive beam of the lighthouse extinguished immediately, plunging the top of the tower into absolute darkness, illuminated only by the faint, secondary emergency oil lamps. 

Thomas screamed in agony as the hot glass pierced his face and eyes. He was instantly, permanently blinded. 

The zombie, impervious to pain, ignored the hot glass raining down upon its dead flesh. It continued its relentless attack, its jaws finding Thomas's unprotected neck in the dark. 

As Thomas bled out on the grated floor, the hot shards of the halogen bulb ignited the massive pool of lubricating whale oil used to keep the heavy clockwork mechanism spinning smoothly. 

The oil caught fire instantly. 

The flames spread rapidly, engulfing the intricate wooden framing of the massive Fresnel lens. 

The lantern room turned into a massive, raging inferno at the top of the stone tower. 

Outside, in the raging storm, the lifeboats from the wrecked freighter saw the brilliant, towering flames. 

Believing the fire was a desperate signal guiding them to safety, the sailors steered their small wooden boats directly toward the light. 

They crashed violently into the jagged, unyielding rocks of the Devil's Teeth, smashed to splinters in the dark, freezing water. 

The lighthouse burned like a massive, horrific torch in the storm, a beacon of absolute death for the living, as Keeper Thomas was eaten alive in the flames. No happy endings.



Story Twenty-Eight: The Bank Robbery

Chapter One: The Heist

The small town of Oakhaven, Oklahoma, was dying in the Dustbowl long before the venomous fever arrived. The only building that still looked solid was the First National Bank, a sturdy brick structure sitting on Main Street. 

It was a Friday afternoon in 1934. 

The heavy glass doors of the bank shattered inward as two figures burst into the lobby. 

"Everybody on the floor! Now!" Clyde roared, leveling a heavy Thompson submachine gun at the ceiling. 

He was a desperate, angry young man, fueled by cheap gin and the crushing poverty of the Depression. Beside him stood Bonnie, a tough, cold-eyed woman holding a pump-action shotgun. 

The half-dozen customers in the bank screamed and dropped to the marble floor, covering their heads. 

The lone teller, a young, terrified man named Arthur, raised his hands high behind the brass grating of the counter. 

"Don't shoot!" Arthur cried, his voice cracking. 

"Open the gate, kid!" Bonnie yelled, pointing the shotgun directly at Arthur's face. 

Arthur scrambled to unlock the heavy brass gate separating the lobby from the teller area. 

Clyde stepped through, grabbing Arthur by the collar of his cheap suit. 

"The vault," Clyde demanded, pressing the barrel of the Tommy gun under Arthur's chin. "Open it. Now."

Arthur nodded frantically, tears streaming down his face. "I will! Just... please don't hurt me."

He led the bank robbers toward the back of the building, where the massive, circular steel door of the main vault stood. 


Chapter Two: The Vault

The vault door was a three-foot-thick marvel of hardened steel and complex gears, weighing over ten tons. 

"Open it," Clyde ordered, stepping back. 

Arthur's hands were shaking so violently he could barely grip the heavy brass dial. 

He spun the dial left, right, left, entering the complex combination. The heavy steel tumblers clicked loudly in the quiet bank. 

With a final, heavy clank, the locking mechanism disengaged. 

Arthur grabbed the massive steel wheel on the front of the door and pulled with all his strength. 

The massive door swung open slowly, revealing the small, brightly lit interior of the vault. Stacks of crisp bills and heavy canvas bags of silver coins lined the steel shelves. 

Clyde whistled low. "Bingo."

He shoved Arthur aside, stepping into the vault. 

"Grab the bags, Bonnie," Clyde said, lowering the Tommy gun. "We're going to Mexico."

Bonnie smiled, stepping toward the shelves. 

Suddenly, a loud, piercing wail cut through the silence of the bank. 

Clyde spun around, raising his weapon. "Did you trip an alarm, kid?" he snarled at Arthur. 

"No!" Arthur cried, backing away. "That's not the bank alarm!"

The sound was coming from outside. It was the town's massive, air-raid-style emergency siren, mounted on the roof of the fire station down the street. 

It was the signal they had been dreading for weeks. 

The horde had arrived in Oakhaven. 


Chapter Three: The Outside

Clyde ran to the front of the bank, cautiously peering over the brass counter and through the large, plate-glass windows facing Main Street. 

"What the hell?" he whispered, his eyes widening in absolute horror. 

Main Street was no longer empty. It was filled with a massive, undulating wave of dark, shuffling figures. 

Hundreds of the infected, drawn from the surrounding farms and the neighboring towns, were migrating through Oakhaven. They moved with the terrifying, relentless momentum of a swarm of locusts. 

"Bonnie," Clyde called out, his voice trembling. "Get up here."

Bonnie ran to the counter, her shotgun raised. She looked out the window and gasped. 

The dead were everywhere. They were swarming over the parked cars, pulling terrified townspeople from the hardware store across the street, tearing them apart in the dusty road. 

"Cops?" Bonnie asked, her voice tight with panic. 

"Ain't cops," Clyde said, stepping back from the window. "It's the sickness."

Suddenly, a massive shape slammed against the heavy plate-glass window of the bank. 

It was a local farmer, his overalls soaked in blood, his milky white eyes staring blankly at Clyde. He raised a bloody, dead hand and began to pound weakly against the glass. 

Then, another zombie joined him. And another. 

The horde had noticed the people inside the bank. 

Dozens of the dead pressed themselves against the large front windows, their jaws snapping hungrily at the glass. 


Chapter Four: The Windows

"They're coming in!" Arthur screamed from the back of the bank, watching the massive crowd of dead bodies pressing against the glass. 

"No they ain't," Clyde growled, raising the heavy Thompson submachine gun. 

He squeezed the trigger. 

The deafening roar of the heavy .45 caliber automatic weapon filled the small bank. The muzzle flash illuminated the lobby as a hail of heavy lead tore through the plate-glass windows. 

The thick glass shattered completely, raining down on the dead in a massive, deadly shower of sharp shards. 

But the bullets didn't stop the horde. 

Clyde aimed for center mass, the standard tactic for gunfights. He emptied the heavy fifty-round drum magazine into the chest cavities of the zombies climbing through the shattered window frames. 

The heavy bullets tore huge holes through the dead flesh, destroying lungs and hearts, but the Romero sickness ignored the damage. 

The dead simply stepped over the bodies of the completely destroyed zombies and continued their relentless advance into the bank lobby. 

"They ain't dying!" Clyde screamed, dropping the empty drum magazine and struggling to load another. 

"Headshots!" Bonnie yelled, realizing the terrifying truth. "Shoot them in the head!"

She pumped the shotgun and fired a heavy load of buckshot directly into the face of a zombie climbing over the brass counter. 

The blast destroyed the skull entirely. The zombie collapsed backward, finally dead. 


Chapter Five: The Shootout

"Aim for the heads!" Clyde roared, finally slamming the new magazine into the Tommy gun. 

The shootout in the bank lobby was deafening and chaotic. 

Clyde and Bonnie fired relentlessly, their weapons blazing as they desperately tried to hold the line at the brass counter. 

The heavy .45 caliber slugs and the widespread buckshot were devastating when they hit the target, destroying the dead brains and dropping the zombies to the marble floor. 

But the sheer volume of the horde was overwhelming. For every zombie they destroyed, three more climbed through the shattered windows, unyielding and fearless. 

The air in the bank was thick with the sharp smell of cordite and the coppery stench of blood. 

Clyde fired short bursts, conserving his ammunition, but the drum was emptying fast. 

"I'm out!" Bonnie screamed, dropping the empty shotgun. 

"Back to the vault!" Clyde yelled, firing a final, long burst into the approaching horde to clear a path. 

He grabbed Bonnie's arm and pulled her toward the back of the bank. 

The dead poured over the brass counter, a relentless tide of rotting flesh, their milky eyes fixed on the fleeing robbers. 


Chapter Six: The Retreat

Clyde and Bonnie sprinted into the back room, sliding on the polished marble floor. 

Arthur the teller was already standing near the open door of the massive steel vault, his face pale with absolute terror. 

"Get in!" Clyde yelled, shoving Bonnie into the brightly lit interior of the vault. 

He turned and fired his last few rounds down the hallway, dropping the two lead zombies. 

The Tommy gun clicked empty. 

Clyde threw the heavy weapon at the approaching horde and dove into the vault, grabbing Arthur and dragging the terrified teller inside with him. 

"Close it!" Clyde screamed, grabbing the massive steel wheel on the inside of the door. 

Arthur, driven by pure panic, didn't hesitate. 

He grabbed the edge of the three-foot-thick steel door and pushed with all his adrenaline-fueled strength. 

The massive door swung shut with a terrifyingly heavy momentum. 

Clyde grabbed the locking wheel and spun it violently. 

The heavy steel tumblers engaged with a loud, final, echoing *CLANK*. 

They were sealed inside the massive steel box, cut off entirely from the horrific screams and the snapping jaws of the horde outside. 

"We made it," Bonnie gasped, sliding down the steel wall and sitting on a heavy canvas bag of silver coins. 


Chapter Seven: The Time Lock

The silence inside the vault was absolute and oppressive. 

The thick steel walls completely muted the chaos raging in the bank lobby. It was just the three of them, breathing heavily in the bright, electric light. 

"Are we safe?" Arthur asked, his voice trembling. 

"Yeah, kid," Clyde said, running a hand through his sweaty hair. "They ain't getting through three feet of solid steel. We just wait 'em out."

Arthur nodded slowly. Then, he looked at the massive, complex locking mechanism on the inside of the door. 

His eyes widened in sudden, horrifying realization. 

"Oh no," Arthur whispered, his face draining of all remaining color. 

"What?" Clyde asked, stepping toward him. 

"The lock," Arthur stammered, pointing a trembling finger at the complex array of gears and timers built into the steel door. "When I closed it... the impact engaged the time lock."

"So what?" Bonnie asked, frowning. "Disengage it."

"I can't," Arthur sobbed, collapsing onto the floor. "It's a mechanical fail-safe. To prevent bank managers from being forced to open the vault after hours. Once it engages, it can't be overridden from the inside or the outside."

"How long?" Clyde asked, his voice suddenly very quiet and very dangerous. 

Arthur looked up at him, tears streaming down his face. 

"It's set for Monday morning at 8:00 AM."


Chapter Eight: The Trapped

Clyde stared at the teller, the reality of their situation slowly sinking in. 

It was Friday afternoon. They were locked inside a small, airtight steel box for the next sixty hours. 

"Are you kidding me?" Bonnie screamed, jumping up and grabbing Arthur by the collar. "You locked us in here for the whole weekend?"

"I didn't mean to!" Arthur cried, trying to pull away. "I just panicked!"

"We'll suffocate!" Bonnie yelled, looking wildly around the small, bright room. 

The vault was roughly ten feet by ten feet, with a low ceiling. It was designed to store money, not people. 

"The air," Clyde said grimly. "How much air do we have, kid?"

"I... I don't know," Arthur sobbed. "Maybe a day? Maybe less with three of us."

Clyde ran his hands over his face, a cold sweat breaking out on his forehead. They had millions of dollars in cash surrounding them, but it was completely useless. 

"We sit down," Clyde ordered, his voice echoing loudly in the confined space. "We don't talk. We don't move. We conserve oxygen."

Bonnie nodded, slumping back against the wall, her tough facade completely broken by the terrifying claustrophobia. 

They sat in silence for a long time, the only sound the quiet humming of the electric lights. 

Then, Arthur coughed. 

It was a wet, rattling sound that broke the absolute silence like a gunshot. 


Chapter Nine: The Bite

"Shut up, kid," Clyde hissed in the quiet vault. 

But Arthur coughed again, doubling over, his hands clutching his stomach. 

Clyde frowned, looking closely at the teller. 

Arthur was sweating profusely, his skin a pale, terrifying gray. He was shivering violently in the cool air of the vault. 

"Kid?" Clyde asked, a sudden spike of dread hitting him. "Are you sick?"

Arthur looked up, his eyes bloodshot and feverish. 

"I'm sorry," Arthur gasped, his voice incredibly weak. 

He slowly pulled back the sleeve of his cheap suit jacket. 

On his forearm, hidden beneath the fabric, was a dark, jagged bite mark, surrounded by angry red veins. 

"When?" Bonnie asked, scrambling backward across the floor, pressing herself against the steel wall. 

"In the lobby," Arthur cried. "When they first broke the windows... one of them reached through... grabbed my arm."

Clyde stood up, drawing a small, .38 caliber revolver he kept in an ankle holster. 

"You brought it in here," Clyde whispered, raising the gun. 

"I didn't want to die out there," Arthur sobbed, collapsing onto the floor. 

He didn't speak again. The venomous fever, accelerated by his panic, finally overwhelmed his system. 

Arthur the teller died on the floor of the vault, surrounded by the money he had died to protect. 


Chapter Ten: The Suffocation

Clyde didn't lower the gun. He kept it aimed directly at the dead teller's head, his finger resting lightly on the trigger. 

"Shoot him!" Bonnie shrieked, her voice echoing wildly in the small steel room. 

"Wait," Clyde hissed. 

A few minutes later, Arthur's milky white eyes snapped open. 

The zombie teller lunged upward with terrifying speed. 

Clyde pulled the trigger. 

*BANG.*

The sound of the .38 caliber gunshot inside the small, airtight steel vault was catastrophic. 

The immense concussive force had nowhere to go. It bounced off the thick steel walls, magnifying the sound exponentially. 

The blast shattered Clyde and Bonnie's eardrums instantly, plunging them into absolute, agonizing silence. 

Clyde missed the brain. The bullet struck the zombie in the shoulder, completely ineffective. 

The zombie lunged again, grabbing Bonnie's ankle as she scrambled backward. 

Clyde fired again. And again. And again. 

*BANG. BANG. BANG.*

The small vault filled instantly with a thick, choking cloud of hot, toxic cordite smoke. 

The rapid gunfire consumed a massive portion of the limited oxygen remaining in the small room. 

Clyde finally landed a headshot on his fifth bullet, destroying the zombie's brain and dropping it to the floor. 

But the victory was hollow. 

Clyde dropped the empty gun, coughing violently, his lungs burning from the toxic smoke and the lack of oxygen. 

He couldn't hear Bonnie screaming, but he could see her thrashing on the floor, gasping desperately for air that wasn't there. 

The thick steel door of the vault held perfectly. 

Clyde collapsed onto a pile of crisp, hundred-dollar bills, his vision swimming, his lungs screaming for oxygen. 

The time lock ticked silently away on the door, but it didn't matter. Clyde and Bonnie suffocated in the dark, silent tomb, completely deaf and surrounded by millions of dollars they would never spend. No happy endings.



Story Twenty-Nine: The Assembly Line

Chapter One: The Factory

Detroit in 1935 was the heart of American industry. The massive Ford River Rouge Complex was a sprawling city of steel, fire, and unceasing noise. 

Henry, a tired line worker covered in grease and sweat, stood at Station 42. His job was simple but physically brutal: grab a heavy steel door panel, lift it onto the slowly moving chassis of a 1935 Ford V8, and hold it steady while the welders secured it. 

He did it four hundred times a shift. 

"Keep 'em moving!" the foreman yelled, his voice barely audible over the deafening roar of the massive stamping presses and the hiss of acetylene torches. 

The conveyor belt never stopped. It was a relentless mechanical monster dictating the pace of their lives. 

Next to Henry, a younger worker named Charlie was struggling. He looked pale, sweating profusely despite the freezing draft blowing through the massive factory doors. 

"You look awful, Charlie," Henry shouted, grabbing the next heavy steel door. 

"Just a fever," Charlie gasped, leaning heavily against a stack of raw steel panels. "Need a minute."

"You don't get a minute on the line!" the foreman barked, walking past them. "Lift that panel or you're fired!"

Charlie grabbed the heavy steel, his hands trembling violently. 

He lifted it toward the moving chassis, but his strength failed him entirely. 

The heavy steel door slipped from his grasp. 


Chapter Two: The Accident

The heavy steel panel crashed downward. 

Charlie screamed as the sharp edge of the raw steel caught his arm against the moving track of the conveyor belt. 

The relentless, unyielding mechanical force of the belt dragged Charlie's arm forward, crushing it instantly between the heavy steel panel and a massive iron guide roller. 

Blood sprayed across the concrete floor. 

"Stop the line!" Henry roared, diving toward the emergency shut-off switch. 

He slammed his hand against the large red button. 

The massive conveyor belt ground to a halt with a heavy, metallic screech. The sudden silence in their section of the factory was jarring. 

Henry rushed back to Charlie. 

The young worker was lying on the concrete, his arm a ruined mess of shattered bone and torn muscle. A massive pool of dark blood was spreading rapidly around him. 

"Doc! Get the doc!" Henry yelled to the stunned workers nearby. 

The foreman pushed his way through the crowd. "What the hell is going on here? Start the line!"

"He's bleeding out!" Henry screamed, tearing off his heavy canvas shirt to use as a tourniquet. 

But it was too late. The massive blood loss, combined with the venomous fever already raging in Charlie's system, was fatal. 

Charlie's eyes rolled backward, and he died on the factory floor, his arm still trapped in the heavy machinery. 

"Well, drag him out of the way," the foreman said callously. "And start the damn line."


Chapter Three: The Assembly

Henry stared at the foreman in disbelief. He reached down to grab Charlie's good arm, preparing to pull him clear of the track. 

Before Henry could touch him, the Romero sickness took hold. 

Charlie's eyes snapped open. The irises were gone, replaced by a dull, milky white. 

The zombie worker lunged upward, incredibly fast. 

It didn't attack Henry. It lunged at the nearest moving object. 

It was a welder named Smitty, standing just a few feet away, his protective mask raised, an ignited acetylene torch in his hand. 

The zombie clamped its dead jaws directly onto the side of Smitty's neck. 

Smitty shrieked in absolute agony, dropping his heavy protective gloves. He thrashed wildly, but the zombie's dead weight and unyielding grip pulled him down onto the concrete. 

The hot acetylene torch clattered to the floor, the bright blue flame still burning furiously, hissing against a pool of spilled grease. 

"What the..." the foreman gasped, stepping backward. 

Henry froze, paralyzed by the horrific impossibility of what he was seeing. Charlie was dead. His arm was crushed. But he was eating Smitty alive. 

The screaming welder bled out in seconds. 

And then, the nightmare compounded. 

Smitty, his neck a ruined, bloody mess, slowly opened his own milky white eyes. 


Chapter Four: The Chaos

The sudden, brutal violence on the factory floor shattered the discipline of the assembly line. 

"Run!" someone screamed. 

Dozens of workers abandoned their stations, scrambling over the heavy machinery and half-built cars to escape the two feeding monsters. 

The foreman, realizing the severity of the situation, ran for the supervisor's office. He didn't lock the doors to protect the workers; he locked the doors to protect the factory ledgers. 

The panic spread like a wildfire down the massive line. 

Henry backed away from the two zombies. He looked down the length of the factory. 

It was a quarter-mile long, a massive canyon of steel and noise. The panic was causing accidents everywhere. Heavy parts were dropped, cranes swung wildly out of control, and men were crushed in the stampede. 

And in the chaos, someone, somewhere down the line, hit the main power switch, trying to restart the massive ventilation fans. 

They accidentally restarted the conveyor belt. 

With a deafening groan, the massive mechanical line lurched back to life. 

The heavy steel chassis began to move again. 

Charlie, still kneeling over Smitty's body, was caught by a moving chassis. The unyielding mechanical force dragged the zombie forward, its crushed arm still tangled in the guide roller. 

Charlie was literally assembled into the line, his dead body dragged relentlessly toward the massive stamping presses at the end of the building. 


Chapter Five: The Welder

Henry watched Charlie disappear down the moving line. 

He turned his attention back to Smitty. 

The zombie welder slowly stood up. It looked different from Charlie. 

In its dead, unfeeling hand, it still gripped the handle of the ignited acetylene torch. The heavy rubber hoses trailed behind it, connected to a massive tank strapped to a nearby pillar. 

The zombie didn't feel the intense heat of the blue flame burning inches from its own wrist. It simply held the torch like a horrific, fiery weapon. 

Its milky eyes locked onto Henry. 

The zombie welder lunged, the bright blue flame of the torch swinging wildly in the air. 

Henry dodged, scrambling backward over a pile of heavy steel bumpers. 

The torch hissed angrily as it passed his face, the intense heat singing his eyebrows. 

The zombie didn't stop. It stumbled over the bumpers, completely uncoordinated but relentlessly focused on Henry. 

The factory around them was descending into absolute madness. The screams of the dying mixed with the unceasing, deafening roar of the massive machines. The venomous fever was spreading exponentially through the trapped, panicked workforce. 

Henry realized he couldn't fight the burning zombie. He had to run. 

But the aisles between the machinery were clogged with terrified workers and the advancing dead. 

The only clear path was the conveyor belt itself. 


Chapter Six: The Belts

Henry jumped onto the moving conveyor belt, landing heavily on the bare steel chassis of a half-built V8. 

The belt was moving at a steady, relentless pace, carrying him deeper into the massive factory. 

He looked back. 

The zombie welder was trying to follow him, its heavy boots slipping on the greased steel tracks. It managed to haul itself onto the belt, the burning torch still gripped tightly in its dead hand. 

It was coming for him, riding the line. 

Henry stood up on the moving chassis, balancing carefully. He had to navigate a terrifying obstacle course. 

Massive, automated robotic arms swung down from the ceiling, blindly performing their programmed tasks. 

Henry ducked under a heavy pneumatic drill that lowered itself to tighten invisible bolts on the chassis. The drill bit missed his head by inches, the loud *whir* deafening in his ear. 

He jumped to the next chassis as the line moved beneath a massive, swinging crane. 

The factory was designed to build machines, not to accommodate fleeing men. It was a deadly, unyielding environment. 

And the zombie behind him didn't care about the obstacles. 

A heavy robotic arm swung down, striking the zombie welder squarely in the chest. 

The blow would have shattered a living man's ribs and knocked him off the belt. The zombie simply absorbed the impact, its body twisting unnaturally, and continued to crawl forward across the moving steel frames. 


Chapter Seven: The Horde

Henry rode the line past Station 60. 

He looked down at the factory floor. It was a vision of hell. 

The entire section was overrun. Hundreds of infected line workers, still wearing their grease-stained overalls, were tearing into the remaining survivors. 

The noise of the massive machinery completely masked the horrific sounds of the slaughter. You could see the men screaming, but you couldn't hear them over the roar of the stamping presses and the hiss of the pneumatics. 

The dead were everywhere. They were falling into the massive vats of industrial acid, they were being crushed by the unceasing hydraulic arms, but they kept coming. The sheer numbers were overwhelming the factory's safety protocols. 

Henry looked ahead. 

The conveyor belt was carrying him directly toward the final assembly area. 

The massive, three-story-tall stamping press. 

It was a terrifying machine, designed to take a flat sheet of heavy steel and crush it into the curved roof of a car with thousands of tons of hydraulic pressure. 

The belt carried the chassis directly under the massive, descending block of solid iron. 

Henry had to get off the line. 

He looked to the side, preparing to jump to a narrow catwalk. 

But the catwalk was completely packed with the dead, their milky eyes watching him ride past, their jaws snapping hungrily at the empty air. 

He was trapped on the moving belt. 


Chapter Eight: The Press

The massive stamping press loomed ahead, a colossal iron mouth opening and closing with terrifying, rhythmic power. 

*CRASH. Hiss.* *CRASH. Hiss.*

The sound was bone-jarring. 

Henry scrambled backward across the moving chassis, desperately trying to put distance between himself and the unyielding machine. 

But he bumped into something hard. 

He turned around. 

The zombie welder had caught up to him. 

The dead man's face was a ruined mess, torn by the robotic arms, but its grip on the acetylene torch was absolute. 

The zombie lunged, grabbing Henry's heavy leather boot. 

Henry kicked wildly, trying to dislodge the dead hand. 

"Let go!" he screamed, his voice lost in the roar of the factory. 

The zombie didn't let go. It pulled itself forward, the bright blue flame of the torch swinging dangerously close to Henry's leg. 

Henry looked ahead. They were thirty feet from the stamping press. 

He couldn't jump off the line. He was surrounded by the horde on the catwalks and the floor. He couldn't go forward. The press would crush him instantly. 

He was out of options. 


Chapter Nine: The Torch

Henry stopped kicking. He leaned forward and grabbed the heavy rubber hoses trailing behind the zombie welder. 

He pulled with all his strength, trying to yank the torch from the dead hand. 

The zombie resisted, its unyielding strength pulling back. 

In the chaotic struggle on the moving chassis, the bright blue flame of the acetylene torch swung wildly in a wide arc. 

It missed Henry, but it struck something far more dangerous. 

The conveyor belt was moving past a massive, fifty-gallon drum of industrial cleaning solvent, used to strip grease from the raw steel panels before painting. 

The solvent was highly volatile, essentially pure petroleum distillate. 

The 5000-degree flame of the acetylene torch brushed against the thin steel of the drum. 

The steel melted instantly, exposing the highly flammable liquid inside to the intense heat. 

The ignition was explosive. 

The fifty-gallon drum detonated with a massive, concussive roar. 

A blinding fireball erupted on the factory floor, instantly incinerating the zombies gathered nearby. 

The shockwave hit the conveyor belt violently, knocking Henry and the zombie flat against the steel chassis. 

The heat was intense, singing Henry's hair and blistering his skin. 

But the explosion didn't stop the belt. 


Chapter Ten: The Machine

The massive explosion sent a shockwave through the factory's electrical system, shorting out the emergency shut-off controls for the conveyor line. 

The massive belt continued to move, perfectly steadily, carrying the half-built car and the two men toward the massive stamping press. 

Henry struggled to his feet, his ears ringing loudly from the explosion. His vision was blurred by the thick, black smoke filling the factory. 

He looked down. 

The zombie welder was still holding his boot, its jaw snapping furiously, completely ignoring the massive inferno burning just a few feet away. 

Henry looked ahead. 

They were entering the massive iron mouth of the stamping press. 

The enormous, solid iron block, weighing hundreds of tons, was raised high above them, waiting for the precise mechanical moment to descend. 

Henry couldn't break the zombie's grip. He couldn't jump. 

He simply stopped fighting. 

He looked up at the massive, grease-stained iron block descending toward him. 

The stamping press didn't care about the venomous fever. It didn't care about the dead or the living. It only followed its unyielding mechanical programming. 

The massive iron block slammed downward with the force of an earthquake. 

Henry and the zombie welder were caught perfectly between the solid iron die and the heavy steel chassis. 

They were crushed instantly, reduced to a perfectly flat, bloody smear across the cold steel roof of the next 1935 Ford V8 rolling off the assembly line. In the heart of American industry, the machines finally won. No happy endings.



Story Thirty: The End of the Line

Chapter One: The Retreat

The venomous fever had swept across the Dustbowl, devouring everything from the Atlantic seaboard to the Rocky Mountains. By the winter of 1939, the remnants of the United States had been pushed to the absolute edge of the continent. 

Captain Miller stood at the southern approach of the Golden Gate Bridge in San Francisco. He was the commanding officer of the 4th Infantry, or what was left of it. His "battalion" consisted of forty exhausted, starving men, their uniforms in rags, their ammunition critically low. 

They had retreated for months, fighting a losing battle against an enemy that never slept, never starved, and recruited from the fallen. 

"Captain," Sergeant Reyes said, stepping up beside him. "The barricades are set on the bridge. The demolition charges are wired."

Miller nodded, staring out at the massive, iconic orange towers of the newly completed suspension bridge rising into the freezing fog. 

"Good," Miller rasped, his throat raw from the cold wind blowing off the Pacific Ocean. "We blow the span. We isolate ourselves in Marin County. It's our only chance."

"Sir," Reyes hesitated, looking across the bay toward the Marin headlands. 

Miller followed his gaze. 

Through the thick fog, a massive, bright orange glow illuminated the northern sky. It wasn't the sunrise. 

Marin County was burning. 

"The sickness," Miller whispered, the last shred of hope draining from his soul. "It beat us across the bay. They're already there."

They were trapped. The city of San Francisco was behind them, a silent tomb. The bridge was blocked by fire. And to their left was the vast, unforgiving expanse of the Pacific Ocean. 

There was nowhere left to run. 


Chapter Two: The Blockade

"What do we do, Captain?" Reyes asked, panic finally creeping into his voice. 

Miller looked at his men. They were hollow-eyed, trembling with exhaustion and fear. They had fought across a dying country, only to reach a dead end. 

"We hold the bridge," Miller said, drawing his heavy .45 caliber sidearm. "We hold it until the ammo runs out. Then, we use the bayonets. We don't go quietly, Sergeant."

Reyes nodded grimly, turning to the men. "Positions! Man the heavy guns!"

The soldiers scrambled to the barricades they had hastily constructed across the massive roadway of the bridge. They had three water-cooled Browning M1917 heavy machine guns, positioned behind sandbags and overturned civilian vehicles. 

Miller stood behind the center gun, peering into the thick fog hanging over the city of San Francisco. 

The city was silent. No sirens, no engines, no voices. 

Just the low, constant rumble of the ocean waves crashing against the concrete piers of the massive bridge below them. 

Then, a new sound emerged from the fog. 

It was a low, collective moan, a terrifying drone composed of millions of individual voices, blending together into a symphony of absolute despair. 

The silence of the city was broken by the sound of shuffling feet. 

Not thousands. Not tens of thousands. 

Millions. 


Chapter Three: The Ocean

The fog began to roll back, revealing the terrifying truth. 

The entire city of San Francisco was a solid, undulating mass of rotting flesh. 

The horde was unimaginable. It wasn't just the population of the city. It was the combined, infected mass of the entire western United States, driven relentlessly westward by the unyielding hunger, finally hitting the ocean. 

They filled the streets, the parks, the hills. They were a terrifying, silent wave of death, stretching from the bridge approach all the way to the horizon. 

And they were all moving toward the bridge. 

The sheer numbers defied comprehension. The entire landscape was literally painted in the pale gray of dead skin and the dark brown of dried blood. 

Miller stared at the horizon, his hand resting on the grips of the heavy Browning machine gun. 

He had fought the dead in New York, in Chicago, in the Dustbowl plains. But he had never seen anything like this. 

This wasn't a battle. This was an extinction event. 

"Mother of God," Reyes whispered from the adjacent gun, crossing himself. 

The vanguard of the massive horde reached the approach to the bridge.
