They didn't charge. They didn't scream. They simply walked forward, a slow, unstoppable tide of the dead, their milky white eyes fixed entirely on the forty living men standing behind the sandbags. 


Chapter Four: The Horizon

"Hold your fire!" Miller yelled, his voice cracking. "Let them get into range! We don't waste a single bullet!"

The tension on the bridge was agonizing. The soldiers sweated in the freezing wind, their fingers resting on the heavy triggers, watching the massive wave of death slowly approach. 

The dead stepped onto the paved roadway of the Golden Gate Bridge. 

They filled the six lanes completely, a solid wall of bodies pressing forward. 

"Three hundred yards," Reyes called out. 

"Wait," Miller said, his eyes narrowed. 

"Two hundred yards."

The smell hit them first. It was a dense, suffocating cloud of rot and decay, carried on the freezing wind. 

"One hundred yards," Reyes yelled, his voice tight with panic. 

Miller took a deep breath, looking down the barrel of the heavy machine gun. 

"Open fire!" Miller roared. 

The bridge erupted into deafening noise. 

The three heavy Browning machine guns opened up simultaneously, spitting a continuous, terrifying hail of heavy .30 caliber lead into the approaching horde. 

The soldiers armed with Springfield rifles joined in, firing as fast as they could work the bolts. 

The devastating firepower tore into the front ranks of the dead. 


Chapter Five: The Last Stand

The heavy machine guns were brutal. The heavy slugs tore through dead flesh and bone, shattering skulls and severing limbs. 

The front ranks of the horde were mowed down instantly, their bodies piling up on the paved roadway. 

But the Romero rule was uncompromising. Unless the brain was destroyed, the dead kept coming. 

And the sheer volume of the horde meant that for every zombie dropped by a bullet, ten more simply stepped over the fallen bodies, pressing the advance. 

The massive pile of dead bodies quickly became a horrific, bloody ramp. The horde simply climbed over their own destroyed dead to reach the barricades. 

"Keep firing!" Miller screamed, his hands vibrating violently as he held down the trigger of the heavy Browning. 

The gun was eating through the cloth ammunition belts at a terrifying rate. The thick brass casings rained down on the concrete deck, piling up around Miller's boots. 

The noise was deafening, a continuous, mechanical roar that drowned out the moans of the dead and the crash of the ocean below. 

They were dropping thousands of zombies. The roadway was completely choked with destroyed bodies. 

But the horde didn't thin out. It didn't slow down. The horizon was still a solid mass of the dead, pressing relentlessly onto the bridge. 


Chapter Six: The Ammo

The sustained, massive rate of fire began to take a toll on the heavy weapons. 

The water-cooled jackets of the Browning machine guns were boiling. The thick steam hissed loudly into the freezing air, creating a dense, white fog around the barricades. 

"Water! We need water for the guns!" Reyes yelled, frantically swapping out a red-hot barrel. 

But they had no water. They had used their canteens hours ago. 

"Keep firing until they melt!" Miller ordered, entirely focused on the advancing wall of dead flesh. 

The barrel of Miller's gun began to glow a dull, terrifying cherry red. The intense heat was warping the rifling, sending the heavy slugs spinning wildly off target. 

The gun finally jammed, a massive, catastrophic failure of overheated steel. 

Miller cursed, racking the heavy charging handle, but it was frozen solid. 

The gun was dead. 

He drew his .45 caliber sidearm and began firing into the horde. 

"I'm out!" a soldier yelled from the flank, dropping his empty rifle and drawing his heavy combat knife. 

"Fix bayonets!" Miller roared, his voice barely audible over the remaining gunfire. 

The soldiers didn't run. They knew there was nowhere to go. They attached their long, sharp bayonets to their empty rifles, preparing for the inevitable, brutal end. 

The horde, no longer suppressed by the heavy machine guns, surged over the pile of bodies and slammed into the sandbag barricades. 


Chapter Seven: The Cables

The barricades held for exactly ten seconds. 

The sheer weight of the thousands of zombies pressing against the sandbags from the front pushed the heavy defensive line backward, completely crushing the soldiers behind it. 

Reyes was pulled over the sandbags, screaming as dozens of dead hands dragged him into the horde. He disappeared instantly, consumed by the unyielding hunger. 

Miller fought like a demon, firing his .45 into the faces of the dead until the slide locked back empty. 

He dropped the gun and drew his heavy trench knife, stabbing wildly at the milky white eyes surrounding him. 

But the battle was over. The forty men were swallowed by the millions. 

But the horde didn't stop. 

Driven by the noise of the gunfire and the scent of blood, the millions of infected standing in the city continued to press forward onto the bridge. 

The Golden Gate Bridge was an engineering marvel, designed to support the weight of thousands of heavy vehicles and withstand hurricane-force winds. 

But it was not designed to support the static, densely packed weight of millions of human bodies. 

The entire one-and-a-half-mile suspension span was completely covered in a solid layer of the dead. 

The massive, three-foot-thick main suspension cables, strung between the towering orange pylons, began to hum with terrifying tension. 


Chapter Eight: The Bridge

The massive bridge began to groan. 

It was a deep, metallic sound that vibrated through the steel and concrete, louder than the moans of the dead. 

The center of the suspension span began to sag visibly under the unimaginable, millions-of-tons weight of the densely packed horde. 

The vertical suspender ropes, the smaller steel cables connecting the massive main cables to the roadway deck, began to snap. 

*PING. PING. PING.*

The sound was like massive, deadly guitar strings breaking. 

With every snapped suspender rope, the massive weight of the roadway and the dead shifted to the remaining cables, increasing the stress exponentially. 

Miller, bleeding heavily from a dozen bites, was pinned against the heavy steel railing of the bridge. 

He couldn't fight anymore. He was dying. 

He looked up at the massive orange pylon towering above him in the fog. 

The massive steel structure was shuddering violently. 

The bridge was failing. 

Miller smiled, a bloody, exhausted grimace. They hadn't needed the demolition charges after all. The dead were destroying the bridge themselves. 

*CRACK.*

A deafening, cannon-like explosion echoed across the bay. 


Chapter Nine: The Collapse

The primary suspension cable on the eastern side of the bridge snapped near the southern pylon. 

The massive, three-foot-thick steel cable, under millions of pounds of tension, whipped violently through the air. 

The release of tension was catastrophic. 

Without the eastern cable to support the weight, the entire massive roadway deck violently twisted and rolled toward the ocean. 

The massive steel trusses buckled and tore. The concrete deck shattered like thin glass. 

The scream of tearing steel was deafening, a horrific, unnatural sound that completely silenced the moans of the horde. 

The entire center span of the Golden Gate Bridge, thousands of tons of steel, concrete, and millions of dead bodies, plummeted downward. 

It was a slow, agonizing collapse, a majestic, terrifying fall of the greatest monument of American engineering. 

Miller, clinging to the steel railing, rode the massive, twisting structure down into the abyss. 

He didn't scream. He simply closed his eyes, accepting the end of the line. 


Chapter Ten: The Dust

The impact was apocalyptic. 

The massive center span of the bridge struck the freezing, dark waters of the San Francisco Bay with the force of a massive bomb. 

A colossal wave of displaced water shot hundreds of feet into the air, instantly swamping the shattered remains of the concrete piers. 

Millions of zombies, packed tightly onto the roadway, were pulverized by the impact or dragged deep into the freezing, crushing depths of the Pacific Ocean. 

The Romero sickness didn't require oxygen, but the immense pressure at the bottom of the bay crushed the dead skulls, finally, permanently destroying the brains of the massive horde. 

Captain Miller died instantly upon impact, his body lost in the twisted steel and concrete wreckage of the fallen bridge. 

The massive orange pylons stood alone in the freezing fog, monuments to a dead civilization. 

The venomous fever had swept across the entire continent, consuming everything in its path. But at the edge of the world, the ocean had finally stopped it. 

The Dustbowl was quiet. The cities were empty. The factories were still. 

The dead had inherited the earth, but they had destroyed everything in the process, leaving nothing but silence and ashes. No happy endings.
